# Chasing Rhinos With The Swami. Volume 1

## About author

A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada changed the world. When he came to America from India in 1965, few Westerners knew the deep spiritual truths behind ancient Vedic culture and civilization. Within a decade most humans on the planet had heard the Hare Krishna mantra.

Shyamasundar das (Sam Speerstra) was one of Bhaktivedanta Swami's earliest disciples, helping him shape the Krishna Consciousness movement in the Haight-Ashbury district of San Francisco, in London, and then, as the Swami's personal secretary, he traveled with Prabhupada in India, Russia, and all over the globe. “Chasing Rhinos With The Swami” is the intimate account of Prabhupada’s amazing adventures, describing in loving detail the seven years Shyamasundar spent at the side of the perfect person. Nothing was impossible for Srila Prabhupada, and he instilled this idea in the hearts of his first disciples: Go for the rhino!

**We should always be enthusiastic to try for capturing the rhinoceros. That way, if we fail, everyone will say, “Never mind, nobody can catch a rhinoceros anyway,” and if we succeed, then everyone will say, “Just see what a wonderful thing they have done!”**

**—A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami**

## Prologue

Blood flew from its four-inch fangs as the feeding jaguar roared just feet from my startled face. I wheeled and ran with Jose, my Venezuelan Indian guide, crashing through thick jungle, certain the enraged three-hundred-pound beast be on us in a moment. “Krishna! Krishna! Krishna!’' I screamed, not stopping until we’d scrambled to the highest point in a clearing of wind-fallen trees. We threw off our packs and sat, back-to-sweat-soaked-back, on a single green log, trembling with effort and dread while hours passed and the big cat shrieked and coughed, broke branches, and circled us unseen, furious that we’d interrupted its fresh-killed feast. From time to time, downwind, we could smell its rancid breath and gore-soaked coat. Low skies opened and rain poured on our huddled forms. I whipped my battered machete back and forth at the slightest sound. Jose had three cane arrows. He dipped their tips into the small clay pot of poison tied at his waist, nocked one arrow in his long bow, and laid two arrows beside us on the log. As night fell we awaited our uncertain fate.

I was looking for gold and diamonds—legendary streams of gold and diamonds—in the uncharted headwaters of Rio Cuyuni, in Bolivar State, south Venezuela. It was late in the rainy season of October 1985. This was my third prospecting expedition into these remote jungles in the past three years and by far the worst. We had left the village of El Dorado a month earlier: twenty-two men in four motorized canoes, headed west up the unexplored Cuyuni. We were now about 150 river-miles somewhere west of El Dorado, and so far, three of our men were dead.

O Prabhupada, you got me into this mess. Where is it all going?!

It was now twelve years since I’d left the Swami’s side. Bhaktivedanta Swami—we called him Prabhupada, “the master at whose feet all other masters sit”—was my life and soul. Pie had taught me about

Krishna, God, the Supreme Person, and how everything in His creation is perfect. He taught me who I am, where I’m going, and what to do along the way. The Swami’s physical beauty and sense of fun, his unlimited daring energy and comprehensive knowledge of every subject, convinced me that I had found the perfect teacher. I became his disciple and am bound to him forever. Prabhupada was my pirate chief, the most courageous person I’d ever met, and he aroused in me and my fellow disciples the desire to chase the rhino, go for the stars, pursue our wildest dreams—but in service to Krishna. Don’t worry, Prabhupada assured us, Krishna has promised to personally protect those who take risks on His behalf. During the seven years I sat at his feet, the Swami encouraged me to seek precious minerals in the earth, and I made it my life’s work to earn millions in this way and spend it spreading the Swami’s message far and wide. I was testing Krishna’s promise to the extreme.

As cold night fell, the jaguar returned to its kill. Its intermittent rumbling or a sudden bellow warned us that the huge cat was still nearby. I made brief, 360-degree sweeps with my weakening flashlight, expecting to see its angry yellow eyes, until the batteries finally died. The Indian and I stared shivering at the dark, fighting sleep and feeling each other’s warm desperation through the backs of our shirts. I called him Jose. He was short and finely formed. His black hair looked like somebody had put a bowl on his head and trimmed around its edge. His face and muscular arms were painted with rosettes and squiggly lines of red achiote and black charcoal. He was barefoot, dressed in beat-up swim trunks and the t-shirt I'd given him for this expedition, now ripped and torn, which had once said “Beer Makes Better Lovers.” Jose was a warrior of the Yanomami tribe, “the fierce people.”

I was forty-three years old, and for twelve years had plunged headlong into the life of a prospector and miner—rubies in India, lapis lazuli in the High Sierras, goldmines in the Mother Lode country and Mojave Desert of California. My ventures had been successful, but in the boom-bust cycle typical to bonanza miners everywhere, as soon as I got big money, events (and Krishna) conspired to take it all away. This happened again and again. I became hooked on the sheer excitement of my adventures, and this passion gradually took over and blurred my goal. By now it was the seeking and the finding alone that flung me to the ever-wilder regions where Nature hides her most precious treasures. My original purpose—to get rich and broadcast Krishna’s name throughout the world—had become secondary to the thrill and novelty of extreme prospecting, to living on the edge.

О Krishna, what am I doing out here? Are You still with me? Have I gone too far?

We had been moving upstream for nearly a month. The huge Rio Cuyuni broke into branches, then streams, then small creeks (que-bradas), and the gold in our samples got richer—and coarser. We were nearing the vein source of the gold. One of the men with us, Chaco, had died an agonizing death from the bite of a fer-de-Iance (mapanare) on the third day out; another man—I never did know his name—had been found dead in his hammock from unknown causes two weeks into the journey; and just a week ago, Sam, my diver and namesake from British Guyana, had brushed against the 880 volts of a temblador, an eight-foot electric eel, and had drowned blue-faced at the bottom of a nameless river. After each death, men were dispatched to bring the bodies downstream to El Dorado. We were down to fourteen men. When the stream got too small for navigation, we parked the canoes and started walking.

Suddenly, Jose began to sing a chant in some god-knows-what language. I joined him as best I could, the twentieth-century man and the stone-age warrior, back, to back, raising our voices to challenge death and utter darkness in a primordial invocation for divine help. Then I started chanting the maha-mantra: “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna...” Slow at first, reviving the taste, remembering Prabhupadas calm beauty, remembering his eyes on mine as he parted the curtains of doubt and showed me the Supreme Person— “Krishna, Krishna...”Jose had no idea what I was singing, but he knew it was a prayer and joined with a nervous wailing version of his own: “KRISH-NA! RA-MA! HA-RE НА-RE!” Hour after hour we sang in synch at the top of our lungs: Krishna! Please save me!

A day earlier, just before dark I had climbed a hundred-foot tree next to our camp in order to see if there were any highlands around us that might indicate quartz veins. From the top I saw a low jun-gled ridge a few miles to the northwest. The gold must be coming from there! Hanging from my arms I did a chin-up to eyeball what looked like a stable perch in the highest branches. In the crotch of the tree, inches from my face, was a small snake, shiny light-green with yellow eyes, coiled and testing the air with its tongue. Viboral Tree viper! No time to think—an eye blink and it strikes—and I let go and dropped, plummeting, crashing through branches, grasping at limbs, yelling KRISHNA! all the way down, and landing with a thump on the forest floor, winded but unharmed.

Krishna, are You saving me for something? By serving You these years, have I somehow earned this right to survive nature’s cruelest tests? Or am I wasting good karma on extreme adventures, frittering away my hard-won spiritual credit? What have I done over these years to spread the Swami’s message? How much closer am I now to You? How much more do I love You? Has my luck run out? Will the next snake nail me? Will the next mosquito end this marvelous, risk-all mining fantasy?

“Krishna!” I shouted again into the dark.

On this morning, leaving the rest of the men in camp, Jose and I had set out, machetes in hand, on a northwesterly compass bearing, expecting to intersect the fabled ridge by late afternoon. Visions of foot-thick veins of solid gold played in my head. Jose led the way, a longbow and quiver on his back and my batea (wooden gold pan) upside-down on his head like a hat. By mid-afternoon the terrain was rising and we knew we were close. As we hacked our way into a steep ravine, Jose pointed to a set of fresh, splayed hoofprints— four toes in front, three in back—made by a running animal. “Danta (tapir),” he said and paused as he smelled the air. He seemed worried for a moment. Tapirs are the favorite prey of jaguars. A few thorny vines separated us from the small stony streambed where I hoped to find lumps of gold-shot quartz, but as I raised my machete to part the veil, the jaguar s roar from hell blasted my eardrums.

The long night wears on, heavy fog descends, and the jaguar is at last silent. For hours now we have been shouting the Hare Krishna mantra into the dark. Adrenalin spent, Jose and I are taking turns watching while the other curls up on the log and tries to sleep. I take the first watch. My mind wanders, too exhausted to brush at the dozens of ants and other insects that crawl across my body. I ponder the fleetingness of life, the frenzied struggle for existence so apparent around me—and think how lucky I’ve been to find the perfect master to guide me through it. Prabhupada called it the secret of all secrets: just serve Krishna and surrender to Him. But can I surrender? Stop this nonsense? What’s holding me back? Despite my failings, will I be redeemed? I ponder the power of Krishna and how He personally guides the galactic interplay of uncountable intelligent activities over untold billions ofyears—all leading to this singular moment in the jungle of Venezuela on November 23, no, it’s the 24th, at—let’s check my watch—1:07 a.m., in 1985, still hours from dawn—and nineteen earth years since that time in the lookout tower in the mountains of central Oregon where my great adventure with the Swami began.

## San Francisco

### 1

In May of 1966 mу girlfriend Melanie and I were arrested near Seattle for possession and intent to sell illegal substances. This experience put the fear of God in us and, out on bail, we split town, resolved to put as much distance as possible between us and our druggie friends. A few days later we were hired on as fire lookouts, isolated for the summer in a tower atop Trout Creek Butte in central Oregon, paid $2.05 an hour by the US Forest Service to spot fires in a vast area of the Cascade Mountains.

From the ninety-foot steel tower built in 1933,I looked out over a thousand square miles of wilderness forest broken only by lava flows and volcanic peaks called, from south to north, the Three Sisters, Mt. Washington, Three Fingered Jack, and Mt. Jefferson. In August, the Oxbow Ridge fire had raged over 66 square miles of thick fir and pine in Lane County, thirty miles west of us, and though the hre was out, even now, in the second week of October, we could still smell smoke. I had spotted six fires during the long summer season, five from lightning, one man-caused, and had radioed their locations to my boss, Bill Foster, at Forest Service headquarters in the small town of Sisters, eight miles away. By early fall, it hadn’t rained in weeks; fire danger was high. But that was about to change. The tiny battery-powered radio set in my tower began to crackle.

“Calling Sugar 501, Sugar 501, this is base—you copy, Sam? Over.” “Yeah, Bill, loud and clear. What’s up?”

“How’s it look up there?”

“Well, there’s a huge wall of black clouds over North Sister, high winds—I already see a few flakes of snow. Yeah, it’s snowing—looks like heavy weather coming in. Over.”

“10-4. Keep me posted, Sam. If we get enough moisture from this storm, you can go home! Over and out.”

Jesus. Almost five months, seven days a week I’m trapped in this damn tower, twelve, fifteen hours a day. “Home?” Where’s that? Looking around my 6' x 6' metal box in the sky, I see the stacks of books: Aldous Huxley, Hermann Hesse, Jung—Gurdjieff and Madam Blavatsky, for chrissakes—every book on Buddhism and yoga and the mystic East I could find. Through the north-facing window, ninety feet below I see our 20' x 20' Forest-Service-green cabin with stovepipe through the roof spouting smoke now that it has turned cold, and I think, This is as much “home” as we’ve got right now.

There hasn’t been much for me to do up here all summer but read. Melanie, well, she rides her horse. Fine, gives the girl something to do every day: feed the horse and ride it. She wanders all over the mountain on that nag. She keeps the deer out of her little garden, putters around, but I’m stuck up here dawn to dusk, reading. Once in a while I sight through my nifty Osborne rangefinder, looking for smoke signals—especially if we get lightning.

And then, of course, there are the drugs. Melanie drives about eight miles on logging roads down Brush Draw to Sisters once a week or so to pick up our drugs at the post office, mostly speed, mailed down from Dave in Portland. Getting stoned every day gets us through the solitude. After the Seattle bust, we don’t have many real friends anyway, friends you’d trust with your deepest thoughts. My friend Mike left last spring for New York to play jazz, and a letter from Joanie last week says she’s back there too, for Mike’s wedding to her little sister, Jan. Wedding? Nobody gets married anymore.

Yeah, but it does get lonely up here. Other than my Dad, who came over from Salem for a day back in July, and a couple of lost hunters, we’ve hardly seen another soul up here. An old rancher named Cleary loaned Melanie the horse and an egg-laying chicken, and he gave us a mixed collie sheepdog we named Ralph. These critters, along with our two cats, seem like the only friends we’ve got these days. Once in a while, Cleary swings by to check on his horse and bring us some weak weed he grows by his house over near Prineville. Lonely days. Boring days. Sometimes we shake things up with a hit of dynamite acid, which Rock Scully mailed up from San Francisco. Nice stuff, but the last time I did it, an electric storm moved in and I could feel the tower about to crash and lightning is hitting the trees and I see these beings in balls of light under the roof overhang shouting my name—freaked me out.

In this remote environment I’m reading all the books I can find about spiritual life, especially anything about spiritual masters or teachers of higher knowledge. On LSD trips Melanie and I had glimpsed a glorious Reality, beautiful beyond words, pulsing with excitement and free from pain and suffering, outside the grim reality offered by our limited senses. We were convinced we could enter this realm without the short-term use of drugs if only we could obtain the roadmap from someone who knew the way: a guru.

Books, drugs—anything to blot out thoughts of what had happened last May, just six months ago.

May 10, 1966, was my twenty-fourth birthday. Melanie and I drove four hours to Seattle from our little rented house in Portland in the ’56 Ford estate wagon I had recently purchased with my ill-gotten gains. Road trip! After a long day selling a couple of apple boxes of peyote cactus, a few ounces of methedrine, maybe 1000 hits of liquid psilocybin—and passing out a lot of free samples on the university campus—and now with almost $2000 cash in my pocket, Melanie and I drove back to our friend’s place in Kirkland, Washington, for my birthday party. We walked in the door. “Hands up! It’s the police!” They poured out of the kitchen, the hall, from behind the couch-ten cops in plainclothes and uniforms, pointing guns. “Lay down on the floor!” The small-town fuzz slapped on cuffs and ratcheted them down hard, like they’d learned in cop school, then grinned at each other over a job well done. Next day it’s the headlines: “Biggest Drug Bust in NW History!” My parents in Salem, Oregon, see my mugshot—hair out to here—on the TV news at six o’clock.

I’m just a kid, from a nice home—a college grad. How’s this happening to me? In the cell I’m thinking, corny as it sounds: Dear God, if I get out of this somehow I will look for You and never do anything again to put myself in this position. Please save me!

I shake off these bad memories to watch a few fat snowflakes spiral past my mountaintop windows. What’s this? A car coming into the clearing below—up here? Must be elk or bear hunters— we’ve seen a couple in the last week or two. Snow is sticking now; I can see the tracks. One by one they get out of the car—a new one, out-of-state plates. Wait a second! It’s Joanie—and Mike—yeah, and who’s the other girl? Jan, the little sister. Better get my ass down the ladder. Maybe some new drugs have arrived.

“Hey, Sam!”

“Michael, I can’t believe it. You found us up here!”

“Sam-ums!”

“Joanie! I thought you guys were in New York!”

“Yeah, we drove out from Manhattan—we’ve been in that damn car for three days.”

We’re dancing around laughing and hugging, and Melanie and dog Ralph run out of the cabin and join the commotion.

“Grab your stuff. Let’s get inside before we freeze to death!”

Wind hammering our tiny Forest Service cabin now, snow scratching the windows in gusts, 5436 feet on top of Trout Creek Butte—Mike, Joan, Jan, and me and Melanie all piled in the small space with our cats, Ralph, and the chicken in her corner cage next to the roaring woodstove. Everyone’s talking a mile a minute. Joanie’s squealing, “We found him, the Teacher!”

Then Mike Grant, at twenty-four, not much expression on his face, says he has a spiritual master. “Yeah, I got initiated by the Swami, big ceremony with like a campfire in the middle of the room—and he gave me a new name.”

Mike seemed so different. But what was it? I handed him a joint, showing off (see, even out here in the wilderness...), and he said no thanks, appreciating the offer, but “We took a vow to the Swami: no more intoxicants.”

InTOXicants? A little grass?

Mike Grant, my Reed College hero: in his black beret and shades, tickling the ivories at 2 a.m. in that dark coffeehouse on Stark Street in Portland, guzzling Gallo Special Stock Tokay from a shared gallon jug on Friday afternoons—the guy who sold me my first matchbox of grass for $10 (mostly stems and seeds), who turned this innocent farm boy from Salem on to Lenny Bruce and John Coltrane—my hero, with a new name like Moo-koonda?—telling me, “Yeah, you got to meet this guy, the Swami.”

**\* \* \***

In this present body, I emerged as the only son of white, middle-class parents and grew up, along with two younger sisters, in post-WWII America. I had the Hopalong Cassidy bike, played kick-the-can on Rose Street with my buddies, took violin lessons, and had a paper route for the Capital Journal. Saturdays I’d ride the bike out to my Dad’s big farm five miles from Salem, Oregon, for solitary expeditions through the thickly forested hills, imagining diamonds beneath the moss, a ruby mine up a slope in those Douglas firs, gobs of gold in the little seasonal creek. Pedaling home I’d collect beer and pop bottles from the ditches (a penny for the stubbies, 2 cents for quarts, 3 cents for Coke and Nehi sodas) to support my candy habit.

At home I’d show Mom my milk-of-magnesia bottle half-full of water and mica flakes, all the “gold” I’d found that day, and she’d say, “Show your grandfather when he comes to visit.” Her father was Jean Paul Mestrezat, the mysterious, white-haired man who showed up from time to time from faraway Alaska, where he lived with Indians, ate salmon, and panned for gold under borealis skies in the Klondike.

My father’s mother, Frances—we called her Gaga—with her Dutch-immigrant husband and three children, came out to Oregon from Wisconsin in the early part of the twentieth century. As a young boy I would visit her small farmhouse in the hills near Falls City, where she taught me to use the hand water pump by her kitchen sink and chop kindling for her stove. She told me how her mother used to hide the kids under the bed whenever Indians stepped onto the porch looking for pancakes. She loved Jesus, and would speak with him every day. On my tenth birthday, Gaga gave me a copy of the New Revised Standard Version of the Holy Bible, and for about a year I read it cover to cover, not really understanding much but getting a feel for, yes, God must be a Person.

Like many kids in the late ’40s, early ’50s with no TV, I collected stamps. My favorites were from British colonies, especially the feudal states of India, like Travancore, Cochin, Hyderabad, Mysore: Queen Victoria and King George with their crowns, sometimes a stately prince in turban and earrings, mustache, a strand of big pearls. My mother subscribed to Book-of-the-Month Club, and I lost myself in books like Коп-Tiki, Seven Years in Tibet, Michener’s Return to Paradise, and I traveled deep into the mysterious Amazon with Col. Leonard Clark in The Rivers Ran East.

I got a radio from Aunt Ellen for my twelfth birthday, and I’d sneak that radio under the covers at bedtime. Some warm summer nights, after the neighborhood had settled down (there were crickets and you’d hear the thumping of freight cars on the other side of town), I could get KSL from Salt Lake City—wherever that was—where they broadcast music called jazz—a cat named Wes Montgomery playing “West Coast Blues.” Some nights, from 10 to 11,l could catch the top twenty hits on “Lucky Lager Dance Time.” Frank Sinatra doing “Three Coins In A Fountain” or Patti Page, “How Much Is That Doggie In The Window?” On a fateful night in 1954 I heard a song called “Shake, Rattle and Roll” by Bill Haley and the Comets, and my life changed forever.

The kids in my neighborhood were all right as far as sharing tree forts, comic books, baseball cards, and innocent games, but I was onto something new. Just then, I entered junior high school and met some kids from poorer neighborhoods across town who dug this new rock-and-roll—and with pegged pants, Vitalis hair oil and a ducktail cut, thick-soled brogue shoes with curling tips, I leaped into their world. The older guys built hotrods and smoked Lucky Strikes; the girls wore bobby socks and lipstick. Cycling home past the high school I’d see the girls throw their books in the back of a chopped deuce roadster and hop in after, showing legs in their red pleated skirts. This was the life for me: rebel without a cause.

But the late ’50s was the time, too, of hydrogen bombs and sputniks, and there was pressure on kids to study science. Captain Nemo, the Shadow, Plastic Man, Batman, even Superman showed us that real heroes triumph by knowing science. Maybe it was subtle coercion by my folks—or a feeling of obligation to them—but I also sensed there was a lot to be gained from teachers and books, so I studied advanced math and physics, kept my grades high, made some sports teams, ran for student office, worked on the school paper—even as the deepest part of me looked for answers from Little Richard, Bo Diddly, and Elvis Presley. I just wanted something more.

**\* \* \***

Eighth grade, 1955. Joanie Oslund sat at the desk in front of mine in homeroom class at Leslie Junior High in Salem, Oregon. Homeroom was a study period when you did homework for other classes. She was cute with her raven-black pigtails, vivacious like Annette Funicello on Mickey Mouse Club—so I was immediately attracted. She embarrassed easily, so she soon became the fall guy for my pranks. I’d pull her pigtails, make her giggle, and watch her explain herself red-faced to the teacher without ratting me out. She was OK.

Joan lived up on Fairmount Hill, a leafy section of Salem where they blocked off Fir Street for sledding on the rare occasions it snowed. We’d walk over to Joanie’s place to dry out and slurp tomato soup and chomp peanut butter sandwiches on Wonder bread. Over that year we became close buddies, sharing songs from the new rock-and-roll, emotional stuff in our lives—her step-dad, my demanding mother—as we clawed our ways deeper into adolescence. We became special pals, not boyfriend-girlfriend, but confidants on a more serious level, like, “How’d you like the hayride with Ricky?”

Then Joan moved away to Portland or somewhere, and I quickly forgot her. In 1957, ninth grade, there was this huge controversy in all the papers and magazines about a book called Howl! by some bearded poet—they called him a “beatnik”—named Allen Ginsberg. I wrote off to City Lights Bookstore in San Francisco, sent a money order for $7.55 + 15 cents postage. I read the small book every night in my bed, hiding it under the mattress. My Mom found the book and threw it away, but not before it had changed my life completely: Wake up, America, there’s a wild new generation howling for change. In the next few months I acquired Jack Kerouac’s On the Road, and in 1958 his Dharma Bums, and practically memorized these books.

On the morning of September 17, 1959,1 was into my second week of senior year at South Salem High, driving to school in my 1946 Ford pickup truck with “Rhoten & Speerstra Farms” painted on the doors, listening to the radio: “Mack the Knife” was number one, followed by Elvis’s “Big Hunk o’ Love” and hypnotic steel guitar “Sleepwalk,” by Santo and Johnny. The DJ was talking about a partial lunar eclipse tonight—all but the very crown of the moon. I thought about the sock hop at noon in the gym, cross-country practice after school. Maybe I’ll watch “The Untouchables” on TV later, and step outside during ads to catch the lunar eclipse. On that same day—unknown to me—across the world in Mathura, India, my eternal spiritual master took his vows of renunciation and became Abhay Charan Bhaktivedanta Swami.

I was raised in a place where most men made a living from the earth: farmers, loggers, fishermen, miners. If your car broke down, you and your pals could fix it. For the “Career Choices” reports we had to make in high school, I studied two occupations: lawyer, like my dad (lawyers averaged $12,000 a year), and forest ranger ($4000 a year). My father made a lot more than twelve grand, but his stuffy offices and boring routines didn’t appeal, and my mind was heavily weighted toward a career outdoors. Nevertheless, I had a hunger for knowledge and thirsted to learn what this world was about. In the fall of 1960 I entered Reed College in Portland, Oregon.

Mike Grant was the mysterious roommate of a Reed drinking buddy named Stewart. We’d start our weekend binges in Stewart’s dorm room on Friday afternoons with a $1.29 gallon jug of Gallo Special Stock Tokay, and sometimes Mike would show up. He never slept in his bed there. He was elusive—had a secret life outside Reed. He played jazz piano in late-night coffee houses, rarely studied or went to class, maybe had a wife and another pad somewhere?

If Mike was there, he’d turn us on to the latest Lenny Bruce or Miles Davis albums, Sketches ofSpain on a rain-gloomy November Friday afternoon. Or some weird Japanese tuba music. He was just so cool, with his dark glasses and new hip talk. Humble to the point of self-effacing, but strong when he did say something. His father owned Madrona Records in downtown Portland—I grew up hearing “Come on down to Al Grant’s Madrona Records for the latest Top-10 hits!” on radios all over Oregon. The son of Al Grant, man. This cat knew.

Dave Colt, my new best buddy at Reed, was a beatnik from&zn Francisco (wow, Kerouac, Ginsberg, Ferlinghetti) who rode a 500cc Matchless motorcycle. He was tall, cynical, wise, laid-back—a bit scary for this provincial farm boy in his boots and greasy jeans, stomping around with wrench in hand but able to write “Crass and feeble imprecations avail not, merely revealing the verbal incompetence of their purveyor” below “Dave Colt is a dork” on the lavatory wall. Both of us were math and physics majors, but when problems in cardinal set theory or quantum physics had me mystified, Dave could solve them in seconds in his head.

In 1961 I was a sophomore at Reed and thought I recognized the good-looking gal behind the cosmetics counter as I breezed into Meier & Frank department store in downtown Portland. “Joanie?!” We picked up where we left off, now with freedom rolled into the mix. I introduced her to my college friends, and she quit her job and quickly settled into our bohemian scene, discarding her bouffant for simple long hair put up with a Spanish comb and wearing leather sandals and long plain dresses.

Sophomore year, Dave and Mike rented an old Gothic clapboard house on a steep hill in Goose Hollow in SW Portland, across the Willamette River from Reed. This battered green house we called “The Beanstalk” after Benzedrine, the little pills of diamphetamine we called “bennies” or “beans.” “Hey, you got any beans, man? I got a test tomorrow.” Drugs were creeping into our lives on top of the alcohol, but it was 1961, so still mostly cheap red wine and West coast jazz and rebellion, not yet revolution. Joanie fit right into this scene, enrolling for calligraphy and pottery classes at Reed, and she took up residence with my pals in the Beanstalk. My best friend Dave became Joanie’s boyfriend, and her best friend, Eve, became my first serious girlfriend.

**\* \* \***

In the summer of 1962, I left Eve and Joanie and Mike and the Portland scene—left Reed College and the stiff regimen of advanced science—hopped a ship in Hoboken and steamed off to Europe for a yеаr, telling my parents I was going “to study” at a small school in Switzerland, but really I went to ski and explore this exciting new world. Albert Schweitzer College was a little coed school of thirty-five students specializing in philosophy, tucked into a tiny village in the German-speaking Alps. There were ski lifts across the street. Perfect. Reverend Doctor Joachim Wolff, who had fled Nazi Germany to settle in Switzerland, instructed us in phenomenology, Edmund Husserl, and the concepts in Heidegger’s Being and Time.

There were five or six American kids; the rest were from a smattering of nations. My assigned roommate was an American guy named Rock Scully, whose dad was the well-known liberal thinker Milton Mayer. Rock had studied in Vienna and knew his way around and, like me, loved to ski. (Later, he would play a major role in my life as a Haight-Ashbury pioneer and manager of the Grateful Dead.)

Another guy who showed up at ASC was Mitch, who had just been booted out of Harvard for partaking in Dr. Timothy Leary’s experiments with hallucinogens. Mitch had the dark eyes of his Honduran mother and wore a silly grin like he always knew something we didn’t. We were all around eighteen or twenty years old, away from home, studying philosophy, and we had money from our parents—the perfect ingredients for mayhem. In those days you could buy LSD-125 over the counter at Sandoz Labs in Basel, about a three-hour train ride from school. I began to see myself as a seeker, although I wasn’t sure of what, yet, but of something higher.

It was still a big world in 1962, only partially explored (Mt. Everest had first been climbed only nine years earlier), and young people had a sense that there were many magical places left to explore—the pioneer spirit of our immediate predecessors. National Geographic magazine strongly shaped our young lives. In the small library at Albert Schweitzer College I picked up a copy of the October 1962 issue, “Wintering in the High Himalayas.” Sir Edmund Hillary and his team spent seven months at the 19,000-foot level near Everest to study the effects of high altitude and cold. One day, a “mystic” walked into their camp; he’d been seen by the Sherpas wandering around up there in subzero temperatures for several days, barefoot, in thin clothes. The photo of his feet stuck in my mind; they were wide and thickly callused. “His powers, akin to those of a yogi, also enabled him to munch on glass tubing from our lab with no apparent ill effect.” Physiologists tested him and found that he had “an amazing ability to control the circulation of blood to his extremities.” Yogi! Yes, here was proof for my young imagination: the inner world, the spirit, is stronger than the outer world, living proof from the mystic East—I must one day go there and search for this...

Christmas break, 1962, I hitchhiked with Mitch down to Morocco, where he had some beatnik friends who smoked kif and hashish and wrote poems for the Evergreen Review. En route we stopped in Zurich to visit some of Karl Jung’s close disciples, who were living in Goethe’s old house. Jung had just died the year before, and his adherents believed that angels interfered in our lives and guided us; one serene, grandmotherly woman, who seemed to know me, said, “You will find your master. All will be well.” Penniless, we stumbled into Tangiers to dream the Arab hashish dream. Then one day I woke up and realized I hadn’t left this tiny unheated room since we had arrived a month earlier. I made my way through the complex alleyways and souks of the Casbah to the American Consulate and telegraphed my uncle to wire me $100. When I got back to school in Switzerland I was totally changed: I must find the source of this great, beautiful creation! Coincidence, chance occurrences, what Jung called “synchronicity,” now guided my life. I’d always sensed this, but now I had words for it. Master: the word took root. But there was still a rocky road ahead.

I realized that I had joined a pilgrimage to the East, seemingly a definite and single pilgrimage—but in reality, this expedition to the East was not only mine and now; this procession of believers and disciples had always and incessantly been moving towards the East, towards the Home of Light.

—Hermann Hesse, Journey to the East

**\* \* \***

I returned to Reed in the fall of ’63, switching majors from physics to philosophy. Dave and Joanie had rented a two-story house in Portland on SW Corbett Avenue near the old Beanstalk. We lost track of Mike, except to hear from time to time that he was playing jazz in New York. Dave, Joanie, and I got deeper into drugs and esoteric studies—she had her calligraphy and pottery, I took up Rimbaud and Goethe.

In December 1963, Christmas break, Joanie and I borrowed Dave’s beat-up car, a ’50s Dodge station wagon called the Bronze Hog for the bright bronze color Dave sprayed over it one drunken day, and we drove day and night to visit friends in San Francisco and L.A. We dropped bennies and yakked about God and poetry and music and the meaning of life all the way down, like Sal and Marylou in Kerouac’s On the Road. We stopped at Rock Scully’s new place at 1334 Waller Street, near Golden Gate Park. These were still the beatnik days, and North Beach was the main scene. But Rock and a few guys had rented this cheap huge Victorian house in the center of a neighborhood called Haight-Ashbury. (Little did Rock and I dream in our freewheeling little island on Waller Street that in just four years’ time, a hundred thousand friends would join the party— the most happening neighborhood on the planet—and that millions more would be counted as our extended psychedelic family.)

What a scene it was at Rock’s house: the early flowering of peace and love. A friend of Rock’s named Sweeney had just scored some pharmaceutical mescaline and asked Joanie and me—if we could use the Bronze Hog—would we like to go up with him and his girlfriend to a beach in Marin County to trip on the real stuff? Yeah! All I can remember is the vast labyrinth of driftwood on the beach, the cosmic beauty of the wind and surf—and the drive back to the city. I was driving, and the Hog seemed so long I was sure it wouldn’t bend around the next corner, but I could feel every bolt and moving part in the vehicle, and like plastic the car would ooze around the bend to hilarious laughter from the four of us while “Ode to Joy” reverberated in neon around the heather-covered hills.

This December of 1963 also saw another major character come into my life. Only about three people really lived at Rock’s house on Waller Street, even though there were usually from ten to twenty wild characters moving about the place. One of the guys who actually paid some rent was Larry, who had a blond, curly beard. He was always playing a bamboo flute. Very calm, smiling, laid-back. His girlfriend was Melanie. She would ride up to the curb on her motorcycle and dash into the house with some wild announcement. I thought, What a chick. Tough, independent, but sweet and unaffected. Later, I would know her quite well.

Back in Portland, I finished my 1964 junior year at Reed, then got a summer job in Salem installing lawn-sprinkling systems. Dave Colt had quit Reed, and he and Joanie had moved to San Francisco. By late June, with my hip friends gone, I was burned out digging ditches, so I quit my job and drove my VW down to San Francisco to hang out with Rock in the Haight district or with Dave and Joanie in their loft above the sandal shop on Grant Street in North Beach, where Dave cobbled leather and Joanie threw pots. Maybe I’d find a job myself.

Some nights I’d crash on Rock’s couch, shout and whoop all day around the kitchen table, passing joints with the young and restless who were emerging as the original hippies. A couple of Rock’s friends, George Hunter and Dan Hicks, formed a band called “The Charlatans,” and they had just returned from a month-long gig at the Red Dog Saloon in Virginia City, Nevada. Their dress—cowboy clothes and Edwardian high-collared suits and vests, pointy-toed boots—looked so great that we all started buying that stuff from Goodwill. Some of the other music guys who hung around Rock’s place called their band The Lovin’ Spoonful.

Other nights I’d crash at Dave and Joanie’s place in North Beach. We’d smoke a stash of opium some guy found when he was cleaning out an old tunnel below nearby Chinatown, and groove to cool jazz.

Rock Scully: We were so much more innocent then. We didn’t have a clue. That attitude, just do what we feel like doing right now, no thought about the consequences. We all got on this wave, and we looked up and down the wave, and there was a bunch of other people surfin’ the same wave. Quite a mix of people and ideas, but everyone feeling that anything’s possible, just do it. We expected magic every day.

All this fun in the 1964 San Francisco summer was great, but I like to start what I finish—and there was so much more to learn—so I returned to the Corbett Street house in Portland to finish my last year at Reed. I went down to SF whenever I could—do some speed, drive overnight, pick up some dope, drive back. Whenever we’d get together, Joanie and I would take up where we left off: What’s the meaning of life? What is Bob Dylan saying? How do these drugs fit in? What is this journey? Where does it end? Is there a purpose? Hey, I read the Lobsang Rampa book you sent me. Did you see this one, Alan Watts, The Way of Zen?

I graduated from Reed in May 1965 and went to West Berlin on a Fulbright to study philosophy. West Berlin was an island, completely surrounded by Communist Germany. Goods and housing were scarce, and the citizens were uptight and mean-spirited. I was accustomed to freedom and an open life on the American West coast, and after a couple of weeks decided I really didn’t like this place. I got into a bar brawl one night, and ex-Nazi jackboots threw me in jail. That did it. The next day I convinced the purser at Freie Universitat to give me some of my scholarship money in cash and rushed off to Tangiers to finance a hashish factory. A few weeks later, with six kilos of dynamite golden-green hash in flat bricks taped to my body, with a crew cut and dressed like a square college kid, I flew to San Francisco and went into business.

Letter to my father from U.S. Department of State, November 22,1965:

The Free University of Berlin has reported to the United States Mission at Berlin that your son, Samuel Speerstra, has been missing from the University since October 12, leaving no forwarding address. He has not picked up his baggage nor the grant for month of November. It would be appreciated if you would notify this office if you know the present whereabouts of your son.

“Hi, Dad, guess what? I’m in San Francisco!”

I move into Rock’s house in Haight-Ashbury and begin to peddle my wares. It’s really happening now, this fall of 1965: Ken Kesey’s in town; Gracie’s gone to a band called Jefferson Airplane and they have a hit record; Ravi Shankar plays sitar ragas and everyone’s digging this haunting new sound that mimics an LSD trip. Underground chemist Owsley Stanley regularly drops by Rock’s place to trial-run his new psychedelic products on us. One night Owsley (we call him “Bear”) holds up this little vial and says, “Took me weeks, but I finally crystallized the purest DMT ever made. Di-methyl-triptamine. You won’t believe this stuff—you smoke it and instantly you’re the highest you’ve ever been on acid—and fifteen minutes later you’re totally straight. It’s kinda scary though—one second you’re holding a pipe in your hand, the next you’re in outer space wondering how you got there. Takes a strong mind to survive a trip on this stuff. Wanna try it?”

Rock puts The Rolling Stones, Now! on his stereo, and we groove to the opening song, “Every-body needs some-body to lo-ove,” while we dissolve some of the DMT powder onto mint leaves in a slightly warm oven and pack it into the small bowl of a hash pipe. Owsley says, “I have no idea what a dose should be. Who’s going first?” I volunteer.

We sit on the couch, and the last thing I remember is seeing the match flick in Owsley’s hand. They said later that I flipped off the chair, did a somersault in the air, and landed head down, legs up, propped in the corner, before flopping over to lie next to them on the floor, eyes wide open. After what seemed an eternity I finally realized I was a living entity, a being, yes, I exist! But who am I— where am I—flying through this vast dark cosmos flickering with intense colored shapes. How did I get here? I looked down from the ceiling and saw two men sitting below me—and my own prone form lying next to them.

After a dozen lifetimes I finally make out the dim shape of his silly grin—yes, it’s Rock! A real person, thank God! and I hug him tight, hearing sounds, celestial sounds, and then words. “Surprise! Surprise! I could see it in your eyes”—Mick Jagger singing at the end of the album.

First thing I said was, “What do you call this stuff, Bear? DMT? I’ll buy all you got!” I traded Owsley for some of my Moroccan hash, and over the next couple of months I distributed fifty grams of the purest DMT ever made, making sure only the coolest people, like a young guitarist from Seattle named Jimi Hendrix, tripped on this extreme and life-changing drug.

On Saturday night, December 18, Rock takes me down to Palo Alto, south of San Francisco, where Kesey has rented the Big Beat Club on San Antonio Road for the night. A sign out front says “CAN YOU PASS THE ACID TEST?” The Acid Test was to see how loaded you could get on LSD and still function—appear normal in your daily affairs. Lysergic acid was still legal in 1965, so all the cops in the parking lot couldn’t stop the fun.

We go in and start drinking the free Kool-Aid. On one side of the room we’re seeing silly Jerry Garcia, who looks like a shoe salesman with crazy hair; members of his band, The Warlocks, are each playing wildly different tunes and riffs, but it all seems to flow together. Tonight they’ve renamed themselves The Grateful Dead, and Rock that night commits to become their manager. On another stage across the room, there’s Kesey dressed as Captain Marvel or something, banging on drums, Mountain Girl in a flowing robe, a lit-up clown named Wavy Gravy—lights flashing everywhere, colors, sounds—Neal Cassidy (yeah, the guy from On the Road!) bare-chested and sweating and wandering around with a sledgehammer in his huge hands, rapping haiku streams of nonstop wisdom, his eyes like shiny quarters. In the early morning we stagger out onto El Camino Real and wonder what’s happening, the rest of the world so unreal to us now.

We’re starting to see this: that we, each other, at first this tiny band, but now maybe dozens of us, are different from the rest of human society. You’d see a guy with long hair and know he was wired and seeing the world in a separate way. Were special. We’ve seen the light, the alternate reality, the stairway to heaven, the infinite cosmic beauty that can only be close to God. Then there was the three-day “Trips Festival” at Longshoreman’s Hall in January 1966, where we’re seeing hundreds, maybe thousands, of new turned-on friends, companions, fellow travelers on the psychedelic path—the enlightened pioneers of our generation.

It was great—Kesey, Leary, Cassidy, all these guys—but I wasn’t that impressed. I mean, what they were saying, it was all so vague-cosmic this, love that—will someone please show me where it’s at? We heard all the records: Dylan, the Beatles. Do they know? No, they don’t seem to know either. Or maybe they know and we just haven’t picked up on it yet. But it was in the air: now were liberated, but who’s gonna lead the way? This master, this great teacher, or a new word, the Guru—where was he? Or she? Or do we find the Path on our own?

My friend Herman told me about some yoga masters, a man-and-wife team who were teaching him and his girlfriend some transcendental tricks. But when I saw them up on this crummy little stage down in the Tenderloin district—looked like an old vaudeville house—contorting around in the spotlights to some piped-in sitar music, the woman with an enormous python wrapped around her shoulders, I thought, no, not this one.

When things get hot in the city, we make road trips into the amazing country around San Francisco—Marin County, the redwoods, or south to Big Sur, the Santa Cruz mountains. January 1966 I go up to visit Jim and Annie, friends who have a small place near Big River in Mendocino County. I drop acid and walk deep into an old-growth redwood forest—gigantic trees. Not sure how long I sat there, or maybe I moved around a little, but when I finally realized I was an individual being living in a human body called Sam, it was dark. Not really dark—the night was lit by fantastic shapes and colors, as I hilariously tried to understand where I was and maybe get out of there. The only direction I knew for sure was up. So I started climbing, first up a little embankment maybe, then, feeling my way in the flashing dark, and ah, here’s a great friendly tree, and up this very friendly tree I went to the very top, high above the rest of the forest. But all I could see for orientation was a night sky filled with swirling rapture, everything moving. The Beatles song had just come out, so I started singing at the top of my lungs: “Help! I need somebody. Help, not just anybody...”

And then I saw over through the sparkling dark a pool of light, slowly getting bigger. It would dip, fade out, then reappear bigger and closer, and I heard a rumbling, through the quacking acid cosmic engine, a different sound—a motorcar—and the light pool stopped at the base of my tree and then winked up at me. She spoke: “Why don’t you come down from that tree now, you fool.” It was Melanie! Come to save me. (And it wouldn’t be the only time.)

I hadn’t seen Melanie for many months. She and Larry were no longer together, she said, and she was living with a couple of girlfriends in an abandoned house in a logging ghost town called Caspar, nearby on the Mendocino coast. She had just happened to drop by Jim and Annie’s place, and when she heard I hadn’t returned from the forest by midnight, she had grabbed a flashlight and come to fetch me in her battered 1950 Studebaker Starlight Coupe.

Melanie was a sweet, innocent-seeming Virgo, but she didn’t take crap from anybody. She had strong opinions, and once set on her path, she was determined. Like all of us, she was transient, good-natured, and she laughed a lot. She had an original way of saying something that showed she was intelligent. She was beautiful, a dark brunette with those down-sloping critical eyebrows that said, This better be good. She was nimble and athletic, but pleasantly clumsy too. Woops! Melanie had her own way of doing things, totally independent. She rode a motorcycle, hitchhiked everywhere by herself, and like me, was a loner with few close friends.

So I was in business now. Markets were expanding. I sold and parlayed the Moroccan hash into a vast assortment of other mind-benders. Dealing drugs wasn’t considered a bad occupation yet. Dealing was still a messianic crusade, an honorable profession among the newly liberated. You had to do something to make money, so why not deal the drugs you loved and turn on the world at the same time?

In February 1966, a friend with a white Ford Econoline van said he was driving to Portland, so I asked him for a ride. The Pacific Northwest was an almost untouched market. Friends up there would surely buy my products, so I packed my stash and off we went. We would drive up the coast on Highway 1, take our time, dig the redwoods on our way north.

We’re driving along, stoned out of our minds, a few hours out of Frisco in the middle of nowhere—it’s foggy, a rainforest in winter— when we shoot past a gal holding out her thumb for a ride. Wait! That’s Melanie! We back up. She says she’s going to Mexico, which is in the opposite direction, but waiting all day out here she’s seen maybe three cars, she says. Hey, were going to Portland. You want to come? Sure, why not? she says, hopping aboard, and our long journey together begins.

Melanie Nagel—her story comes out as we drove north through the rain. She’s a couple of years younger than me, from Sacramento, her dad’s a career man at Pacific Gas & Electric. She’s lived on her own a couple years in different parts of San Francisco and is now relaxing in the Mendocino woods. Pretty but spunky, quick but gentle, she listens well, and if you take your time to let her say everything that’s on her mind, it comes out pretty good. I like her. She’s a fair judge, sees all sides, but can cut like a torch with her tongue when the subject deserves: “Those pants look ridiculous” or “Don’t you ever lock the door?”

Melanie and I rented a small house on SE 8th Avenue, near the Sellwood Bridge in Portland, next door to a big 1920s house rented by Dave, Joanie, Mike, and Jan. Jan? Wow, Joanie’s little sister is now Mike’s girlfriend (she calls him Michael, so we sometimes call him Michael too). We’re all together now, this little band of gypsies, spawned by the new energy from San Francisco, looking to spread our culture to savages on the Northwest frontier.

My first drug deal in Portland ends in near-disaster: some guys with guns and a fake badge jump me and steal most of my stash—a set-up. But the pipeline to Frisco is still open, so I recoup and inundate Portland with thousands of hits of acid. We pass back and forth between the two houses on 8th Avenue, having fun, pulling pranks, wondering where all of this will go. Joanie tells me she wants an aquarium, so I walk a couple blocks down to the Willamette River and catch a half dozen foot-long crappies or bass, put them in a bucket, and dump them in Joanie’s big bathtub. She comes home. Look, Joanie, your new aquarium. She puts rocks in there, a little aquarium pump, and feeds the fish.

I get called up by Selective Service to appear for a physical. Drafted. That dreadful word. I’m supposed to go to the Armed Forces Examining Station in downtown Portland for a “pre-induction physical” in one week’s time. Some of my high school buddies have already been killed in Vietnam, and I will do anything to avoid it. Why should I go off to this meaningless war, suffer and maybe get killed? I’m having too much fun. So I start dropping acid every day, take speed so I won’t sleep, poke the veins up and down both arms with a needle to make obvious tracks, and don’t shower or eat much. By the time Melanie drops me off at the Examining Station, I’m the scroungiest-looking kid in that big hall full of beef-fed Oregon farm boys. They tell me to strip down to my underwear, and I’m the only guy in the place not wearing any. On the list of previous and current medical problems I check “yes” to everything: ТВ, cancer, polio, everything. Are you a homosexual? Yes. Do you have suicidal tendencies? Yes. After several hours of being passed from cubicle to cubicle, I sit buck naked before the Army psychiatrist, and he says, “I’m sorry to inform you, Mr. Speerstra, but Uncle

Sam does not want you in this man’s army.” Hooray! I spend too much money on a cab home and sleep for three days.

Mellow days, but the six of us often wonder, What will happen to us? We are six very independent souls, outlaws really, but joined in a common search. Our reading of eclectic books intensifies. Joanie and I meet every day in one of the sacred spots around her house. She always keeps little altarlike places, with a few flowers, dried or fresh, and some pretty rocks or shells neatly arranged. On one wall there’s a little framed vellum in her perfect calligraphy that says, “Miracles are natural. When they do not occur something has gone wrong.” Or maybe there’s a photo of some learned man, or a seascape with birds. Here, we sit and listen to music and talk about the purpose of life incessantly. Drugs aren’t where it’s at, but what’s next? We kind of expected that answers would come if we tried real hard to find them. The times were right for this: the gods were speaking through popular music, new ideas were floating around at warp speed. Surely a new world was being born.

On March 23, 1966, Joanie and I went to see Bob Dylan play at the Paramount Theater on SW Broadway in Portland. We saw him go electric: “Just Like A Woman” and “Like A Rolling Stone.” This is what we would do: look at each other, Joanie very grave and going yes, yes, and then I’d know too, yes, this is very cool and pointing the way where the future must go, and us along with it.

We pretty much concluded that we had to find a Master to take us out of all this confusion.

Melanie: Well, Sam and Joanie were the ones—I have to say I was still very doubtful because I had had all these weird experiences. Those two were very into this, and Sam said to me many times that we have to find a teacher, and I remember saying if you want someone to tell you what to do with your life, that’s fine, but I want to make my own mistakes, you know—real enlightened mentality.

Meanwhile Michael took off with Jan to New York City. We got letters. He was breaking into the big-time jazz scene there—we hear he jams with Thelonius Monk and Roland Kirk. He was also dealing a bit out there to pay the bills, so we swapped drugs through the mail whenever there was a drought on either coast.

**\* \* \***

Spring 1966 and the fateful road trip to Seattle: “Biggest Drug Bust In NW History!” Melanie and I spent a few days, scared to death, in separate wings of the Kirkland city jail. We had been caught redhanded with a lot of drugs. Jailhouse scuttlebutt had it that we’d get a minimum of five years. I kept my mouth shut, denied everything. One day, a detective was interrogating me from his confiscated property report when he slowly fell asleep at his desk—“We’re pulling twenty-four-hour shifts to nail you guys”—so I quietly reached across and turned the report around. Let’s see, they got the grass, the speed, the quart jar of “unidentified liquid,” but no heroin? Did they miss the heroin?

O Lord! I really believe in You! I’ve been so bad. Please show me the way! You are my only recourse, my only hope! Please get me outta here! I promise I’ll be good!

Thinking, OK, this is real, this is what’s happened, but don’t those deeper feelings count, that you are on a special course, protected by angels? That maybe this is just a bump in the road to your spiritual awakening?

Yeah, right.

My parents put up the bail. Melanie and I walked out of police HQ beaten and spent, relieved to see each other, even chuckling a bit, reliving events, but really scared. We walked over to the fenced lot to reclaim my impounded Ford. The cops had torn it apart, and seat covers are split open, hubcaps gone. It’s totally ransacked—they must’ve watched Reefer Madness a hundred times: “Clever drug pushers will hide narcotics in the most difficult-to-find places in their vehicles: in the upholstery, behind doors, beneath the floorboards, wheel covers. No place is too secure for their evil poisons.” First thing, I looked in the glove box. With a wave of relief I pulled out the Ohio matchbox and opened it for Melanie to see: “They missed the smack!”

They did seize from that Ford (and this is taken from official Court records) a dozen fishing flies; a brown checked coat; two pillows (which they’d slashed with sharp blades as they looked for the demon weed); food items valued at $10; one passport; a Bell & Howell 8mm movie camera; one pair lightweight large, dangly, gold pierced earrings (valued at $9.50); one colorful oval mosaic pin from Italy ($5); one necklace ($7); one turquoise beaded bag with cosmetics ($8); small colorfully-striped woven bag with personal effects ($50); one Brownie Instamatic camera and flashbulbs ($25); one cloth, tapestry, small knitted handbag with black wooden handles ($2.50); one small black Italian goatskin billfold with gold embellishments with $20 inside; a good wooden hairbrush ($5); two special photographs of “my grey cat who got killed, and one of Sam and Dave and Joanie on their front porch”; one small oblong, antique incense tin with a scrap of paper inside with a name and San Francisco phone number on it ($1.50).

What they didn’t get: two ounces of Mexican brown heroin in the most obvious place.

We were elated, but kept it down as we hit the freeway back to Portland. First thing we did when we got back: Melanie went off to buy a needle at Owl Drugs (she could always talk them into giving her one, even though you needed a prescription at the time) while I cooked us up a lovin’ spoonful. She was late, like an hour? Finally she comes running in with the Yale #12 and we’re high. Eventually I look out to the street and see the left side of my car totally smashed in. What?! Melanie calmly explains she missed a corner in her haste to return and wrapped the car around a telephone pole, reversed out, and got home before the cops came. Jesus, another day in the life.

Meanwhile I’m looking all over the place for the quart jar of LSD (a million hits?) I had stashed in the house foundation before we left for Seattle. It was gone. Only one other guy knew where I kept my stash. I ask him, Where’s my acid? He says, “I buried it in the yard in case the cops searched your house.” I dig all over the garden where he said he’d buried it—no acid. I’m out of business. Jail, a smashed car, best friend burning me, that’s it. I’m finished with this whole scene.

I immediately start going through the house, grabbing stuff, throwing everything that fits into the damaged car, tying a rope around the driver-side door to keep it closed.

Melanie: Everything we owned was packed into the station wagon, and Sam said, “If you want to go, jump in.” So I didn’t ask too many questions, just grabbed our two cats and got in the car, and we started driving east and at one point I asked Sam where are we going and he didn’t reply, so I didn’t ask more questions, and then toward evening we drove by that Forest Service office in Sisters, Oregon, a real one-horse town.

In his khaki Forest Service uniform with the little green patches, Bill Foster said, “Well, you’re very lucky, yes, we do have an opening. The couple that usually mans Trout Creek Butte lookout called from Eugene today. They can’t make it this summer.”

“Yeah, well, we’re free, and I grew up in these woods.”

“OK, we’ve got a long waiting list for this job, but you look like a nice couple. Can you run a chainsaw?”

"Sure, like walking the dog!” (Never had a chainsaw in my hands before...)

“Well, fill out these forms and find yourselves a place to stay here in town for a few days. Fire season will start next week, June 1. Tomorrow we’ll run you up to the tower and get you geared up. The job requires you to stay up there alone for four or five months, eight miles from town—can you handle it?”

Yes!!

**\* \* \***

In the second week of October 1966, three of my closest friends have arrived on Trout Creek Butte to change the lives of Melanie and me forever. Mike, Joan, and Joan’s sister Jan had driven nonstop from Manhattan to the West Coast to tell us about the incredible man they had met that summer: the Swami. Swami Bhaktivedanta was his name, and as my friends began to talk, I was impressed not so much by his philosophy or whatever else they were saying, but more by the profound change I saw in beloved friends of so many years. They seemed to glow, and with gentle ease they refused the venison and drugs, even the coffee, they were offered. This Swami must be the real thing, I thought.

We’re packed tightly in the small Forest Service cabin as night descends, soft lantern light through the two windows framing ponderosa pine branches whipped by gusts of snow.

“But what exactly is a swami, Mike? I mean, you keep talking about this Swami.”

“Swami is a title, really, it means someone who can control his senses.”

Wow!

Joanie’s getting into it now: “Sammer, I’m so excited! We drove straight out here to tell you about him. I think this is the one, this Swami. We were chanting, chanting, chanting, all the way out here— you get really high,” she said.

They did seem pretty happy, grinning all over the place with those big red beads around their necks, mumbling something or other. Their cat, Skutzelbrunzer, isn’t doing as well—they brought this big black cat all the way out here in a packed car? He’s under one of two chairs in the place, hissing with bared fangs at our dog, Ralph. Jan says, “He put twenty-seven stitches in one guy’s leg.” Reddo and Frieda, our two young cats, are confused, but excited too.

“High, huh? Like what do you mean?”

“I mean higher than you’ve ever been before. And you can do it all the time, chanting.”

“See, you’re saying Gods names over and over,” said Mike, “And because Krishna is nondifferent from His name, really, God is dancing on your tongue, He’s with you and you feel it, calm, aware-like, how can anything go wrong when Krishna is there?”

“Wait a minute, who’s this Krishna character?”

“‘Krishna’ is the main name for God. It’s the name He likes the most. It means ‘all-attractive’. He’s so beautiful He attracts everything to Himself like a magnet. Wait a sec, I’ve got some books out in the car. I’ll run out and get them.”

“Hey, let Ralph out with you.”

Melanie is quiet, puttering over the propane stove with Jan (now Jana-kee—that’s an easy one). Venison burgers have been rejected by our new arrivals—“No more meat, fish, or eggs for us,” they said—so Melanie’s cooking up some rice and beans. These are my friends mostly, but Melanie digs them and goes along. Jan’s rolling dough to make some kind of round flatbread thing they learned from the Swami. “See, they puff up!” she says.

A whiffle of snow blows in before Michael, with three books under his arm, slams the door and says, “These are some books the Swami translated. Five thousand years old. The most ancient knowledge on the planet. They’re called Srimad-Bhagavatam. Dig this.”

I grab one, Melanie grabs one, and we’re trying to understand this far-out book jacket, with its bright colors, gaily dressed people dancing inside flowers, galaxies spinning off.

Melanie: I mean you look at the cover, here’s this blue-faced guy with four arms with a flower coming out of his stomach with another fellow with four heads on the top of it, and I’m asking, What is that? “That’s God,” they said, and I didn’t blink. I guess that’s another good thing about hippies—we were really open to possibility and we didn’t automatically reject something that we couldn’t comprehend.

"What’s this writing here, Joanie, this language?”

“That’s Sanskrit, the mother of all tongues. These books—there are like a hundred more the Swami hasn’t had time to translate yet— describe Krishna in great detail, and who God is, who we are, the universe, how everything fits, the whole system, all there is to know about everything.”

“Yeah? Far out. But like, give me an example: Who am I?”

“First thing Swami teaches: You are not this body.”

OK, I can understand that, like that time on a DMT trip I was in the ceiling watching my body on the floor below, eyes wide open, or the time on acid—in a blossoming blackberry patch where I watched my soul pass through thousands of bodies while bumblebees buzzed symphonies.

“What is this ‘Supreme Personality of Godhead’?” Melanie asks.

“That’s just it,” gleams Joanie. “That’s the key. God is a person, but He’s the supreme Person, and just like you and me He laughs, cries, does everything we do—and we can relate to Him, get close to Him, play with Him by chanting His name.”

“But what about the Old Man in the sky, the Christian God? Is He different from Krishna?”

“No, no. God is God. Like Allah—or what’s that, Jehovah-even what Native Indians call the Great Spirit—all the same guy. ‘Krishna’ is just another name for God, the Supreme Person. And these ancient writings from India have more information about God, I mean thousands of times more info than you can find in the Bible or Koran or the Torah.”

“Yeah, it’s like the encyclopedia in fifty volumes compared to a little brochure,” Mike says.

“And this Swami, what’s he like?

“Ooohhhh.” Joanie rolls her eyes like she does when something’s really outta sight. “He’s too much. Old man, maybe seventy, but he looks like a kid. He glows. Always got a huge smile. He’s the real thing.” Joanie says it and I’m starting to believe her.

Mike describes for us how he met the Swami, stepping over drunken bums in the Bowery. “There he was, this humble older man, dressed in wrinkled saffron robes, but he was clean and fresh and full of energy. I’ve never met anyone so cool in my life.”

Mike tells us how he found and rented a small storefront for the Swami. It’s on Second Avenue on the Lower East Side of New York, and the Swami speaks there every day to his growing number of followers. “Allen Ginsberg is good friends with the Swami,” Mike adds, and that gets my attention! “Ginsberg’s lawyer got us legally registered as the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, or ISKCON, as the Swami calls it. We’re legit.”

Snowflakes fly by the window while we eat and chatter some more. “So you got busted too, by the cops in New York, huh Michael?”

“Yeah, really scared me for a while, but it’s pretty much blown over now. What about you and Melanie? Will you get off?”

“No, we probably have to go to jail for a while. We went up to Seattle for the trial in August and it was a miracle, the judge threw out all kinds of evidence—my dad got us a really good lawyer—but it looks like we’ll have to do some time. It’s on appeal now. I don’t know, man, we’re really worried.”

Then Melanie asks, “So you guys got married?” (Such a radical idea for those so opposed to our parents’ ideals.)

“Yeah, the Swami wouldn’t take us as students unless we did.”

I ask, “Joanie, what’s going on with David?

“Oh, since you guys got busted a few months back, I can’t live with Colt any more—let’s just say it’s time for a big, big change. New life, new times, pardner.”

“So where are you guys going from here? You said San Francisco, right? Then you’re going to India?”

Jan says, “We’re going down to Klamath Falls from here. What is it, a hundred miles? Did you ever meet our Aunt Edna? She’s got a cabin up near Crater Lake, so Michael and I are going there for our honeymoon. In New York we threw the I-Ching and it said, ‘You are going to the base of a volcano’—Crater Lake. Then after, I don’t know.”

Mike says, “We have to go to San Francisco to get visas for India. I told this to the Swami, and he said why don’t I look around for a temple there, while I’m at it?

Melanie wants to know what he means by a temple.

“That’s a storefront painted white inside. The Swami sits in there, and people come to hear him speak.”

“Oh,” I say. “Hey, were going to Frisco when we get off this mountaintop. Maybe we could help you down there. I know lots of-”

“No, wait a minute,” Mike says. “I don’t think we’ll have time for much looking. I told the Swami I’d look around, but we really want to get over to India.”

India? No one we know has ever been to India. Mike—so far ahead of me on the curve of experience.

We filled the woodstove and curled up in our sleeping bags, packed tight in that tiny space, while the wind roared all night in the ponderosas. When it really got going, you could hear support wires from the tower thrum with an eerie whistle.

At first light I ran up the tower and radioed Bill. “How much snow you got up there, Sam?”

“About two inches, but the road still looks good.”

“OK then. Guess what? Fire season’s over. I’ll come up around noon and we’ll close her down.”

Whoopee! Sugar 501’s outta here.

Everyone cheered when I gave them the news. I said, “Mike, you guys better get out of here fast. Bill says there’s a major storm coming in later today. Melanie, we’ve gotta get that horse back to Cleary’s ranch and pack all this stuff up. You guys know how to get to Klamath Falls from here? Turn right when you get to Sisters, then south on 97. Take you three, maybe four hours in this weather. We’ll meet up again in San Francisco, right? Maybe in a couple of weeks?”

“Hare Krishna!” they shout from their car, heading out.

US Weather Service records show that 9.2 inches of snow fell on Trout Creek Butte on October 12,1966. Another 2.5 inches fell on the thirteenth. We got out just in time.

**\* \* \***

About a month earlier, returning from her drug/mail run to Sisters—and probably doing 60 on the old logging road—Melanie had run over a tree limb, which tore out an oil line and burned out the big Ford’s V-8 engine. I was so pissed off—another Melanie go-for-it-and-be-damned trick of hers. I had had the Ford towed to town and traded it straight across for a much-used ’52 Chevy two-door coupe.

We had so much stuff by now that it wasn’t all going to fit into that smaller car. While Melanie caught and readied the semi-wild horse for transport, I drove into Sisters and bought a six-by-eight flatbed trailer with side rails and a hitch. I also phoned Cleary to come get his horse and chicken.

As we were packing the trailer, a black wall of clouds from the west raced overhead. We heard loud honking above the shrieking wind, and a mighty flock—maybe a hundred huge birds, Canadian geese—came gliding below the tower and plopped and skidded onto the snow around us, glad to find refuge from the storm in an open space in this otherwise unbroken forest.

While snow flew—almost a foot of it on the ground by now— we loaded the dog, the cats, and lowered the huge American flag (this was a federal installation) and used it to cover our stuff in the open trailer. On Thursday, October 13,1966, we took off sideways down the slippery slopes of Trout Creek Butte—the great American hippie road trip—to meet our destined master on the streets of San Francisco. The Chevy had an AM radio, and as we descended the mountain Grace Slick was singing, “If only you believe in miracles, so would I...”

We drove south on Highway 1 through redwoods along the California coast, digging our new freedom like prisoners released from jail. At one big redwood tree with a tunnel carved through it, we stopped and asked someone to take our photo: two idiots and a dog waving from windows in an old jalopy pulling a beat-up trailer covered with an American flag and parked inside a giant tree.

Whenever we could get radio reception we heard songs like “California Dreamin’,” “I Fought the Law, And the Law Won,” “Good Vibrations,” “Do You Believe in Magic?,” and my all-time favorite, “Wooly-Bully,” by Sam the Sham and the Pharaohs.

Between songs Melanie and I talked about this new Krishna stuff, and we agreed that the Swami sounded like a very cool, for-real master, but we wanted to hear a lot more, maybe meet the Swami first before giving our lives to him.

We stopped at Melanie’s cabin in Caspar to pick up some of her belongings, and two days after leaving Trout Creek Butte we crossed the Golden Gate into the sparkling sunrise of San Francisco. We had nowhere to go, so first thing I called Rock Scully, Melanie phoned a couple of her girlfriends, and we found out there was a dance that night at the Fillmore with Jefferson Airplane, Paul Butterfield Blues Band, and Big Mama Mae Thornton. Out of the woods and into the city.

We left our animals at Melanie’s friend’s house, but kept our stuff with us. Luckily, I found a parking spot for the car and trailer right in front of the Fillmore on Geary Street. We dropped a couple of tabs of Owsley’s latest product, which Rock had laid on me, and strolled in, not realizing for a short while just how much the Bear’s formula had improved.

Suddenly, everything exploded! The lights, the music, the huge crowd of distorted dancing people overwhelmed me. I took off, wandering around, trying to figure out who I was, where I was, and how I got there. It was all one big maddening explosion. I saw a guy stabbing his girlfriend with a knife. Blood everywhere. I ran.

The only direction I knew for certain was UP, so I climbed, climbed, climbed up through the auditorium to the very rafters. There, in a dark corner of the ceiling was Merle. Merle! He was Melanie’s friend, a Buddhist guy, who drove a taxi. Merle had maybe come up to smoke a quiet joint. He immediately knew what was happening to me—a bad trip. I couldn’t speak; no words formed in a mind so blown. Merle understood. After a while he took me calmly by the hand, led me outside, and told me to wait, he would go find Melanie.

When at last they came, I could finally say “the car” and point to it. But when we got there, the window was broken and almost everything had been stolen. All gone. We had a few clothes left, not much more. This was the ultimate bad trip. We cooled off for a few hours at Merle’s place in Bernal Heights and made some decisions: No more LSD; get a place big enough for Mike, Jan, and Joan to live with us; listen to what they’re saying about Krishna and the Swami.

The next day, driving down from Merle’s place through the Mission District, Melanie saw a for-rent sign on a telephone pole. I slammed on the brakes, and she got out to look, came back with a number. We drove to a pay phone. One dime and ten minutes later we were standing on the porch of 24 Lucky Street, our first home halfway to Krishna. It has three bedrooms. It’s perfect.

Mukunda, Janaki, and Joanie arrived a few days later—the cabin at Crater Lake in winter wasn’t quite the honeymoon they’d thought it would be, and Aunt Edna was a little freaked by this motley crew and their killer cat. We settled in—the Grants in one room, Joan in the big sunny, south-facing room, and Melanie and I in the dark bedroom off the kitchen. Because Melanie and I are still eating meat, were assigned separate shelves in the refrigerator. Mike and Jan are into macrobiotics, so their shelf is pretty small: a little rice, a bottle of tamari, a finger of dried ginger.

I still had some money saved from my fire-lookout job, and Mike had a stash for his tickets to India, but money was tight and we didn’t know how far it had to stretch. We would never deal drugs again, and the last thing we wanted to do was get jobs. So the girls augmented our food supply by supermarket runs to the nearby Piggly Wiggly, walking around the aisles and heaping up three shopping carts to overflowing, then just walking out the door. No one stopped them; no one would dare shoplift that much.

Joanie was kind of in the middle—sometimes chanting on her big red beads, sometimes smoking a little pot. But Mukunda and Janaki toed the line. We watch them closely: they get up early, chant on their beads, refuse all drugs, even refuse coffee. Hard to imagine Mike as such a straight arrow. Were more impressed each day.

Mukunda really doesn’t know a lot himself yet, but he tells us more. The Swami is in a long line of gurus. Each guru learns from his master and then passes the teachings down unchanged. This “disciplic succession” means the Swami is “bona fide,” not like some of these gurus who just make stuff up. The Swami has books and Sanskrit authorities to back everything up.

“And it’s easy. See, time progresses through many ages, and this present age is called Kali-yuga, the ‘iron age,’ when people fight and there’s chaos everywhere. So this process, chanting Krishna’s names, is recommended for this Kali age because it’s easy. You don’t have to learn yoga contortions or how to meditate—just chant the Hare Krishna mantra. It’s the shortcut to nirvana.”

“But how do you know what to do? Does the Swami tell you?” “Yeah, the Swami will teach you everything.”

My jackrabbit mind, so hard to silence, likes this more and more. Melanie and I know we’re going to do this, but when?

Melanie: I remember Mukunda trying to teach us simple stuff, like the words to the mantra. Even though there are only three words put together into a sixteen-word format, getting the Hares and Krishnas and Ramas in the right places was difficult.

I felt, “We need to get this Swami out here to Frisco, pronto!” But Mike seemed reluctant to go out and look for a temple. I pushed him a little. “Mukunda, I’ll help you. Let’s find a place and get the Swami out here. What do you say? You won’t believe what’s going on here. Thousands of hippies are pouring into the Haight. It’s the perfect place for the Swami. Come on, Mukunda. Stick around. This is where the action is. This is going to be fun.”

Shortly after we returned to San Francisco, we heard that Ken Kesey was having an Acid Graduation Party on Halloween night at the Winterland Auditorium. All the acidheads in SF would gather, get stoned, and at midnight, something was supposed to happen that would allow them to “graduate” to another level of existence or something—a level without acid. We didn’t go, but we heard about it the day after. Midnight came, they’re wound up, expecting to get zapped by the cosmos—and nothing happened. “But they were chanting that Hare Krishna chant you’ve been telling me about,” my friend reported.
