Tom Wolfe describes the moment in his best-selling book, The Electric Kool-Aid Acid Test:

\[Kesey\] turns back into the crowd, sinks into a time warp... Like his life is an endless tape loop... Black spirits keep bubbling up out of the most ancient pits of licorice detergent

TRAP

That! Hare Krishna Hare Krishna Krishna Krishna Hare Hare Hare Rama Hare Rama Rama Rama Hare Hare and as he chants he becomes... Krishna!... Christ!... God... And he pops out of the time warp into the silver haze of... The Universal Mind...

“We almost had it,” says Kesey, opening his eyes for the first time. “We would have had it. There’s too much noise...” But it’s like the cloud has passed.

**\* \* \***

The Haight-Ashbury district was a quiet neighborhood in the geographical center of San Francisco, bounded on the west by Golden Gate Park, on the east by Buena Vista Park, on the north by Oak Street, and on the south by Parnassus Street. It’s scarcely more than half a square mile in size: thirty-one square blocks with about ten thousand permanent inhabitants.

A lot had changed in Haight-Ashbury since I’d been here nearly eight months earlier. The sidewalks were now shoulder to shoulder with bells and beads and colorful outfits and thousands of kids like us, their faces bright with the dawning of a new age. The Haight had become world headquarters for the exploding hippie movement.

This is major. Even Mukunda is deeply impressed. We start putting together a plan: meet community leaders, get them interested in helping us, find a place to rent, and raise money.

Fresh from the north woods I am timid to meet these movers and shakers, but Mukunda has such a smooth, businesslike way—he’s growing a beard and looks so cool in his white NASA jumpsuit with the big red beads—and we soon become a persuasive team.

Almost daily we cruise up to the Haight in my ’52 Chevy, which is on its last legs and will soon be abandoned on a side street in the Mission. After the car dies, we take buses from Lucky Street north on Van Ness, change to a bus going west on Fell, get off at Ashbury Street, and walk across the Panhandle.

Most Haight-Ashbury leaders seem to have heard about Swami Bhaktivedanta—he’s already famous from the Village Voice and East Village Other articles. They know that Allen Ginsberg thinks he’s cool, so they’re ready to help us.

We meet Allen Cohen, who publishes the San Francisco Oracle, the world’s first psychedelic newspaper, already selling fifteen thousand copies a month and read cover-to-cover by everyone in the neighborhood. Allen declares in Issue #1: “Applying the teachings of Eastern religions and meditations of yoga and chanting to the extraordinary and transcendental experiences of LSD is the essential concern of the Oracle”

Mukunda is getting fired up now. He knows about the Swami and Krishna consciousness, so he takes the lead, preaching to these guys. Cohen is a New Yorker—a lot of the leaders here seem to be from New York—and he’s heard about the Swami. He offers to put a free ad in the next Oracle for us. It will go to press later that day, so we have to hurry.

We run back to Lucky Street. On bright gold paper we mount a black-and-white photo of the Swami under a tree in New York, his left hand atop his head and his eyes shut—he’s laughing, like, “Get outta here!” Above the photo Joanie does the calligraphy: BRING KRISHNA CONSCIOUSNESS WEST! She scribes the Hare Krishna mantra around the edges, and we make the deadline. The quarter-page ad slides onto page 8 in Oracle #4, November 16,1966.

Other articles in Issue #4 include an interview with Timothy Leary; “Enlightenment Poem,” by Lenore Kandel; an article about the Diggers; local news by the “Gossiping Guru”; two photos of

Bhaktivedanta Swami alongside a poem by Lawrence Ferlinghetti; “Yoga and the Psychedelic Mind,” an article by Bob Simmons, which concludes, “It’s up to the young people, the new Aquarians, to manifest change. Love, service, beauty—Yoga seems to fit. Meet with friends, find council, sing together, chant.”

Psychedelia and spiritual awakening are big topics of conversation in Haight-Ashbury. Seldom have historical events come together so decisively.

I watch Mukunda tell Janaki, “OK, we’ll stay in San Francisco until we get a temple going. The Swami wants us to do that. Then we’ll go to India right after. How’s that sound?” Like music to my ears.

Melanie and I are now pretty clear about our lives too. Were going to get initiated by the Swami whenever he gets here. We know from Mukunda that the Swami will not initiate boyfriends and girlfriends unless they’re married. Initiation means accepting the four sacrifices to the spiritual master that will open the pathway for surrender to Krishna: no meat-eating, no intoxicants, no gambling, and no sex outside of marriage—and within marriage only for the purpose of procreation.

We don’t gamble, and stopping meat is easy—we simply adopt the Grant macrobiotic diet. The refrigerator gets very empty, very fast. The sex part we’ll see about later.

But drugs, that’s another thing. Melanie and I have stopped dropping acid, but we re still shooting speed and a little heroin. We know it’s just a matter of time. We agree: we’ll stop when we know the Swami’s coming. I find a wooden stairway abandoned in Lucky Street and install it floor to ceiling in the middle of our living room. I paint it bright primary colors and call it the Stairway to Heaven; Melanie calls it the Stairway to Nowhere. Joanie and I listen to Bob Dylan and the Moody Blues all day and talk about the great times to come.

Though Mukunda and Janaki had known the Swami since May of 1966, they weren’t too clear yet about the rules and regulations. One night we went for dinner in Chinatown and some of the food we ordered smelled a little fishy. We ate it anyway, but picked obvious chunks of meat out of our pork-fried rice and set it aside. We were trying.

**\* \* \***

Then Roger joined our scene. One day, Melanie, Joanie, and I are wandering around the Mission neighborhood with Ralph when this German shepherd mix with one floppy ear runs out and gives Ralph a hard time. A tall, scraggly guy in a long Merlin robe comes out of the gate and yells, “Que Tai! Que Tai!” and the dog comes to him. He says his name is Roger, and he asks if we want to come in.

Melanie: So I looked in, and it was this dirty hippie pad, and I didn’t want to go in, but Joan decided to hang around, so we came on home and a little while later she came back with that guy and the dog, and that’s how Roger Siegel Joined the scene at Lucky Street.

Roger moves in with Joan, bringing Que Tal, so now there’s six of us, plus three cats and two dogs who don’t like each other much. But Joanie is very happy—lots of laughter behind her closed door now that she has a companion. One thing, though: Roger brings lice into the relationship, and we scream with hilarity when they emerge from the room totally shaved. The others go on crab alert and we scrub our hair with vinegar.

Roger is a cool guy. He’s an East Coast hipster from a good family. He’s done the civil rights marches, knows poetry and jazz, been to Mexico, and likes to smoke dope. But we’re a tight group and he’s the new kid in town. Sometimes I pass Roger on the back stairwell leading to the basement where we both keep our stashes. We nod, chuckle, and go about our business.

After a few days of this, Mike and Jan move out. They get a small place nearby, so we still hang out together. Janaki wants a husband and a settled life, and the Lucky Street scene is just too much for her.

Janaki: It was difficult. We were trying to stick to what Swami said, and I was having a hard time with Melanie, because she was such an intense kind of personality. I was kind of this passive personality, and she was very aggressive, and we just didn’t connect sometimes.

By now Mukunda is more determined than ever to find a place for the Swami. He still mentions going to India later, but not so much anymore. We look at several places as close to Haight Street as we can find. We beat the bushes, talk to people, pretty sure something will happen. One day in early December, Mukunda comes running into the Lucky Street house and says, “I think I found the place!”

It’s on Frederick Street, number 518, by Kezar Stadium and just two blocks from Haight, next to a Chinese laundry. And he’s lucky to get two one-bedroom apartments just above the storefront—one for him and Janaki and one for the Swami. I chip in some cash from my Forest Service earnings, Mukunda takes money from his traveling fund, and he signs the lease. “First thing,” he says, “I’ll call the Swami and invite him.”

“Yeah, Mukunda, but what about money? We just barely made the rent. How are we gonna pay for a plane ticket and, you know, all the rest?”

Mukunda phones the Swami in New York, tells him we have a place for him now in San Francisco, and that he will fix a date and send him a plane ticket soon.

Letter from Bhaktivedanta Swami to Janis Dambergs in Montreal, December 10, 1966: “I think I shall be engaged in California during January and February. So, April and May will be quite suitable for me to go to Montreal. If, however, I do not go to California, then I shall write to you.”

**\* \* \***

With the imminent arrival of the Swami, Melanie and I and Ralph and the cats find a second-story apartment on the corner of Haight and Lyon across from Buena Vista Park, maybe twenty minutes’ walk from the new temple. Local hippies call it the “Crystal Palace” for all the methamphetamines used in that place.

Janaki: Mukunda would begone and then come home and say this is what’s gonna happen, and then with Sam, the two of them, it just manifested in a larger scope: we’re going to get a temple. And all of a sudden 518 Frederick turns up. Whatever those guys went out to do, they would find a way for it to happen. I don’t know what they did out there, but they hustled, and their hustle was so authentic that people were willing to go for it and listen to them.

Michael Bowen was a well-known beatnik visionary artist, schooled in Eastern mysticism, who had met Swami Bhaktivedanta the previous summer at Tompkins Square Park in New York. He had cofounded the Oracle with Allen Cohen. We sat in Michael Bowen’s psychedelic pad on Haight Street one day and kicked around some ideas with him. Now that we had a place, how do we flood San Francisco with Krishna consciousness? How do we get the Swami out here? How do we raise money for all this?

Bowen said, “Why don’t you guys put on a rock dance at the Fillmore, for charity? The Mime Troupe has done it; the Zen Center put on a benefit. I can introduce you to Bill Graham—he runs the place.”

Not a bad idea. I said I knew Rock Scully, manager of the Grateful Dead. So we hustled over to Rock’s place at 710 Ashbury, and we sat around with Jerry Garcia and Pig Pen, told them a little about the Swami, the maha-mantra, how music is the heart of our spiritual practice, and that we wanted to put on a charity concert to bring the Swami out here, and Jerry said, “Sure, why not? Sounds groovy, man.”

In mid-December 1966, Mukunda—spiffy in a sports coat over his NASA jumpsuit—and I go to see Bill Graham at his office at the Fillmore Auditorium. Bill was an older, hard-nosed New Yorker who said he didn’t do charities anymore, so you’d better go see Chet Helms over at the Avalon Ballroom. Chet was a tall, laid-back Texan with long blond hair, a beard, and little granny glasses. He had driven out from Austin to the Haight in early ’66 with Janis Joplin. Gradually he and Rock and other artists, promoters, and musicians had formed a loose-knit association called the Family Dog, a communelike group who had started putting on concerts, happenings, trips, festivals—whatever you wanted to call them—in places like Longshoremans Hall and, recently, the Avalon Ballroom on Sutter Street at Van Ness Avenue.

So Mukunda and I went over to see Chet, told him about the Swami and his friendship with Allen Ginsberg. So Allen might come too, we said, and the Grateful Dead have committed, so could we hold a benefit rock dance in your Ballroom? Chet said, “Yes. Tell ya what, I’ll donate the hall and the sound system, but the city and the unions have rules, so you’ll have to pay base scale to the bands and something for security cops. It’ll save money if you guys run the ticket booth. And you’ll need a light show.”

Chet said, “You’ve got the Dead. OK, but you’ll need some other groups. I’m their manager, so I’ll talk to Janice, see if Big Brother will play. But we’re really booked up for weekend nights, Fridays and Saturdays, for several months. What about a Sunday night—will that work? Far out. A Sunday night it is. Let’s see, how about January 29?”

“Outta sight!” we say, and the tingles go up my spine. This must be some kind of Krishna magic for sure. At last we had a target date. We scraped together a hundred and fifty bucks for a one-way ticket for A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami to fly to San Francisco, arriving here at 2:30 p.m., Tuesday, January 17,1967, on United Airlines flight #21 from LaGuardia—and mailed him the ticket. (If he’s really a swami, can’t he just float out here?)

One day, Melanie’s walking down 24th Street in the Mission and she hears rock-and-roll coming from a garage. She goes in and meets Skip Spence, formerly with Quicksilver Messenger Service and Jefferson Airplane, now forming a band of his own. They had practiced a lot and were looking for their first big gig. “These guys are good, really good!” she says, so I go over to hear them practice, and she’s right, they’re good. So we sign “Moby Grape” as our third band. (Moby Grape will later be tagged as one of the best original ’60s Bay-area bands.)

Then Michael Bowen tells Mukunda, “I’ve spoken with Allen Ginsberg. He’s coming out to San Francisco anyway in a few weeks for an event were planning. Were calling it ‘The First Human Ве-In.’ Trying to get Golden Gate Park. Anyway, Allen says he’ll love to do a show at the Avalon with the Swami and you guys. That’s January 29, right?”

Now we have our line-up! Mukunda goes to meet two guys who are putting together a light show—Ben Van Meter and Roger Hillyard—and they agree to do the show, but they need us to make up some slides with the Hare Krishna mantra and pictures of Krishna.

In high school I had a cartoon strip in the school paper and did posters in the halls for dances and student body elections. I liked to draw. One day I sketched “Hare Krishna!” in foot-high psychedelic letters on a big piece of cardboard, cut them out to make a stencil, and very late at night Mukunda and I went all over Haight-Ashbury and, with cans of spray paint (you shook them and the little ball rattled around) we painted our motto in bright primary colors on walls and sidewalks. The neighborhood wakes up and sees HARE KRISHNA! all over the place.

With a date set for the dance, the storefront rented, and the Swami coming—man, this project is getting big, fast. Mukunda is phoning back and forth to Krishna devotees in NYC, pleading, “Hey, we need your help out here.” We knew a little about what the Swami had done in New York—how he had come to America in 1965 with no money, no friends, and had gradually built up a devout little following at 26 Second Avenue on the Lower East Side. We knew there was a hippie scene back East similar to what was happening in San Francisco, but it was smaller, seemed more serious, less expressive and free, not as much fun.

An artist named Harvey Cohen had met the Swami in New York shortly after he had arrived in the US and had helped him out a lot. The Swami had written in his diary:

1/26/66: Today Mr. Cohen met. He gave his address and invited me to see his apartment in the Down Town.

3/2/66: Cohen brought one cotton jersey for me. Thanks.

3/12/66: Cohen is preparing to go to California. One-half tape-record prepared for him. He bought a pad for sitting.

He assured the maintenance of rent of my room.

Harvey had moved out west to the Mendocino coast, so Mukunda phoned and convinced him to come down to San Francisco on the bus, telling him that the Swami was coming and “he’d really like to see you.” Melanie and I had an extra room in our Crystal Palace apartment, so Harvey moved in with us. He didn’t know yet how desperately we needed his help, but he picked up on our 24/7 energy, rolled up his sleeves, and said, “You know, this could be fun.”

He set up an easel in the front room, and together we started designing the Avalon poster. Posters had recently become the accepted way to advertise hippie events in San Francisco, as radio and newspaper ads were too expensive. Hundreds of newly liberated artists were making their way to the Haight, and already their exotic posters were creating a nationwide sensation. Harvey was working in rare company.

What should we call this dance? Other dances had names like “Tribute to Dr. Strange,” “Rock for Peace,” “Love Pageant Rally.” We come up with the name “Mantra Rock Dance.” There are very few photos of the Swami yet, but we go through all that we have and decide on the same shot we used in the Oracle ad: the Swami with one hand on his head, laughing, as if saying “Omigod!” Harvey polishes the poster with psychedelic patterns and lettering, we get it printed, and then rush around town. KRISHNA CONSCIOUSNESS COMES WEST soon grabs your eyes from store windows and telephone poles all over the city.

Harvey owns a 1950 black Cadillac Coupe Deville back in New York, and he thinks this might just be the car to drive the Swami around San Francisco, so he writes to the NYC devotees, asking them to drive it out. About this time, too, a born-again Christian named Brother David gives us a 1950 Ford sedan, and it becomes the official Krishna-mobile—we use the same stencil and spray-paint HARE KRISHNA! all over it. Flash is the theme in this booming hippie mecca of the world.

One day, Harvey brings home a girl named Hope (who will later become Harsharani), and we are surprised one morning to see her walking out of his room. Once in a while we can smell a little pot smoke in there. Harvey and I still smoke cigarettes, although we talk daily about giving them up. We are not ones to judge.

Harvey’s total dedication to making the poster impressed us all. And it was a masterpiece. He also helped make leaflets, slides for the light show, and started painting pictures of Krishna and Lord Chaitanya in our apartment, giving living shape to what the Swami called “devotional service.” We talked a lot about how all great art through history has been God-centered.

Harvey’s obvious love for the Swami inspired us, made us all the more convinced. He had fled to the West Coast to escape the kind of harsh discipline that goes with being an artist in New York, but he loved the Swami and his Lower East Side students—we heard a lot of good things about Rayarama, Kirtanananda, and others back East, and were appreciating these guys before we even met them.

Oracle #5 (the “Human Be-In” issue) comes out in late December 1966, with Mukunda’s article, “The New Science,” filling pages 4 and 8. The print is artistically imposed on the torso of a giant, shaven-headed man of the future who is juggling planets. Mukunda describes astrology and Chinese acupuncture—how these and other spiritual paths are really sciences—and then in language hippies love to hear, he cleverly announces:

The Haight-Ashbury district is about to be honored by the presence of His Holiness A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, a 71-year-old Sannyasi, who, by disciplic succession, is a genuine spiritual master. He was instructed by his spiritual master in India, Srila Prabhupada Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati Goswami Maharaj, to continue teaching the science of God in the United States. Swamiji, as he is known to his devotees, will conduct daily classes in the Bhagavad-gita, discussions, chanting, playing instruments, and devotional dancing in a small temple in the neighborhood. He arrives this month.

Swamiji’s use of the Hare Krishna Mantra (Hare Krishna Hare Krishna Krishna Krishna Hare Hare, Hare Rama Hare Rama Rama Rama Hare Hare) is already known throughout the United States. Swamiji’s chanting and dancing is more effective than Hatha or Raja Yoga or listening to Ali Akbar Khan or acid or going to a mixed-media rock dance. I know from personal experience because I am his devotee.

Once the date was set for the Swami’s arrival (January 17, 1967), some of the New York devotees trickled out to San Francisco to be with him. Rayarama and Rabindra Swarup arrived first, driving out in Harvey’s Cadillac a few days before the Swami was due. Rayarama, Ray Marais, was a mellow, longhaired comic book writer for Marvel comics (Dr. Strange and The Hulk), and Rabindra, Bob Lefkowitz, was a firebrand Rasputin type, mysterious and strong. Both were extremely intelligent and attached to the Swami, and they impressed us greatly. They immediately started filling us in on the finer points of Krishna conscious behavior and etiquette, along with the rules and regs, bringing asceticism into the deal.

We’re not so sure about this heavy NY stuff—too much do-this, do-that. Hey, this is the Haight, where “do your own thing” is the prevailing motto. Loosen up, man, you’re in California now. But when Hayagriva arrives from New York, wow, he’s a Ginsberg man—wild-haired and wild-eyed and spouting lines from Howl! and the Bhagavad-gita. He’s a college grad—majored in philosophy and poetry. He comes with his longtime pal, Kirtanananda, who seems so knowing, calm, and relaxed around this whole Krishna thing. He even wears a dhoti sometimes, the traditional long, folded lower garment worn by men in India. Both of these guys had been to India, so we listen to them and learn a lot.

The NY devotees tell us about spiritual names: das means “servant of,” so Mukunda das means “servant of Mukunda, the giver of liberation.” They show us how to use beadbags, put on tilak. (white marks on our foreheads, using Fuller’s Earth we buy at the drugstore), how to cook in a particular way. They tell us what the Swami means by karma, reincarnation, and the heavenly planets. They bring an LP they’ve just made in New York on Happening Records. It’s called Krishna Consciousness, and it starts with the Swami giving a concise, five-minute explanation of the maha-mantra, followed by a long kirtan. Kirtan is the chanting of mantras in congregation-one person sings the mantra, then the crowd responds. We listen to that record over and over and over. (We didn’t know it yet, but the Beatles were listening to it too.)

The Beatles had been the voice of our generation since 1962. A huge segment of the world’s population examined every word the Beatles said. Many thought the Beatles knew the answers to life’s mysteries and were teaching them to us. They were by far, then and since, the most famous music group of all time. There’s no historical comparison. They influenced every development of thought and activity in our world.

We didn’t know this, but shortly after the Krishna Consciousness album was released in late ’66, the Happening Records office in New York received a telephone order from Apple Corps, London, for fifteen records to be sent by “overseas air express.” The records were shipped the next day to Beatles HQ. Just as we were hearing Bhaktivedanta Swami’s spoken introduction to the record, George Harrison was also hearing it:

Krishna consciousness is not an artificial imposition on the mind. This consciousness is the original energy of the living entity. When we hear the transcendental vibration, this consciousness is revived. And the process is recommended by authorities for this age. By practical experience also, we can perceive that by chanting this maha-mantra, or the Great Chanting for Deliverance, one can at once feel transcendental ecstasy from the spiritual stratum... The chanting is exactly like a genuine cry by the child for the mother. Mother Hara helps in achieving the grace of the supreme father, Hari, or Krishna, and the Lord reveals Himself to such a sincere devotee. No other means, therefore, of spiritual realization is as effective in this age as chanting the maha-mantra.

We rented the 518 Frederick Street storefront in early December 1966—the Swami was coming in a few weeks. It was getting serious. I was still using drugs; Melanie too. That December there was a shortage of grass, speed, and smack in the city—even our dealer friend Bruce, an old-time SF junkie, who looked like he was about sixty even though he was maybe thirty, couldn’t score. Melanie and I heard about this pharmacy downtown that had a big bottle of liquid Demerol, a powerful narcotic, high on a shelf behind the counter, and we quickly devised a plan: we’d go in there, Melanie would make a ruckus (which only she could do with such skill), and when the pharmacist was distracted, I would swoop behind the counter and grab it. Our plan worked, and suddenly we had more smack than anyone else in town.

Yeah, but what about cleaning up our act to get ready for the Swami? We made a decision: shoot some good stuff for a few days, then give it all to Bruce. Go clean on New Year’s Eve. And that was it. I’ll never forget the look of immense relief on Bruce’s drug-ravaged face when we showed up at his dirty pad on the last day of 1966 and handed him the Demerol and our much-used Yale #12 needle, saying, “Here, man, it’s all yours. We’re off it now. Hare Krishna!”

**\* \* \***

With the advent of 1967 we knew this much: Krishna is the Supreme Person; we are tiny spirit sparks of His sunlike Fire temporarily inhabiting bodies made of inferior material energy; we have somehow fallen from our original position as servants of Krishna, misusing our free will in order to enjoy our blunt, fleshy senses; and to reattain our actual transcendent state of being, full of bliss forever, we simply have to redirect our senses back to Krishna, offering everything we do or think or say to Him and thus become Krishna conscious. The Swami calls it devotional service: work for Krishna and chant His names.

“Yeah, Mukunda, but what about karma? Everyone’s talking about good karma and bad karma.”

“The devotee has no karma. Whatever you do, you do it for Krishna, and He accepts it—doesn’t matter if it’s a success or a failure, big or small—so there’s no reaction, no karma. Krishna consumes your deeds. No more guilt, no regret, no letdown, but also, no success, no pride that I did this, I did that. Krishna is the doer, not you—so no karma.”

I thought Bhaktivedanta Swami might be my permanent teacher—Melanie was pretty much convinced he was hers—but I wanted to try it first, see how it played out. Mukunda was positive the Swami was a great man, but I saw his reservations from time to time, torn between his music career, a demanding wife, and his duty as a husband to have a home and family. Joanie was the most profoundly affected. She had a deep longing to follow the Swami very strictly, but her sister and closest friends had spouses, we were family units, and she wanted—or thought she wanted—a husband to go down this path with her, and Roger was dragging his heels.

We began to feel the path wasn’t going to be easy. Coming down from years of intense drug use and maximum sense gratification, we were about to put our new knowledge to the test. But we were committed, and Melanie and I poured ourselves into cleaning and preparing the storefront temple for the Swami’s arrival.

518 Frederick Street was just one long room—maybe seventy feet long and twenty feet wide, with a fourteen-foot ceiling. A big glass window dominated the street end, and you stepped off the sidewalk into a little alcove to the right to open a wood-framed glass door into the place. The back was walled off, and on your right was a doorway and three stairs that led up to another room behind, maybe fifteen by twenty feet, that looked out onto the parking lot for Kezar Stadium. At the back end of the main room and on the left was a wooden door that led downstairs to a full-length, windowless, concrete basement.

So we had to get this place cleaned and ready. Create a temple? Mukunda and Janaki were the only ones who knew anything about what a temple should look like. My mentality was to just work, just do it, build this place, forget about my cold turkey—this is a great new adventure and the Swami promises I can stay high forever without drugs. We swept, scrubbed, and painted. People brought oriental-looking pictures and wall hangings. A couple of local artists volunteered to paint a sign along the building above the front window. It read “Radha Krishna Temple” in beautiful metallic gold letters on a deep red background.

I brought basic tools into the place, locked the door, and started banging nails. According to photos from Second Avenue, we needed an elevated place for the Swami to sit and lecture, a dais. In the center of the back wall was an opening, four feet wide and seven feet high, which looked into the back room and which might have once been a doorway but was now perched three feet above the floor. I sealed this opening with plywood, and framed it with 2" x 12" redwood planks on all four sides. The Swami would sit on a four-foot square plywood platform set into the base of the opening and supported in front by 4" x 4" redwood legs.

Building the dais was easy, but the front door was going to be a problem—it was fragile, all glass, and wouldn’t withstand the public crunch. It banged and rattled, and if we left it open for ventilation, the dogs wandered in. So I built a solid wood, two-part Dutch door out of heavy redwood planks. I had no idea how to do it at first, but it came out nice, driven by the frenzy of youth and drug withdrawal and the increasing magic of Krishna coming into my life.

Melanie: We expected a living representative of something connected to the East, so we had to prepare for the Swami to come. Cleaning out the temple, painting... I remember being in there one day and this guy came in and asked what we were doing. I said we were making a temple, and he said, “Oh, I fust came back from India. Гт an artist. Гll paint a picture for you.” And he did. He painted this picture of Vishnu, and He was blue and bad four arms. Then he used poetic license and had Him coming out of a volcano. This was a huge picture, maybe four feet square. So that was like the only piece of art we had except for the prayer mat Sam had brought back from Morocco. So when the Swami came, that was it: Vishnu coming out of a volcano and an Arabic prayer mat hanging on the wall.

We put a sign in the window:

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY FOR KRISHNA CONSCIOUSNESS  
A. C. BHAKTIVEDANTA SWAMI  
LECTURES ON THE BHAGAVAD GITA  
MONDAY, WEDNESDAY, FRIDAY  
7 P.M.  
DAILY MORNING CLASS 7 A.M.  
HARE KRISHNA HARE KRISHNA  
KRISHNA KRISHNA HARE HARE  
HARE RAMA HARE RAMA  
RAMA RAMA HARE HARE  
ALL ARE INVITED TO ATTEND

There was an ice cream shop called The God’s Eye next door to the temple at 520 Frederick Street, and while working to get the temple ready, we’d go in there for refreshments. They went out of business in early January and were immediately replaced by the Diggers. In the early days of Haight-Ashbury, there were four main groups that shaped community consciousness: (1) The local rock bands, (2) the Oracle newspaper, (3) the Communications Company (which published daily broadsheets and passed them out on the streets), and (4) the Diggers, a loose-knit group of hippie activists led by Emmett Grogan and Arthur Lisch. The Diggers believed that everything-food, lodging, clothes, drugs, transport-should be free. Money had ruined society, they said, and should be banned. The Digger Manifesto was published in the fall of 1966:

MONEY IS AN UNNECESSARY EVIL

It is addicting.

It is a temptation to the weak (most of the violent crimes in our city in some way involve money).

It can be hoarded, blocking the free flow of energy, and the giant energy-hoards of Montgomery Street will soon give rise to a sudden and thus explosive release of this trapped energy, causing much pain and chaos.

As part of the city’s campaign to stem the causes of violence, the San Francisco Diggers announce a 30-day period beginning now during which all responsible citizens are asked to turn in their money. No questions will be asked.

Bring money to your local Digger for free distribution to all. The Diggers will then liberate its energy according to the style of whoever receives it.

The Diggers had opened a free store at 520 Frederick Street called Free Frame of Reference. Immediately, there was trouble. The police raided 520 on January 8 as we were cleaning our storefront temple next door. Some of the Diggers ran into the temple and we hid them in the basement.

We were all still kind of in this hippie mood, thinking we were liberated and nothing belonged to anybody, so it was a smooth transition to Krishna conscious thinking—nothing is ours; everything belongs to Krishna. We became good friends with the Diggers next door, and with the Hell’s Angels too, who gradually made 520 their quasi-headquarters. We could hear this gang of hairy, leather-clad bikers one door down chanting “Harley Krishna.” By January 1967, you could add two more major influences to the Haight-Ashbury scene: (5) the Hell’s Angels, and (6) the Hare Krishna temple people.

The Swami was coming, but the big news in town was the Human Be-In. The front page of Oracle #5 showed a naked, bearded, Indian holy man with a third eye blazing from his forehead, announcing “A Gathering of the Tribes for a Human Be-In.” This page was made into posters and put up all over town:

A union of love and activism previously separated by categorical dogma and label mongering will finally occur ecstatically when Berkeley activists and hip community and San Francisco’s spiritual generation and contingents from the emerging revolutionary generation all over California meet for a gathering of the Tribes for a Human Be-In at the Polo Fields in Golden Gate Park on Saturday, January 14, 1967, from 1 to 5 p.m.

The result of this “Meeting of the Tribes for a Human Be-In” would be:

A new concert of human relations being developed within the youthful underground must emerge, become conscious, and should be shared so that a revolution of form can be filled with a Renaissance of compassion, awareness, and love in the Revelation of the unity of all mankind. The Human Be-In is the joyful, face-to-face beginning of the new epoch.

Over twenty thousand souls gathered in the park on that sunny January 14,1967, afternoon, and the hippie era was born. Mukunda and I watched from the crowd as Timothy Leary proclaimed, “Turn On, Tune In, Drop Out!” and Ginsberg represented the “spiritual generation” by leading mantra-chanting from the stage on this highly festive occasion. Owsley arrived by parachute from the sky. Yes, we said to each other, it’s happening.

The Swami would arrive in San Francisco in just three days. Talk about timing.

**\* \* \***

On the eve of the Swami’s arrival, here’s kind of how it stood: most of my generation of twenty-somethings had been raised on a diet of sense gratification at a very high level. We were born of wealthy parents in the booming, post-war economy. US per capita income in the ’50s and ’60s had risen 300%. We were nurtured by the phrase “Better Living Through Chemistry,” which was the motto, the way of life, of our parents. For the past many years we had used chemical substances to boost our sense enjoyment, almost daily.

We hadn’t met the Swami yet. Except for his words on the Happening record, we hadn’t even heard him speak. The only words of his we had were in broken English, in the hard-to-follow volumes of Srimad-Bhagavatam that the Swami had translated in India and brought over in a steamer trunk. Even these volumes we had to borrow from Mukunda—we didn’t have our own copies. There was almost no literature about Krishna available in bookstores. On the cusp of global travel, very few Americans had been to India. Melanie and I had never met an Indian person in our lives.

Mukunda didn’t know a lot himself, and being a very private man, he almost never confided in us what he did know. Same with Joanie—she didn’t know much, but the few days she’d spent with the Swami in New York were enough to convince her about how great he must be. Mike and Joanie were the saintliest people I knew. So we were attracted by what might be. We had faith that Mukunda and Joanie were bringing us the teacher most qualified to guide us to the highest level of spiritual perfection.

What really helped swing the deal for me was that the Swami was Allen Ginsberg’s friend. The original beatnik, Ginsberg had influenced me more than any other living person to date. If I had any gurus, they were Allen Ginsberg and his friend Jack Kerouac. And Ginsberg was going to be at the airport to meet the Swami, too!

Melanie had recently bought (at Tandy’s craft store on Market Street) some of the same big red beads that Mukunda wore. We strung them up and wore them from time to time, but we didn’t really know how to chant on them. We were too shy to chant with others in kirtan. We didn’t even really know what Krishna looked like. Mukunda had a couple of bright-colored posters of Krishna, printed in India. I thought they were kind of kitschy, but they showed a blue-skinned man with long black hair, very handsome, wearing a peacock feather, earrings, and jewelry made of gold and precious stones. He was playing a flute, being nuzzled by a Brahma cow. OK, this was a nice way to see God—as a beautiful person. But we really didn’t have a clue what Krishna consciousness was all about, except that you had to give up all your vices, the daily resort to better living through chemistry, and learn how to chant the Hare Krishna mantra.

“But who’s the woman?”

“That’s Radha, Krishna’s female expansion, so He can experience love.”

“So Radha is Krishna’s girlfriend?”

“Sort of.”

See what I mean?

It seemed better to just meet the Swami and hear what it’s all about from him.

Mukunda had told us that the Swami would not accept unmarried couples as disciples. Either they got married or lived separately. Mukunda and Janaki had had a civil marriage in New York City, then been married by the Swami at the Second Avenue temple. OK, sounds like a plan. But Melanie and I kept putting off our marriage. Who knows why. It was a pretty big step, and maybe we were doubtful. But more likely we just didn’t think about it because we were so busy preparing for the Swami’s arrival. One morning, after I’d been up all night working, Melanie came running into the temple and said, “Come on, let’s go get hitched.” So we drove down to the Civic Center in San Francisco.

We got to City Hall early. The marriage office was just opening and we were the first ones in the door. We bought the license ($14) and went into the next room, where the Deputy Marriage Commissioner asked us, “Where’s the ring?” We had remembered to bring ID, and Mukunda and Janaki were there as witnesses, but a ring? Mukunda was chewing some Wrigley’s spearmint gum, so he opened a stick and fashioned the metal foil wrapper into a perfect “O”—and that was our wedding ring. The date was January 17,1967.

The Swami would arrive at the airport in three hours.

### 2

We mill around the United terminal in front of the arrival gate at SFO, about fifty of us (in early 1967 you could still do that) mostly dressed in our very best clothes— suits and ties if we had them, the girls in normal dresses, our dog Ralph roaming the place without a leash. Aspiring devotees were clicking finger cymbals, banging bongos, in a timid kirtan. Some of the Haight Street crowd is here too, the ones who do Tarot and I-Ching, fellow seekers of deliverance, dressed in a wild array of robes and outfits, beads and feathers. And a few reporters. Allen Ginsberg shows up in a dark suit, white button-down shirt, and a red tie, a big yellow chrysanthemum in his lapel: our hero. Allen had starred in the Human Be-in at Golden Gate Park just three days ago, an event that had focused the world’s attention on the exciting new phenomenon that was the San Francisco scene. Now here he was, greeting Bhaktivedanta Swami at the airport.

We watch the planes land one by one, sure that the one carrying the Swami will be glowing. The Douglas DC-8 from New York pulls up to the gate, and maybe two hundred people get off, slowly, mostly squares—“straights,” we called them—ogling and uncomfortable to see all these strange people in the arrivals lounge. But no Swami. Maybe he’s in disguise? Maybe he missed his flight?

Then we see him through the sun-bright windows, last man out, a lone figure stepping out of the fuselage, looking around, a little white bag in his hand. He descends the ramp with noble steps and slowly walks toward us, not exactly grinning with anticipation, but you can see he’s happy.

The girls had raided Golden Gate Park earlier and made a simple garland of red roses, which Mukunda slips around the Swami’s neck as he enters the room. The Swami is resplendent in saffron dhoti and long silk shirt over a white turtleneck, a saffron cape draped around his shoulders. Janaki hands him a bouquet, and they exchange loving greetings. The Swami has a big, big smile that reveals all his teeth. His face is a bright gold and youthful, unwrinkled by anxiety or fear. He lifts his head and surveys the room: you can tell he understands everything at a glance. Joy swells in our hearts to see him. Such a welcome relief, hopeful, uplifting. Is this what they call ecstasy? Swami slips back into gravity, looking at each of us in turn as if to say, “Well, what did you expect?”

Melanie: So suddenly there were flashbulbs going off, and they blinded me, and I realized I was on the floor. And there were a few others on the floor. You might think we were offering our obeisances, but we had no idea about obeisances at that point. So I'm on the floor looking up, and there’s this shimmering personally gliding by. I saw these plastic white shoes. My first thought was, “He isn’t so big.” Then right behind him was our dog. I remember thinking, “Ralph’s smarter than 1 am; he’s on his feet and following him.” So I got up, and everybody started to follow him.

Full-bearded Allen Ginsberg is leading the way, singing the Hare Krishna mantra loudly and ringing his little cymbals, the Swami beside him accepting more flowers from well-wishers as he passes. Allen looks left to grin between mantra and chorus, his face lit up seeing the Swami marching along as if he knows the way, while Swami swaps a few words over his shoulder with Mukunda, who is right behind him in a black sports coat pulled over his white NASA jumpsuit and a red carnation in his lapel.

Ralph darts out in front of the mob and runs to sniff another small dog on a leash, the Swami not even noticing as the cymbals ring and kirtan roars in the enclosed passageway that’s leading us outside. I’m scoping the Swami from the right flank. I’m wearing my best duds—a four-year-old ski sweater. A movie camera is strapped to my shoulder (although I still can’t figure out how it works). Melanie’s behind me in a purple paisley poncho and her usual dangly earrings, and Roger’s there in red beads and a gold-colored sweater. We’re a resounding parade, most people in street clothes, but a few long-haired hippies in striped pants and Merlin outfits. Janaki in a bright-red, knee-length dress is beside the Swami now, at curbside, waiting for Harvey to swing in with his Cadillac. She’s directing the Swami to dump his flower load into a cardboard box.

Melanie and I look at each other, delighted that we’ve made the right choice. No one else has ever looked this great to us—glowing and knowing, a little gray fuzz on his shining head. He seems to float across the linoleum, lighting everything around him. We can’t keep our eyes off him.

Melanie: Seeing the Swami at the airport was like seeing the sun rise in a clear sky. Later, I heard that other devotees had the same experience. We were pretty heavily contaminated people, and this was our first contact with pure goodness. It just kind of pierced through all that covering and you got this little lightning jolt of clarity, which you couldn't understand, and it just knocked you over, literally speaking.

**\* \* \***

The Swami walks into the crowded temple on Frederick Street, our little storefront painted white, and Mukunda shows him where to sit, on his dais. He’s just looking around, sizing the place up, seeing maybe a hundred hippies sitting there on the floor, standing against the walls. Geronimo is there, Jesus too, Mountain Man and Persephone, beads and bells, “love and peace, brother,” perfume and sweat. “Just open a little,” the Swami says, indicating the door. The Swami removes his kartals—four-inch round brass cymbals—from a small pouch and leads us in kirtan. Not many join in—no one knows the words yet, and we’re all a little shy.

Then Swami begins to speak in Sanskrit. Wow! The language sounds so good, so old, so full of rhyme and meter. Rayarama says the Swami knows thousands of these verses by heart. How could someone who knew this stuff be sitting in this room with us?

“This shloka, Sanskrit verse, it means: What is called renunciation is the same as yoga, or linking oneself with the Supreme. For no one can become a yogi unless he renounces the desire for sense gratification.”

Getting right to it, quickly sorting out the willing from the hesitant.

“Nityo nityanam cetanas cetananam. Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, is the leader of all other personalities. He’s also person, we are also person, but He is leader. Of all living entities, He is the supreme living entity. Of all eternals, He is the supreme eternal.”

Then he drops a bombshell: “There is no love in the material world. No love and no peace.”

He speaks honestly, in such a way—quoting Sanskrit authorities, giving logical examples—that he does not offend. He’s giving the truth in a way that you accept it. He talks of divine love, that the source of divine love is Krishna. He says that this material world, all this madness around us, is an illusory state of consciousness called “maya.”

“Just like above the cloud there is sunshine, fully. We have seen it in an aeroplane today.” (Ah, and it hits me: this was his first-ever trip in an airplane.) “This airlines, United Airlines, they say ‘friendly skies.’ So go to the friendly sky. Why do you remain here, nonsense sky, always covered with cloud? The cloud is maya. Go above the maya. Then you will see.”

When the Swami leads another kirtan, everyone is now eager to join, and the room fills with uproarious sound as Swami closes his eyes and gets into it.

Melanie: What I felt like, he had such love in him and that love was being extended to all of us in that room without any condition. I think, for many of us at that point, we were falling in love with the Swami.

He says, “You may come every morning here, at seven o’clock, and we shall speak. And in evening at seven o’clock—what is that?”

Mukunda says, “Monday, Wednesday, and Friday.”

“And we shall try to answer your questions to your full satisfaction. Mukunda, you are ready? One thing, just make one sign and put here, with maha-mantra printed in big size.”

**\* \* \***

The next morning, January 18, he comes down to speak from a book called Chaitanya Charitamrita. We don’t have a clue what that is, this book, but we drink in every word. For thousands of years a major portion of the world practiced Krishna consciousness. But by about 1500 AD, after two thousand years of Buddhist and Muslim influence, the ancient Krishna science was all but extinct. Mayavadi philosophy swept the land. “Mayavadi” means impersonalism: I am God/you are God; God is an idea, not a person; spiritual perfection is merging into the void.

Lord Chaitanya—almost seven feet tall, long-limbed, with a golden complexion and long black hair—appeared in 1486 in Bengal, India. He was an incarnation of Radha and Krishna combined, who came to earth as a Krishna devotee to experience love for Himself. By His teachings and example, Chaitanya revived chanting of the maha-mantra as the simple way to make direct contact with God as the Supreme Person: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. Chaitanya would meet and debate the greatest scholars of his time, using evidence from Vedic scriptures, not “argumentum baculum” (wow! Swami knows Latin too?), to defeat them. Chaitanya reintroduced the maha-mantra and sankirtan, congregational chanting, and proposed:

Glory to the Sri Krishna sankirtan, which cleanses the heart of all the dust accumulated for years and extinguishes the fire of conditioned life, of repeated birth and death. This sankirtan movement is the prime benediction for humanity at large because it spreads the rays of the benediction moon. It is the life of all transcendental knowledge. It increases the ocean of transcendental bliss, and it enables us to fully taste the nectar for which we are always anxious.

Feel the bliss of love for Himself? I don’t quite get this, but it sounds all right. I’m thinking, “OK, this Chaitanya fellow is a kind of hippie—one of us—with His long hair and yellow robes. I can dig that. And He was a contemporary of Shakespeare, so not that long ago. He’s always singing and dancing and going into ecstasy. This could be good.”

I can’t take my eyes off the Swami. He’s just so beautiful—I mean, physically beautiful. He says, “Another name for Krishna is Bhagawan. Bhagawan means He has all the opulences in full. There are six opulences: Krishna is all-powerful; He knows everything—He’s got all knowledge; He’s got all wealth because everything belongs to Him; He’s all-famous—everyone knows Him; He’s all-beautiful; He’s all-renounced. That means He is the all-attractive. Krishna means ‘all-attractive.’”

I get it. Because Bhaktivedanta Swami is the most attractive person I’ve ever seen, and this guy only works for Krishna—he’s the messenger—so how beautiful Krishna must be! The Swami’s physical beauty seems ever fresh and energetic, fueled by transcendental energy, like the fluid grace you see in youth. The Swami’s eyes, his large mouth, his facial expressions—all play together with everything he says. He would break mid-sentence, a dramatic pause— “Uhhh”—like returning to the tamboura drone in an Indian raga. He seems to know everything—and I can’t take my ears away.

When a couple of the New York devotees ask him questions after his lecture, they call him “Swamiji.” Should I call him Swamiji?

Yes, Rayarama, says. “Swamiji is more intimate, like ‘dear Swami,’ so you can call him Swamiji.”

The Swami goes upstairs to get settled. About fifteen minutes later, Mukunda comes back and tells Melanie and me that Swamiji wants to see us. So we follow him up to the Swami’s apartment. “Put your shoes over here,” Mukunda says.

Mukunda had already told us to bow down, so we do, and as we rise, the Swami looks at me with a big smile and says, “Oh, this big blood-vein in your forehead—it means you were devotee in your last life.”

Mukunda introduces us and tells him we want to get initiated. Yes, we were already married by civil ceremony (leaving out that we only got married this morning). Mukunda must have talked us up a little because the Swami doesn’t hesitate. “I shall accept you as my disciples.” He picks a date for the ceremony three days from now and tells us he will prepare a list of ingredients we’ll need for it. Mukunda and Janaki had been telling us about their fancy Hindu wedding in New York, so we ask the Swami if we can also get married that way, and he picks a date for that too: January 23, two days after our initiation. Two other aspirants, Harvey Cohen and his girlfriend, Hope, will be initiated with us.

The senior devotees in those days were just a few weeks or months “older” than we were, so they didn’t know a lot about this stuff either. We were told to have a few things ready. We couldn’t understand any of it—bricks, sand, wood, rice, five pounds of butter. I asked, “Butter, for what?” They said, “To put on the fire.” Fire? I asked, but they said margarine was not an option. I was nearly broke, and butter cost over a buck a pound, so I went to a creamery and got butter that had touched machinery, unfit for human consumption by USDA standards. I signed a disclaimer and was given ten pounds as a donation “for religious purposes” provided we agreed not to consume it. Don’t worry, I said, we’re putting it in a fire.

So there we were in this little storefront, crowded with people who didn’t really know what was happening. What we did know: this ceremony would change our lives forever.

We had put down a metal sheet so that the oriental carpet wouldn’t burn. Then we constructed a fire pit about two-feet square of sand enclosed by bricks, laid out some wood from broken-up orange crates, lit dozens of incense sticks, and stacked grains and powders and fruits and flowers around the edge of the pit. The girls had scraped the machine grease off five pounds of the butter (saving the other five pounds for the wedding in two days), and it sat melted in a little pot. The temple is packed to the brim—even the Chinese lady from the laundry next door squeezes in to check out this wild scene.

I’m in white shirt and skinny dark tie, a borrowed sports coat over black jeans with a twenty-nine-inch waist. Melanie’s wearing one of my shirts, long-sleeved with a blue paisley pattern, and a brown cor-du toy skirt. They told us the Swami would chant on our big red beads, so we had those ready, and that someone would tie three strands of small wooden beads around our necks at the end of the ceremony, signifying that we were now initiated Krishna devotees.

The Swami comes in. While everybody’s chanting he starts building the fire, and smoke begins to fill the room. The four of us sit on the floor opposite Swamiji and on the other side of the fire. It’s getting hotter by the second. I discard my coat and loosen my tie. Devotees are chanting on every side, edging closer to the flames to catch the Swami’s words. He motions for us to take a few grains of rice and throw them whenever he says, Sva-ha! “Sva-haaa!” and we toss the rice at him, four handfuls of rice all over his gleaming head and robes. Unfazed, he shakes his head and points to the fire. Oh, so rice goes into the fire. Slow, but were catching on.

His accent is thick, or maybe we just haven’t learned to hear him yet. He’s asking for bananas? Someone hands us bananas, and we start to peel them to eat. No, no, the Swami gestures. Put them in the fire. Man, then the smoke really starts pouring out: four bananas smoldering, everyone coughing, eyes watering—and in goes the rest of the rice, then the lentils, flowers, grapes, flour, and, yes, more butter, which Swami ladles profusely over the flames between rounds of cryptic Sanskrit prayers ending with Sva-hal The smoke doesn’t seem to bother the Swami at all. He chants on our beads, hands them back with our new names, which we don’t understand—wait, I got that one, Harry das, that’s Harvey—and then devotees come forward to tie our neckbeads on with a knot.

Then the Swami says, “Bo dun,” and we’re trying to figure out what he’s saying. Harry das thinks he has it figured out and rolls onto his knees, leans forward—we do the same thing—and he blows on the fire. We all start blowing on the fire, hard as we can. A cloud of smoke flies into the Swami’s face, and his eyes get big as saucers.

Malati: One thing that gave us great delight in the hippie lifestyle, you liked to blow someone’s mind. But how could you blow the Swami's mind? You couldn’t. He was with Krishna and Krishna could lift a mountain with His little finger. But once in a while we did something that was close to mind-blowing. His eyes would get big and you knew that was, whoa! This was my first time seeing what we called his “saucer eyes.”

We stopped blowing on the fire when Swamiji picked up his kartals and started a kirtan. Later, we understood, of course, that he was trying to tell us to “bow down.” This was just a small sample of the Swami’s tolerance of our barbaric behavior. Were we worth it? Maybe the Swami had an eternal vision and we were somehow part of it.

**\* \* \***

So we were now officially Shyamasundar das and Malati devi dasi. Rayarama tells me that “Shyamasundar” is one of Krishna’s names when He’s playing His flute and dancing, and it means “beautiful like the peacock feather.” With “das,” my name means “servant of the All-beautiful.” “Malati” is a jasmine flower, so she is servant to one of Krishna’s favorite flowers.

Our wedding day was coming next. My attitude was, OK, I want to have a family, kids, and I guess Melanie is as good as I’ll find. We’ve gone through some heavy stuff together. Now we have the same spiritual desire—to follow the Swami. Mike seems to be OK with being married, and he’s always done the right thing in my eyes. And the Swami seems to be saying that we are not really these bodies, that wife, children, and parents are just artificial designations, and the main thing is to make a personal connection with Krishna—so any wife, what does it matter? It’s almost arbitrary. The Swami had said, “Unrestricted sense enjoyment is the cause of material encagement, but one who follows the rules and regulations of the revealed scriptures does not become entangled by the sense objects. For example, sex enjoyment is a necessity for the conditioned soul, and sex enjoyment is allowed under the license of marriage ties. A man is inclined to have sex relationships with women. This propensity is to be curbed; otherwise it will be a stumbling block on the path of self-realization.”

We make wedding preparations. I call my parents in Salem, Oregon, and my Mom agrees to fly down. The wedding is set for a Tuesday, so my Dad has to work in his law office. Melanie’s Uncle Bob (without wife Sue) will drive over from Sacramento. My sister Carolyn, who has recently moved from Salem to San Francisco (she’s pretty square and has a job) says she’s coming too.

The Swami ordered a big feast for our wedding. Swamiji himself showed the girls how to do everything, and they cooked day and night to prepare an elaborate Indian feast for distribution to two hundred devotees and guests.

I’m wearing a new shirt with Nehru collar and Malati is wearing the sari. (There was just one sari available—Janaki’s wedding sari— so the girls will share it as needed.) Malati looks good in its bright orange with gold-embroidered borders. She has a white flower in her hair. Janaki said we had to bind our clothes together—it’s a traditional Hindu custom, she said—so Malati slips a corner of her sari through my belt loop and ties a knot.

Carolyn: The storefront on Frederick Street was hot and filled with lots of young hippies, like a slightly wonky rainbow. I was standing just inside the front door, feeling grateful for the fresh air. And I was self-conscious, because in the traditional mode of “going to a wedding,” I had selected a green wool dress with nylon stockings and little black pumps, accessorized with boyfriend Jeff in navy blue blazer. I was hopeful Sam's flower friends would forgive us for being clueless victims of the material world. Our mother was there too, her face inscrutable and nonjudgmental. Perhaps her maternal barometer registering fear for Sam’s unknown destination was tempered by her admiration for him as a torchbearer for the truth-seekers in her own family history.

The wedding fire ceremony went off without a hitch—at least this time we knew not to throw rice at Swamiji and not to peel the bananas or blow smoke in his eyes.

Malati: I had to answer the call of nature but held it through the whole ceremony. But we were tied together with my sari. Afterward, Shyamasundar went into the bathroom first, and I stood outside the door dutifully, and then I went in and he stood outside the door. And then they said we had to stay like this for a week. We realized this wasn’t going to work, so we took our clothes off later and left them tied together in the closet.

For our wedding day, Rock and the Grateful Dead had sent over bunches of long-stemmed zinnias and calla lilies—a huge amount of them—and we stuck them in large metal milk jugs and put them right by the door. After the wedding, on his way out, the Swami paused and looked at the lilies and said, “No fragrance, simply useless. Like a handsome man without intelligence, simply useless.”

At last! I had a master, a real spiritual father, and I was now his bonded servant. I had a wife, a well-defined way of life, and a new freedom from material anxiety. The Swami had lightened our spirits, and he quickly became our hilarious leader and playmate in this blissful game called Krishna consciousness.

Carolyn: I lived not far from Sam in San Francisco in an apartment on Pine Street. It was only minutes from Haight-Ashbury, but it was miles from his world. I brought home a paycheck every two weeks as a salesperson at Shreve & Company, so although my life was somewhat pedestrian at age 23 (awaiting the time when a brilliant and successful husband would take me away from all this), I was able to pay my rent and make good on my charge account at Joseph Magnin. But Sam, he had always been an unconventional traveler in a conventional world, both of us born of the same two parents, but so different in navigating the adulthood into which we were thrust. He fearlessly accepted risk without the burden of consequence, while I could be known to fret endlessly over a bad hair day. The mind-video that has played in my head these many years is that of an orange-robed Swami seated in front of a fire on the floor, Sam and Melanie across from him. His dark eyes reflected a mysterious history, and his aged face had seen a thousand years. He was chanting and throwing rice into the flames, sending up billows of smoke into the murky air. The atmosphere among Sam’s friends was euphoric. Joyful. Who knew this old storefront on Frederick Street was actually the launching pad for Sam’s life’s trajectory?

On January 25, the Swami wrote to his disciples in New York: “On the marriage ceremony day there was extraordinary gatherings (150 at least)... In the morning class there are not less than 25 students and in the evening there are 30 to 50 students. And I think they are gradually understanding the philosophy seriously. It is very encouraging.”

**\* \* \***

OK, the Swami is here, were initiated, and now were hitched, but the Mantra Rock dance is just a few days away and we have to hustle. We split up the chores: Mukunda liaises with the light-show people and with Chet for stage arrangements and the sound system. My job is to make sure the bands show up and have everything they need, and to get them on and off the stage. Roger’s job is decorations and, along with the girls, to prepare a couple of thousand oranges for prasadam (sacred food) distribution. Malati, Joan, and Janaki are in charge of the box office and ticket taking. We don’t worry too much about these things anymore: Krishna has taken over and He makes things happen for the Swami, His confidential devotee. It’s all pretty smooth, routine; the bands and Avalon management do this so often, and everybody shows up and it all happens.

On January 29, we go over to the Ballroom early to set things up. The stage is draped with flowers and palm branches, banners, and every picture we could strip from the temple. By 7 p.m. people are pouring in. “Krishna Consciousness Comes West” at Avalon Ballroom is a sold-out show before 8 p.m. Latecomers have to wait in line until someone comes out before they can go in. The main room is about 100' x 180', space enough for five to seven hundred dancers on the polished wooden floor, but the red velveteen walls are lined with mirrors, so it looks much bigger. It’s dark, but a revolving mirror-ball with a thousand facets, three feet in diameter, hangs from the room’s center and 10,000-watt sparkles move over every surface in the packed hall. The whole place smells of weed and incense.

Only yesterday the Swami had read to us a five-thousand-year-old verse from Srimad-Bhagavatam ("The Book of God”) that predicts the future: Kali-yuga, the final age of decadence, will be recognized when the world is populated by low-born men dressed as kings, wearing long hair to beautify their bodies. Looking around the Avalon that night we could see that Kali-yuga is here and now. Owsley is skulking around in mufti, but everyone knows he’s there; a guy in a top hat and a red, white, and blue sash is calling himself the Mayor of San Francisco; Chet Helms; Allen Cohen; Mike Bowen; Tim Leary—everyone’s there, except Ken Kesey, who is laying low from the FBI.

Janaki: Yeah, one of my idols came in, Timothy Leary, and he was so drunk that I couldn’t believe it. He was with a girl who looked to be about 14, falling all over her—and Malati wasn’t going to let him in, but he was Tim Leary after all.

The stage is maybe fifty feet long, and a dozen Krishna devotees self-consciously assemble up there—we hardly know each other yet, and in front of so many people—and Haridas takes the mike and talks a bit about the mantra. He says that Bhaktivedanta Swami and Allen Ginsberg will be coming later. Then Mukunda with electrified tamboura strums a solo haunting drone to calm the crowd and we warm up with the Nama Оm prayer and a mellow Hare Krishna kirtan. Refrigerator-sized speakers fill the huge space with sound. The light show is a bit jerky at first, but the boys in the booth soon find their stride, and colored images of Krishna, mandalas, Sanskrit mantras, and flowers and abstract pulses of fluorescent oils wash over walls and stage.

I rush backstage to cue Moby Grape—five minutes, guys! Sam Andrew and the Big Brother band members are there too; Janice is slamming Southern Comfort in the dressing room, getting a buzz on for the main event. But where are the Dead? I told Rock nine o’clock...

Then Moby Grape takes the stage and, man, they are good. This is their first major gig, and the fans are impressed. Their three guitars, driving rhythm, and polished vocal harmonies are a cut above what’s been heard around San Francisco before. Moby Grape’s leader, Skip Spence, announces a special song he wrote for the Swami that night (it will appear on their first album later in 1967):

What’s that song you’re singing?  
Just a bell ringing in my mind  
What’s that tune you’re bringing?  
Just part of what there is to find  
What a difference a day has made  
What a difference a day has made  
What a difference and more of the same  
What’s this deeper here?  
Something that you’ve always been  
Is it you I’ve finally found?

As their set winds down, Allen Ginsberg and his friend Peter Orlovsky wander up to the stage and are greeted with a huge ovation. I’m up in the balcony, looking down: Allen in bushy black beard and horn-rimmed glasses is in a rumpled, white, open-necked dress shirt with four pens stuck in his pocket, a single strand of multicolored beads around his neck, and bearded Peter is wearing his trademark Russian Cossack hat. Wait! Who’s that on Allen’s right? That’s Gary Snyder. Wow. Gary Snyder, another of my beatnik poet-heroes.

Gary Snyder was a Buddhist monk in Japan, went to Reed College, was a fire-lookout in the Cascade Mountains and, like Ginsberg, wrote poems that heavily influenced my generation. Snyder was the key figure in Jack Kerouac’s Dharma Bums—looking for Zen in the wilderness. His poems in Myths and Texts and Mountains and Rivers Without End inspired the hippies’ love for nature—and the subsequent environmental movement. He and Haridas looked so much alike with their matching beards I hardly noticed him at first. It stuns me to see him here at this crossroads event.

The stage is in chaos. Roadies move equipment and everyone looks around for a place to sit on the floor. Mukunda directs: “No, Allen, you’re over here—you guys move back.”

At the strike of ten o’clock, a spotlight shifts to the entryway, and Swamiji walks in, draped in a big gardenia garland, escorted by Kirtanananda and Ranchor. Ginsberg stands and greets the Swami with folded hands, what we later learn is a “pranam.” We’re on pins and needles: will the Swami like this scene? I mean, he’s never seen anything like this noise and confusion. Maybe he’ll just look around and walk out? Slowly, Bhaktivedanta Swami climbs up to the stage and sits like a saffron Buddha, as one by one garlands are heaped over his shoulders. He removes them while everyone settles down. He’s in the same silk shirt he wore on the flight, but buttoned up, his cape knotted at his right shoulder. He is absolutely effulgent. Ah, I’m seeing it now: he’s at home here in the preaching arena. He doesn’t make judgments. Maybe this will turn out all right.

Ginsberg takes the mike and introduces His Holiness Bhaktivedanta Swami. He tells about his own interest in the timeless knowledge of India, explaining how you connect with the transcendent by chanting mantras, especially the maha-mantra. “Maha means ‘great,’” he says, “and man means ‘mind’ and tra ‘that which delivers.’” He gives a brief background on the Swami—how he landed penniless on the Lower East Side of New York to bring this mantra to “the dispossessed, the lost, the anarchists, the seekers.” Shouts of approval from the crowd punctuate his words. Ginsberg tells them: Now you have a Krishna consciousness temple at 518 Frederick Street, and I invite everyone, recommending especially the early-morning services to LSD “reentry” people.

Then Swamiji clears his throat and speaks: “Thank you for inviting me to your beautiful city of San Francisco to speak here. This chant comes from India. It will lead us to the spiritual world. You may begin tonight or any time. The mantra is not only for Indians. Hare Krishna chanting is for all people, because Krishna is everyone’s father. We should not think that Krishna is Hindu god or is for the Indians and not others. He is for everyone. If He were not, how could He be God?... So this sound is the sound representation of the Supreme Lord. Material or spiritual, whatever we have got experience, nothing is separated from the Supreme Absolute Truth. Nothing is separated. There is link. The whole material cosmic manifestation, what we see, it is just like the smoke. The fire is behind it. That is spiritual sky. But still, in the smoke, you can feel some heat also. So similarly, this sound vibration of the spiritual world is here, so that even in this material world, where there is scarcity of that spiritual fire, we can appreciate, we can feel the warmth of that fire.”

Whew! He doesn’t mention rules and regulations, or half the people in the place would’ve gone out the door.

“Thank you very much,” he says, and takes up his kartals, gesturing to Allen to lead the kirtan.

What followed was amazing: cued by words flashed on the wall, the crowd quickly picked up the chant, band members ran onto the stage with instruments—one guy had a saxophone—and we had a jam session of celebrities: Don Stevenson on drums, Phil Lesh and Sam Andrew on guitars, Ginsberg with his cymbals, Snyder with a bongo drum. And Malati, with a big white flower behind each ear, Janaki in her paisley shift—we were pounding out the Hare Krishna mantra in sound that rocked the rafters.

Suddenly Swamiji stood and threw up his arms and started dancing—first time we re seeing the Swami two-step: one leg crosses in front, down, then the other, down, sway back and forth to the music—and the crowd goes wild. Everyone’s dancing and spinning as strobe lights erupt in ecstasy.

We’re seeing yet another dazzling side to the Swami: the showman, hippest of the hip, at home in this bizarre celebration. He loved it. All of our icons on one stage, and Swamiji is the star—he’s on a roll, mesmerizing everyone. I was to see this many times more in the future, gazing with wonder as the Swami takes over the show for another perfect performance.

After Swamiji leaves, Janis Joplin wails out “Down On Me” and “Ball and Chain” (“You can run all your life, but not go anywhere”), followed by the Grateful Dead, who did “I Know You Rider,” “Death Don’t Know No Mercy,” and took us to the edges of our minds: just when you thought they’re losing it, they’d hit a solid chord and it would all rush back.

Rock Scully: Yeah, out of may be a thousand gigs, that one really blew my mind—the Swami, he was one of us, and you could tell.

This Mantra Rock event stands out for me as the first time I “chased a rhinoceros” with the Swami. Swamiji had explained in a lecture that Krishna most appreciates devotees who risk the most on His behalf. And if you risk everything for Krishna, He must personally intervene to remove obstacles to your risky service. In this regard, the Swami said: “It is better to chase the rhinoceros. If you fail, no one can say he has failed—everyone knows you cannot capture a rhinoceros—but if you succeed, ohhh, they will say, he has done the impossible.”

Most of us in Haight-Ashbury in 1967 were there at great risk, giving up the comforts of homes, educations, careers, and society to become outcasts searching for meaning. Many of us had already at a young age traveled widely, taken great risks with drugs and the law, and carved out an independent lifestyle. So we immediately connected with the Swami’s courage vibe. We dug his message that to get Krishna you must take risks. You can say that we were all on a great adventure with the Swami. It was the most exhilarating adventure of our lives.

And you can say that most of our little crew had tasted inde-pendence—we were free-roaming and familiar with the lonely frontiers of ego-exploding drugs. Like me, these souls had been saved from near-impossible situations over and over, so we came to expect magic, a certain specialness that we’d been saved, a conviction that something higher was guiding our lives, that there was a purpose to our lives. It was not yet clear, but we were meant for some unusual destiny.

Swamiji was always up for an adventure, secure that whatever he did would come out OK because he had Krishna. He told us right away: you surrender to Krishna, He will protect you. OK, let’s try that one. I mean, in my life so far I had lived by the sword and, yes, I had died by the sword: jail and a terminal direction with drugs. But now I could keep the same excitement in my life, having adventures and taking risks, exploring the edges of possibility—but doing it for the Swami, for Krishna.

Perhaps not many at the Mantra Rock dance understood what the Swami was saying, or not much of it, but it was the rare few who do understand that he wanted to attract. He’s been telling us: Out of many millions, one may be attracted to understand Krishna; and out of millions of these, one may understand Him in full. The Mantra Rock affair becomes the Swami’s perfect introduction into the hippie world—the Hare Krishnas are accepted now. The Krishna consciousness temple is quickly established as the foremost spiritual center in Hippieland.

Next day I’m called to the Swami’s room. Mukunda (always Mr. Calm) briefs me a little, like take off your shoes. But I’m really nervous (What do I say? Will he...?). Big red beads clacking down the hall as I hang my coat and pad to the doorway, and peek in:

Swamiji sits lotus-style on a thin mattress on the floor behind a low coffee table, morning sun pouring through the window. I mark his radiance, his beaming, wide-eyed face, his eyes looking straight into mine, the swollen bow-curve and tremble of his dark, full lips around big teeth. So beautiful I nearly faint.

“Ahhh, Shyamasundar! So Mukunda has told me.”

Still standing, unsure, I plunk awkwardly to my knees and then bow down, maybe saying Hare Krishna as I’ve seen some others do.

“So you have made this nice dance last night?”

“Well, all of us worked...”

“No, no, Mukunda has told me, you are the leader for this. So, what was the income?”

“Uh, I’m not sure. I mean, Malati sold the tickets, she must know—but I think about $2000.”

His eyes grow even bigger, glancing with delight to Mukunda, who is still standing in the corner, then back to me.

“So, income was $2000. What was the expense?”

“I’m not sure. The hall was free, but we paid the bands something.”

“No, no. Leader means you must know what is the profit. Do you know bookkeeping?”

“Well, I took a class in high school, but...”

“Then I will teach you. Mukunda, just bring some little paper here.”

Motioning me to scoot over and sit beside him behind the low table—his grace, his fragrance, which I later learn is sandalwood— he slips his glasses from a little case, takes his pen from another case and—brushing my arm with his shoulder, the soft rustle of cotton, and jolts of electricity shoot through my neck like a heroin rush and I am overwhelmed with well-being.

“Now, in keeping books there are always two sides, income and debit” (debt? dabit? What did he say?), and he slashes a bold line down the middle of the page and writes INCOME above one column and EXPENSES above the other. Under “Income” he jots down Sales, Donations, and Fees—fees?—and under “Expenses” he writes Rent, Food, and Flowers. His script is flowery and bold. Minutes (feels like hours) pass as I reel with comfort, his voice a gentle murmur in my ear.

Then: “So, what is your understanding of the purpose of life?” What! Did he just say... ? “Well, er, to become one with God?” “Yessss. God is one, yes, but you are also one. So there are two.

You and God. What is that relationship, you have to understand. Gradually you will come to understand. Now, this is the important thing: bookkeeping. So you come every morning and sit down here and I will show you... Mukunda, what is our next program?”

As I slip out the door I know, I know, I know: this guy is the real thing, the magic teacher I’ve always wanted. But I’m an action man. I don’t like keeping track of money, doing books. I go up to his room a few more times, just to be close to him, and get chewed out if I don’t have a receipt for 37 cents, but am very pleased when Haridas, our new temple president, soon assigns the treasurer job to a more qualified devotee.

Malati: The first time I went into the Swami’s room was to clean it while he went out for a. walk, and there was no toilet paper in the bathroom, and there were these twigs, and the ends of them were kind of chewed. A succession of absurd thoughts about mysterious swamis went through my head. Later, Janaki clued me in, like he uses water instead of toilet paper and eucalyptus twigs instead of toothbrushes.

**\* \* \***

I was getting used to calling her “Malati”—close enough to Melanie— and except for the no sex part, she was still pretty much the same gal: quiet, but man could she talk if you got her started. She was determined, busy, always planning something. She had taken the Swami’s instructions to heart and was very rigid in following them. She set the disciplined tone to our new lives as disciples: up early, chanting the mantra on beads—what we learned to call “rounds”-stumbling off at dawn down the foggy streets of San Francisco to the temple to hear the Swami speak.

Swamiji. We had only a vague idea of what a swami was. Weren’t those the guys who walked on coals over in India, played flutes in front of swaying cobras, climbed ropes up into the clouds?
