But he told us straight away: “There is a definition. ‘One who can control the tongue, one who can control the mind, one who can control the words, one who can control the belly, one who can control the generative organ, he is swami.’ And prthivim sa sisyat: ‘He is allowed to create disciples all over the world.’”

The Swami tells us about our line of disciplic succession, our sampradaya: “My Guru Maharaja was in the tenth generation from Lord Chaitanya. We are eleventh from Lord Chaitanya. The disciplic succession is as follows: 1. Sri Krishna, 2. Brahma, 3. Narada, 4. Vyasa, 5. Madhva, 6. Padmanabha, 7. Nrihari, 8. Madhava, 9. Akshobhya, 10. Jayatirtha, 11. Jnanasindhu, 12. Purushottama, 13. Vidyanidhi, 14. Rajendra, 15. Jayadharma, 16. Purushottama, 17. Vyasatirtha, 18. Lakshmipati, 19. Madhavendra Puri, 20. Ishvara Puri, 21. Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, 22. Rupa, 23. Raghunath, 24. Krishna dasa, 25. Narottama, 26. Vishvanatha, 27. Jagannatha, 28. Bhaktivinode and Gaura-kishora, 29. Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Saraswati, 30. Sri Srimad Bhaktivedanta.”

Mind-boggling stuff to find out that we’re in this august lineage of great sadhus stretching back to time immemorial. The contrast between these ancient glowing saints and our present company—scruffy hippies weaned on Cheerios, Mickey Mouse, and J. C. Penney—seems ridiculous at times. Sometimes we joke about it. “What d’ya suppose Chaitanya would think about the Mantra Rock dance?” “Hey Hayagriva, when are you going to shave your head?” “You think Lord Brahma ever wore a NASA jumpsuit, Mukunda?”

And it was hard to keep all the players straight: Wait, Chaitanya, got that, but Nitya-who? and this Rupa Goswami, Sanatana, what? Of course, some devotees were more scholarly, like Upendra and Joanie, and they began learning some Sanskrit. But I was just too excited, too busy—I wanted to act for Krishna, and to heck with Sanskrit and minute details of philosophy.

We rise each day before dawn, excited to see our newfound master giving wisdom to his assembled devotees: “So far salvation is concerned, nobody can be liberated without bhakti, without devotional service, without love of Godhead. That is the verdict of Krishna in Bbagavad-gita: sarva-dharman parityajya mam ekam saranam vraja, ‘You give up all other engagement. Just be surrendered unto Me and be engaged in My service.’”

Surrender'. That’s the hard one.

“Krishna says, bhaktas te ’tiva me priyah: ‘Who completely engages himself in My service, he is very, very dear to Me.’”

And this is the kicker: “For one who explains this supreme secret to the devotees, there is no servant more dear to Me than he.” In other words, get out there and spread Krishna consciousness if you really want Krishna to notice you.

The Swami was just so cool, so calm and collected. Nothing seemed to disturb his continuous happiness. I didn’t get to see him in private much at first. I just watched him sit up there on his little dais in that safe storefront island in a sea of historical madness and talk to us. His speech came from a time when there was grammar and diction; discourse and conversation were well thought out before spoken. The Swami’s language was novel—and memorable-in our world of imprecise description, slang, acronyms, words that take form quick but miss the mark. With lilting precision, Swamiji lays out the deepest imaginable subject matter: “We are trying to have ananda or bliss”—wow, another great new word—“by the limited resources of our senses, but actually there is no ananda, no pleasure. It is all miserable. This miserable body is condemned in practically every chapter, every shloka, every verse. Practically every one of us, we are mad. Pramatah means mad. Because we have identified with this body as ‘I am.’”

And then he goes on to tell us—I don’t catch it all, my mind is reeling-something about the material and the spiritual energies, two energies, and we really belong to spirit but have become trapped in matter—and this is how we get free: “Engage your senses. You can’t deny your senses—that is a useless struggle—just engage your senses for Krishna, for His enjoyment, not yours. Then you are a yogi.”

He told us that Krishna consciousness is a science, meaning an absolute truth verifiable by reason and logic. Most of us were educated, we knew about the rise and fall of empires, Darwinian evolution, E=mc2, but we knew too that material knowledge had failed to make people happy. Swami was taking us to the root of knowledge, Vedanta, and we sensed that what was happening around us, this whole hippie phenomenon, was a historic moment in time when real knowledge, real science, was being revealed. Swamiji said, you learn this science, then you will know everything, the absolute truth, holding out the possibility to us: “Yes, you can be like me.”

And finally we find out who Radha is: “This material attraction certainly cannot attract the Supreme Lord. When He wants to be attracted by a woman, He has to create such a woman from His own energy. That woman is Radharani. It is explained that Radha is the manifestation of the pleasure potency of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. When the Supreme Lord wants to derive transcendental pleasure, He has to create a woman from His internal pleasure potency, hladini-shakti. Radharani is the tenderhearted feminine counterpart of the supreme whole, resembling the perfectional stage of the worldly feminine nature. Because Krishna wants to experience love, He creates Radha.”

It takes two to tango—something like that. In other words, She is His girlfriend.

It’s almost too much to absorb all at once, but the Swami speaks every morning for almost an hour and three nights a week, and gradually pieces of the jigsaw puzzle begin to fall into place. And it’s personal; he’s speaking to me. Each person in the crowd feels the Swami is talking only to him. We’re hooked. It’s painful, this sitting on the floor, getting up so early, chanting on beads without much sleep, but a new joy is kicking in, and the neophytes slowly start relating with each other. Even though they are mostly people who have almost nothing else in common, they are becoming fellow Krishna devotees: students of one master.

He makes everything so simple, using practical, logical examples. For example, referring to the austerity of spiritual life: “Just like a sick patient, he is suffering so many symptoms of a disease. Doctor is giving him medicine. He is being treated nicely, but suffering is there. And then? What does the patient think? He has to tolerate, because he knows that ‘I am going to be cured very soon.’”

Rock Scully: I came down for a lecture or two and met the Swami. I thought he was a nice, gentle soul. What he was teaching didn’t seem all that different from what I was thinking anyway. So an older guy like that giving me good advice that seems just right—it was good. And well, he’s the only guy from the Eastern culture who came and sat down with us and lived with us in Haight-Ashbury. So cool.

Right off, one of the things I like best about the Swami: he made you laugh. He was always calm and content, sure, but more than that: he was jolly. Even discussing very serious subjects there was often the play of a smile around his lips, a chuckle to make a point, chuckle by chuckle building our inner glee. Then, suddenly the guffaw, the quick delight. And when he’s really surprised by the overtly absurd: a full-rounded belly laugh trembles his body, tears streaming from his tight-squinted eyes.

“You’ll feel yourself transcendentally joyful in spite of your present physical body. Just like if you eat, you’ll know yourself that you are eating, you are feeling strength, you are getting enlightened.

Similarly, if you take this process of Krishna consciousness, in spite of your present physical body, you’ll know that you are having something transcendental. That feeling will come with you. Krishna is always jolly. If you join His society, you will also become jolly. Chaitanya Mahaprabhu said, anandambudhi-vardhanam, which means there is an ocean of joy. Now this material ocean is stagnant; it does not increase. But that anandambudhi, the ocean of joy, transcendental bliss, that increases, increases. And there is unlimited space also to allow that increment. Anandambudhi-vardhana prati-pada purnamrita vadanam: In every step you’ll feel, oh, that spiritual bliss.”

Malati: I remember just staring at his earlobe, his nostrils. You’d take one small part, his fingertips as he played the kartals, his eyelids, the back of his head, and you would stare at that one portion of his body. And it was like it wasn’t real. But it was more than real. He was so beautiful. The hippie motto was “Never trust anyone over 30.” And here was the Swami at 70 and we never thought of him as an old man. He would say it sometimes—I am an old man—but we didn’t think of him like that at all.

And the kirtans, that’s what really convinced us. Those early kirtans would go on, starting slow, back and forth, building. If you searched through the noise you heard absolute silence—hearts beating in rhythm, high-vibration zinging—to a tremendous crescendo a half-hour later. All of us were blasted out of our minds. Afterward, Swamiji would intone a low Vedic prayer like peace itself and say, “Thank you very much.”

**\* \* \***

In most photos from San Francisco in the winter of 1967 the Swami is very happy. He’s smiling, laughing, in almost every photo. In many photos he is surrounded by the girls. In SF he had his family around him. It was different from New York, where most of his students were unmarried men. (We didn’t know much about the Swami’s family life back in Calcutta—the 1920s and ’30s were as remote to us as the dinosaur age—but we knew he had been married and had had kids back then.)

More and more young people flocked to Frederick Street, and some of them fell in love with the Swami, understood what he was saying, and asked to be initiated as his disciples. With some it took only a few moments to decide, others waited a week or more, but gradually Swamiji blessed a dozen new students with eternal spiritual names: Goursundar, Govinda dasi, Lilavati, Upendra, Subal, Krishna devi, Jayananda, Dayananda, Nandarani, Shyamadasi, and others—and the Swami’s little family grew quickly.

Joanie and Roger were having a problem. Joan was one of us, the original Oregon band, and she was determined to become a pure devotee with Swamiji as her spiritual master. But her best friends and fellow travelers were all getting married. Maybe she wanted to be married too, but Roger was undecided—should he follow the Swami, get initiated, marry Joan? Roger was a free-wheeling spirit, knew a lot about current spiritual crazes. Was he willing to surrender once and for all? On February 18, Mukunda and Janaki drove Joan and Roger two hundred miles north of San Francisco to a small cabin in Rockport, in the redwoods of Mendocino County, and dropped them off. In this remote place, Joan and Roger would take some time to ponder their individual commitments to the Swami and their relationship as a couple. We were on pins and needles as the days flew by and they worked it out.

And within our group there was a kind of competitive spirit, too. Swamiji became the total focus of our lives, so just like any family with a bunch of children, we all wanted to do something, maybe even something extraordinary, to attract our father’s attention. With our varied personalities, most of us would not have even been friends if it were not for the Swami. “Godbrothers” and “godsisters” became common parlance, so we had to get along. The Swami had told us that none of this was accidental, that after many births we had finally got this opportunity to learn from a bona fide spiritual master, and that practicing this dharma in harmony with other devotees was key to individual spiritual progress.

Right from its early roots in 1964-65, the hippie culture in Haight-Ashbury was tribal: people of like mind or with similar interests stuck together. These tribes were loosely organized, democratic, almost floating as members moved in and out. Pooling money and responsibilities, members of one tribe would live together or meet often to socialize. We were kids; we needed families. The Human Be-In was heralded as “a meeting of the tribes.” So when Swamiji appeared, it was natural for a tribe to form around him— we were already wired that way: one person gives money, another cooks, another does the laundry, and someone drives around to buy the stuff we need. It was an easy transition for us to become Krishna’s tribe.

**\* \* \***

Malati and I are consistently haunted by the fact that we may have to go to jail soon, maybe for a few years, we don’t know. Five months ago we had been found guilty of possession of narcotics with intent to sell. Our case is under appeal in the Washington State Superior Court in Seattle. We understand that we are in Krishna’s hands now, so we try not to think about it. Will Krishna somehow free us from this karmic reaction to our crimes? Or will we sit in jail—maybe for years—and try to stay Krishna conscious without the association of the Swami and the devotees?

One morning, Swamiji reads a verse from Srimad-Bhagavatam that puts the looming jail time into perspective: “‘My first evidence of mercy to My devotee is to plunder all his wealth, whatever he has got.’ You see? ‘Whatever he has got, I take it away. When he becomes confused and baffled, he fully surrenders unto Me.’ Sometimes Krishna gives special mercy: He takes away everything so you will surrender. That is the perfection of Krishna consciousness, that ‘I have nothing more except Krishna.’”

**\* \* \***

The Swami lived upstairs in one apartment above the temple on Frederick Street, and Mukunda and Janaki lived in the same building, down the hall. Both apartments faced the street. No one else lived in the building, so the doors were always open to both apartments. Upendra was appointed as Swami’s personal servant and lived in the basement of the temple with about ten other brahm-acharis (single men). Sometimes he would be in the Swami’s apartment giving the Swami a massage or taking dictation, but for the most part, Mukunda and Janaki looked after the Swami’s personal needs. The Swami liked to cook his own food, mostly rice and dal (lentils), and he would slip into the kitchen in his bedroom slippers and teach Janaki, Upendra, and sometimes Joanie and Malati how to make his simple, tasty preparations. He called this food “prasa-dam,” which means “mercy,” because you offered it to Krishna first, He ate it, and you ate His merciful remnants.

Malati: We just wanted to please the Swami, so each person had his or her own way of doing that, so in that way it was competition, but not in terms of beating the other one. Janaki was more intimate than the rest—she definitely didn’t work on the awe and veneration level.

Once in a while, Janaki’s cat, Skutzelbrunzer, would wander down to Swamiji’s room and take up residence in his bathtub. Swamiji would call down the hall, “Jan-kee! Come and get your cat!” Janaki had a special relationship with the Swami. She was at once his doting mother and naughty daughter. “Swamiji, that dhoti has grease spots. Now go in there and get a fresh one. You can’t go out looking like that—and put your sweater on, it’s cold outside.”

Janaki: I loved mothering him. I just loved it. But 1 was so naughty, so full of mischief. The Swami had never tasted whipping cream, so one day I was whipping some up and Swamiji said, “What is that?” So I said, “Try it. You’ll love it.” And I dipped my finger in the pot and he licked some off my finger. His eyes shot up and he said, “Mmmmm, that is good.”

Just as we were learning an entirely new way of life from him, it can also be said that the Swami didn’t know a lot about Americans and how we did things, so he was learning much from us too. As we moved around together he would ask, “What is this, Do-not?”

Donut. It’s a kind of sweet.

Or, “What is that, ‘Be Happy, Go Lucky?”’

Oh, that’s a billboard. You’ll be happy if you buy their product.

“Then we should also have, ‘Be Happy, Go Krishna.’”

“What you are doing?”

I’m checking the oil, Swamiji. Without oil, the car won’t go.

“No, without driver the car won’t go.” You couldn’t stump the Swami.

One day the Swami got a letter from his sister in India, and Janaki saw him sitting in his rocker in a melancholic state. His sister had been very ill. So Janaki massaged his feet, and Swamiji began talking about his sister—how she always took care of him, how she was like a mother to him, he adored her so much. Janaki said, “You seem so sad, Swamiji, and yet you believe in the eternal spirit soul. How is that?” And the Swami replied, “You must appreciate the human condition and how much attachment we develop for one another. Yes, we are not this body, but attachment is still there.” Then she asked him, “Do you have any kids?” Without missing a beat he said, “You are not my child?”

A week later he got another letter from India. Janaki took it up to him and after a moment said, “Well, aren’t you going to read it?” To please her he opened it, read it, then folded it up.

She asked, “Good news, Swamiji?”

“Yes, my sister has died.” Calmly, he added, “She was a devotee. She went back to Godhead.”

Janaki: One time I gave him artichokes, you know, with the dip? I put mayonnaise in it. I showed him how to eat them: dip, then clamp your teeth down, and pull. After one time he looked at me kind of perplexed and said, this is too much work for so little result, and put them aside. Krishna forgive me, I was so bad.

The Swami loved Janaki so very much. He loved all of us. We could feel it, like the unconditional love we might have had from our parents. He often used the “watering the root of the tree” anal-, ogy: “If you love Krishna you love all His creations. Just like, if you water the root, every leaf is nourished.” He said if you really love a person, you must also love his dog.

At morning and evening lectures we would leave our dogs outside the temple door. By now there was quite a pack: Ralph, Que Tai, and a few other mutts owned by various devotees. If the door was open, sometimes the dogs would come inside, and if Swamiji was speaking, he’d stop until they were shooed out. Then he’d launch into one of his favorite themes: “Just like animals, you have also got four propensities: eating, sleeping, mating, and defending. You eat, the dog must eat; you sleep, the dog also sleeps; and just like dog you are having sex life and defending yourself. But you have got this human form of life, you have got special ability, consciousness. You can know what is God, who am I, where I am going. This is special. Do not waste this opportunity. You choose dog or God, it is up to you.”

After lectures, with the Swami gone back upstairs, several of us would step out onto the sidewalk to smoke a cigarette. We kind of knew we had to quit this vice, but jeez, we’d barely stopped using LSD and heroin. For a while the Swami tolerated this behavior, although hints were always flying around: “Such class of men is simply trying to understand what is God, what is not God, but practically he will be engaged in material sense enjoyment. He’ll not give up anything, even not smoking. He’ll go on doing everything, but he’ll discuss very scholarly, ‘This is not God, this is God.’ This is simply waste of time.”

The Swami kept everything simple, little by little spoon-feeding the complexities of genuine spiritual life to his sincere, loving neophytes. His lecture topics ranged over a vast area, but he repeated himself often, especially basic ideas: Krishna is the Supreme Personality, chant His name and serve Him, give up all nonsense, your life will be perfect. Most of us couldn’t remember any Sanskrit beyond Hare, Krishna, and Rama—maybe karma, bhakti (love of God), and ananda (bliss)—but we were learning.

Most of the men kept their hair long, in tune with the times, and Hayagriva, Haridas, and even Mukunda had beards. When Kirtanananda showed up from New York with a shaved head, we went, Whoa! Way too extreme.

Janaki: Swami would constantly tell us you cant stop anything, there’s nothing you can stop, you can only replace it. So he really knew we were stopping a lot of behaviors and having to replace them with something better. It was a big fob—a big Job—and he was doing it.

Gradually, the Swami’s personal example and his descriptions of what was harmful and what was beneficial to spiritual progress began to sink in and we began to change. The Swami had told us that living with cats and dogs was not hygienic and would distract from our spiritual progress. Janaki took Skutzelbrunzer up to Klamath Falls and left him with Aunt Edna. Our cats died when Melanie accidently gave them too much deworming medicine. We were so attached to Ralph—and the Swami seemed to like him toothat we couldn’t even think about giving him away yet, but we knew it was probably just a matter of time.

The Swami walked early every morning, usually around Stowe Lake in nearby Golden Gate Park. The fog would still be thick, the air cold and zingy with the resins of eucalyptus and cypress. A few of us would join him and get to know him in a less formal way. We’d walk along, chanting quietly, and listen when he might point his cane at a nesting duck and say, “As you have got your apartment, so has he.” Or at some crawling ants: “Just see, millions of living entities everywhere, even the ants, and they are all eating nicely, Krishna is providing—so do you not think Krishna will provide for His devotees?” One morning the Swami asked us, “How many days this bard \[bird\] has lived in this tree?” Someone answered, “I don’t know.” And Swamiji told us, “Just count the droppings. Say, at five per day, this bard has lived here for three days.” We’d stroll a couple of times ith Rock Scully and Mitch at Albert Schweitzer College, 1962.

At outside programs, the Swami raised his arms and the crowd went wild around the lake. It was a good chance for us to ask the Swami personal questions like, “Should I get married?” or spiritual questions like “Who is Brahma?” Or “Is it better to chant silently or out loud?”

Swamiji, in his silk aviators cap (a skullcap with a flap on each side), heavy black overcoat, and saffron-dyed thermal undershirt— the clip, clip, clip of his cane as he nailed the universe around us. We were learning to see the world through the Swami’s eyes.

And we had picnics with the Swami. Sometimes in the late afternoon we would take Swamiji to the beach at the western end of Golden Gate Park to watch the sun set over the Pacific, chant Hare Krishna at the top of our lungs, and bake foil-wrapped corn and potatoes in driftwood fires. Hayagriva found a five-foot-long piece of dried sea kelp on the beach, its end bulbous, and discovered that if you blew on one end it made a fine bugle. From then on, our kirtans were marked by blasts from Hayagriva’s kelp horn, like the charge of the Light Brigade!

Joanie: We were at an ocean kirtan one night. At the very end of the prayers the Swami said, “All glories to the Pacific Ocean!” and it moved us all. We were thinking how liberal Swamiji was that he was appreciating the place where we were and the majesty of it.

Besides devotees and persons interested in hearing the Swami’s words, very often in these early San Francisco lectures people attended who thought they already knew everything—challengers, even antagonists. Whenever someone yelled out, “No, I am God!” we’d cringe, then marvel at how the Swami handled them, mixing gentleness with strong argument so that the person, most times, would be totally charmed, or at least pacified, his or her aggression gone. One morning, after Swamiji’s class, a young lady stood up and said, “Swami, I think that in having sex you unite with God.”

“That is not possible. God’s body is made of spirit; your body is made of matter.”

“No, no, when I have sex, I am one with God.”

“All right, you may have God as you like. We will chant Hare Krishna.”

The Swami often said, “We have only one medicine for all of your ills: chanting Hare Krishna. Please take it, and see.” He taught us that if someone was unable to hear our message, we shouldn’t waste our time. He even quoted the Bible: “Do not cast your pearls before swine.” In 1967 San Francisco the Swami faced some of the most challenging audiences ever assembled—the zealous hippies with their radical beliefs—and he always seemed to satisfy everyone.

**\* \* \***

Everyone around us was looking for guidance from the East. These are some of the articles in Oracle #6, which hit the streets in February 1967:

Aquarian Age Symbols  
Ecstatic Isolation and Incarnation  
LSD, Revolution, and God  
The First American Mehla, A Baptism  
\[about the recent Human Be-In\]  
Sounds from a Seedpower Sitar  
Reflections of Truth  
Basic Myth

The Swami fit right into our scene. And there was so much awe at this point. All we knew about swamis and yogis was that they could appear and disappear at will, charm cobras, travel through space. High on LSD, most hippies had had profound mystical experiences, and everyone wanted to acquire these powers on a permanent basis. In fact, many of our early questions to the Swami were about mystic powers—what are they? How do yogis and meditators achieve these powers?

Swamiji amazed us with his answers. He said that mystic powers were well defined in Srimad-Bhagavatam. I took notes as he read from the Sanskrit text and explained them to us. He told us there were eight major mystic powers, each giving the adept a specific transcendental ability.

Whoa! And then there are ten more minor mystic powers, which I also scribbled down:

But there are even more. There are five perfections of yoga and meditation that are thought to be of inferior quality to the powers listed above:

Of course, said the Swami, Krishna devotees are totally uninterested in these powers; they want only Krishna’s love. Even if Krishna gives them a hard time, never mind. This bhakti yoga, devotional service, is the supreme yoga process. And the perfection of bhakti is to go “back home” to Krishna’s abode and associate eternally with Krishna, full of bliss and knowledge. One of Krishna’s other names is Yogeswara, the master of all mystic powers, so if you live with the master, automatically what the master has is also yours. The fact that Swamiji even knows all this stuff...

**\* \* \***

By late February 1967, the weather in San Francisco had warmed and Spring saw the flower children flocking to Golden Gate Park. Every day hundreds of hippies would gather on an open and undulating meadow in the park, which soon became known as Hippie Hill, just ten minutes’ walk from the temple. Here people, dressed with wild imagination, would lounge in the sun, play flutes and guitars, share food and joints, or maybe drop some acid. Anticipating that this might be a good place to tell many people about the Swami—and have some fun ourselves—we made up four flags: The Christian cross; the Star of David; the Muslim star and crescent; and one flag with “ISKCON International Society for Krishna

Consciousness” embroidered inside an Om symbol. We grabbed our harmonium and kartals, some bongo drums and tambourines, and on nice afternoons we’d stroll over to Hippie Hill and have kirtan. Mukunda was in charge of our precious mridanga clay drum— newly arrived from India—and he would sometimes bring that too, cradled in heavy wraps, to get the whump-whump sound.

The Swami heard about our outings to Hippie Hill, and one day kirtan stopped in mid-whump as Mukunda set down his drum and fell prostrate on the grass. We turned around, and there was Swamiji, like, “Carry on, I just came over to check it out.” He sat on the grass with us in our circle, and with a huge smile he restarted the kirtan. Someone brought him a sweater to sit on. He sat a while, then stood and beamed as we all stood and started to dance, singly, then in a circle that widened as dozens of hippies joined, forming a long conga line that snaked all over Hippie Hill.

On February 28, the Swami wrote a letter to Rayarama, who had returned to New York: “On Sunday afternoon there was Kirtana in the Golden Gate Park and there was about 3 to 4 hundred audience and most of them danced in ecstasy. I have asked them to arrange for loudspeakers and microphones so that next week we may have greater audience to join us.”

Though I was pretty shy, even timid, before the Swami, sometimes I got to be with him in a very personal way. When he had to go somewhere in San Francisco, like to get his visa renewed at the consulate or to a speaking engagement, sometimes I would drive him. We’d pile into the ’50 Ford Krishna-mobile, covered as it was with psychedelic writing, and head for the freeway. I was worried because the hood of the car would sometimes fly up if we went over fifty, so I roped it down tight. The front passenger seat was faulty too, and leaned way back. I’d look to my right and see the Swami’s perfect round head leaned back; he could barely see over the dashboard, yet he took in the skyscrapers and a sea of traffic, chanting on his beads, calm and relaxed as we flew down the freeway at 70 mph. And I had to laugh: he loved the speed. “Shyamasundar, just go little faster, we are late.”

“Swamiji, do you know how to drive?”

“No, but I had two cars.”

Sometimes my dog Ralph would be laying in the backseat, the Swami making no comment about him until we arrived. Then, “Ralph will be all right out here, waiting in car?”

Joan and Roger returned from Rockport on March 1. First, Joanie went up alone to see the Swami. Then Roger went up for his own one-on-one. Then sometimes they’d go up together. Days went by. We were in deep suspense: Joanie was our best friend. Would she stay with us? We were very fond of Roger too. He looked grim. Would he stay? Finally, one day they came downstairs together. Joan’s face was alight. Roger still looked a little stern until he looked at Joan, then at us, and then with a big grin he announced, “Joan and I will be married.” “Hooray!” we shouted and congratulated the pair. Their initiation was held on March 7 and they were married the next day, using the same fire pit for both ceremonies. We were all happy to greet them now as Gurudas and Yamuna devi dasi, husband and wife.

Days went by, and each new day with the Swami was more special than the day before. We gradually drifted from our previous lives and into a separate reality, distinct from the world around us. And yet we were still part of the world, and we kept abreast of what was happening in it by reading newspapers and magazines and articles that described our radically changing times. We especially watched for any news reports about Bhaktivedanta Swami and his Hare Krishna movement, and when we found them, we would bring the clippings to show him.

**\* \* \***

The explosive cultural revolution taking place among American youth at the turn of 1967 was being noticed more and more in the nation’s mass media. In its December issue, Time magazine announced its fortieth “Man of the Year” Award for 1966 as “Youth,

25 and Under.” 90 million Americans were under 25—nearly half the country’s population. Most of the devotees—and practically the entire hippie population—were in their early 20s. It was our time.

In particular, the Haight-Ashbury district in San Francisco quickly became the focus of national attention:

Harper’s magazine, January 1967 issue: “Turned On and Super Sincere in California,” pages 42-47.

Newsweek magazine, February 6 issue, “Dropouts with a Mission,” declared that “San Francisco has arrived as the hub of the hippies.”

On February 17, 1967, Life magazine, read by millions of Americans every week, published an article called “The Other Culture.” In describing the underground scene the author wrote:

They gather in colonies of reassurance where the talk is mainly of India and the perilous struggle to stay high in New York—O, Krishna, guide us today, cause the cops could come kicking through the door. There is a huge amount of unused spiritual energy floating around in these circles, an obvious need to grab onto somebody’s oar. I liked them for their flamboyance, their talent for living without money, their spiritual energy... they are gentle, scroungy, loose and free, the only society with poet-kings.

Five weeks later, on March 31st, Life magazine would issue a cover story called “Strange New Love Land of the Hippies”:

Just in case anybody’s still got his head stuck in the sand, something is happening with American youth, and if others hope they will wake up one morning and find ended the spreading youthful rebellion against virtually all the fine, old, established values, I think they can forget it. In fact, I believe the hour of the hippie—which could well enlist enormous numbers of young people from all over the country—is coming and that the most sensible thing we straight types can do is take a good look at this bizarre new scene.

As news about the hippie phenomenon grew, so too did news about the Krishna consciousness movement and its leader, Bhaktivedanta Swami, who was quickly becoming an important part of the scene. From July 1966, when he had established his first temple at 26 Second Avenue in New York, a steady wave of media publicity had been growing up around the Swami’s astonishing impact on American youth. Articles appeared in New York in the East Village Other, the Village Voice, and other popular newspapers.

In late January 1967, eight repetitions of the entire maha-mantra appear in half-inch letters to form a border around pages 8 and 9 of issue #7 of the International Times in London, the UK’s biggest underground newspaper. The same issue contains Allen Ginsberg’s famous address “Public Solitude,” delivered at a Boston church on November 12, 1966. Ginsberg described the exploding youth movement:

We have an international youth society of solitary children all aghast at the space-age horror world they are born to— and now questioning the nature of the universe itself.

He recommended that elders come forward to instruct the questioning youth:

Yea, when the old are tranquil and not nervous and respect the sport of the young, Holymen do inspire respect. One conservative Vaishnavite Swami Bhaktivedanta moved into the Lower East Side this year and immediately dozens of young LSD freak-outs flocked to sing the Harekrishna Mahamantra with him—chant for the preservation of the planet.

The February 1967 issue #45 of Evergreen Review, a prestigious New York literary journal, published a long article by John Nathan from his interview with Bhaktivedanta Swami in October 1966, in its “Notes from the Undergound” column:

To the young rebel on a frantic search for something more valid than Respectability, to the Bohemian and the Hippy, Swamis have never appealed. At least not until this year, when an old man in faded saffron robes stepped off an Indian steamship and set about demonstrating to New York’s Lower East Side that the key to infinite Ecstasy was awareness of the Cosmic Godhead.

When the Swami arrived in San Francisco the wave of media attention grew. On January 18 the main SF papers all carried articles: “Swami Invites the Hippies,” “Swami Invites Hippies to Hippieland Temple,” and “Swami in Hippie-Land.”

Neighborhood Haight-Ashbury media also carried news about the Swami. The Oracle had advertised “Krishna Consciousness Comes West!” in Issue #4 and printed Mukunda’s article on “The New Science” in its Issue #5. Oracle #7 of March 21 carried a special, twenty-two-page conversation by four de facto leaders of the hippie movement: Allen Ginsberg, Gary Snyder, Tim Leary, and Alan Watts. The four men met at Watts’ houseboat in Sausalito. Their discussion, called “Changes: The Houseboat Summit,” covered all aspects of the hippie revolution. At one point, Ginsberg asks the others, “So what do you think of Swami Bhaktivedanta pleading for the acceptance of Krishna in every direction?”

LEARY: Should be encouraged.

GINSBERG: He feels it’s the one unity thing. He feels a monopolistic unitary thing about that.

WATTS: I’ll tell you why I think he feels this. The mantrams, the images of Krishna, have in this culture no foul associations. The word God is contaminated. Anything except saying God... Whereas when somebody comes from the Orient with a new religion, which hasn’t got any of those associations in our minds, all the words are new, all the rites are new, and yet, somehow it has feeling in it, and we can get with that, you see, and we can dig that!

SNYDER: Sure it’s acceptable. There’s only one trouble with India and that’s goats.

The Communications Company was a group of local hippies headed by Chester Anderson, who had a Gestetner mimeograph machine and hung out at the Diggers headquarters next door to the temple at 520 Frederick Street. The CC printed thousands of free, daily, one- or two-page broadsides about events in the Haight-Ashbury, purporting to be the “conscience” of the community. They usually expressed this conscience with strong opinions. Each leaflet had a title, like “Survival School” or “A Moving Target Is Hard to Hit.” They liked the Swami and his followers. In fact, they sort of adopted us.

“Uncle Tim’$ Children” was a long CC diatribe against the commercialization of hippie values: Tim Leary selling tickets to his speeches, hippie businessmen taking money from starving children—but concluded with: “For all of their messy imperfections, the diggers are the only human beings in the psychedelic ghetto. They’re the only people here who aren’t out to pick your pockets. They’re the only people here who aren’t so full of moldy bull\*\*\*\* that they have to wear perfume to mask the stench. The diggers & the Radha Krishna Temple, & the diggers don’t even require you to believe in anything.”

The Communications Company printed thousands of invitations to the Radha Krishna temple, advertising “singing, dancing, chanting, and lectures by Swami A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami,” adding: NON-SECTARIAN IN THAT KRISHNA IS ANOTHER NAME FOR JEHOVAH, ALLAH, LORD JESUS CHRIST, LORD BUDDHA, AND COUNTLESS OTHERS.

Chester Andersons group printed out a page with words to five Krishna kirtan mantras and a page of prayers in both Sanskrit and English. They printed a list of thirteen devotees’ spiritual names (all there were at that point). On March 9,1967, they printed, as a “CC Flash,” a comprehensive, three-page article explaining Krishna consciousness, written by Rabindra Swarup (Bob Lefkowitz) and titled “Right Wing Invades Bohemia.” They even printed song sheets complete with musical notation for the “Hare Krishna Mantra” and “Govinda Jai Jai.” Thousands of these sheets were passed out on the streets to the growing influx of young people arriving in the Haight.

We caught on that the Swami relished the preaching opportunities offered by the media. On February 28 and again on March 21, the Swami was interviewed on two popular San Francisco television shows, interviews arranged by Jayananda and Gurudas.

I was struck by the fact that Bhaktivedanta Swami, who was born in 1896—in the last century—appeared in San Francisco exactly in the three-month period when localized protests turned into a nationwide revolution, and when other-culture/underground/ beatniks became hippies. It’s so amazing that Swamiji, from such a far-off place and time—a world before electricity and motorcars— popped through this small historical window and into our hearts, and then onto newspaper pages and television screens in the big cities of America.

Krishna consciousness was becoming an important part of the hippie culture. The Human Be-In on January 14 had been a “spiritual gathering of the tribes,” with an emphasis on spiritual. Everyone was looking for something to believe in, a path to follow. Other gurus came and went, or were so obviously self-serving or eclectic that few followed them. But Bhaktivedanta Swami and the Krishna’s sat down in Haight-Ashbury and stayed, built a temple, fed people, got them singing, became friends to everyone.

We were recognized—by the Diggers, the Communications Company, the Oracle, the rock bands, and by all the leaders of the Haight community—as the emerging spiritual group. There were a few other spiritual groups canvassing the Haight community at the same time, of course. Swami Kriyananda from the Himalayan Academy (what was left of Yogananda’s SRF people) wandered Haight Street talking to lost souls; Anton LaVey and his Satanists (yeah, trying to convince the stoned-out guy on the sidewalk who’s watching a flower turn into rainbows that he should follow the devil); and there were some Christian evangelicals and Unitarians (but isn’t this the wrathful God of our parents?). And then there was Chief Thunder Pony, who dragged a few hippies to a sweat lodge and howled at the moon.

Even the Hell’s Angels, the notoriously violent motorcycle gang, liked us. The Angels made the Diggers’ place next door their Haight-Ashbury headquarters, and Sweet William or Sonny Barger would often smell chapatis and clomp over to the temple—“Do I hafta?” “Yeah, you gotta take your boots off”—and eat three plates of whatever Malati had cooked that day. They became a protective force around us, watched out for us in public places, and were kind enough to turn their jukebox down whenever Swamiji was speaking.

We were mostly shy young people—sure, Hayagriva was a bit bold, and Rabindra Swarup—but most of us were timid before our hipster peers who questioned everything. I mean, we knew some philosophy, but not very well yet, not enough to argue and hope to convince somebody. We left that to the Swami. But what we could do was advertise—bring people to hear the master—so that became our main service. And it seemed to please Swamiji when we brought others to hear him or when we planned what he called “engagements” or “programs” outside the temple.

The word went out like wildfire that Swamiji was in the Haight, and different spiritualists and yoga people started dropping by to hear him speak. Several asked if the Swami could speak at their place, their temple, their school, and Swamiji said sure, why not. On February 11, the Swami spoke at the Himalayan Yoga Academy, and that night he wrote a letter to Rupanuga, in New York, about it:

“This evening we had very successful performances of Kirtana and discourses for two hours (7-9 p.m.) at the Himalayan Academy, the Christian Yoga Church, and there were about 100 respectable gatherings. All the ladies and gentlemen were cultured and decent and all of them appreciated our Kirtana and lectures. They profusely garlanded me and exposed me for photo snaps. They handed over to me some collection of about $12.00, and most probably they will invite us again for such performances. Tomorrow we are going to see Dr. Haridasa Chowdhury, one Calcutta man who has a similar institution under the name of Self Realization Organization of San Francisco. An American gentleman, Kriyananda (J. Donald Walters) will take me there in his car, by engagement.”

Even Anton LaVey, dressed in black, asked the Swami to speak at his place. LaVey’s Satanist cult was well-known in the Haight due to an article published in Life magazine on February 17 that described his “new religion” as based on medieval Christian concepts of black magic. Would we join him at one of his regular Friday night Magic Circle meetings? We thought not and advised the Swami not to go, but a couple of devotees went out of curiosity to a Satanic wedding at LaVey’s place, where the altar was a naked woman.

Some students from the University of San Francisco asked the Swami to lecture at their school. They made all arrangements and the auditorium was full. The Swami was surprised to see two hundred ordinary college students, inquisitive about yoga and Eastern philosophy, chanting in unison and then suddenly standing and dancing with joy.

This led to an engagement at Stanford University, down in Palo Alto, on February 27. In a letter to Rayarama in New York, the Swami wrote, “Last night we had very nice function at Stanford University. More than three hundred students gathered and all of them sang and danced with our Kirtana for full one hour. There was lecture for 15 to 20 minutes, questions and answers about 25 minutes and the balance for Kirtana. So altogether there was two hours program and it was very successful.”

The Swami could see very quickly, especially after the Mantra Rock dance, that these young people were thirsty for knowledge about yoga and meditation—and they loved to sing and dance. He wrote to his disciples in New York to expedite a shipment to us of drums and other instruments from India.

In late February, the Communications Company organized a benefit rock concert called Bedrock One, to be held at California Hall on Polk Street on March 5. Their widely distributed broadsheet describing the event stated:

OPENING & CLOSING CEREMONIES:  
The Radha Krishna Temple

To, among other things tune the audience & make it receptive to what’s about to happen, make it spiritually ready to love, and then, at the close, to prepare it for the outside world again. Any rock dance that isn’t a religious event is a stone drag.

The poster for Bedrock One was created by a new arrival in the Haight named Robert Crumb—this was his first poster—who would go on with his “Zap Comix” to become perhaps the most popular cartoonist of the hippie era. This wasn’t to be an event for our distinguished spiritual master, but some devotees attended and led rousing kirtans chorused by a thousand voices.

Malati: One time, this couple invited the Swami—and they had been donating food to us, a hundred pounds of rice, a hundred pounds of flour—for a brunch at their nice house, which had a nice view. They had on this table a common bowl of strawberries with cream, brown sugar, each on separate plates, and you took your strawberry and dipped it into whatever you liked. They were obviously gay. Swamiji accepted their hospitality without reservation.

One thing about the Swami, he was cool. No, cool is more a word of the ’50s. Cool is Marlon Brando and James Dean. What the Swami was was hip. Hip means cooler than cool: with-it, groovy, far out, you dig? The hippies who dwelt in Haight-Ashbury were independent thinkers, unattached to cultural norms and dress, keenly aware. In their midst the Swami, with his knowing look, his easy manner, and exotic dress, seemed hipper than the hip.

We go to the Psychedelic Shop on Haight Street and the Swami speaks to a few stoned-out hippies in the candlelit, madras-draped Calm Center; we go to the YMCA where the Swami speaks to children; we go to Berkeley where three hundred University of California students hear the Swami speak very high philosophy. We go to the Glide Unitarian Church one Sunday afternoon where maybe ten people show up and there are three hundred empty seats. The Swami speaks as if it’s a packed stadium. Afterward, one young man asks, “What if I begin this Krishna consciousness and don’t finish? What happens?” Swamiji likes the question and replies, “When you put your money in the bank and you leave it there, it collects interest, and in the material world when you take the money out, the interest decreases, isn’t it? But in the spiritual world whatever you put into your account it remains there, waiting for you to put in some more. You will never lose anything. Is that all right?” And the person says, “Thank you, Swami,” with that look of love and rapture on his face that we were to see for many years. That person became Tamal Krishna Goswami.

Malati: Going back, in the car from that Unitarian church engagement, Swamiji’s in the front, and Гт in the back with Govinda dasi and Goursundar, feeling a little sorry about the turnout. Govinda dasi said, “Sorry, Swamiji, that nobody came.” “Oh,” said the Swami, “you did not see? Narada Muni was there, and Lord Brahma.” And he wasn’t at all perturbed.

The Swami fit right into the scene. We felt proud to walk with him along crowded Haight Street or in Golden Gate Park—see how hip we are? We’ve got a swami. In the seas of outrageous behavior around us, we’d look at him, so unflustered by it all. “I’m an old Calcutta man,” he’d say, like, I’ve already seen everything. This is nothing. (Later, when we would go to India, we would understand this better.)

Mukunda and Haridas were usually the “straight” guys to represent Krishna consciousness to established authorities, like city officials. They had suits and ties and were pretty smooth talkers. We needed permits to chant in public, parade permits, letters of recommendation, stuff like that, and many of us were in some sort of trouble with the law or needed help to clear up misunderstandings with parents, a parole board, or courts—and of course, the draft board. This was the time of the Vietnam War, and all males over 18 were subject to the draft, which meant they had to become soldiers and fight in the Far East. We were devotees of Krishna; we didn’t want to go die in the jungle. To a devotee who had faked insanity to be disqualified from the army the Swami said, “Yes, it is correct as you have handled the draft. As you have played the part of crazy, there is no necessity of reporting. That is quite in conformity.”

Mukunda prepared a little booklet of letters of praise from officials in New York and San Francisco, along with newspaper cuttings and testimonies from devotees’ parents that their child had benefitted greatly by joining ISKCON. Malati and I were never far from the catastrophic thought that any day we may have to go to jail—and maybe for a long time. We submitted one of these booklets to our lawyer in Seattle to include in our Supreme Court appeal as evidence that we had cleaned up our acts.

Check this out: “Authorities, doctors, and humanitarians in various cities have praised ISKCON for being instrumental in reducing the rates of crime, mental illness, unwed mothers, abortion, and venereal diseases. We have been counseling young people on these and other problems. It is our hope that by uplifting our youth, we can make America and the world a better place in which to live.”

Thanks, Mukunda, you silver-tongued angel.

**\* \* \***

Except for a couple of Indian posters, we still didn’t really know what Krishna looked like. Someone had seen a Krishna statue in the window of India Imports, a store downtown on Ellis Street, so Mukunda, Roger, and I drove over to see it. Young Krishna was about three feet high, made of plaster painted blue, standing in a languid, teenaged pose, one hand cocked on His hip, loose strands of beads around his neck, and a crown and jeweled armbands—wow! They wanted $60 for it, but we only had $30 among us. There was no place to park on the street, so I double-parked, and Roger went into the store to see if he could talk them into giving us a discount. Mukunda and I had the radio on in the car, and the announcer said, “Now for the first time on radio, the Beatles singing ‘Strawberry Fields Forever.”’ We were stunned by this wonderful song. We could hear the lyrics were spiritual (“nothing is real”) and that the Beatles were growing more transcendental.

After a time, we saw Roger come out onto the sidewalk, panhandle money from a pedestrian, and go back inside. He soon returned to the car, Krishna wrapped in burlap under his arm. Roger was from New York and he knew the art of first browbeating people, then sweet-talking them into giving him what he wanted. “They took $35 for it,” he said. “I had to hit a guy up on the sidewalk for the extra five bucks.”

“Strawberry Fields” was released in US on February 13, 1967, the day Krishna came to live at Frederick Street.

Now we saw what Krishna actually looked like. There were no books yet, and just a couple of crude paintings made by neophyte devotees, which we hung on the wall. Govinda dasi painted Radha and Krishna. Haridas painted a large picture of Lord Chaitanya and associates (the Pancha-tattva), and when the Swami saw the first version, he said, “Their breasts are too big” (maybe Haridas thought they were supposed to be women?). We’d had to imagine Krishna from Swamiji’s words in lectures.

But now we could see Him, as a young rascal chasing cows, running around with His flute and a stick in hand. Swami told us that this form of Krishna is called Kartamashi, or “The Boss.” He stood on the podium at the Swami’s right elbow, but after several days, Swamiji told us that Krishna should have His own altar—the current dais would be perfect for that—and that we should build Swamiji a new dais at the side of the room.

So imagine the temple layout now: Swamiji sits on the east side, about halfway down the length of the temple room, on a raised cloth-covered platform with a bookstand. To his right, at the back of the temple room, is a plain, wooden five-foot-wide altar, standing four feet out from the wall and about three feet high. It supports Kartamashi Krishna, the Pancha-tattva painting, flowers, candles, incense—and a Northwest Indian totem pole somebody put there, although the totem pole didn’t last long.

To Swamiji s right are three stairs descending from the backroom kitchen. To Swamiji’s left, the front bay window well houses a few scraggly potted plants. Several paintings hang on the walls, along with two Brijbasi & Sons Indian posters: Krishna holding a plate of sweetmeats for a large cow, and cowherd Krishna with His arm around a calf, the calf’s chin resting on Krishna’s thigh.

I finally bought the three-volume set of Swami’s First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam, paying him in three, five-dollar installments. Swamiji was lecturing every morning from later cantos of Srimad-Bhagavatam or from the Bhagavad-gita (Song of God, spoken by Krishna Himself), and in the evenings he spoke from Chaitanya Charitamrita or, as he called it, Teachings of Lord Chaitanya. The Swami possessed the only copies of these books, so it was hard to follow up on things he said in class, as we had no books to deepen our understanding. We had a few copies of two little booklets the Swami had printed in New York, Who is Crazy? and Easy Journey to Other Planets. Devotees in New York would periodically send us mimeographed and stapled ten- or fifteen-page magazines they printed called Back to Godhead, which the Swami had been publishing off and on since the early 1940s.

In those days, Back to Godhead was basically a literary journal, perfect for the times, influenced by Hayagriva, the poet. The April

1967 Back to Godhead consisted of two short lectures by Swami A. C. Bhaktivedanta, and these contributions by his disciples:

The day he had arrived in San Francisco, the Swami had instructed us to feed the people. So we advertised free food, breakfast and lunch. Swami said that devotees of Krishna would not eat unless everyone around them also ate. The girls, led by Malati, took feeding everyone who came to the temple as their main service-porridge for breakfast (with raisins, if available), and mostly rice, dal, and chapatis for lunch. But money was tight, so everyone hustled as best they could, gathering leftovers from supermarkets and day-old bread from bakeries. Malati used to swipe cream and butter off the back of a dairy truck that would double-park in front of the temple to make deliveries at the little store across Frederick Street. One day, Swamiji asked her, “So every day I see from my window you are going to some lorry, Darigold lorry, but what are you doing?”

Malati: I chose my words very carefully—you couldn’t lie to the Swami—so J said, “I’m getting butter.” His answer was, “Yes, Krishna was also a butter thief.” When he said that, it was without condemnation. But neither was he saying, “Oh, very good.” So I understood I could do it, but also that I shouldn’t be doing it. So I let up on that habit after that.

We needed a stove. Forty, fifty, even a hundred or more hippies were coming for lunch every day, and we just had a few small hotplates to cook on. Our friends Jimmy and Annie used to cook on a huge iron gas-fired stove, restaurant-sized. When Malati asked if they still had it, they said no, we gave it to the Grateful Dead, it’s over at their place at 710 Ashbury. So I went over there with Malati and saw the stove. By this time the entire band, their girlfriends, and half a dozen hangers-on were living there with all their instruments and furniture, and the house was packed. The unused eightburner iron stove was just in their way, I thought, so I asked if we could “borrow” it. Rock said, “Sure, take it.” We somehow hauled it over to the temple. It was so big that in order to fit it into the backroom kitchen, we had to break down the wall, then rebuild it after the stove was installed. (A year or so later, the Dead came back to reclaim the stove, but it was impossible to remove.)

**\* \* \***

Swamiji seemed so magic and special and so far removed from people like me that I was very shy about seeing him in private or even going along on his walks. I never pushed myself to go upstairs. But after a few weeks, his quick familiarity with other devotees slowly broke down my introverted behavior and I could see that you got the most juice the closer you got to him. How to do that? What could I come up with that would turn his special attention onto me? What excuse would bring me to his room?

Leaving the drug scene and getting a spiritual master, and now a wife, I had to get a job. The money from the fire lookout job was gone. Five years in college studying poetry and philosophy didn’t prepare a person for making money. But a 9-to-5 job would keep me from seeing the Swami. I had worked as a carpenter’s apprentice for two summers in high school, so I let it be known around the Haight that I could build stuff out of wood, and Peggy Caserta, who owned Mnasidika, a hip clothing shop on Haight Street, asked me to build her a Dutch door and install some shelves in her store. This led to remodeling the whole place. In the true spirit of the times, she said to me, “Just do your thing, build whatever you think looks cool.” With my last cash I bought a 1950 Chevy panel truck for $100 from a down-on-his-luck hippie. It was drab olive green, with many dents. To start it, you had to set the manual throttle and push a starter pedal on the floor with your foot. Once you got it going you could haul a half a ton of tools and materials in it.

Peggy gave me money for supplies. Many Victorian-period homes were being torn down in those days, so I went out to Cleveland Wrecking Yard in South San Francisco and bought used redwood lumber and sheets of etched copper and old curved glass. With these items, I built groovy fitting rooms and storefront displays. I was living the creative carpenter’s dream, financed by a rich hippie chick who gave little thought about tomorrow. Working for Peggy would have a big effect on my spiritual adventures to come.

Our Haight Street apartment was constructed after the great earthquake of 1906. There was an old iron fireplace with a mantel in the front room. On the mantel we kept our little doo-dads and sacred objects, like our gods-eye, a couple of photos of the Swami, and some shells and pretty rocks. One day in mid-February I noticed a brightly painted wooden figurine there. It was about three inches high.

“What’s this, Malati?”

“I, uh, I got it the other day at Cost Plus—it’s from India. There was a whole barrel of them.”

“How much was it?”

“Well, let’s just say there was a big discount that day.”

“What is it?”

“I have no idea, but it says ‘Made in India’ on the bottom.”

Aha! My scheming mind began to click. Maybe I can take this up to the Swami, this thing made in India, and ask him what it is, get closer to him, get some attention.

Malati: I had gone into Cost Plus Imports, just wandering around, and I had no money, but I was looking at stuff from Mexico. I always wanted one of those embroidered Mexican jackets with pockets in the front. But the attraction wasn’t the same any more. I wondered then what I was doing in there. But as I was leaving, in front of the cash registers were these three wooden barrels, each full of different colorful painted figurines. I was attracted to a black one with big eyes and a red smile, and on the bottom was a little label, “Made in India.” So I thought, Swami is from India, the maha-mantra is from India, and I just put Him in my pocket and walked out.

Whenever you came to the Swami’s door, your heart was in your throat. (This feeling never left me, even after a thousand trips to that door.) There was a heightened sense that something marvelous was about to occur, a tingling in the blood. You never knew what to expect, just that it would be a wonderful surprise, whatever happened. He’d be sitting behind his little table or in his rocker clicking his beads, dressed in saffron, maybe with a white sweater, sunlight through the south-facing window shining on his freshly-oiled head and his face a radiant gold, emerging from some deep bliss—and he would welcome you no matter what hour of day or night.

Dropping the doll in his outstretched hand I said, “Swamiji, we found this in a shop. It’s from India. Can you tell us what it is?”

This startled look comes over the Swami’s face. He leaps from his rocker and places the doll on his metal trunk and falls down on the floor before it, beckoning “Down, down!” to Mukunda and me, who are trying to make sense of this. We bow down beside him. And then he starts praying in Sanskrit, saying, jagannatha swami nayana patha garni bhave tu me over and over.

Flushed and beaming, the Swami rises and asks me, “From where you have got this doll?”

“Malati found it.”

“Then tell her to come here, immediately.”

Malati: I was cooking in the temple one morning when someone came down and said, “Swamiji wants to see you.” So I turned off the flame and ran upstairs, went in, and Shyamasundar and Mukunda were there. And on Swamiji’s metal trunk was that figurine. I thought, “Oh, no, I’m busted again!”

“Malati, you have found this?”

“Yes.”

“There are others?”

“Oh, yes, a whole barrelful.”

“No, no. Two others?”

“Yes, two more barrels with different figures.”

The Swami held up the little doll and said, “This is Krishna. The other two figures will be Krishna’s sister, Subhadra and His brother, Balaram. Bring them.”

Then we sat, and he told us the story of Lord Jagannath:

In ancient times, King Indradyumna was anxious to worship the form of Krishna as Lord of the universe. In a dream Krishna told him that on a certain beach he would find a huge log, and from that sea-borne log he should have the Deity carved. All sculptors who tried to carve the log failed; their chisels broke. Finally, an old man—who was really Krishna in disguise—put his chisel to the wood and easily made his mark. The king was jubilant. But the old sculptor said that he would only carve Lord Krishna if he were allowed total privacy for twenty-one days. After fourteen days had passed, the king, overcome by curiosity, entered the workplace. There, he saw the forms of Lord Krishna, Subhadra, and Balaram, with bodies, arms and legs unfinished, exactly as we see them today. Because the king had broken his promise by opening the doors seven days early, Lord Jagannath manifested Himself in this crude way, as part of His sporting play with His devotees.

The Swami looked at us and asked, “Is there anyone who can carve this statue, big size?” and he indicated a height of about three feet.

“I can do it, Swamiji,” I said. “I’m a carpenter and know how to carve wood.”

I thought wow, this is really what people think of as an idol, an Indian idol—I thought Krishna was handsome, and blue, and played a flute—how will people... I mean, now they’ll really think we’re idol worshipers. But it’s what the Swami wants, and now I can do something personally for him. This must be what he calls devotional service, direct service for my spiritual master, the real thing.

Almost every day the Swami had recited a certain verse from Bhagavad-gita, and I had written it down: “My dear Arjuna, only by undivided devotional service can I be understood as I am, standing before you, and can thus be seen directly. Only in this way can you enter into the mysteries of My understanding.” Too much! If I carved the first ISKCON Deities, it will be a second rhino in my bag.

Gradually we learned more about Lord Jagannath—that He stands six feet tall and has a six-foot arm span in His temple in Puri, Orissa, on the east coast of India. Since time immemorial millions of worshipers have gathered there to worship this immense form of Krishna.

We learned that Jagannath means “Lord of the universe” and that He is accompanied in Puri by His brother, Balaram, and sister, Subhadra, who are equally large. Early British travelers wrote home about it, and Jagannath became “juggernaut” in English: “Something (such as a force, campaign, or movement) extremely large and powerful that cannot be stopped; an object which crushes whatever is in its path.”

But for now it was just OK: this is God, Lord of the universe, and I get to carve Him.

Malati and a new devotee named Murari jumped into my truck and we all drove down to Cleveland Wrecking Yard in South San Francisco. I said to the guy that ran the place (Malati called him Porky, because he was short, fat, and chewed a cigar) that we wanted to look around his yard for a very big piece of wood. He said, “Feel free.” The place was spread out over many acres, but I finally spotted this huge Douglas fir timber that looked perfect. It was three feet wide by fourteen inches thick and twelve feet long. Better yet, it was from a dismantled barge, so like the story of Jagannath the Swami had told us, it was a single, large log from the ocean. Somehow we got it through the back door and into the van. The wood stuck out the door about six feet and our back bumper nearly touched the ground under its weight. We waited ten minutes by the exit booth for Porky to show, honking the horn, but he was gone, so we were gone too.

So we stole the little Jagannath and we stole the wood from which to carve Him. What kind of people were we? “Krishna is the greatest thief,” the Swami had told us. Krishna is the sole proprietor of everything, he said, so devotional service means to use everything for Krishna. Offer back to Him whatever He has given. We took that kind of literally. We already felt special because we were hippies, the newly liberated, entitled generation who thought that everything should be free; for us, the urges to self-expression and higher pursuit came first, so when the Swami told us stuff like “Krishna is the greatest thief,” it was just what we liked to hear.

I cut that big slab of wood into three four-foot sections out on the sidewalk in front of our Haight Street apartment and laid the pieces on the floor of our living room, upstairs. I made elaborate calculations from the tiny statues (finally using the years of math from the quarter of a million dollars in education my folks had paid for) and sketched equivalent enlarged dimensions onto the blocks. When I had to saw and chisel and make noise and wood shavings, I dragged the blocks out of my apartment window onto a little portion of flat roof overlooking Buena Vista Park. This was late February 1967. Slowly, I formed the wooden bodies of God and His brother and sister. I occasionally went to the Swami’s room to ask him technical questions, like, “Can I use wood putty to fill the cracks?” or, “What is this symbol on Jagannath’s hand?”

Many days went by. The Swami started asking me, “So, what is the progress? When it will be ready?” I stretched it out, a little work each day. Some days I’d do something else, like work for Peggy. We knew the Swami wouldn’t leave San Francisco until the Deity was installed in the temple. It was a job I probably could have done in a few days, but I wanted to prolong it, taste the juice of it, and keep Swamiji with us and not in New York.

One day in mid-March, I was in the apartment when Ralph barked and I heard a knock at the door. To my great surprise, there was the Swami, cane in hand, Upendra standing behind him. He must have walked a very strange mile down Haight Street from the temple to my place. I bowed down, totally flustered, as the Swami floated in and said, “So, where is our Jagannath?” I pointed to His big-eyed form standing upright against one wall. (Balaram was flat on the floor, the white paint undercoat dry but no detail yet on face or body; Subhadra was out on the roof, partially carved.) I started making excuses for my consistent delays.

“Oh, and what is this?” The Swami gestured with his cane to a half-finished pack of Pall Mall cigarettes resting on the Lord s head.

“Er, uh, those are cigarettes, Swamiji.” And with a flick of his cane the Swami knocked the pack off Krishna’s head and across the apartment—whack!—against the wall and into a corner. Got me.

(Hey, I thought I’d really cleaned up my act. All those years the day started with a joint, then a needle in my arm, years on end staying high all day and night with whatever substance I could buy, beg, or steal...)

Malati: But Swamiji said, instead of chastising us, he said yes, it is very difficult to give up. Do not let such a small thing as a stick of tobacco come between you and Krishna. Just smoke one less every day until it is finished. So he was saying, if you want Krishna, you want to give this up, here's how to do it. He didn’t leave us stranded.

“You must work faster. I have fixed the Deity installation for March 23, Lord Chaitanya’s birthday.” He got me again, and I would have to buckle down and finish this, fast.

And the altar. We had our four-foot by five-foot altar for Pancha-tattva and Kartamashi, but there was no more lateral space on the back wall. Where would we put the Jagannath deities? The Swami had drawn a little diagram of a flat board on which the deities could stand: Balaram on the left, Subhadra center, Krishna on the right, with a puffy round cupola above it, and some curtains. We could only picture altars from our Christian churches, with Christ on a cross way up near the ceiling. 518 Frederick is one of the old San Francisco buildings with fourteen-foot ceilings on the ground floor. So I measured from the ceiling down: a couple of feet between ceiling and canopy top, three feet for the canopy, the deities are three feet high, so they should sit on a platform about six, maybe seven feet off the ground and above the existing altar.

I got a 4" x 16" slab of old-growth redwood six feet long, again from Cleveland Wrecking Yard, sanded it to its original, purple-heart color, and mounted it on the back wall, two feet above most people’s heads, with 2" x 8" redwood planks running up from the floor on both sides. I then bent aluminum pipe into a flattened V-shaped crown above the deities’ heads, which the girls covered in gold velour with a tasseled fringe. On top of this canopy we stuck a chakra, which I’d seen in photos above Indian temples. I copied that with a jig saw, plywood, and bits of dowel, and added a little flag to hang down. We ran a bathtub curtain rod around the whole thing and hung blue cloth you could pull closed when the deities needed privacy. Hundreds of little bells hung from ropes someone slung back and forth across the room, just above the devotees’ dancing arms.

Swamiji said we should have some lights. We mounted three small floodlights under the canopy—red, yellow, blue—and set them on once-a-second flashers so the deities jumped and glowed in psychedelic, strobelike display, straight from a Grateful Dead concert.

On Lord Chaitanya’s appearance day, March 23, 1967, the spring solstice full moon day, Lord Jagannath and His brother, Balaram, and sister, Subhadra, were officially installed at 518 Frederick Street. None of us had ever fasted for a full day, but the Swami declared no food “until moonrise,” and he oversaw preparation of a break-fast drink of lemon water with salt and black pepper. We didn’t really know how to conduct ceremonies, so Swamiji sat on his dais and instructed us on every point: “Hayagriva, come here, come here. Now this should be lighted up, and when there is kirtan, one must be doing like this before the deity. You see? Yes, with the kirtan. "When he stops, he should hand it over to another devotee. When he stops, he should give to another. So, so long kir-tan will go on, this will go on. You just, with the kirtan just now... You do not follow? You begin, and when you are tired, you hand over to other. It will go on.”

Hayagriva asked, “Like this?”

The Swami replied, “This temple is now completely fixed. This is the worshiping process. This is called arati. So at the end of kir-tan this arati will go, and the worshiping process is to take the heat of the light, and whatever your condition is, pay something for the worship. I request you, all the devotees, when you come to the temple, you bring one fruit and one flower. And offer to the deity. So, this simple process, if you follow, you just see how you realize the Absolute Truth.”

Malati: So we went up there one by one. We had a tray with a candle on it, everybody had a stick of incense, a few people had bells or kartals, and we just rang those bells, clanked those kartals, waved the incense, and passed this tray with the candle on it so everybody could make circles, and that was our installation.

The Swami explained to our scroungy bunch why Lord Jagannath had appeared in San Francisco at this particular moment: “So Jagannath is another feature of Krishna, and He is especially favorable to the people who are not strictly advanced to the brahminical culture of Vedic rituals. Lord Jagannath is situated in India, at Puri; this place is one of the towns of Orissa province. And the people of Orissa and Bengal, they are ninety percent fish-eaters. But Jagannath Swami is in Puri. He accepts service from these people although they are sometimes fish-eaters. So in the Kali-yuga the people are supposed to be not so clean, and therefore, service to Jagannath Swami is preferred. So far service to Radha and Krishna, it requires little more highly elevated position. But still, either we serve Jagannath or Radha-Krishna, the effect is the same. So in the temple, although we offer to Jagannath, it is also accepted by Krishna. Be rest assured of this fact.”

Swamiji then said that now that the Lord has come, we had to feed Him. He told us to get Him His own plate, bowl, spoon, and goblet, and they had to be new—no one else should have eaten from them. Malati bought these items, silver-plated, from an overstock store along with a silver serving tray, cheap but new and still packaged. Now officially offering food to Krishna became a part of our lives. Curtains were drawn in front of the deities for their afternoon nap and nighttime rest. We felt a new power within: the Lord and His family were living with us now and we had to take care of them. This was a great feeling—to sit before the Supreme Persons and glorify them, feast your eyes on their beauty (with these somewhat exotic figures you really had to develop the eyes to see) and maybe share some thoughts, ask for guidance, get into it.

And then—as was to happen again and again in the brutal rush to meet Swami’s deadlines—I crashed. The second rhino was in the bag. The deities were done, delivered to the temple late afternoon on March 23, the green paint still sticky on Subhadra’s dress. Late that night, after the installation ceremony, I was rushed to the UC Hospital on Parnassus Street on the hill above the temple with a 103° temperature and a swarm of red veins crawling up my left arm. For two days a constant parade of student doctors passed my bedside to examine a rare but classic case of blood poisoning.

“Where were you?” the Swami asked a couple of days later. I showed him my bandage-wrapped hand and said, “I’m so sorry, Swamiji, I got a splinter in my hand when I was carving Jagannath and it just got worse, infected. The doctors said it was blood poisoning.” Swamiji’s eyes grew huge, and he laughed and said, “Oh, congratulations!” (What?) “Just see, Lord Jagannath has taken all of your sins away at once. Now, do not sin again.”

A few days after the deities were installed, we got the bright idea that Lord Jagannath might like an afternoon in the park with us, so we hauled Him off the altar and carried Him over to Hippie Hill. By this time, to augment the percussion for our carnival-like kirtan presentations, Mukunda had rented a symphony-sized timpani drum from his friend who owned a music store on Market Street. The huge drum was played by two men with four felt-tipped mallets, and the deep reverb seemed to grab your soul from across the park. And Lord Jagannath was standing there on the grass with somebody’s coat under Him, His big eyes wide with wonder and affection, watching this primitive celebration of His glories.

We’re whaling away, getting into it—and here comes the Swami with a funny stern look on his usually sunny face. He doesn’t say much, and we just carry on the kirtan, even more ecstatic now that both Lord Jagannath and the Swami are here. Later, as we head back, Swamiji tells us we should never remove the Lord from the temple—only on certain days, when He goes to the beach.

The beach?

**\* \* \***

The Swami was due to return to New York on April 9, 1967. We were dreading this day, having done everything we could to keep him in San Francisco. Swamiji asked me to carve a set of Jagannath deities for the New York temple, but that wasn’t much. What other work could I do for Krishna while the Swami was away?

Then in early April the Swami called me to his room after the morning lecture. He handed me a little drawing he’d made, some sort of wagon, and said: “Lord Jagannath, there is one festival in India, He goes to the seaside to enjoy,” and then he told the story of the Ratha-yatra festival:

Once every year around the end of June or early July—exact date determined by astrologers—Lord Jagannath and His family take a one-week vacation. They are brought out from the temple and placed on three huge wooden carts, or raths, which are pushed and pulled to the beach by throngs of celebrants. There they rest for a week before returning to the temple. This annual event has taken place in Puri since time immemorial, Swamiji said, and crowds of a million or more make it the largest yearly festival in the world. These carts are made totally of wood and are super-huge—Jagannath’s cart alone stands fifty feet high, weighs sixty-five tons, rolls on fourteen wheels each eight feet in diameter, and requires the lumber from three hundred trees to make.

“Of course, we cannot make so big, and three raths will be too much endeavor, but if you can make one cart, very big, that will be nice. Can you do it?”

The Swami had a flair for the spectacular, the hugely scaled, challenging the limits of what could be done, and we loved him for this. Big? Yes! Let’s do it.

A day or two later he called me up again and said that he had been watching these big lorries (took me a while to realize he meant trucks) from his window. This big cart, he said, it will be too much work in a short time, “so you can make on the back of one lorry.” He then sketched a modified version: a small truck with a simple house built behind the cab. He even added a puff of exhaust coming out of the back, and motion lines like you see in cartoons.
