The Swami consulted his lunar calendar and chose a date: In 1967, Krishna and His brother and sister will go to the beach on July 9 and vacation there until July 16.

So now that we have a date, the devotees meet and we divide up the tasks: Mukunda and Haridas will obtain permits, I will supervise making the cart, Gurudas will liaise with the Diggers and enlist their help, Yamuna will decorate the cart once it’s ready, and Malati and others will arrange for food and flowers.

Now I had a big project, a service to do for Krishna and the Swami while he was away in New York. And the best part? He says he will return to San Francisco for the festival. Another rhinoceros chase with the Swami is underway.

Only the Swami could have conceived that Lord Jagannath— who could be misinterpreted by so many as a pagan idol—would capture the hearts of the hippies. “You see, this is God, this is what He looks like.”

“OK, I can dig it.”

I often thought then and in the years since that maybe it would have been better to present Krishna to the world in His beautiful three-bended pose playing His flute, along with His gorgeous girlfriend, Radha. But this is how Krishna chose to come to hippie-land San Francisco. Insane, unreal, out of this world: Lord Jagannath, the perfect image of God for those times.

On April 1, a well-known folk singer named Lou Gottlieb drove Swamiji to Morning Star Ranch, near Sebastopol, in Sonoma County, sixty miles north of San Francisco. Also known as the Digger Farm, about sixty hippies lived on Lou’s thirty acres, which was one of several experimental communes popping up around the Bay area—hippies pooling their resources, growing their own food, and trying to live in harmony with the land and freely sharing everything, including drugs and sex.

Gina Stillman describes the Swami’s visit in her memoir, Home Free Home:

It was about that time that Swami Bhaktivedenta visited. He was just becoming well known as a leader of the Krishna movement. Some of the people at Morning Star were already singing the ‘Hare Krishna’ mantra. For the Swami’s visit, we set up a temple in the orchard and made him a flowery throne. It was an exquisite spring day, the air soft and clear, the blossoms on the apple trees, the birds singing like something out of a fairytale. A large crowd greeted him. He spoke and began chanting ‘Krishna’ while everyone danced and sang. We absolutely floated in bliss. He was a really great teacher because he just became one of us. It was a great experience of springtime and love of God.

After the Swami’s visit, chanting Hare Krishna became a regular part of life at Morning Star Ranch, and several of the Morning Star refugees began to hang out at the Frederick Street temple—guys like Mike, Tom, John, and Robert, later to become some of Swamiji’s most stalwart disciples: Madhudvisa, Tamal Krishna, Vishnujan, and Rebatinandan.

**\* \* \***

Knowing Swamiji was leaving us soon, Malati and I went up to his room. “Swamiji, we just wanted to tell you that we may not see you again for a long time.”

He was surprised and asked why.

“We may have to go to jail soon, Swamiji,” I said.

“Oh, and what is the reason for that?”

“Well, in the past, before we met you, we were selling some drugs.”

An irritated look passed over his face: “That is not a good reason for jail. I was also dealing in drugs for many years.” (The Swami had had a patent medicine business in India during his younger years.)

This statement immediately put us at ease. We laughed. Then he asked, “And for how long must you stay there?”

“Maybe up to five years,” I said.

He closed his eyes and seemed to go very far away. Then he smiled, opened his eyes and said, “Never mind, Krishna will help you.”

To put us yet further at ease he said, “Krishna was born in jail, so a prison is nothing. It is only a temporary position.”

We laughed, and then he told us the story how the evil king Kamsa was informed that he would be killed by Krishna Himself, who was soon to be born in his kingdom, and so Kamsa ordered that all expectant mothers were to be jailed and their offspring killed. Then he told us that Krishna was born in the jail but by His illusory potency was able to escape.

In just a few brief moments he put the whole imprisonment threat into perspective: where you are doesn’t really matter, because Krishna is always near at hand, and the serenity and pleasure of interactions with Him are always accessible, never-ending, and that there are no obstacles in the transcendental world. He taught us how to make chanting beads by rolling little balls of tinfoil between thumb and forefinger. He then tore a length of thread from his dhoti and showed us how to string 108 beads in a circle, and then how to chant the holy names on them: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare. “And after each round of 108, then you move thumb, like this,” showing us how to count rounds by moving thumb tip up and down the three segments of each finger.

On April 9, Swamiji left for New York. Everyone was sad while we waited with him at the airport. We were his new family in San Francisco. He had come to stay a couple weeks and had stayed almost three months. He loved the devotees here, so eager and capable, so open, fun-loving, and adventurous. And he loved the California weather. “You just prepare for Ratha-yatra festival and I will come back in June,” he said.

Summing up, 518 Frederick Street was now the Swamis second temple in the world, and the first temple with an installed Deity. In San Francisco, the Swami had realized that mass media, public engagements, and lectures to students were potent ways to reach a larger audience with his message. Here he had also seen how much music meant to the youth, and that chanting and dancing with strong musical accompaniment was key to attracting young seekers, so he stepped up his efforts to obtain more kirtan instruments from India.

The Swami s San Francisco lectures, delivered between January and early April of 1967, are remarkable for their detailed descriptions of the innermost secrets of Krishna conscious history and teachings. Who knows? Maybe Swamiji thought he didn’t have that much time—he was already over seventy—or that we new disciples were more advanced than we really were. But he really laid it down, zinger after zinger. Like these:

The hand and body is created all at a time. It is not that the body is created first and then the hand is created.

Our relation with Krishna is like the hand and the body. Therefore, Krishna and we both are eternal, and there is no history of eternity. It has no beginning and no end.

Krishna is blue because He is all-attractive. The sky is blue because it reflects Krishna’s blue color in the spiritual sky, as much as the sunlight is reflected through skylight glasses.

You simply pour the water, it will go down, just like the rivers are flowing to the sea level. Similarly, a highly developed Krishna conscious person, at once he will hear about something of Krishna, he will be in ecstasy. He becomes so glad that he is faltering in speaking. These signs of ecstasy are there. You become at once transferred to the spiritual atmosphere and platform. These are very higher stage. Sometimes you laugh; sometimes you cry; sometimes you fall down. These are the symptoms.

There are so many physical changes also. We are experiencing practically. Because my face is the index of mind, so similarly, what is going on within you, that will be reflected. It is such a nice thing. By seeing the face, by seeing the appearance of a person, if you remember Krishna, you should know that “This man is in Krishna consciousness.” So there is change of physical feature also. He becomes glowing.

Now, after so many years, a servant of that disciplic succession has come here again to preach. ChaitanyaMahaprabhu has practically sown the seed of this Krishna consciousness, and it is spreading, and it is sure it will spread. Now it has come to your country, and I am sure the students who are taking very seriously about this movement, they will spread in the Western world. This is not coincidence. This is destiny.

Yes, Lord Chaitanya was a hippie with long hairs. He has come to save you—and shave you.

Birth, death, disease, and old age: I can make these things disappear. That is the real magic.

**\* \* \***

On March 21,1967, Arthur Lisch from the Diggers addressed clergymen attending the monthly meeting of the Episcopal Diocese of California, warning them to start preparing for the upcoming invasion by “at least 100,000” hippies this summer.

The press picked up on this idea: “Hippies Warn San Francisco.” Mayor John Shelley and Chief of Police Tom Cahill replied with headlines like “Mayor Warns Hippies to Stay Out of Town” and “The War On Hippies.” But the message was clear: Starting very soon, the City of St. Francis would be inundated by pilgrims from all parts of America, looking to live the hippie dream.

A few days before the Swami left San Francisco, the leaders of Haight-Ashbury gathered to form a committee called Council for the Summer of Love, preparing for the coming onslaught.

In mid-April, 150,000 copies of Oracle #8 announced to the world that it was “The Summer of Love.” Page one of their “Summer of Love” issue was a multicolored Day-Glo drawing of the ascetic monk Saint Francis, along with his famous prayer:

Lord, make me an instrument of Thy peace.  
where there is hatred, let me sow love  
where there is injury, pardon  
where there is doubt, faith  
where there is darkness, light  
and where there is sadness, joy.

O Divine Master, grant that I may not so  
much seek to be consoled, as to console  
to be understood as to understand  
to be loved as to love  
for it is in giving that we receive  
it is in pardoning that we are pardoned  
and it is in dying that we are born to  
eternal life.

The Summer of Love was officially underway.

### 3

We missed Swamiji terribly. But he had accomplished much in his eleven weeks in San Francisco. We now had a big family, our basic troops, each with a new name and a spiritual foundation. We had Krishna’s service, jobs to do, directions to expand. The ship had been built, the sails unfurled, and our Pirate Chief was at the helm. Now forward, into history.

The day after he arrived inNew York, on April 10,1967, Swamiji addressed a letter to each of his twenty-three San Francisco devotees: “I am so much obliged to you for your devotion and affection for me. I thought of you all throughout my journey from Francisco to New York... I was not least tired after landing from the air ship, although there was blockade in my ear still I continued meeting for three hours. I am now very well although I am feeling your great separation.”

By now, about fifteen brahmacharis lived in the temple basement, and 518 Frederick Street was like a beehive of people buzzing around all day. Those of us who lived elsewhere gathered at the temple twice a day for kirtan and classes; we listened to recordings of Swamiji’s lectures and studied the First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam, the only book we had. The Swami authorized the pocket-sized Gita Press version of Bhagavad-gita for us, so we got some of those and started reading the Gita. This amazing book succinctly answers every possible spiritual question, and we gained confidence that at last we could understand the big picture: Who is God ? Who am I? Where have I come from? Where am I going? And all points in between.

Dozens of aspiring new devotees flocked to the temple, and we fed an increasing number of people lunch and breakfast prasadam each day. Thousands of young people across America were leaving school and swarming to San Francisco. On Sunday afternoons we met at Hippie Hill for kirtan and led many of those kids back to the temple for a Love Feast and chanting: “Try it out, you might like it.” The Krishna consciousness movement was launched in New York, but in San Francisco, the second-stage rockets had ignited and were blowing KC out of gravity’s pull and into outer space.

**\* \* \***

Goursundar, Govinda dasi, and the New York devotees go back East to be with the Swami, but Hayagriva stays with us in San Francisco—with his wild hair and poetic prophecies he fits right in. Dayananda and Nandarani, Subal and Krishnadevi, Murari and Lilavati—so many new talented and energetic devotees. Our core group is expanding rapidly.

Money is always scarce, so Malati, Harsharani, and Yamuna have an idea: "Why don’t we advertise that the Hare Krishnas are available to, like, entertain people at parties and social events, for a donation? I write up a press release and send it out to all the local newspapers. I title it “Malati and Her Dancing Gopis”: “Whirling, sari-clad gopis (young maidens) dancing on lotus feet to the exotic strains of ancient Vedic melodies.”

Writer Meria Zellerbach married an heir to the Crown-Zellerbach fortune and used her high social standing to write a popular weekly column for the San Francisco Chronicle called “My Fair City,” with her comments on snobby current events around town. A couple of days after we send out our press release, she appears at the temple, asking for Malati. Next day, “My Fair City” features the Dancing Gopis:

Whatever happens to old hippies? Well, some of them actually live long enough (22,23) to deny hippiedom, and accept the values they rejected when they were rejecting the values they now accept... The young people call themselves the “International Society for Krishna Consciousness” after an Indian religious order that practices much of what the hippies preach... Much is to be said for this group.

Zellerbach ends with: “By simply dialing 564-6670, you can have these gopis whirling through your own living room. There’s no fee, but as Malati puts it, ‘a donation would be nice. And it’s kind of a conversation piece.’”

The straight citizens of San Francisco (along with much of the nation) were fascinated by the hippie explosion taking place in their city, so the next day our phone started ringing off the hook and we were swamped by invitations to entertain at upscale events all over town. Someone had donated a ruined 1956 Studebaker station wagon, which burned quarts of oil a day but still ran. We were pretty much an ad hoc and ragged bunch, but we’d cram ten or more devotees into that Studebaker along with harmonium, drums, banners, and dogs, and fly off down freeways to “Dancing Gopis” gigs, all windows down to prevent asphyxiation.

One evening, we headed to an exclusive address in Mill Valley, across Golden Gate Bridge in Marin County. After halting twice to add oil, we finally sputtered up this long, winding, white-gravel driveway into the parking area of a palatial mansion, stopping in a cloud of blue smoke, half-dead from the fumes.

We open the car door and Ralph, the dog, jumps out first, runs over to a marble fountain, and pees on it, visibly relieved. Then he starts tearing up a manicured row of daffodils while pedigree white poodles bark from the windows. Our hosts walk out, shaken by this amazing spectacle: ten clowns pouring out of an old car in rumpled robes and saris, white lines on their noses, big red beads dangling from their necks, but grinning all over.

We go into a faux-glitz chandeliered living room and greet the fifty invited guests, who are as blown away by us as our hosts. We rearrange the furniture—careful with that Louis Quinze chair!—to make a space for the kirtan. Gurudas is running around taking photos. His head to the viewfinder, focusing, he backs up-hold it right there!—and his butt smacks against—and down goes—the priceless Ming vase. Luckily, it wasn’t broken.

We lead a round of the Hare Krishna mantra, the gopis whirl, Mukunda gives a short talk, and then someone shouts from the crowd: “Do you know Havana Geela?”

What?

Gurudas says, “I think he means ‘Hava Nagila.’ It’s a Hebrew chant.”

So they teach us the words, and soon were knocking out a pretty fair version of it. We finish, and the grimacing host jumps up and announces, “Please give the Dancing Gopis a nice round of applause. They have another engagement and must be going now.”

But wait, we just got here!

As we’re hustled out the door, the host slips Malati a hundred dollar bill, his face slack with relief that we’re leaving.

As April 1967 passes into May, I finish carving another Lord Jagannath and Balaram—Subhadra will follow when I have time—and we ship them off to the Second Avenue temple in New York.

Brahmananda: When we got the first two Jagannaths in New York, we didn't know what they were. We thought maybe you stacked them, one on top of another, like an Alaskan totem pole. We were standing around cracking Jokes, when the Swami came in the door and said, “This is God!” And everyone just hit the deck. It changed the whole mood right away.

Janardan has just opened a Krishna consciousness center in Montreal, and with this third branch of ISKCON, we are now truly an international Society. Most of the men start growing sikhas, the little ponytails traditionally grown by men who worship Krishna.

On the rare occasions when we trim our hair, we leave a little circle on the crown of our skull and let it grow out.

Kartals, brass cymbals about four inches in diameter, were the mainstay of our kirtans, but they were hard to come by. Swamiji had the only set of authentic, made-in-India kartals, and the zinging sound he made with them was so rich that we knew they were special. Rumor had it there was silver in the metal. Mukunda and I made a plan to manufacture kartals in San Francisco. We scoured the Bay Area and finally found this little foundry in South San Francisco, where a Mexican gentleman named Mr. Godoy fashioned bells and candelabras for Catholic services. He said he could make our kartals from bell metal, a type of brass, if we told him the exact ratio of copper and tin.

Just before the Swami had left for New York, Mukunda borrowed his kartals for a day. We took them to a metals lab downtown, where the guy drilled out a very small sample from the inside of one kartal for analysis. Then we ran over to Godoy, who made a plaster mold. The lab reported no silver but a very high content of tin with the copper. Godoy had copper in stock, he said, but we had to provide our own tin.

Tin was over seven dollars a pound, and we needed maybe twenty pounds. Where would we find tin, especially cheap tin? Godoy told us to look for old refrigerators, because the cooling coils were made with tin. So we scrounged South Bay scrapyards, tearing cooling units from refrigerators, and brought them to him. He made us fifty pairs of kartals. We sold a few pairs to devotees, but they didn’t sound very good. They were too thick. Instead of the high ring we got a clunk. It turned out that Godoy had shorted us on the tin—or maybe we hadn’t given him enough—but at least we had enough kartals to deepen the kirtan sound. We put whatever kartals devotees didn’t buy into little white cardboard boxes with an instruction manual and sold them to head shops up and down Haight Street for five bucks a pop.

The walls were bursting at 518 Frederick. We needed a bigger place, but we were always broke. We needed money to finance the

Ratha-yatra, pay the rent, feed the hippies, and spread Krishna consciousness. Plus, someone was willing to donate a piece of land for a Krishna ashram near Mt. Shasta in northern California, home of Lemurians, the hidden city of Telos, the St. Germain people, and an alien UFO base, and we needed money to develop that. So we got the idea to hold another Mantra Rock Dance benefit. This time, Bill Graham offered us his auditorium. On May 16, Haridas wrote a letter to Allen Ginsberg, asking him to again join us, if his schedule permits:

We are planning a benefit dance at the Fillmore Auditorium on June 12,13,14,15, or 18. Instead of the usual rock dance, we would like to have an evening which includes Indian music, and we are presently contacting Ravi Shankar, Ali Akbar Khan, and local groups who feature Indian sounds. Swami Bhaktivedanta expects to fly out from New York in time to appear on the program, which would of course begin with the chanting of the Hare Krishna Mahamantra.

We missed Swamiji so much. But we continued to pour our energies into preparing for the Fillmore dance, now set for June 26. At the same time, we had to organize the Ratha-yatra, which was scheduled for July 9, the date that had been fixed by the Swami. Every few days, one of us got a letter from the Swami, and we crowded around while someone read it out loud, relishing every word. The Swami promised to return to San Francisco for both the Mantra Rock Dance and the Ratha-yatra. We doubled our efforts.

Silver-tongued Mukunda somehow got a Ratha-yatra parade permit. With the growing hippie phenomenon, Haight Street was now one of the most congested streets in America, so the cops gave us a route on Page Street, one block over and parallel to Haight. We would travel west to Stanyan, turn left up Stanyan, turn right on Irving, then travel four miles west to the Pacific Ocean.

In the last weeks of May we were hearing rumors from New York that the Swami was ill. No one in San Francisco really believed it. How could the Master suffer any physical difficulty? My thought at the time: He’s just playing with his devotees to see how much they love him.

Then Mukunda got a phone call from New York: Swamiji had had a heart attack and was in the hospital! We rushed to the temple and held an all-night kirtan, praying fervently to Krishna to spare him. He soon improved, and on June 5 we got a letter from Swamiji addressed to all of us saying, “Because you prayed sincerely, I am saved.” Then another letter arrived saying he still planned to come to San Francisco. He wasn’t sure about the Fillmore date, he said, but would definitely be here for Ratha-yatra.

Swami had told us that once he accepted a disciple, from that point on, he was responsible for that disciple’s sins and must accept the karmic reactions on his disciple’s behalf. That’s why sometimes the spiritual master might seem to suffer, and we were to see his suffering with this understanding—sort of like the Christ-on-the-cross deal, we guessed.

We sent out lots of advertising for the Fillmore dance on June 26. But Swami’s letters were equivocal, and we didn’t know whether he would be healthy enough to attend. On June 10 he wrote to Hayagriva, “I am thinking of going to San Francisco just after getting some strength, which I hope I will get by the end of the month.”

There was another big event planned for June 16-18, the Monterey Pop Festival, two hours south of San Francisco. One writer describes the MPF as “the first real rock festival ever held, featuring debut performances of bands that would shape the history of rock and affect popular culture from that day forward.” We were having trouble contacting Ravi Shankar, so when we heard he was playing in Monterey, a few of us drove down there in the Krishna-mobile to ask him to play with us at the Fillmore.

The Monterey Pop Festival was a huge event—something like ninety thousand people—and security turned us away when we tried to go backstage. We waited by the stage exit after Ravi’s performance—“There he is!”—and Mukunda ran over and started yakking at him. We could see Ravi shaking his head side to side in that Hindu yes-no-maybe way. Mukunda handed him the first volume of Swamiji’s Srimad-Bhagavatam, which he’d signed on the inside cover, “To Ravi, Hare Krishna! Mukunda das Adhikary.” (Years later, a devotee will find this book in a secondhand bookstore in L.A.)

Time is ticking, we realized. Ginsberg can’t make it, Ravi’s uncertain—and a Mantra Rock Dance without the Swami just wouldn’t work. Looks like a no-go. We canceled the dance.

We went on preparing for the Jagannath festival as if Swamiji would be there, healthy again—he can’t just leave us untrained kids adrift in hostile seas. We needed to rent a truck and sound system, print posters and flyers, and find money for Swami’s plane fare from New York. Where were we possibly going to get all this cash?

In the second week of June I got a check in the mail for $890. Totally unexpected, this was a refund from the US Treasury for money the government had seized in my drug bust almost one year before. (This was about half of what I had on me at the time; the rest probably went into cops’ pockets.) But nine hundred bucks in 1967 was the equivalent of about $6300 in today’s money. This sudden cash reinforced our strong belief that Krishna would always send money when needed for His service. Everything around the Swami was magic.

Haridas set up his easel in our Haight Street apartment and created yet another unique poster, this time featuring a grainy photo on bright yellow stock of the gigantic Jagannath cart used every year in Puri, India, with a skinny guy standing in front of wheels that were three feet taller than he was. The image was surrounded by the words “San Francisco Car Festival, Sunday, July 9” in multicolor crayoned letters drawn by a child, with little powder-puff clouds.

Swamiji had said we could use a flatbed truck and had given me a simple sketch: the deities will stand like this, with Balaram facing left (driver’s side), Subhadra facing back, Krishna facing right (passenger side). The 81 x 8' deck and canopy would be joined by two redwood 4" x 4" x 6" vertical columns at each corner, with a pyramid-shaped canopy framed above and covered by yellow cloth, flags, flowers, and festoons.

We asked Arthur Lisch from the Diggers next door, and he said, “Yeah, you can use our truck.” Their truck had “Digger You” painted on the front bumper and an OM painted on the grill-but it wasn’t really big enough for our purpose. So we rented a new model GMC twenty-foot open-sided flatbed truck. Eight feet at the rear was reserved for the deities’ house, and the twelve-foot middle section was open for devotees to stand and sit for kirtan with harmonium and drums. We would put the sound system in this area too, along with a little generator to power everything. The Diggers’ truck would precede our truck in the parade, and carry celebrants and free lemonade to pass out to bystanders.

We set up sawhorses in the Kezar Stadium parking lot behind the temple, drove the rental truck in there, and got to work. I had some wood left over from a job I had done for Peggy and bought the rest with my drug-bust refund. A guy named Bob Simmons-we called him Beach because he was a surfer and always wore shorts-came over to help. (Bob had written the article in Oracle #4, “Yoga and the Psychedelic Mind.”)

Jayananda pitched in too. Jayananda was several years our senior—nearly 30—so we looked up to him like a big brother. He was a solid Midwesterner, born Jim Kohr, straight as an arrow, but the fact that he fell in love with the Swami and was strictly following this Indian yoga process, so foreign to his upbringing, impressed us deeply. He would sit in suntan pants and a white dress shirt with his eyes closed tight, hearing the Swami, rocking back and forth in deep concentration. His simple take on everything, his unwavering humility and personal strength, convinced us of reincarnation: Jim had been a yogi in his past life for sure. He drove a taxi for a living and sold Back to Godhead magazines to his passengers, giving all his earnings to the temple, practically supporting it. And as with most good Ohio boys, Jayananda knew some carpentry. Within twenty-four hours we had Lord Jagannath’s chariot built and ready to go.

About a week before Ratha-yatra, devotees came into the temple one evening to see that Krishna was missing from the altar. Balaram and Subhadra were there, but where was Krishna?

The Swami had told us a story about Lord Jagannath: Just before Ratha-yatra, the deities are given a bath, and sometimes, Krishna catches a small cold and will disappear from the altar for a few days to get well. This is His sporting play with His devotees. So we had this bright idea that Krishna should be taken somewhere to recover from His cold, and we secretly took Jagannath out of the temple one day and put Him in Haridas’s room, in his bed, at our place on Haight and Lyon. Haridas came to the temple that night, and when the curtains opened, Krishna was gone. Everybody was stunned. Haridas yelled, “The Hell’s Angels have taken our Deity!”

Malati: We didn’t say anything, and that night he came home and walked into his room, and he just about fell over because there was Lord Jagannath in his bed, just His big eyes sticking out from under the covers.

But we didn’t have the story exactly right. What is supposed to happen: two weeks before Ratha-yatra there is a ceremony in Puri called snana-yatra, where all three deities are publicly bathed. Then, because they each catch a small cold, Jagannath, Balaram, and Subhadra are taken to a special room where they are pampered for several days and given special treatments and offerings to make them well.

Meanwhile, back East, the Swami had moved into a beachside cottage in New Jersey to rest and recuperate before his trip to San Francisco. We thought, why not find him a beachside place near SF? The closest beaches were in Marin County, across Golden Gate Bridge from the city, an area quite familiar to some of us. (How many acid trips had we taken below Mount Tamalpais on those empty golden beaches fringed by windswept Monterey cypresses and giant redwoods?) Mukunda took charge of finding Swamiji a place. He found a nice little cottage out in Stinson Beach called Paradisio and rented it for a month. (Little did we know that June-July is fog season on the Marin County coast.)

**\* \* \***

Swamiji arrived at the San Francisco airport on June 25, again to a rousing welcome, but this time we were greeting an intimate friend, our confidential father and beloved commander, who was back in action. Our joy knew no bounds. The Swami looked OK, a little tired—you could see he had suffered—but he was beaming and bouncing around like always. Jayananda drove him away, and by the time we could find our cars and get there, Swami had already been to the temple, greeted the deities, and taken off with Jayananda to his new digs in Paradisio, Stinson Beach. Hayagriva, Upendra, and Kirtananda stayed out there with him. In the meantime, Jayananda and I frantically finished the Ratha-yatra float, and the other devotees plastered posters all over town, passed out thousands of leaflets, and gathered food and flowers for our first big public function since the Mantra Rock Dance.

Page Street runs up a steep hill, and the Ratha-yatra parade started at the base of the hill, on the corner of Masonic. Hundreds of people had gathered, the cops were busy diverting traffic and maybe worrying about riots by these drug-crazed hippies. Our Tiny Tiger generator buzzed, and amplified sounds of kirtan shook nearby windows. It was time to shove off.

“Any chance the Swami s coming, Mukunda?”

“No, Hayagriva says he’s just not gonna make it over today.”

“Nuts! Well, maybe we can go out to Stinson afterward, bring Lord Jagannath to see him. What do you think?”

Then I popped the clutch and started up the Page Street hill. But so many people were dancing in front, so many kids and dogs running back and forth, that I had to keep shoving in the clutch and hitting the brakes. Halfway up the hill I was a bit late with the clutch and the truck died—total chaos. I cranked the emergency brake, keeping left foot on clutch, right foot on accelerator—flooded!— turning the key start start start finally to a cough, cough—and the engine starts. Now, one foot on brake, one on clutch, release emergency brake and take foot off brake while hitting the accelerator-all at the same time—with this huge load of devotees, the Lord, His entourage, and a ton of equipment. We start rolling back— thump!— we hit something! I pound the accelerator and the truck jerks forward up the steep hill—and nobody even notices. “Anybody hurt?” I yell at Mukunda as he jumps into the cab, shoving Ralph aside. “No, but the generator is so noisy we can’t hear the kirtan.”

We decide to bring Tiny Tiger into the cab once we’ve reached level ground and I can safely stop. Ralph gets kicked out. Fumes and noise from the little gen-set are horrible, even with the windows open. Mukunda stays with me for a while, but he has to keep jumping out to check on things and lead the kirtan. Somehow, very slowly, a few hours later we make it to Ocean Beach and park the truck. I stagger out into the fresh sea air and join the kirtan.

After a few hours of kirtan at the beach—it was midsummer in SF, so it stayed light until about 9 p.m.—a bunch of devotees piled onto the back of the rented truck and we drove with the Jagannath deities twenty-five miles out to see Swamiji in Stinson Beach. The last twelve miles on Highway I over the flanks of Mt. Tamalpais are very twisty, and with no sides on the truck, everyone had to hold on for dear life.

When we got to the Paradisio house, the Swami greeted us in a jolly mood, like his children coming home from the war. Everyone cried tears of joy. We described our adventures of the day, and he said ours had the same tone of madness, near-accident, and jubilation as the sacred Ratha-yatra festivals in India. “Yes, in Puri sometimes the cart will roll backward, and only with great effort can they make it move. Krishna enjoys this sporting adventure, seeing His devotees cry out for Him.” The deities were off-loaded with care and installed in the Swami’s beach house, where they would remain for a few days’ vacation.

Two days later, the Swami wrote a letter to Brahmananda in New York:

On the plane from New York I had no inconvenience, and I ate all the puris brought by Kirtanananda. Sometimes on the way there was little jerking (air pockets) and I got little nervous. Anyway I got down safely and was received by the anxious devotees here. Jayananda in a nice car brought me to the house, which is situated in an exceptionally nice spot, and the house itself is aristocratic. The only difficulty is that I cannot go to the temple on account of the zig-zag course of the road in crossing the mountains. Anyway, the devotees are coming here, and the Ratha-yatra Festival was performed with great pomp. More than 500 people followed the procession to the beach, and there were about two dozen cars. They distributed thousands of chapatis, and at last Sri Jagannatha, Subhadra, and Baladeva kindly came here in our house and will stay here for one week and then return. So far my health is concerned, on the whole it is nice, but sometimes I feel not so good. Everything depends on Krishna, and as He desires it will happen.

But July is fog season on the Marin coast, and Swamiji realized after a few days of constant drizzle, unable to walk in fresh sunny air, that his health would not improve much in this gloomy place. He decided that very soon he would return to India, back to warm weather and his familiar Ayurvedic doctors. He would take Kirtanananda with him.

After a week, the Jagannath deities returned from their beachside vacation and moved back into the temple. We took turns going out to see the Swami, and he instructed us in a new responsibility: “You are now in charge of your own spiritual destiny. I may not be around much longer. I have taught you everything. Now you must be strong enough to reach Krishna on your own. I will be with you—I am always with you—so do not fear.”

One Sunday in mid-June, a handsome young guy showed up at Hippie Hill and really got into the chanting. Afterward, he stayed to talk with us, and with special excitement he told us his story. His name was Rick. He was the lead singer in a band called The Misunderstood, which had just made a #1 hit record in England. But then Rick got drafted by the US Army and sent to Fort Ord for basic training. The night before shipping out to Vietnam he went AWOL. Rick goes on to say that he’s now holed up with friends in the Haight—turns out he’s in the same building where I live—and he really wants to learn about Krishna.

Next morning I pound on his door and he comes with us to the temple. Rick exhibits a genuine desire to become a devotee, so he and Mukunda hitchhike over to Stinson Beach to see the Swami. The Swami likes Rick, initiates him with the name Rishikesh das, and tells him he can flee to Vrindaban in India and wait for him— the Swami will be there soon and Rishikesh will be safe. Swamiji is so magnanimous, so nonjudgmental; he embraces any sincere soul, no matter how criminal, crazy, or dysfunctional to the rest of the world, and leads them to Krishna.

On July 22, the day of his departure for New Delhi, Swamiji came to the temple to address us, maybe for the last time. He’s sitting there, so noble and aware, saying, “I am an old man, I may die at any time. So boys and girls, I want you to carry on this Krishna consciousness movement in my absence. Just maintain as I have taught you, and whatever you know you teach to others. Become yourselves Krishna conscious and bring others to Krishna, that is all.”

Someone raised his hand. “Swamiji, can you ask, can we ask one of your godbrothers to come and teach us in your absence?”

The Swami remained very erect and still, eyes closed. He seemed to stop breathing. After several minutes, eyes still closed, he said in deep voice, tears streaming down his lotus cheeks: “My spiritual master was not an ordinary man. He saved me... If one person comes and changes even one iota of what I have taught you, then the whole thing is null and void.”

We got the message. We were part of a movement now, a spiritual movement—we had the teacher, his instructions, all the toolsit was time to buckle down and really help Swamiji spread his message around the world. We were on our own now, but he would always be with us.

Ten days later, we got this letter:

Vrindaban, India

2 August 1967

My Dear Students,

Please accept my blessings. I am always thinking of you, and I am feeling separation. I wish to return at the earliest opportunity. I cannot stop my western world activities and I have taken leave from you for only six months; and it may be that on or before I will come to you again. So continue your activities with great vigor. I shall always pray to Krishna for your steady advance, but try to follow the principles which are necessary to strengthen oneself in the matter of spiritual advance. Never think that I am absent from you. Physical presence is not essential; presence by message (or hearing) is real touch. Lord Krishna is present by His message which was delivered 5,000 years ago. We feel always the presence of our past Acharyas simply by their immutable instructions. I hope you will understand me right and do the needful. Kirtanananda says from my bodily feature that I am improving; I am also feeling like that.

Your ever well-wisher, A. C. Bhaktivedanta

The main news in San Francisco was the Hippie Invasion. By May 1967, nearly 20,000 hippies had settled in the small Haight-Ashbury neighborhood, and thousands more were pouring in every week. By July, the “Summer of Love” was in full swing: 80,000 to 100,000 young seekers swarmed around us in the City of St. Francis.

Haight-Ashbury was crawling with press reporters and writers trying to get a hold on one of the greatest social revolutions in America’s history. On May 14, Hunter S. Thompson published his famous essay, “The Hashbury Is the Capital of the Hippies,” in the New York Times magazine. He wrote: “The word ‘hip’ translates roughly as ‘wise’ or ‘tuned-in.’ A hippy is somebody who ‘knows’ what’s really happening, and who adjusts or grooves with it. Hippies despise phoniness; they want to be open, honest, loving, and free.... They see themselves as the vanguard of a new way of life in America, where love abounds and work is fun and people help each other. The young hippies are confident that things are going their way.”

Thompson also provided a definition of “The Hippy” for Colliers Encyclopedia:

1967 gave way to a nationwide avalanche in Look, Life, Time, Newsweek, The Atlantic, the New York Times, the Saturday Evening Post—the publicity rumble, which seemed like a joke at first, turned into a menacing landslide.

The concept of some vengeful God sitting in judgment on sinners is foreign to the whole hippie ethic. Its God is a gentle abstract deity not concerned with sin or forgiveness but manifesting himself in the purest instincts of “his children.”

But the awful possibility exists that the hippies may be right, that the future itself is deplorable and so why not live for Now? Why not reject the whole fabric of American society, with all its obligations, and make a separate peace? The hippies believe they are asking this question for a whole generation and echoing the doubts of an older generation.

Well-known British historian Arnold Toynbee, at age 78, toured Haight-Ashbury for three days in mid-May. Toynbee’s textbooks on Greek history were required reading for college students around the world. This was serious. If such luminaries as Arnold Toynbee were taking strong interest in the Haight, the world better pay attention. Toynbee wrote his impressions of the hippie movement, published in the London Observer (“A New Challenge to Conformity” on May 16, 1967) and the San Francisco Chronicle (“Toynbee Tours Hippieland” on May 17): “The leaders of the Establishment will be making the mistake of their lives if they discount and ignore the revolt of the hippies and many of the hippies’ non-hippy contemporaries on the grounds that these are either disgraceful wastrels or traitors, or else just silly kids who are sowing their wild oats.”

Hunter Thompson commented on Toynbee s visit to Haight-Ashbury: “Toynbee never really endorsed the hippies; he explained his affinity in the longer focus of history. If the human race is to survive, he said, the ethical, moral, and social habits of the world must change: The emphasis must switch from nationalism to mankind. And Toynbee saw in the hippies a hopeful resurgence of the basic humanitarian values that were beginning to seem to him and other long-range thinkers like a tragically lost cause in the war-poisoned atmosphere of the 1960s. He was not quite sure what the hippies really stood for, but since they were against the same things he was against (war, violence, and dehumanized profiteering), he was naturally on their side, and vice-versa.”

Newsweek (circulation, millions) appeared on June 12 with a feature called “The Hippies Are Coming.”

Time (circulation, millions) ran a cover story on July 7 called “The Hippies: Philosophy of a Subculture”:

For California’s Bishop James Pike, they evoke the early Christians: “There is something about the temper and quality of these people, a gentleness, a quietness, an interestsomething good.” The hippies hope to generate an entirely new society, one rich in spiritual grace that will revive the old virtues of agape and reverence.

Indeed, it could be argued that in their independence of material possessions and their emphasis on peacefulness and honesty, hippies lead considerably more virtuous lives than the great majority of their fellow citizens... In the end it may be that the hippies have not so much dropped out of American society as given it something to think about.

\[Hippies are\] spiritually motivated crusaders striking at the values of straight society where it is most vulnerable: its lack of soul. In a sense, hippiedom is a transplanted Lost Horizon, a Shangri-La a go-go, blending Asian resignation and American optimism in a world where no one grows old.

Look magazine also sent one of its reporters, William Hedgepeth, under cover, into Haight-Ashbury. His article “Inside the Hippie Revolution” hit the stands on August 22, and millions of readers read: “If life to the hippies has any ‘purpose’ in the ordinary context of the word, the purpose is religious discovery.”

By the summer of 1967, Bhaktivedanta Swami and his growing Hare Krishna movement—now an integral part of the hippie phenomenon—are a frequent topic on the national news. The country was waking up, and what was underground yesterday was quickly becoming mainstream banner headlines today.

In late April 1967, author Joan Didion was walking down Haight Street and somebody handed her one of our Stay High Forever!” leaflets. She then tracked down Mukunda and, in her famous essay “Slouching Towards Bethlehem, which appeared in the September 23 issue of Saturday Evening Post (circulation, 6.9 million), she wrote:

Maybe the trip is not in Zen but in Krishna, so I pay a visit to Michael Grant, the Swami A. C. Bhaktivedanta’s leading disciple in San Francisco. Michael Grant is at home with his brother-in-law \[Gurudas\] and his wife \[Janaki\], a pretty girl wearing a cashmere pullover, a jumper, and a red castemark on her forehead.

“I’ve been associated with the Swami since about last July,” Michael says, “See, the Swami came here from India. I helped him get his storefront in New York. Now it’s an international movement, which we spread by teaching this chant.” Michael is fingering his red wooden beads and I notice that I am the only person in the room with shoes on. “It’s catching on like wildfire.”

“If everybody chanted,” the brother-in-law says, “there wouldn’t be any problem with the police or anybody.”

“Ginsberg calls the chant ecstasy, but the Swami says that’s not exactly it.” Michael walks across the room and straightens a picture of Krishna as a baby. “Too bad you can’t meet the Swami,” he adds. “The Swami’s in New York now.”

“Ecstasy’s not the right word at all,” says the brother-in-law, who has been thinking about it. “It makes you think of some... mundane ecstasy.”

And meanwhile, unbeknownst to any of us, over in England George Harrison had been listening very carefully to Swami Bhaktivedanta’s “Krishna Consciousness” record album. George brought it along with him on May 19, 1967, for an incredible interview with Barry Miles for the International Times, London’s main underground newspaper. The interview, entitled “The Way Out Is In,” begins immediately with:

George: If you could just say a word and it would tell people something straight to the point, then, you take all the words that are going to say everything, and you’d get it in about two lines. Just use those, keep saying those words.

Barry: Like the “Hare Krishna” chants, except there the meaning of the words gradually fades away anyway.

George: That’s right. “In the beginning was the word,” and that’s the thing about Krishna, saying Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, so it’s not the word that you’re saying, it’s the sounds Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, Krishna, and it’s just sounds and it’s great.... Through the music you reach the spiritual, but the music’s very involved with the spiritual, as we know from the Hare Krishna record we just heard That’s what were doing here now, talking, because we’ve got to save them, because they’re all potentially divine.

In the interview George also talks about gurus: “The Guru and Disciple relationship is where the person has a 100% belief in the Guru, that he’s going to get you out of this mess.” George’s amazing International Times interview was picked up and reprinted in the Helix (Seattle) and other underground newspapers all over the world.

We didn’t know this either, but in the third week of July 1967, George and the other Beatles went to Greece together on a vacation. Later, George wrote, “We rented a boat and sailed it up and down the coast from Athens, looking at islands. Somebody had said we should invest some money, so we thought: Well, let’s buy an island. We’ll just go there and drop out.’ It was a great trip. John and I were on acid all the time, sitting on the front of the ship playing ukuleles. Greece was on the left; a big island on the right. The sun was shining and we sang ‘Hare Krishna’ for hours and hours.”

On August 7,1967, word swept like wildfire up and down Haight Street: George Harrison is here! George was playing sitar, and the Beatles were writing songs using Indian philosophy to shape their lyrics, songs like “Fool on the Hill” and “Within You, Without You.” The Beatles were by far the most famous people in the world at that time, and the fact that George liked Indian religion and music made him one of our main heroes. Now he had come personally to see our exciting neighborhood, the most happening place on the planet.

I was working on Peggy’s new house out in Stinson Beach that day, so I missed the show: George, his wife Pattie Boyd, and the Beatles’ publicist, Derek Taylor, had walked around Hippie Hill, up and down Haight Street, grooving with the hippies. George was laughing and strumming a guitar someone had shoved at him. He was wearing heart-shaped, rose-tinted sunglasses, and everyone said his smile was a mile wide. Damn. I missed him! I was swept by dismay and feelings of loss, certain that my ill timing had forestalled the inevitable. I just knew deeply that George was supposed to be a devotee of Krishna, that he was our Swami’s man.

I stewed around the apartment and over at the temple for days after that, dreaming, you can say, about one idea: let’s go to London and meet the Beatles, especially George. This thought sat in my brain for months as Krishna arranged everything.

Later, George told this story about his trip to San Francisco that August day in ’67:

We went up to San Francisco in a Lear jet, and then we went off to Haight-Ashbury. I went there expecting it to be a brilliant place, with groovy gypsy people making works of art and paintings and carvings in little workshops. But it was full of horrible spotty drop-out kids on drugs, and it turned me right off the whole scene. It certainly showed me what was really happening in the drug culture. It wasn’t what I’d thought—spiritual awakenings and being artistic. That was the turning point for me. It made me realize: “This is not it.” And that’s when I really went for the meditation.

George got back on the Lear to return to L.A., and his return flight was no less terrifying than Haight-Ashbury had been. He said:

I was sitting right behind the pilots-two big brown-brogue-shoed Frank Sinatras. As it took off, the plane went into a stall—we hadn’t got very high before we went into a steep turn and the plane made a lurch and dropped. The whole dashboard lit up saying “UNSAFE” right across it. I thought, “Well, that’s it.” Alex was chanting “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna,” and I was saying, “Om, Christ, Om.”

On September 13,1967, in an interview with Miranda Ward for her BBC Radio 1 show called “Scene and Heard,” George Harrison, who was on the Magical Mystery Tour movie set, explained:

Miranda: When did you first start becoming interested in Indian culture and religion?

George: Probably about two years ago... I don’t really know exactly when, but when I first noticed that I was interested in Indian music, and along with that I’d heard stories of people in caves. Yogis, as they’re known. People levitating and dematerializing \[laughs\] and doing all sorts of wondrous things. And with meeting Ravi Shankar. It was great because he’s a brahmin, which is a high sect, just all the groovy people are brahmins, like the scientists, religious people and musicians. And then in the end, I’d like to become this myself. I’d just like to have this quality that these people have, which is a spiritual thing. And I think with us having all the material wealth that we need—you know, the average person feels that if they have a car and a telly and a house, that’s where it’s at. But if you get a car and a telly and a house, and even a lot of money, your life s still empty because it’s still all on this gross level. What we need isn’t material; it’s spiritual. We need some other form of peace and happiness. That’s why the Indian people all seem very peaceful, as though they have found something, because they haven’t had the material wealth. They ve had to look at themselves for some answer, and they’ve found it inside themselves.

**\* \* \***

It’s the Summer of Love, but for us it’s a very slow time. When the Swami’s in town or about to come to town, our lives are unbelievably hectic. When he’s gone—and this time to faraway India—we slow down, dig deeper, practice chanting and devotional service in a measured way. Swamiji is recovering his health, that’s the main thing, and he says he will return to us in a few months. Malati, Yamuna, and the other women are strong in their Krishna consciousness, and they force us by example to maintain our daily sadhana, our spiritual routine. Swamiji remembers me in letters, but I don’t write him much; he’s busy, and I’m really nobody. I can’t even think of any particular spiritual questions to ask him.

I remember stuff the Swami told us, trying it out, like, there are basically six types of human beings: Those who see only the bad in someone else; those who see both good and bad in others and choose the bad; those who see a little bad in someone and choose to make it very big. These are three. Then there are those who see both good and bad and choose the good; then those who see only good in others; and then there are the devotees, who see a little bit of good in someone and choose to make it very big.

(I realize Mukunda’s a lot like that; he sees a little good in everyone he meets and makes it very big. I strive to be more like Mukunda in that way.)

And the jail thing still looms. On August 18, Jayananda (James Kohr) and Mukunda (Michael A. Grant) sign a letter on behalf of Mr. and Mrs. Speerstra to send to Judge Clay Agnew in Seattle, begging his leniency. Get a load of this: “We would earnestly beg your indulgence and sincerely pray that you will heed the fact that an internment of Mr. Speerstra would, at this time, seriously affect the lives of thirty youths who are directly under his supervision.... At present he is, in addition to his daily theological studies, both treasurer and rehabilitation group leader of the Society. Mr. Speerstra has proven himself an accurate and responsible bookkeeper, who has given our Society a financial stability without which our daily food and educational programs would virtually cease to exist.”

And the kicker: “The latter function of rehabilitation group leader also held by Mr. Speerstra involves at least four hours out of every day working patiently and earnestly with between twelve and twenty mentally and physically handicapped youngsters, as well as with three to seven former drug addicts. The group leader (1) Heads group therapy sessions where youths discuss their previous pitfalls, and (2) Takes the group in a large school bus or truck on tours of points of interest and natural beauty in order to reaffirm the positive values in life. He also leads them in sports for their physical well-being, and to help co-ordinate body and mind with the aim of inspiring a tactile, aural, and visual interpretation of God and his Creation.”

Can you believe it? We laugh till tears come, while composing this letter.

I was still working for Peggy Caserta a few days a week. Peggy had opened her hip-mod clothing shop, Mnasidika, at 1510 Haight Street in 1964, so she was a pioneer in the district. She was dressing the bands and other elite hippies, and her shop became a hub for the in-crowd. People came from all over San Francisco to buy her trendy clothes: bell-bottom striped hip-hugging pants, blousy psychedelic shirts, shirts with Nehru collars, tie-dyed t-shirts.

Peggy was from Louisiana. She was very beautiful, with a bubbling personality that she laced with у alls and groovys. Building her shop’s Dutch doors was the beginning of a long-lasting and memorable relationship between us. She supported Malati and I (thus much of the temple lunch program) for almost two years, while I remodeled and expanded her shop, built new stores for her in Seattle and Hermosa Beach, rebuilt her get-away cottage in Stinson Beach (later purchased from her by the Grateful Dead), and did construction work for her friends, people like Joan Baez, Janis Joplin, and Bobby Boles, who owned the boot shop next door to Mnasidika.

Peggy only ever had vague ideas about how she wanted to improve her shops—I need more shelves or I need a dressing room-so she usually said, “Do it however you want, Sa am” (which rhymes with ma’am). I went for natural woods, copper and stainless steel, glass, nephrite jade and polished marble—whatever I wanted to do. And she paid for it. Carte blanche—the creative builder’s dream. My skills and familiarity with various materials developed as I worked for Peggy, and then played a great part in shaping Jagannath carts and my other future devotional building projects. She also provided me with a steady income, so I could focus on becoming Krishna conscious. She asked many questions, like, “So what does the chanting do?” “No sex? How can you live without sex?”

I wasn’t very good at preaching yet; what did I know? I was just trying to learn the basics, and I was too shy to preach. But Peggy remained a total hedonist. She appreciated that mine was a spiritual path, that I had given up a life like hers in order to pursue Krishna (perhaps I was the only man she knew who could refuse her seduction), and ours became a special relationship. She came in and out of my life, a key player.

Nicholas von Hoffman was a popular writer for the Washington Post. In the summer of 1967, von Hoffman got a hiatus from his paper to come out to Haight-Ashbury, live with the hippies for a while, and write a book about what he saw. And he did come out, kind of a flamboyant character himself, but dressed in neatly pressed khakis, loafers, and a safari jacket. He met Peggy right away, and she took him under her irresistible wing and introduced him to her friends in the neighborhood. One day, von Hoffman was visiting the Diggers at 520 Frederick Street. They told him they were going next door to eat lunch at the Krishna temple, so he came along. He later wrote:

In the back of the storefront Melanie \[Malati\] was cooking lunch in the Radha Krishna Temple of the International

Society for Krishna Consciousness. Melanie was a thin girl who always wore a sari, which, with the flower behind her ear, gave her an air of maidenish docility. “Swami said Sam and I had to get married.” “Swami says you’re not your body.” “Swami says we are consciousness,” Melanie recited, working on the food that would serve the Temple devotees and the free-loaders who turned up for lunch. Swami says we shouldn’t take drugs and Swami says we shouldn’t smoke and Swami says we shouldn’t eat the flesh of any living thing.

Sam, her husband, was at Peggy’s store, putting in a new door. He would not go back to the Temple for lunch. “I’m fasting,” he said. Sam fasted a lot, for whole days at a time. He was a good carpenter, a Reed College boy. Sam said he was an ex-speed freak, and that may have been why he gave the impression of spiritual fragility. For an ex-speed freak he looked healthy and muscular, even if he was thin...

Peggy was in a dither about Sam. She had some work for him to do, and he was spending some of his time getting letters of recommendation to show the judge when he came up for sentencing on an old drug charge. Melanie grated on Peggy with her talk about Swami’s food edicts. “Oh, she’s all right,” Peggy declared, “an’ Sam’s good, but the rest of them are dirty Hindu beggars. There’s one I call Das Gruber Peas who’s always stoned and lying on the sidewalk next to Peter.” The Swami was last reported either ailing or short of funds in Calcutta.

Not a super great image of the Krishna people, but Swamiji used to say, “Any publicity is good publicity.” Von Hoffman’s book came out in February 1968 as We Are the People Our Parents Warned Us Against. It was an immediate bestseller. What was happening? Any discussion about hippies had to include something about the Hare Krishnas. The Swami’s Krishna consciousness movement had entered the mainstream of the new American culture.

For example, the Oracle, circulation now up to 100,000, continued to mention Krishna in every issue. Oracle #9 came out in August, and on page 11 included a poem called “Park Song,” by Pamela Millward:

Krishna-girls, lotus-girls, wrapped in gauze banners. Collage of prayer-flags in the brilliant snow of California.

Hare Krishna Rama Rama Hare Krishna Rama Rama  
I will put a white stripe down your nose  
I will put a red dot on your brow  
She did it with lipstick in the ladies’ john  
at California Hall. “There! That means  
you’re married to Krishna,” her girlfriend  
sighed, “I’d rather be married to George.”  
Hare Krishna Rama Rama Hare Krishna Krishna Rama Rama  
Let us end time. Let us go on forever.

**\* \* \***

Malati and I move to 635 Ashbury Street, eighty feet south of Haight, to an apartment building owned by Peggy Caserta. Peggy lives upstairs on the top floor. She makes me building manager, meaning I collect monthly rents from the tenants, call the plumber as required, and in return get a rent-free, ground-floor apartment with a tiny backyard. In that backyard, I slowly carve Subhadra, the final deity for the New York temple.

On September 10, 1967, the dreaded news at last arrives: our Supreme Court appeal has been turned down. But Judge Agnew reduces our sentence to only thirty days. So it is bad news/good news. Thirty days. At least the anxious wait is over.

Thirty days doesn’t sound too bad, and we start to come back to life. Our lawyer starts negotiating the date when we must appear in Seattle for incarceration. But the best news is that the Swami is feeling better and he plans to return to San Francisco soon. On October 7 he writes Mukunda from India: “I understand that you are all very anxious to know about me. By the Grace of Lord Krishna I am now 90% well although the basic difficulty, namely that I cannot walk very long distance, is there. While sitting on my seat I do hot feel anything abnormal; even nowadays I can type letters. I am eating also more than what I was eating in the states. On the whole I am now fit to go back to your country.”

To comply with what Swamiji told us about pets interfering with our spiritual practices, we knew we’d have to give Ralph away at some point. But he had become so much a part of our lives we kept putting it off. Rock Scully and the Grateful Dead lived a block up the street, at 710 Ashbury, arid Ralph often hung out over there, so one day I asked Rock, “Would you take him?” “Yeah, man, Ralph’s a groove—we’ll take him.” Rock then gave Ralph to Ken Kesey, who at that time lived on a farm in Marin County. That dog must have been a great devotee in his past life. He kept reappearing at the temple door whenever the Keseys were in town, but with less and less heartbreak I’d take him back over to Rock’s place.

Near the end of September, Peggy summons me to her apartment upstairs and excitedly tells me she’s thinking of opening a store near the University of Washington in Seattle and will I go up there and build it for her? Yes, I reply, and I travel to Seattle to construct Mnasidika North, just off University Way and 42nd Street. Mnasidika North will be the hippest clothing boutique in the district. I cover every square inch of wall space with rough planks of white hemlock accented with western red cedar—counters, shelves, everything is heavy natural wood. One day I’m working there and this gruff young man walks in and says, “I noticed your wood. It looks great. I’ve just come down from Alaska. I’m on my way to San Francisco.” He sees my neck beads and sikha and says, “I’ve been looking for the Krishnas!”

He is overjoyed to hear about the Frederick Street temple. (This man later became Naranarayana das, nicknamed Vishvakarma, master builder for the Krishna movement.)

Mukunda and I were trying to find ways to make money that didn’t involve so much work with nondevotees. We had manufactured fifty pairs of bell-metal kartals, but they had sounded kind of clunky and didn’t sell well. Then we sent $125 to SS Brijbasi & Sons company in New Delhi, and a few months later, in September 1967, we received three thousand brilliant posters depicting Krishna, Shiva, Durga, Saraswati, Vishnu, Rama, Hanuman—all the Indian gods—and sold them up and down Haight Street to the Print Mint, Psychedelic Shop, In Gear, Blushing Peony, and to shops in Berkeley and Marin County—wherever there were emerging hippie merchants.

Meanwhile, Malati had also started a new business. A few months earlier, the Swami had asked her to find him some sandalwood oil. She tracked down the one wholesaler of essential oils in San Francisco—George Lueders & Co., on Sacramento Street-but they would only sell a minimum of one pint, which is a lot of sandalwood oil. She decided to put the rest in little bottles and sell it to hippies. With her profit she bought more sandalwood along with other oils, such as patchouli and musk. She was making good money fast. I saw this and sort of preempted her business. Using her connections, Mukunda and I bought large quantities of ever more exotic oils and started our own business. We called it “Flash Transactions” after the hippie term for a drug rush—a “flash.” We sold little bottles of patchouli and sandalwood to our poster customers and the other head shops springing up all over town. In fact, patchouli soon became the favorite scent of the hippies. The ’60s would forever be associated with the patchouli fragrance, inspired by serving our spiritual master, A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, the ultimate cultural pioneer.

So now we had a little extra cash. Mukunda bought a battery-powered record player and sent it to the Swami in New Delhi. The Swami liked our new business, and from India he wrote to Mukunda on November 11, “Regarding your business, I have seen many merchants \[here\] who will supply us incense, frankincense, saris, musical instruments, dancing ghungru (jingle bells for feet) etc.”

But there was never quite enough money to pay for everything. The free breakfast and lunch program was really expanding as the Haight filled with tens of thousands of penniless newcomers. In addition to the monthly temple rent, we had to buy food to feed all these people every day. The temple room itself got so much use that the original rugs were already threadbare, and now, in the fall of 1967, the floor was cold. We needed new wall-to-wall carpeting, big time.

One day, Malati and I began our day as usual, leaving the apartment building to walk up Ashbury to the 7 a.m. program at the temple, a twenty-minute walk away. The streets were abandoned at that early hour, socked in with fog from the nearby Pacific. We had walked to about opposite the Grateful Dead house at 710 Ashbury, and we saw a bit of paper coming toward us on the wind. It was a hundred dollar bill, a Ben Franklin! We looked around and saw hundreddollar bills flying everywhere. There wasn’t another person on the street. We ran around and excitedly grabbed every bill we could find and then almost ran to the temple with the good news. Krishna comes through again! With this unexpected money, we not only paid our overdue rent but Malati also bought a beautiful royal purple carpet to cover the temple room floor, plus lots of groceries for the hippies. Our bare feet were warm and comfy during those cold-weather kirtans. We had received, literally, a windfall—and a godsend.

And then we got the really good news: on October 23, in another letter to Mukunda, Swamiji wrote: “When leaving from San Francisco I promised to Janaki and other girls that this time when I come back I shall first come to San Francisco. So I shall return by Pacific route and possibly make a break in the journey in Tokyo and Hawaii.” We were in ecstasy, waiting for a confirmed date when our spiritual master would return.

Pretty soon, the essential oils business skyrocketed, and sickening fumes overwhelmed our apartments, so Mukunda and

I rented a little storefront on Frederick Street, just a few blocks east, up the hill from the temple, and started bottling our oils there. We printed my hand-drawn labels and pasted them on these skinny bottles, and they sold like hotcakes. Soon, Gargamuni, Brahmananda’s younger brother, who had just come from New York, saw us making all this money and decided to start his own business, producing stick incense. He developed the manufacturing side and rented a storefront one door down from the temple to sell incense and other goods, which he imported from India. He hung a little sign out front that said “Spiritual Sky,” and this eventually became a huge business.

In early November, Malati went to the Haight-Ashbury Free Clinic on Clayton Street and found out that she was pregnant. “So,” I said, “by the middle of June next year we’ll have a baby?”

Then we the got news in a letter from my father: Malati and I were to report to the King County jail in Seattle on November 27 to start serving our jail terms. Just as we were about to leave San Francisco, Mukunda phoned and told us that he had just gotten a letter and that Swamiji was arriving on November 24. Our hopes soared. Maybe we’d have time to see the Swami before we left for jail.

Then we got more news: there was a Communist-inspired general strike in Calcutta, and all flights in and out of Calcutta have been canceled—so the Swami was delayed.

On November 27 we flew up to Seattle, heavy-hearted, to surrender ourselves to the jailers. Malati drove our rental car downtown from the Seattle airport, and as she pulled into an open slot in front of our lawyer’s building, she rear-ended the car parked in front of us. We lost our cash deposit on the car. We were processed, and became inmates of the King County jail. At least we knew we’d be out in thirty days and would see the Swami soon after that.

The lawyer wrote a letter to my Dad: “Sam and Melanie came in this morning as scheduled. We first went to the County Clerk’s Office, where I paid the $500.00 fine for each, and $68.77 in Superior Court costs. We then went to the King County Jail, where

I left Sam, and then I took Melanie to the Seattle City Jail. I shall do my utmost to see that each of them will be eligible for release at the earliest possible time, though at the moment each does face thirty days confinement.”

Grim. But we had Krishna now, and the Swami’s instructions came flooding back: You are not this body, this material arrangement is temporary, you can chant the maha-mantra under any condition and Krishna will be right there with you, giving delight and liberation. I immediately bummed empty cigarette packets from some of the more affable of my thirty fellow prisoners and tore out the foil inner wrappers. As per Swamiji’s instructions, I rolled pieces of foil into little balls and squeezed these onto a string pulled from my mattress to make chanting beads. And I started chanting as never before, but quietly, fingering my beads in the pocket of my gray prison coveralls. There was an air of paranoia and violence around me that I didn’t want to disturb. I focused on baby Krishna, the form they call Gopal, perhaps because His innocence—He has jam and yogurt smeared all over His face and had Himself been born in a jail—seemed to lift me out of this gruesome place.

Right away the power structure became clear: Gomez, a guy who had killed his wife and children and was awaiting transfer to a full-time prison, ran my cellblock. He sat silently in dark glasses at one end of the dayroom, smoking a cigar and surrounded by his acolytes, giving them instructions from time to time: change the channel, get me a Coke. Gomez conducted a nightly poker game, played with canteen coupons. We were allowed five dollars a week for snacks and tobacco; when vendors came they’d punch your coupon and hand you candy through the bars. I was a pretty good poker player and saw a way, maybe, that I could get close to the boss. I sat as close to Gomez as possible, read his face, usually folded but stayed in games whenever he did, then bet big sums with crummy hands and lost to him time after time. He began to like me.

There wasn’t much to do, so inmates rested a lot, lying down on the dayroom floor. One day, a couple of gangbangers walked by my reclining form and one guy kicked me in the butt, hard, sniggering something. Gomez immediately yelled across the room, “Hey!” and motioned them to lay off. No one touched me again.

I chanted beads all day, hundreds of rounds, and tried to remember what the Swami had told us earlier: “Haridasa Thakura was taken to the jail, and he told the prisoners there, ‘Oh, you are so fortunate to have this opportunity to sit and chant the holy names without the maya distractions of the outer world.’ So you should think like that, and use your time in the prison house to the greatest advantage for spiritual advancement.”

In jail I saw friendship—and murder.

Blackie, my cellmate, was an old-time classic safecracker. He looked like one of the Beagle Boys from the Scrooge McDuck comics. He and his partner had been robbing a Seattle tavern at about 4 in the morning. The partner had stood below as lookout while Blackie climbed up on the roof. Blackie was about to drop through a skylight to the safe below when he nudged his flashlight and it rolled down the roof and went Blam! onto a car top in the parking lot. Bad luck—a patrol car was parked a block away and heard the noise. The partner ran, but Blackie was caught red-handed. As a repeat offender he was looking at ten years. He offered to teach me the tricks of his trade, but instead I taught him the tricks of my trade, and he started chanting on the beads I made for him.

One night just before lights out, the bars opened and guards threw a newcomer into the tank. He was a drunken college kid, huge, probably a football player, who yelled and staggered around the cellblock slugging inmates, really belligerent. Gomez made a motion with his head and one of his sidekicks grabbed a heavy mop bucket and crashed it over the back of the kid’s head. His brains splattered the cell floor. Alarms rang and they hauled the body away. They questioned all of us the next day, but nobody saw a thing.

Then I got a letter from Mukunda: “The Swami is arriving in San Francisco, for sure, on December 14.” My heart soared. When we got out, I’d be able to see the Swami again.

The days flew by with thoughts of Krishna and Swamiji lighting up the confines of that otherwise horrible place. Malati finagled an early-release work program and got a job working at Peggy Caserta’s Mnasidika North shop in the university district, returning to her cell at night.

Malati: I wrote this letter—my dear judge, you have completely changed my life—you know, a real work of art—I have realized the folly of my ways and I would never have anything more to do with drugs. I am married now and pregnant, and don’t want my child to be born in jail, and I don’t think it’s fair for society to support me while I could be out working. Please give me this chance. So I was working at Peggy’s store. Shyamasundar was across the street from my jail, in the men’s prison, and a couple of times on my way home from work I brought him some little items.

Thanks to the personal intervention of Krishna, who is always merciful to his sincere devotees, we both were released a few days early. Twenty-seven days after our arrival in Seattle we were on our way back to San Francisco to rejoin our spiritual master. Peggy had paid Malati for her work, but it wasn’t enough for two adult plane fares. In those days, they had cheap student fares, so Malati borrowed a student’s ID and got us two one-way tickets. We had a dime left to our names. We planned to use it at SFO to call Mukunda for a ride home from the airport.

But when we got to the San Francisco airport, there, to our great surprise, was Mukunda, waiting for us. He said he had called Peggy to see what was happening, and she had told him we were on that flight. “Hurry up now,” he said. “Swamiji s back from India and wants to see you both.” Driving from the airport to Haight-Ashbury in the Krishna-mobile, I shared with Mukunda my main realization from jail: “We must preach Krishna consciousness to the leaders of the world, Mukunda— and who are the most famous people in the world right now? The Beatles, that’s who!” The plan to go to London was hatched in a jail.

The Beatles song “I Am the Walrus” was #1 on the hit parade in December 1967:

Semolina pilchard climbing up the Eiffel Tower Elementary penguin singing Hare Krishna Man you should have seen them kicking Edgar Alan Poe I am the eggman, they are the eggmen

I am the walrus, goo goo g’joob goo goo g’joob

In early December 1967, George Harrison was interviewed for the BBC’s Educational TV channel:

Interviewer: But you think that there’s a particular reason why people are ready for Ravi Shankar now, where they wouldn’t have been fifteen or twenty years ago?

George: Yeah, I think the reason is because the evolution, like one generation’s struggle against the older generation, it’s changing and slowly getting better and better all the time.... Not just with the music, but with the spiritual thing like in New York in particular, there’s so many swamis... there’s so many people now going in for yoga, meditation, and things like that. People want something a bit more solid in their life. You’ve got to go a bit beneath the surface to get something real out of life. Interviewer: Do you think this is what Shankar and his music and his world is offering?

George: Well, I think it’s all part of the big plot, the music and the spiritual thing. The younger generation want something more out of life, it’s all part of the big thing that’s going to change the world eventually. It may take two thousand years more, I don’t know. But the climax of it all will be the golden age and everybody will be very spiritual and very friendly, there’ll be no more wars, and in actual fact, earth will be heaven, as it were.

**\* \* \***

The Swami had taken over Gurudas and Yamuna’s apartment at 1253 Willard Street, just a couple of blocks up the hill from the temple. Hearts in our throats, we cracked the door, and he said, “Come in! Come in!” He greeted us as if nothing had happened and no time had passed. “Oh, you are feeling well?” he asked. “Just last night I was thinking five years is too much.” He pointed to a suitcase—he had just arrived in town himself—and asked Upendra to open it. He pulled out a folded Bengali sari, white with a red and green embroidered border, and handed it to Malati, saying, “Krishna likes it when the gopis are dressed in nice saris.” It was Christmas Eve 1967, and Santa Claus was here.

Swamiji put the whole jail thing into perspective: “This instance of suffering by a devotee should be carefully noted. When a devotee is seen in trouble, it should be accepted as God’s mercy. A devotee always accepts his distress as minimized by God’s mercy, as though he deserves much more suffering than he receives. Anyone who accepts this philosophy of God’s mercy in suffering conditions, and still makes progress in Krishna consciousness, it is said that he is sure to go back to home, back to Godhead.”

The bliss was palpable as we sat again at Swamiji’s feet while he lectured each day on “The Krishna Consciousness Yoga System.”

Lord Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, speaks about the topmost system of yoga in the sixth chapter of Bhagavad-gita.
