Please remember that we are preaching this Krishna consciousness movement on the authority of Bhagavad-gita. It is nothing manufactured. The bhakti-yoga system is authorized, and if you want to know about God, then you have to adopt this bhakti-yoga system because in Bhagavad-gita it is concluded that the topmost yogi is he who is always thinking of Krishna within himself.

And:

The more you make advancement in Krishna consciousness you will see Krishna everywhere. Not only on the bank of the river, but also on streets, trees, lampposts, and so on. The more you see like that you know you are making tangible advancement in Krishna consciousness. This is explained in the Gita. He is the taste of water, light of the moon, the fragrance of the flower, light of the sun, sound of the sky, the power of the strong and so on. So one who is actually making progress in Krishna consciousness, he can see Krishna everywhere. Krishna does not live in Vaikuntha \[heaven\]. But He lives there where His pure devotees chant His glories.

Krishna created us all to be His lovers, His playmates—His audience.

We feasted our eyes on the Swamis beautiful face, realizing how much he had changed our view of the world in just one year’s time. We now saw every person, every living entity, as an individual spirit spark of Krishna, each as precious as the other. We saw the world around us as Krishna’s energy, reflecting His own beauty and form. We knew that all events and activities taking place within time, everywhere in the inconceivably huge cosmos, were full of purpose and intelligence. We knew who God was, who we were, where we were going, and how we would get there.

In just a short time the Swami s eyes became the filter through which we saw the world. The stars at night, the behavior of a germ or a horse or a political movement or a house on fire—we now understood everything for what it was. Swamiji says Krishna consciousness is the end of all knowledge.

We caught up with the news and the gossip: Subal and Krishna devi had gone to Santa Fe and were opening a temple there. New centers were opening in Boston, Buffalo, and Washington DC. The Swami had signed a contract with MacMillan Publishers to publish his Bhagavad-gita As It Is. In San Francisco, the Swami had initiated a bunch of new devotees on December 16. Goursundar had a job as a bicycle messenger, and he and Govinda dasi were looking after the Swami. Upendra and Uddhava were cooking for him, and Mukunda was trying to get him a permanent US visa.

Swamiji seemed so enlivened by his return to San Francisco after nearly five months in India. He was happier and even more energetic than before, and he glowed with joy to be with his San Francisco family again. Gurudas took many photos of the Swami at ease in his room on Willard Street, laughing, the saucer eyes, joking with his devotees. But his recent illness had been a wake-up call. Time was running out, and Swamiji was swinging for the center-held wall: “Lord Chaitanya wanted that the message should be distributed in every village and town of the globe. Let us do this service as far as possible in all seriousness.”

The Swami’s energy level was astounding. He translated all night, took morning walks with devotees, answering their spiritual questions. He lectured in the temple mornings and nights, dictated many letters each day, directing his quickly expanding movement down to the smallest detail. He spoke at public forums, sometimes traveling great distances to do so, and appeared on TV, the radio, and for interviews with journalists.

In a letter to Brahmananda, Swami wrote: “The answer, why Maya dealt me a near-death blow: This is replied in Bhagavad-gita, ‘O, My dear son of Kunti, just declare it throughout the whole world that My devotee is never vanquished.’ So it will take some time to understand what Krishna’s plan is, but rest assured, a devotee of Krishna is never vanquished.” (Swami added at the bottom of his letter: “Ask Mr. Kaliman to give us his nice car.” That Swamiji—he never lets up!)

After the Swami had left for India the previous summer, Dayananda and Nandarani had moved to Los Angeles. Dayananda had a job with the telephone company there, and the couple opened a small Krishna temple on Pico Boulevard, not far from Beverly Hills. When he heard that the L.A. climate was nice, the Swami wrote to them: “Whatever improvement is made here \[India\] in the matter of my health it is all due to warm climate. As such I shall be pleased to know about Los Angeles with special reference to its climatic conditions.”

Swamiji had promised Dayananda in a letter in November that he would soon come to Los Angeles. The weather in San Francisco in early January 1968 was cold and rainy (and a 5.2 magnitude earthquake shook the windows at Willard Street), so the Swami decided to fly to L.A. on January 6, 1968. The sunshine and laid-back atmosphere made Southern California a good place to rest and recuperate, and he could inspire and oversee establishment of his new center in one of the world s most important cities.

Shortly before the Swami left San Francisco, Mukunda got a call from the producers of the Les Crane Show, one of Hollywood’s biggest national TV shows, and Swami committed to appear on the Show on January 17. Mukunda was sort of Swamiji’s media advisor, so he and Janaki drove the Krishna-mobile down to manage events in Tinsel Town. The Swami promised us he would return to San Francisco very soon.

Bhaktivedanta Swami was about to go nationwide.

**\* \* \***

For nearly all Americans, Life magazine was the closest you got to having your fingers on the pulse of the times. Life had gone to color format in the 1960s, and the large pages of exceptional photographs were seen by millions every week. The February 9, 1968, issue of Life had a nine-page feature article called “Year of the Guru,” with three pages of photos and commentary on Swami Bhaktivedanta and his new movement. Krishna consciousness had arrived. It was a stunning landmark on the Swamis remarkable journey. Even my parents were impressed. Maybe their prodigal son was doing something worthwhile, after all. The opening paragraph to “Year of the Guru” reads:

“Whenever virtue subsides and vice prevails, I come down to help mankind,” declared the Hindu God Krishna in the Bhagauad-gita. It may indicate something about our present virtue/vice index that a host of messengers of Krishna have arrived among us, turning their ancient rites into a pop-culture fad. Gurus (pronounced goo-ROO, Sanskrit for “weighty” and meaning a spiritual teacher) are packing them in.

The Beatles had gone to India in early February to spend a month at Maharishi Mahesh Yogi’s ashram near Rishikesh, and this was very big international news. Much of the article was about Maharishi-Maharishi has got 4 pages, compared to Bhaktivedanta’s 3—but at last the news was out: The youth of America were flocking to gurus.

Two pages of dramatic photos highlighted Swami Bhaktive-danta and his followers; the caption beside the photos reads:

The Society for Krishna Consciousness, which has chapters in six US cities, holds thrice-weekly ceremonies called kirtans. For hours the members, such as those of the New York temple, shown here, chant and dance in what their Los Angeles-based guru, Swami Bhaktivedanta (above), says represents “a spiritual call for the Lord and his Energy.” Finally, exhausted and elated by the ‘transcendental vibrations’ the ceremony brings on, they bow their heads to the floor (above right), chant in chorus “All glories to the assembled devotees” and then listen to a lecture. The swami’s talk is generally taped.

In the main text of the article, author Jane Howard writes:

The goal is bliss from meditation (samadhi) and the routes to it vary. At the Krishna Consciousness temple the route is Bhakti Yoga, or “The Yoga of Ecstasy,” the most colorful and, to hear its disciples talk about it, the easiest to master. “All you have to do is chant with us. Come on, join in!” The unvarying lyrics, which are repeated for as much as an hour at a time, are “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare, Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare.” It means, simply, “Hail to God,” but you won’t get the tune, or those words, out of your head for days. The chant itself is a form of meditation, and the dance that inevitably accompanies it is, Krishna only knows, a fit substitute for exercise.

Swamiji liked the article and wrote us from L.A.: “I have seen the Life Magazine publication about ourselves, and it is not bad.”

In another letter he wrote, “They have said: ‘The chanting of Hare Krishna continues to vibrate the brain for several days.’ We want to inject this transcendental poison into the heart of the Maya-serpent bitten people in general. Krishna Consciousness by the sound of Hare Krishna, it continues for a moment in the brain of the rascals, then certainly it will act in future to help them to become Krishna conscious. Even a slight injection of this transcendental vibration can save one from the greatest danger.”

To give an idea of the times, other articles in this February 1968 issue of Life are titled:

A new Beatles single is released in March and instantly becomes a #1 hit—“Lady Madonna” on one side, George Harrison’s “The Inner Light” on the other:

Without going out of your door  
You can know all things on Earth  
Without looking out of your window  
You could know the ways of Heaven.

Pattie Boyd, George’s wife, in her book Wonderful Tonight writes about their 1968 journey to India: “George retreated into himself. He had become very intense in India: the experience seemed to have answered some of the nagging questions he had about life but it had taken some of the lightness out of his soul... In India George had become fascinated by the god Krishna, who was always surrounded by young maidens...”

The Year of the Guru took a long time to arrive. Since the late 1800s, a number of swamis and yogis had visited America. They tried for a century to introduce something of ancient Vedic culture to these shores without much success, converting maybe a handful of true followers among the elite. Swami Vivekananda, a Bengali from Calcutta, addressed the Parliament of the World’s Religions in Chicago in 1893, made two lecture tours of the States, and initiated several disciples. Branches of his Ramakrishna Mission can still be found in a few US cities. Paramahansa Yogananda, another Bengali swami, sailed to the US in 1920 and settled in California until his death in 1952. He established two branches of his Self-Realization Fellowship and initiated a few dozen disciples. His book, Autobiography of a Yogi, familiarized millions with the mystical side of Indian spiritual practices. Indeed, this book formed many of our expectations of what a swami should be like.

It’s 1965. Enter a third Bengali swami: A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami. With dozens of serious new disciples every week, with new ISKCON centers opening each month all over America, Bhaktivedanta Swami quickly establishes himself as one of the most successful Indian gurus to have come to these shores.

Before Swamiji left for Los Angeles, he called me to his room and tasked me with building a throne for a set of fourteen-inch Radha-Krishna Deities, which were being designed and carved by Goursundar das. The Deities were to be cast in bronze. Swamiji drew a sketch for the throne, and advised that it should be covered in silver foil. I was bored carving simple Jagannath deities, and I had had a lot of recent woodworking experience building stuff for Peggy, so the timing was perfect. I researched ancient Indian temples and got to work. The throne design grew in scale, and I could see it was going to cost a lot of money to do it right. So I went to one of my old drug-dealing friends and asked for a donation. Maybe it was from a sense of guilt, but he gave me five hundred dollars for the exotic materials I had in mind. Building this throne for Radha and Krishna became an intense labor of love for me.

I got a two-inch-thick white marble base, 5' x 5', and set it on two sawhorses in the living room of our Ashbury Street apartment. Next I bought big slabs of Kentucky black walnut from MacBeath Hardwoods in the Bayview district, down by the docks. I read that walnut is prone to cracking, so to prevent this, I devised a big plastic-lined plywood tub and soaked the wood for days in a special chemical solution, under constant agitation by a little aquarium pump. We lived with that smell and noise for weeks—all for the Swami’s pleasure. This feeling never went away: I’ll do anything to see that smile on his face that shows he’s pleased by something I did to serve Krishna. This was by now the sole meaning of our lives—to please the Swami in some unique way.

I adapted the Swami’s sketch to the “piled-sections” technique you see in most ancient Indian temples, such as the Madan Mohan temple in Vrindaban: one crenellated layer piled on top of another— but I was using wood instead of stone. Dominating most of our living room, the intricate structure grew and became more beautiful each day: black walnut inter-layered with iron-hard green lignum vitae and purple-heart from Brazil; black ebony from Africa; osage orange wood from Kansas; rare vermilion-red wood from Honduras; and Pau Amarillo, a bright yellow wood from the Amazon. The Swami wanted silver foil, but should I cover all that expensive, bright-colored wood in silver? By now the thing was several feet high and filled much of the apartment, so Peggy let me use an empty garage attached to the building. It takes six strong devotees to manhandle it out there.

But Swamiji's silver foil idea is good, so I buy sheets of real silver and gold foils and start pressing it onto the structure here and there. Wow! This thing is getting gorgeous. Then young Krishna das brings over a jar of tumble-polished fire opals from Mexico, and I start carving little niches, setting these fiery, color-changing opals into the throne—by now it’s more of a temple than a throne. I buy abalone shells and cut them into tiny highlights. This work will consume much of my time over the next six months.

On February 15, Swamiji writes me from Los Angeles: “But your design is not so plain, it is gorgeous and if it is covered with silver foils it will be still more gorgeous ... If the friend who wants to donate is willing to pay any amount of expenditure, then your design is very nice. You do it nicely for the service of Sri Sri Radha Krishna. I am feeling well so don’t worry about me. As soon as you like me to go to San Francisco, I am ready to go.”

While the Swami is in L.A., I design a “Drown in the Ocean of Bliss!” leaflet using psychedelic script over a photo of Tom Herzig’s rapture-struck face. Thousands of them are handed out around the Haight. I also draw an advertisement for the Happening record, which we insert in local papers. It has a clip-out coupon: “Please send me the HARE KRISHNA record. I enclose $3.00 for each record.”

We re busy, yes, but Malati and I are getting itchy feet. We’ve been in one place now for more than a year, and the Frederick Street temple no longer requires our constant attention. The Swami wants us to spread Krishna consciousness. What are we doing? Gargamuni, Upendra, and Uddhava are trying to start a center in Berkeley, across the Bay; Mukunda and Janaki are down in L.A., pioneering a new temple; Gurudas and Yamuna are antsy too, and they talk about going to India.

Performing big services for Krishna seems to be the best way to catch the Swami’s attention. Malati and I want to do something big—get out of Frisco and just do something big. The jail thing is behind us but, well, there’s the baby coming in June, so we’ll have to wait a few more months. But what then? I really want to go to London and meet the Beatles. Every chance I get, I talk up this plan with my dearest friends, the devotees I’d trust most to go with me: Mukunda and Yamuna. If they are convinced, their spouses will agree. “Come on, Mukunda, it’ll be such a great adventure—the Swami wants a temple in London more than anywhere else. You’ll love England. I’ve been there. We’ll have so much fun!”

The Swami’s mood of taking great risk for Krishna was infectious. He wrote to Gargamuni on February 12, 1968: “So far I am concerned I always take risk for Krishna. I came here in your country risking my life. And still although I am physically unfit, I am trying to execute the orders of my Guru Maharaja as far as possible. So to take risk for Krishna is very good. You know the history of my starting the society in 1966, it was all risky, and I was alone, but I took it depending on Krishna.”

I want to go somewhere, chase another rhinoceros with the Swami.

**\* \* \***

The peace-and-love scene in Haight-Ashbury was declining rapidly. The youth of America had had their fling; now, more sinister elements were creeping into the neighborhood: crass commercialism (such as the Gray Line bus tour called “Hippie Hop”), hard drugs, crime. There was just not enough space or organization to feed and house so many penniless hippies. As Joan Didion put it, “The center was not holding.” The hard-core hippies were moving out to Marin County, Big Sur, other cities, and getting away from the mob. The Oracle, voice of Haight-Ashbury, printed its last issue in February 1968: “Final City/Тар City.” In fact, the main hippie scene was shifting to London, where flower power was still in its infancy. The hippie dream may have been disappearing around us, but our dream was just beginning.

The Swami wrote from L.A.: “I am now scheduled to go to S.F. by the 10th March 1968.” Hearing this, Jayananda, Gurudas, and others go into high gear, arranging speaking programs for him. “I am so glad to learn that you are making nice arrangements for my lecturing in different places, and I wish to execute all programs within 20-30 days from the date of my arrival before starting for New York.” Coming up: Another month with the Swami!

Swamiji arrives in San Francisco earlier than expected, on March 8. We greet him at the airport with the usual exuberance, so happy to see him again, our life and soul. Without missing a beat, he jumps right in.

On March 9, a couple of reporters show up at Willard Street from KPFK radio station in Los Angeles. They want to tape an interview with the Swami for a future broadcast. The interviewer asks one question: “Your grace, what is the basis of your teachings?” The Swami takes off and explains for a half hour straight, uninterrupted, the essence of Krishna consciousness. In a conversational tone, without heavy preaching or any Sanskrit, the Swami doesn’t pause (except near the end, when he asks for some water) while the interviewers sit, stunned by this comprehensive answer to their one simple question.

On March 10, Swami initiates new devotees, including Tom Herzig, who becomes Tamai Krishna.

Tamai Krishna: Bhaktivedanta Swami was Krishna’s pure devotee, and I was ready to surrender my body, mind, and words to him. I had very little idea who Krishna was, but I was certain that the Swami had an intimate relationship with Krishna and that by pleasing him I might one day also be able to please Krishna.

On March 12, the Swami appears with four devotees on a prime-time KGO radio show, arranged by Gurudas. The well-known talk show host is very sharp, but the Swami is sharper still. They bat one-liners back and forth like a well-played tennis match. Opposite to the lengthy speech he gave to KPFK two days back, this time the Swami uses terse sentences to fully elucidate Krishna consciousness. The mood is light, Swamiji laughs, and everyone is having fun, especially near the end when the host takes on-the-air phone calls from listeners. One listener asks, “In other words, you are a free-loader?” “Yes,” says the Swami.

On March 13, Gargamuni and Yamuna arrange for a professional sound studio, and the Swami stays for hours recording Chintamani and other Vaishnava prayers before heading out to San Francisco State College to lecture to a full house.

In a letter to Hayagriva in Ohio, dated March 17, the Swami declared:

I am very much encouraged by seeing the atmosphere from S.F. Temple. The other day I delivered lecture in San Francisco State College, and the students enjoyed the atmosphere very much. They heard our Kirtana very attentively, and although my lecture was a little terse, on account of our prohibitory regulations, still the students heard me very attentively. And they stood around me even after the meeting was over. One girl questioned me why I am against meat eating, and she was very satisfactorily replied.

March 14 was Lord Chaitanya's Appearance Day, or “Gaur Purnima” (golden full moon), and Swami instructed us to fast until moonrise and to sit in the temple all day, chanting and reading from the Chaitanya Charitamrita. By mid-afternoon we were really antsy. We had been reading how Lord Chaitanya and His pals used to chant loudly in the roads and villages of old Bengal, so someone got the idea: Hey, let’s all walk up to the Swamis place and serenade him from the street! We chanted, with kartals and drums, up Willard Street, paused in front of 1253, and banged away until Upendra came out on the balcony and motioned us to come in, saying, “Swamiji wants to see you.” Inside, the Swami was elated, and he said Lord Chaitanya has now appeared in the form of sankirtan, and that we should go on performing this street kirtan every day from now on. This was the beginning of public sankirtan in San Francisco, soon to spread to main streets all over the world.

By the time the moon rose at 6:18 p.m., we were starving, and the Swami told us to eat a light snack of fruits, peanuts, and milk-no grains. The next day, there was a full-scale feast. Some of us were a little concerned about this new sankirtan idea. Did we have to wear those silly bed sheets, like, out on the streets?

The Swami cleared it up: “Householders may wear dhotis in the temple, or as they like, but not of the saffron color. They may wear white, yellow, or whatever. Outside the temple they may wear gentleman’s dress, with tilaka, sikha, and beads. It is not required to wear dhotis, as this American society does not understand, so outside the temple dress suit is more socially acceptable. If they so desire, for ceremony, they can dress in dhotis for kirtan.”

Whew!

In addition to outside engagements, the Swami lectured on the Bhagavad-gita in the temple every morning.

Mayy aasakta. Mayy means “unto Me.” Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, says “unto Me, Krishna.” You cannot interpret in a different way. When I say that “Give me a glass of water,” it means that I am the person who is in want of a glass of water.

“Be attached to Me.” So as it is possible that in spite of our all engagements, if I love somebody I think of him always materially it is possible, why not spiritually? That is the whole teaching of Bbagavad-gita.

It is mental concoction that “Krishna is Indian,” or “Krishna is Hindu god.” Just like the sun. If you say, “It is American sun,” is it possible, American? Sun is sun.

There are five kinds of direct attachment to Krishna: You may want Krishna as your lover. You may want Krishna as your son. You may want Krishna as your friend. You may want Krishna as your master. You may want Krishna as the supreme sublime. These five different kinds of direct relationship with Krishna are called devotion, bhakti.

Just like, if a car is running at seventy-mile speed, and a cyclist catches the car, he also runs in seventy-miles speed. Similarly, although we are minute particles of God, if we dovetail ourselves with the consciousness of God, or Krishna consciousness, then we become equally spirit. This is the technique.

Most evenings, the Swami lectured to us from Srimad-Bhagavatam, taking up wherever he had left off the night before.

Five thousand years before, the memory was so sharp that one could keep in his memory simply by once hearing from the spiritual master. So these Vedic scriptures were recorded by Vyasadeva because he could understand that in future people will be born of so lower grade that it would be not possible to keep in memory without book record.

Five thousand years ago it was predicted in Bhagavatam that in this Kali-yuga all good qualities will gradual diminish: religiosity, truthfulness, cleanliness, forgiveness, mercy, duration of life, strength, and memory. These eight symptoms, just see how you have reduced.

Now, if you have got money, whatever you may be, you are very respected. “Might is right.” Selection of husband and wife will depend on agreement: “I like you; you like me.” Finished. And if you have no money, then you cannot get justice. Another item, simply if he can satisfy the mass people by false propaganda, then he will be accepted. So everyone can manufacture in his own way some cheap thing, and people will like it. This is Kali-yuga.

Or this zinger:

This is a little technical. Try to understand. Now this universe, which you find just like a big ball, there are innumerable universes like this, and they are floating in the Causal Ocean. Lord Vishnu in a gigantic form is sleeping within that Causal Ocean, and by His inhaling and exhaling, breathing, there are bubbles. And those bubbles are manifested as universes.

The month went by so fast, and just as we were getting used to Swamiji’s close association again, he told us he would depart for New York on April 17, 1968. There wasn’t much time left. If I was going to present my London proposal to Swamiji, I’d better hurry. I buttonholed Mukunda. “Are you in? Yes or no?” and got a tentative, “OK, I guess it might be fun, but I’ll have to convince Janaki.” Yamuna said she might also go, if Swamiji approved.

The Swami was born in India in 1896, during the peak of the British Raj. His schooling, his language, many of his mannerisms and gentlemanly behavior were deeply influenced by English colonial rule. We knew right from the beginning that

Swamiji really wanted to start a center in London. America was now the world leader, so he started his mission in America, but he often spoke how his own guru, Bhaktisiddhanta Swami, wanted a Krishna conscious center in London. Bhaktisiddhanta had dispatched several of his disciples to London back in the 1930s, and they had made a distinct impression on the British upper classes, but had not established a permanent foothold. The times were just not right.

As far back as March 1967, the Swami had mentioned in a letter to Gargamuni: “If I can establish one branch in London it will be a great success of our mission.” And he kept writing to devotees, encouraging them to go to London. In a letter to Janardana in Montreal dated February 7,1968, for example, he wrote: “Regarding your proposal for opening a center in London: I am prepared to go there at any moment. Even I do not mind the severe cold there.”

By 1967-68, the Beatles, the Rolling Stones, and other British rock groups were the most popular musicians in the world and, as the love-and-peace Haight-Ashbury hippie scene started to decline, the London hippie scene was expanding. I had been to the UK a couple of times—I had hitchhiked around England in the summer of 1963 and spent a month in London in 1965—and I loved the place.

So the day before he leaves for New York, I screw up my courage, go up to Willard Street, and sit down in the Swami’s room.

“Swamiji, I have a proposal. Malati and I want to go to London to start a temple there. Mukunda and Yamuna say they will also come, the six of us. What do you think?”

“Hmmmm. And do you have sufficient funds?”

“Yes. I just got a tax refund for nearly a thousand dollars, and we have some money from our essential oils business. I think it’s enough.”

“I will also loan you, that is not a problem...”

“And just now these Beatles in London are the most famous people in the world, and they like Krishna consciousness. I want to meet them. I think we can meet them and make them devotees. It’s a good chance.”

“But what about Malati? She is having her baby soon. When is that?”

“She’ll have the baby in June, maybe middle of June-but we can go right after that.”

"It is a nice proposal. My Guru Maharaja wanted more than anything to have one temple in London. I shall consider. Meanwhile, let Malati have her baby. Then we shall decide.”

With Swamiji gone, I work in the garage on Ashbury Street, detailing the elaborate throne I’d built. Goursundar has pretty much stopped Carving his Radha-Krishna Deities, so there’s no rush to finish it. Peggy sends me down to Hermosa Beach to build her Mnasidika South branch there; I live in the near-empty L.A. temple on Pico Boulevard and commute to Hermosa Beach each day. Early mornings and evenings, I carve a set of Jagannath deities in the alleyway behind the temple, glad for Southern California sun. Back in San Francisco, I join the devotees some days down on Fisherman’s Wharf for sankirtan, but I am really embarrassed by this public display. I’m shy and don’t know enough to answer questions from bystanders. I find myself using any excuse to avoid going down there.

Waiting for the baby, waiting for Swamiji’s decision about London.

On May 6, the Swami writes Mukunda:

Shyamasundar wanted to go to London; what is his opinion about this? I am very much eager to open a center in London. If Shyamasundar and Malati go there, then one or two other Brahmacaris may go there to organize a center in London; and as soon as it is in makeup then I may also go there for some time, to deal with the Beatles who are so much anxious to understand about spiritual knowledge. Any sincere person, never mind whether he is hippy or Beatle, if he is actually searching for something beyond this hackneyed material sense gratification, surely he will find the most comfortable shelter under the Lotus Feet of Lord Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, Who is non-different from Lord Krishna.

Then, finally, the long-hoped-for letter came:

Montreal

7 June, 1968

My Dear Shyamasundar,

I think it is Krishna’s desire that now I shall shift my activities at least for some time in the European countries. So it is almost certain that I’m going to London by the month of August. And if you all wish to come there, then you can prepare for the trip... If Malati delivers her baby during the Ratha-yatra festival, then she can take one month’s rest in July, and may be able to accompany you with her newly born Krishna Consciousness baby. For the time being, finish your work in the hands, and side by side try to chalk out a program how we shall propagate Krishna Consciousness in the European countries.

In a letter to Yamuna, also dated June 7, the Swami writes: “I am sure if you and your husband, Mukunda and Janaki, and Shyamasundar and Malati join, certainly the London program will be a grand success.”

Were on! Fresh energy pours into our lives, and the mission of missions awaits us: London with the Swami. About as big a rhino as you could possibly chase.

“Finish your work in the hands,” he says. First consideration: money. In late March, I had received a tax refund from Uncle Sam for my 1966 fire lookout pay. It came to $986.08. Enough to cover the cost of having a baby—with maybe some left over—but we needed more money. Mukunda and I had our business Flash Transactions, but it was becoming too big for us to handle, demanding almost our full time and energy. Our storefront “factory” lacked ventilation, and we suffocated on perfumes as we filled hundreds of little bottles with strong essential oils: patchouli, lemon, ylang-ylang, verdigris, cinnamon, and a dozen others. We had to run all over the Bay area distributing orders, going after tardy payments, buying wholesale supplies, keeping accounts—it was way too much work for two guys. We proposed selling Flash to my drug-dealer friend—“Hey Nick, it’s the perfect cover for you!” And he bought the business from us lock, stock, and bottle for two grand, a thousand dollars now, and the other thousand he’d send to us later, in London.

**\* \* \***

The second annual San Francisco Ratha-yatra was coming up fast. Swamiji had set the date for June 30,1968. The Swami was in Montreal, and he wouldn’t be able to come to SF due to visa and immigration problems. This time he instructed that the rath car should be built from scratch, with wheels, decks, canopies—the full cart, not an open-sided box on the back of a flatbed truck. So I started designing it, with Jayananda’s help. Jayananda was still driving his taxi, and I was doing jobs for Peggy, but we worked in shifts and recruited a few other carpenter types to help us. It was an immense project: four 5'-diameter heavy wooden plank wheels, a 10' x 15' chassis, tapering out and up to a 20' x 30' second deck, rising to a third-story framework for eighteen billowing canopies, rising still higher to a 6'-diameter umbrella-shaped canopy, crowned by Lord Jagannath’s flag. The cart would be twenty-three feet high, from pavement to flag. (We’d measured the lowest electric and phone lines crossing our proposed route, and twenty-three feet seemed to fit.)

We started with a boxlike chassis made of redwood and Douglas fir beams. We ran ten-foot lengths of 1" round steel for axles. The rear axle was mounted to the frame with heavy bearings, but we weren’t sure what to do with the front axle, because it had to turn to get around corners. Jayananda knew automobile mechanics somewhat, and I had built a hot rod in high school, so we welded a car wheel to the center of the front axle as a pivot, and ran two 5" x 5" beams, 20' long, about 8' apart, straight out from the front axle. These beams were joined by a 5" x 5" strut about ten feet out, and a two-inch steel pipe joined the beams at the ends so that people could pull the cart. Two long, one-inch ropes were also tied to this pipe, so dozens of people could pull in tandem.

5" x 5" redwood beams ran up at the diagonal, to support 2" x 6" joists supporting the heavy deck where the deities would ride. Each joint on the car was fastened by two or three carriage bolts with two-inch washers. The upper deck was made of 2" x 8" used rough redwood planks. A team of devotees hand-sanded these timbers for days in order to make them look new. The mood was “Make it stout for Lord Jagannath.” I found some long redwood laths, a quarter-inch thick and four inches wide, easy to bend into curves and circles, so we nailed their ends onto the roof above the second deck and curved them around to anchor the eighteen billowing canopies. These cloth canopies were dyed by the girls and were rather more orange than saffron. A six-inch yellow fringe was sewed around the canopies and little bells fastened around the entire deck at six-inch intervals. Big posters of the seated Swami were mounted front, side, and back of the car. This year, the Tiny Tiger generator was mounted at the rear and underneath the car on its own frame to somewhat diminish the sound and fumes. Their three Lordships would sit on a simple altar at the front of the top deck, looking forward—best seats in the house. And for the first time, they would be clothed.

“But clothes will cover up my beautiful paint job,” I said. Yamuna overrode me, saying that Swamiji had directed her to make clothes for the divine trio. “OK, but they already have clothes on—I painted them on.” No, no, the Swami said real clothes—turbans, shawls, little skirts. So Yamuna and Harsharani sewed up fancy outfits in bright red satin.

Malati’s getting more and more pregnant, but this doesn’t slow her down a bit—she’s till rustling up free lunches at the temple and now diving with the other girls into preparations for the Ratha-yatra festival. I was busy working on the rath car almost every day. We knew the baby would be born sometime in mid-June, but I didn’t really think about it much. That was Malati’s department.

On June 17, I’m banging away up on the top deck of the rath car when someone yells out the back window of the temple that Malati has taken a taxi to French Hospital over on Geary Street to have her baby.

Malati: Yeah, Shyamasundar didn’t even pick me up. He had somebody leave the panel truck for me with the keys inside, and I drove myself home. There was only the one driver’s seat, so I just put the baby in a little wicker basket on the floor. I hadn’t bought anything at all for the kid, not even a diaper. So my first thought was Sears and Roebuck. So I walk in with a baby, just a few hours old, and the woman said, ‘‘Can I help you?” I said, “I don’t know. Can you tell me what to do?” I remember she looked at the other lady-they couldn’t believe it. So we got diapers, stuff like that. When I got home I phoned Shyamasundar at the temple.

Again, a yell came from the back window: “Malati’s home from the hospital with the baby!” I rushed back to the apartment and walked in, stunned: Is it a boy or a girl? She said girl. I’m jumping up and down, seeing that little pink face. I ran out to the pay phone in the hall and rang the Montreal temple. After some time, plugging nickels and dimes, the Swami’s voice comes on: “Bhaktivedanta here.”

“Yes, Swamiji, our baby was born today here in San Francisco. It is a girl. Will you name her?”

A short pause. “She shall be called Saraswati devi dasi.”

Over the moon, I yelled down the hall to Malati: “It’s Saraswati!” Saraswati is the goddess of music and knowledge, and she became the first child conceived and born in the Krishna consciousness movement.

Swamiji wrote a letter to Sachisuta on June 17,1968: “Just now I received one telephone message from San Francisco that a girl baby is born of Malati and Shyaynasundar. And they asked me for registration of the baby’s name, and she is named by me as Sarasvati devi. Please pray for the newly born baby in Krishna Consciousness.”

Malati: And my father, to whom I almost never spoke, called and asked if I wanted anything, so I said diaper service, because I didn’t really dig those dirty diapers everywhere.

Now we have a deadline for London. Swamiji said the baby should be at least six weeks old before traveling. So we quickly meet with Mukunda and Janaki and Gurudas and Yamuna to pick a date for getting out of here. We decide to go to Montreal first to get blessings from the Swami and instructions on what to do in London. We shoot for late August.

But first, Ratha-yatra. On June 30, the weather was glorious. All the West Coast devotees were in San Francisco—Uddhava, Shyamadevi, Gargamuni, Woomapati, Krishnadas (blowing a conch), Upendra (fanning the deities). Vishnujan, Tamai Krishna, and Aniruddha in yellow dhotis (I wouldn’t be caught dead in a dhoti!); and look—there’s the new guy, Jayapataka. He’s even shaved his head! More than fifty devotees pulled the cart or sold Back to Godheads or dispensed prasadam—everyone whooping and hollering the Hare Krishna mantra.

As sunlight flashed through windswept flags, Lord Jagannath, Balaram, and Subhadra—their jewels, blousy red-satin clothes, and flower garlands bouncing up and down with the roll of the streets—winked and flirted and came alive for us: The Lord of the universe and His family out to enjoy an exciting day at the beach with their friends.

Five hundred people surround the ponderous rath car, chanting and dancing, as we push and pull it mile after mile, lumbering down Irving Street—I cringe at every pothole, every phone line we pass under, wondering if this monstrosity will hold together—across Great Highway and into Ocean Beach parking lot. Along the way I notice certain devotees seem to disappear. Where’s Chidananda? Where’s Upendra? They were here a while ago.

The beach was crowded on this perfect day, with thousands of San Franciscans enjoying around us—bonfires, kirtans, bliss. Then came the news: the Diggers, the Angels—or somebody-had passed out fruit punch or candy laced with LSD, and many devotees had left to deal with an exploding universe. We had made a deal with the police that we would dismantle the entire rath cart and haul it off the beach by sundown. Yipes. It was down to me and Jayananda—we didn’t drink the punch—to undo every bolt, every screw, and pull out every nail. We quickly recruited a half dozen hippie helpers, and told them we have to tear this thing down from crown to wheels. With Malati and a few other sober devotees holding flashlights, by midnight we had pretty much finished—sawing to pieces the longer boards to fit into my panel van—and after maybe twenty trips back and forth, we had at last stacked the shambles of the once-magnificent rath cart behind the temple.

Malati: I had baby Saraswati in my arms—she was two weeks old—and I was lagging behind the crowd. For this reason, I didn’t drink any of the LSD-laced beverage. And I recall the dismay of Shyamasundar and Jayananda having to dismantle that big cart practically single-handedly, because many devotees had fallen mysteriously ill. With the help of a scruffy but willing hippy, I drove the deities back to the temple and he helped get them back up on the very high platform that was their altar.

I don’t know, maybe the acid enhanced the Ratha-yatra experience for some of the devotees.

Yamuna: At the end of the procession when the cart was at the beach, this bhajan of Swamiji played for hours and hours over the sound system, the devotees said. I remember just the short time that I was at the beach, that when the wind would move back and forth it would catch the sound of this magnificent prayer. And in my mind, it’s always been the theme of our 1968 Ratha-yatra festival, this mood of Lord Chaitanya’s longing for Krishna.

15 July 1968

My Dear Shyamasundar,

Please accept my blessings. I beg to acknowledge receipt of your letter with thanks, dated July 8,1968, and I must say that the Ratha-yatra festival was so successful for your diligent labor only. If you would not have constructed such a nice ratha (car) in such a short time, it would not have been possible to perform the grand festival. You have carved so many Jagannatha Murtis, and the car also, so Krishna must have been very much satisfied with your service. Srimad-Bhagavatam says that the highest perfectional stage of everyone’s talent is to satisfy the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Hari, by his craftsmanship, scientific knowledge, philosophical research, or similar any other energy. Please continue this attitude of service and success of life is assured for you. I have learned the description of the Ratha-yatra festival verbally from so many persons who came to see me from San Francisco.

The 1968 San Francisco Ratha-yatra was picked up by the wire services, and the news was spread around the world, even to India-One Sumitra Sarkah, a correspondent for Calcutta’s Amrita Bazar Patrika, wrote an amazing story on page three of the July 12 edition:

As I was walking with the crowd I sang a song in chorus that I have known in my blood since my childhood. But after a while, the intense sincerity of the whole movement did not make it seem strange at all, and I forgot they were Americans. They had all become one: the hippies, the straights, the bramcharies, the devotees, even the dogs, for they were all bathed in His glory and in His blessings... Words failed me for some time, and my feelings were too deep and sensitive to be expressed through words.

Mukunda, Hayagriva, back on the sidewalk

The Ratha-yatra was a remarkable success—about fifteen new devotees followed us home and moved into the temple. The baby had been born. Now it was time to get ready for London. Malati and I had to get our passports squared away and permission from our probation officers to travel abroad.

We set a departure date and bought tickets to Montreal leaving SF on August 10, 1968. After a few days of instructions from the Swami, we planned to fly from Montreal to New York City on August 19. Icelandic Airways would carry us on August 27 from NYC to Luxembourg.

My passport had been confiscated during the drug bust two years back and I needed to get a letter from my parole officer before the government would return it to me. Off I go, down to see my parole officer at the Hall of Justice—Mr. Silva, his name is—and I exhibit my new religious zeal. He issues the required letter on August 7: “Mr. Samuel Speerstra has my permission to travel outside the continental limits of the United States while on probation with this department.”

I sent this letter to my lawyer in Seattle, who promised to reclaim and mail me the passport. Meanwhile, Malati got a new passport with Saraswatis name and picture in it too, and a letter from her probation officer giving her permission to leave. A couple of weeks go by, but my passport did not come. Still, we were certain Krishna would arrange everything, so we carried on with our London preparations.

I was so confident that Krishna would arrange a nice center in London that I picked out a design for the temple room from my illustrated book of Indian temples: the main cave temple at Ajanta looked just right to me. I drafted the design onto paper and sent it to the Swami. He replied, “It is a Buddhist design, but it is very nice.” So I went to a lumberyard down on the Peninsula and bought sixty tongue-and-groove finished redwood planks, 8' long x 12" wide x 3/4" thick, and had them strapped to a pallet and shipped by sea freight to London with a “Hold for Pickup” label and “Notify On Arrival c/o American Express, Haymarket Street, London W.C. 1, England.”

We didn’t have a lot of stuff to pack. We had no furniture in our house—just a kitchen table. We slept on the floor. (One day, some of the Grateful Dead guys came over and asked, “Where’s the baby’s bed?” Later, they sent over a hand-carved redwood rocker bed as a gift for the baby.) Malati bought a front-pack, a papoose, some diapers, a couple of pacifiers, and a few little toys.

What to do with that fabulous deity throne I’d been working on for six months in the garage on Ashbury? It was almost finished, but not quite. I call Chidananda over—he’s a pretty good carpen-ter-and give him detailed instructions: glue these fire opals into the little niches I’ve carved in the ebony filigree around the opening. Then clean it all up and it’s finished. The plan was, Chidananda would ship it to London later for our new Radha-Krishna temple over there. I hated to leave this masterpiece of love, but the Swami had taught us well: remain unattached to the fruits of your labors.

As manager of Peggy’s apartment building, I collected the August rent from David Cocking, who lived upstairs, and noticed he was English. I asked him if he could suggest a good bank in London, and he said, “Yes, the Chartered Bank of London is the best, just off Piccadilly, and it’s the only British bank with a branch in San Francisco.” So I went down to the local branch of the Chartered Bank of London at 465 California Street. It looked pretty good. It had been chartered by Queen Victoria in the 1800s, so it had an air of old wealth and competence. I read in a brochure that the bank’s original trade had been in cotton from Bombay, indigo and tea from Calcutta, rice from Burma, sugar from Java, tobacco from Sumatra, hemp from Manila, and silk from Yokohama. Their West End Branch, the brochure said, was located at 2 Regent Street, SW1, in the very heart of London. Perfect.

Malati went downtown in her brown wraparound corduroy skirt to the British Consulate-General on Sansome Street, and she picked up a load of travel brochures, mostly about castles. This was so that when we came through British Customs and they asked what we planned to do in England, we could say we were coming to see the castles.

Yes, we believed in magic in those days; anything that had to do with the Swami and Krishna consciousness, it was all in the hands of higher powers. We picked up on the Swami’s sense of adventure and willingly charged headlong into situations that would force Krishna’s hand: Krishna said He’d protect us, so let’s see Him do it.

Malati: I still believe that—it’s always been our experience. Back to the basics: Krishna is taking care of the elephants out in the desert, but you don’t think he’s going to take care of you? And it was because we weren’t going to England to be rock-and-roll hippies; we were on a mission for the Swami. We would do the most far-out things without thinking it was far out. Even now I can’t believe we did some of those things.

One-way tickets to Luxembourg, a seven-week-old baby, almost no money, no addresses on the other side—and we’re going to meet the Beatles?

**\* \* \***

Montreal, Quebec, was the site for the World Fair Expo in 1967, and in the summer of 1968, the city was still riding a wave of international attention. We arrived in Montreal on August 10. We found the International Society for Krishna Consciousness at 3720 Park Avenue/Avenue du Mont-Royale, on the second floor. As we walked up the stairs, Himavati greeted us and said right away, “You’re going to get second initiation!” We didn’t really know what she was talking about—something to do with the piece of string Swamiji wears over his left shoulder and looped around his torso. There’s some kind of ceremony, she says, and you get the string, a special mantra, and now you’re bound to Krishna forever—something like that.

We go inside. It’s a former bowling alley—one huge room with a beautiful maple floor. They’ve run ropes all over the place and hung bed sheets over them for “privacy.” We’re assigned our own partitioned cubicles and throw down our meager belongings. The Swamis staying in an apartment somewhere nearby, and he’ll come over to the temple this evening.

Malati and Janaki can’t wait. They scout the neighborhood till they see saffron cloth hanging out to dry, rush up to the door, and knock. Govinda dasi answers and says loudly over her shoulder, “The devotees from San Francisco are here!” Then the Swami greets them: “OOOOH, this very morning I dreamed about this child, this exact child. I dreamt about her this morning.” He looks at Saraswati for a moment and says, “Remember me? I’m you’re old friend.” Gurudas, too, had spotted the saffron laundry, and he is already here.

Swamiji wrote a letter to Hayagriva on August 12, 1968: “Just on Saturday, several devotees have arrived from San Francisco: Mukunda, Janaki, Gurudasa, Yamuna, Shyamasundar, Malati (and their little child, Srimati Sarasvati devi). All of them have come here for two weeks to practice together as our Sankirtana party, then they will go to London and begin preparations for our center there. While they are here, we are having rehearsal of Kirtana daily.”

**\* \* \***

So begins our tutelage. Right away, the Swami conducts a fire ceremony and we are given brahmin threads and Swamiji whispers the Gayatri mantra into our right ears. This seven-verse mantra is to be spoken silently three times a day, ten repetitions of each mantra, and he patiently teaches each of us how to count on one hand, moving thumb from finger segment to finger segment: “No, no. Like this.” Someone had typed the Gayatri mantra on little slips of paper for the initiates. It takes me days to memorize the simple Sanskrit prayers. This “second initiation” signifies our spiritual birth: as brahmins, brahmanas—we are now twice-born. It’s a formal recognition that we are now fully competent Krishna devotees—now go out into the world and act that way.

“Anyone who knows this, that ‘I am the self,’ and acts in that way, he is a brahmin.” The Swami said that now that we are brahmins, we must clean up our acts. We didn’t know it yet, but I guess Swamiji knew there would be lots of Indian people in London watching us. He said we must be clean, well-mannered, and respectful of everyone. (Try telling this to a bunch of American kids, more used to “Hey, man!” than “My dear sir.”) Swami several times says that his own spiritual master, Bhaktisiddhanta Swami, had sent only swamis to England back in the ’30s, and that they were largely unsuccessful. Now the Swami was sending grihasthas, married couples, to do what the swamis could not. For this the Swami was being criticized by his godbrothers back in India: Oh, he is mixing with women, he is contaminating brahminical standards. The Swami would tell us, “When the elephants pass, the dogs will bark.”

Day after day we practiced kirtan, taking turns leading the public chanting, getting over our fear of crowds, and gaining confidence. We had seen only the occasional Indian at the San Francisco temple, but here in Montreal there was a substantial Indian congregation, and we started to understand their sensitivities, their over-polite chatter, and their roundabout way of getting to a point.

The Swami met with his London crew to examine our finances. He thought we didn’t have enough money to make a good impression, and offered to loan us about $1500. I gave him the name and details for Chartered Bank, the London bank recommended to us by Peggy Caserta s tenant, David Cocking.

Saraswati did well in Montreal in those far-from-private conditions. We never heard a peep from her, as Malati kept her at the breast under her sari. The Swami joked, “I have seen that baby Saraswati is always eating.” I was a little worried that the baby would not travel well, maybe cause everyone grief on this long journey, but thought, well, that’s Malati’s problem: “Just keep the kid quiet, OK?” Swami writes to Krishna devi: “I have seen Malati is nursing her child so nicely that she attended my meeting every day and the child was playing and she never cried.”

We had noticed that the Indian people were always trying to touch the Swamis feet and that he didn’t like this very much. Someone told us that if you touch the feet of a saintly person you get rid of ten thousand lifetimes of sinful activities. Malati and Janaki asked Govinda dasi to ask Swamiji if it would be all right if we touched his feet before we left for London.

On the day of our departure for New York, the Swami met with us at his apartment. We re sitting there, and suddenly Swamiji sticks his legs out. We get the message. One by one, ladies first, we approach the Swami and put our heads on his feet. And he touches and rubs each of us on the backs of our heads and necks—it feels so good! the rush and shivers of electric well-being—we sit back dizzy with pleasure.

Yamuna was so bold that she grabbed his feet. Tears streamed from her eyes. People are always talking about getting zapped by a guru—you know, instant enlightenment—but with that touch to the back of the head we all felt that Swamiji had truly blessed us, and that our mission would be successful without a doubt.

And then the Swami asked Malati to hand Saraswati to him. He grabbed her little swaddled body gently in one hand and swung her to his chest, looped his garland around them both, and with such obvious love he said, in his sometimes cheeky way, “Now they will say, ‘What kind of sannyasi is he?”’

Malati: I don’t think, there was any photo of that. It was a very heartfelt departure. We were thinking, even. Will we see you again? We had one-way tickets. So we were intent on drinking in that vision of Swamiji behind that little desk, knowing we might not see him again, maybe ever.

And indeed, this was the last time we saw Swamiji’s face for almost exactly one year.

**\* \* \***

On August 19, we flew to New York City and took taxis to 26 Second Avenue, the birthplace of the Swami’s pastimes in America. The problem was the heat wave; it was so hot and sticky on the Lower East Side that it was like moving underwater. Brahmananda acted the perfect host. This legendary, tough commander of so many early struggles was for real—and a sweetheart under his bluster. He showed us the Swami’s apartment, the sweltering temple where it had all started, and we got a feel for what Mukunda and others had been describing. Out of this incessant street fight in the heaviest city on earth, somehow the Swami had established Fort Krishna and slowly pushed back the relentless armies of Maya. Brahmananda chaired meetings with the other New York leaders—Rupanuga, Purushottam, and Rayarama (whom we knew from SF), and they offered us unlimited support. Brahmananda informed us that he had just that day received a telegram from the Swami in Montreal: “Please inform Shyamasundar that I have sent yesterday afternoon $1655.00 Dollars to the Charter Bank in London as desired by him.” We felt we had strong anchors on this side of the pond.

We were scheduled to leave from LaGuardia on August 27 at 11 p.m. on a Loftleidir Icelandic Airways DC-8, arriving at Reykjavik at 10:30 the next morning. We would then leave Reykjavik at 11:30 a.m., arriving in Luxembourg at 5 p.m. on the 28 th. Cost of a one-way ticket from NYC to Luxembourg was $52.50, but with taxes and travel agent fees, the price went up to $65 per person.

Malati and I stayed in Adwaita and Balai's apartment, sweating and lying prone near an open window with the fan on high, listening to our baby cry, traffic and sirens below, pondering the leap we were about to take. And then Adwaita rushed into his apartment with a package in his sticky hand: “This came for you today, at the temple.”

My passport. We were good to go.

## London

### 1

Malati and I and baby Saraswati staggered off the train into the morning rush at Victoria Station. Thousands of well-dressed Londoners were milling around us in their three-piece suits and miniskirts, along with an occasional longhaired hippie, brushing by us as if we didn’t exist.

I ask a guy in a bowler hat, clutching a briefcase, “Excuse me, sir, is there a drugstore nearby?” He looks at me, but doesn’t see me.

“Pardon me, madam,” Malati says to an older housewife type in a dowdy hat with feathers, “can you please direct us to a drugstore?” The woman, perhaps remembering a Yank accent from twenty-five years ago when our GIs helped save this precious island, says, “Wot?”

Malati explains, “I need diapers for the baby.”

“Oh, you must mean nappies. You need the chemist. Yes, there’s a Boots just ovah theah.”

It was August 30, 1968, Radhasthami, Radha’s birthday, our first moments in London. “And it’s my birthday today too,” Malati says. (Yipes, I forgot!)

We’d been on the ground for forty hours, and Saraswati had been pooping a lot.

Gurudas, Yamuna, Mukunda, Janaki, Malati, the baby, and I had arrived at the airport in Luxembourg on the evening of August 28. We had taken taxis from there to the Hauptbahnhof, where we’d sat many hours in the Buffet de la Gare until the Siegels and Grants boarded their train to Amsterdam. Then Malati and I caught the overnight express via Calais and Dover to London. There were no more clean diapers, and the situation smelled.

British immigration rules were very strict in 1968. The colonies were breaking up and savage hordes were knocking at Britain’s door, putting tremendous strain on the nations socialist coffers. If you were a tourist with a one-way ticket, you had to prove you had sufficient funds to stay in the UK and then get yourself home. We had about six hundred dollars for the six of us—the minimum requirement for one couple, so we made a plan: Malati and I would enter England first, then wire the six hundred to the next couple, who would wait in Amsterdam. They would enter the UK, then wire the money to the third couple.

Another problem arose that summer that affected our plan to enter Britain. In July and August, a big stink had been raised in the UK about Scientology. Scientology was a new-age religion founded by a guy named L. Ron Hubbard. London newspapers had whipped up a huge controversy about how Scientology was destroying British youth. The papers also accused the cult’s leaders of misappropriation of funds and other scandalous behavior. As a result, the July 28, 1968, edition of the Sunday Mirror ran an article called, “Scientology, Sex, Hypnotism and Security Checks”:

Minister of Health, Mr. Kenneth Robinson, last week in the House of Commons finally announced steps to curb the activities of the Scientologists in Britain... These curbs are that Scientology centers will no longer be accepted as educational establishments; foreigners will not be allowed to come in as Scientology students.”

The last thing they wanted in the UK at that time was a bunch of penniless foreigners starting a new religion.

At British customs, our tour-of-British-castles ploy seemed to work for “What’s the purpose of your visit?” And we had a letter Mr. James Kohr (Jayananda) certifying that Malati and I were “licensed teachers” at a well-known academy in America.

“‘ISKCON?’ Wot’s that, then?”

“Oh, it’s a well-known school for humanitarian studies.” “Hmmmm.”

We showed the guy our traveler’s checks—and just then Saraswati started to poop and scream. Flustered, the customs agent stamped our passports and said, “Welcome to the United Kingdom. Next!”

We bought the nappies and an overpriced perambulator (“Baby buggy? Oh, you must mean a pram”) at Boots, the “chemist,” so our immediate problems were solved. But where to go next in this vast metropolis? I remembered from previous visits that there were a lot of bedsits, cheap bed-and-breakfast rooms, in Earls Court, not far from Victoria Station. We rented a room for £3 ($7.50) a day, and Malati quickly crashed with the baby. It was a Friday, so I had to rush over to American Express at 1 Haymarket Street, to change our travelers checks and wire the money back to Amsterdam. Gurudas and Yamuna would come over next, so I sent the wire c/o Roger Siegel.

The next day, Saturday, we started looking through want ads for a place where three couples could live together. Not so easy in post-war, bombed-out London, especially on our budget. We looked at a flat in Hampstead Heath, but it was too small and expensive. Walking back to the tube station, we passed a tobacconist shop that had small, handwritten ads tacked to a bulletin board outside. One said, “For Sale: 1948 Fordson Poplar 4-Cyl. Pickup, £65/-,” and had a photo of a red, toylike truck, and a phone number. Wow, we hadn’t seen a pickup truck in this town yet—at $163, it looked like a good buy to a country boy from Oregon. We didn’t even haggle with the guy, just bought the little four-banger on the spot. We now had wheels.

“Watch out! You’re on the wrong side of the road again,” Malati said over and over. Luckily, traffic moved slower in England than it did in the States. The clutch and floor shift was like a smaller version of my panel truck back home—even the manual throttle/ foot-starter setup was the same—but I had to do everything lefthanded, from the wrong side of the cab. The truck’s owner showed me how to use the four-foot foldable hand crank, kept under the front seat in case the vehicle wouldn’t start. That only happened once on our way back to Earls Court, when a bobby raised his hand for me to stop at an intersection and I hit the brakes without first hitting the clutch and the engine died. The carburetor flooded, so the motor wouldn’t start with the key. I walked around to the front of the Fordson, crank in hand, stuck it in the slot below the grill, and cranked while Malati sat inside and worked the controls. Crank, crank, over and over. It wouldn’t start. The bobby came over, and when I told him I’d just bought this thing (in American twang), he took the crank, motioned me to get behind the wheel, and cranked that old girl and she started right up. The Swami used to say, “There are only two places in the world where a gentleman can live: India and England.”

We had the new disposable nappies, the pram, the truck. Now we needed a roof over our heads. Scanning newspaper ads, we stood in adjoining royal-red phone booths and made calls, trying to sort shillings from sixpence from ha’penny coins. One guy out in Brixton said he had an upstairs flat for rent, three bedrooms, living room, and a bawth, and gave his address as 80 ’erne ’ill.

“Say again please?”

“’erne ’ill.”

“Can you spell that, please?”

“H-E-R-N-E H-I-L-L-number 80 ’erne ’ill, SE 24.”

We buy a “London A-Z” book of roadmaps and “motor” over there.

London has evolved over centuries into a huge, circular city, with Trafalgar Square and Piccadilly Circus roughly at the hub. Roads go out like spokes on a wheel, with ring roads encircling, so it was pretty easy to drive around, especially if you had a “London A-Z” book. Earls Court to Herne Hill is about eight miles—and maybe a forty-minute drive.

An Indian man answers the door (sounded like a Brit on the phone) and was as surprised as we were when Malati and I said “Hare Krishna!” in unison. Mr. Kohli wanted twelve pounds a week for the second-floor flat, and was astonished to hear that we were missionaries for the Hindu faith, here in London to start a new Krishna center. We told him that two more couples were coming in a few days. The place looked perfect: three bedrooms around a central living room. It was a bit far from central London, but he said we were near main roads and could easily travel by “tube,” the underground train. No shower, but a big, cast-iron bathtub. Kohli and his wife lived downstairs. (“What’s that awful smell?” I whisper to Malati. “Turmeric,” she says. “They use it for cooking”)

Mr. Kohli showed us how to plug shillings into meters to buy electricity and hot water, Then we fetched our stuff from Earls Court and moved in that day, Sunday, September 1,1968.

Swamiji had told us to write frequently—he wanted to be kept up to date on everything we did in London—so first thing, I buy some 9-penny (“9d”) blue aerograms at the tobacconists down the road and write a letter to the Swami: Krishna has helped us, we have a place—the full report so far. Were not really sure where the Swami is right now—he was on the verge of getting his US visa sorted out when we were leaving Montreal—so I send the letter to New York. We are far away from Swamiji again, and transatlantic calls are expensive, so communication will be by letter. An airmail letter from the UK to the USA takes about a week.

On Monday morning we drive down to the Chartered Bank, at 2 Regent Street, to see if the $1655 the Swami wired from Montreal has arrived yet. The manager, a distinguished-looking man named Mr. Balfour said, “No, nothing has come for you.” We explained our situation: that we re tourists, that we re relying on those funds for our travels to various British castles, the money should certainly be here by now, please help us sort this out. Balfour takes a liking to us Yanks; he’s very upper-crust British—sports a monocle, a carnation in his lapel—and it’s like we’re watching an old Ronald

Colman movie: hearing the King’s English and the quick wit and understated manners of aristocracy. “Aohh, I say,” he says. But he knows we’re up to something, you can just tell. We give him Kohli's phone number, and he promises to call us “straightaway” when the money comes. Toodle-oo.

We walk over to American Express, a few blocks away. There’s a telegram waiting from Gurudas: “Arriving Tuesday 3rd Victoria Station Hoek Van Holland Harwich Train 11 PM.”

The next night we hold our breath, standing on the platform, wondering if they’ll make it through customs—and there they are! Yamuna and Gurudas, running to embrace. Gurudas sits in the cab with me and we catch up; the girls are huddled in back under blankets, laughing through midnight London. “Elephant and Castle is the name of a crossroads?' Gurudas asks.

Gurudas had our six hundred dollars in Dutch guilders now, so we decided to go down to Chartered Bank on Wednesday morning, all of us, to check on Swamiji’s transfer, change money, open a bank account, then wire most of the money over to Mukunda. There were only two seats in my truck, so we decided to try out the train system—we walk down the hill about half a mile, catch a train at Herne Hill station, go one stop north to Brixton, switch to the underground (Victoria Line), go five stops to Green Park, switch to the Piccadilly Line (direction, Cockfosters), then one stop east to Piccadilly Circus. The bank is a five-minute walk from Piccadilly.

Mr. Balfour greets us like old friends. “No, nothing has arrived yet from Montreal,” he says. We hand him a wad of guilders to open a checking account. This seems to take a very long time, but the “cheques” look fantastic with the coat of arms, lions rampant, incorporated by Royal Charter in 1853. As Balfour hands me the cheque book, I say to him, “Now we’d like to leave £10/- in the account and wire the balance, £230/-, to Michael A. Grant, in Amsterdam.” Aha! One eyebrow goes up—the other still clinches a monocle—and a sly grin grows below his rakish mustache. He really knows were up to something now. (A few months later, when we could openly walk into Chartered Bank in shaved heads and dhotis, I think Balfour felt proud to have been part of our fledgling conspiracy,)

Mukunda and Janaki arrived three days later, Mukunda cradling his precious mridanga (I wonder how he explained that at customs?). “They asked us if we were Scientologists,” Janaki said.

We are Swamiji’s London team, together again at last.

**\* \* \***

The girls quickly got us organized at the erne ’ill flat, assigning rooms to each couple and making arrangements to cook in the Kohlis’ kitchen downstairs. We would come together in the living room, where the girls set up a little altar with small Jagannath deities. These were the original figurines Malati had found at Cost Plus Imports in San Francisco, and which had triggered worship of Lord Jagannath in the Western world. We continued with our San Francisco sadhana program of rising early, chanting rounds, kirtan, and reading from the First Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam. The six of us hadn’t lived together since Lucky Street almost two years ago, and a lot had happened since then. We were all strong personalities, but the rough edges of communal life were quickly smoothed as we settled into our unified mission: to create an outstanding Krishna conscious center in London for Bhaktivedanta Swami.

While Malati and I had been waiting for the other couples to arrive, I had scoured the local underground newspapers to find out what was happening in the London hippie scene and where. The main underground paper, sort of like our Oracle back in SF, seemed to be the International Times (IT), headquartered on Betterton Street in Covent Garden, downtown London. I showed a copy of the IT to the others, and we agreed that this newspaper would be our best first target. We’ll go there tomorrow. Before we’d left Montreal, the Swami had given us Xerox copies from his growing collection of news clippings, so we made up a press kit composed of articles about КС and the Swami, with photos, letters of commendation, and the like and began to take it with us wherever we went.

London was an exciting new world, so at first we went places together, two in the cab of the truck, four in the back. It was late summer, so the weather was pretty mild. We bundled up and sailed downtown, taking the scenic route past Big Ben, the Houses of Parliament, Westminster Abbey, Buckingham Palace (“That’s where the queen lives!”), and Trafalgar Square. It’s all just so... quaint. When we got to Betterton Street, Peter Stansill, Graham Keene, and other IT staff were happy to meet us. They were super busy preparing issue #40 when we arrived. Barry Miles was there too. He was the guy who had interviewed George Harrison back in May 1967, and he was friends with Allen Ginsberg. So everyone seemed to know about the Swami and his movement, and they welcomed us as a spiritual addition to the emerging hippie scene. Peter was especially impressed by Yamuna’s calligraphy on some of our sample flyers, and he proposed an idea: “Why don’t you draw mantras in Sanskrit for our next IT cover? The deadline is in three days. Can you do it?” Yamuna agreed that she’d hustle and get the job done on time.

Miles took us around the corner to a five-story building on Drury Lane called The Arts Lab, which was apparently the new “centre” of hippie activity in London. He explained that the London scene was different from the San Francisco scene: London’s hippies were spread out, not clustered in one neighborhood like in Haight-Ashbury. The high-class “mods” like Mick Jagger and Twiggy hung out in the World’s End district on King’s Road in Chelsea and shopped on Carnaby Street, which had become the global trendsetter for youth fashion. There was a small enclave of working-class hippies living in Portobello Road in West London. But for the most part, hippies were dispersed all over this huge city. Hippies would meet at central places like the Arts Lab or at the Roundhouse in Chalk Farm for rock concerts. Barry introduced us to Jim Haynes and Jack Moore, the two Americans who ran the Arts Lab. Jim said, “I’ve been to the Second

Avenue temple, and I love your Swami. Can you guys have kirtans down here a few nights a week?” Yes! We agreed on two nights a week, 7 p.m., Wednesdays and Fridays, at the Arts Lab.

Our first day together in London and we were on a roll! We decided to hit Trafalgar Square and try out some public sankirtan, see what happens. We started off slow, timid before the crowds of busy Londoners and gawking tourists at the base of Nelsons Column, but when Gurudas gave full off-key voice to the Hare Krishna mantra, it broke the ice and we all got into it, dancing around and having fun with our first public appearance. Aside from the white tilak on our foreheads and the big red beads around our necks, we didn’t look that much different from anybody else—and we had no printed information to pass out, so the publics response was, “These people are crazy!” A bobby appeared and politely shooed us out of there. We drove around looking for a better place. We landed in a small park called Soho Square, away from the main streets. The police didn’t hassle us there, but it started to rain heavily, so we went home to Herne Hill, everyone soaking wet but jubilant about our first day in London together.

We spent the rest of the day talking about what had happened, ways to improve our act, and where to go from here.

First thing: we don’t stand out in this huge city. We need to look different, to make a statement with our appearance. The women had saris, but the men... uh oh, the dreaded business of shaving our heads and wearing dhotis was brought up. You know, guys, if we really want to stand out, we’ve got to do this. OK, if you’ll do it, I’ll do it.

I was probably the most reluctant to shave my head. Proud of my long, curly hair, I imagined I looked like Bob Dylan on his Blonde on Blonde album cover. Gurudas had the most brass—he volunteered to go first. Up there in the main room at Herne Hill, we all watched as Malati scissor-cut and then shaved his head with a straight razor to a shining white with little bloody nicks, leaving a sikha on top. You could see all the contours of his skull. Ugh. Mukunda went next, and I was last. We stood around and howled with laughter at how dumb we looked, but hey, no way they will miss us now. The girls had a couple of saris each and already knew how to wear them, and Mrs. Kohli was downstairs to show them little stuff about blouses and such, but none of us had any idea about dhotis—how long they should be or how to tie them to our bodies. The girls would go out tomorrow to buy cloth for them.

Second thing we discussed: the band. 1968 London was the music center of the world. The Beatles, Rolling Stones, the Who, Cream, Moody Blues—these were the British voices of global pop culture. Even Jimi Hendrix often hung out in England. We knew after that first day in London that if we were to make any sort of impact spreading the Swami’s message in the UK, we had to become a band. Just look at any 1968 IT newspaper and you’ll see on every page articles, announcements, and advertisements for dozens of local bands. London was a music scene.

Reel-to-reel tape recorders were a hi-tech gizmo in 1968. Big and clunky, and the Germans made the best models. Yamuna and Gurudas had bought a Grundig TK247 reel-to-reel tape recorder at a tax-free shop in Luxembourg and hauled it with them to London. Yamuna had some tapes of the Swami singing prayers and mantras, and she was our expert in Sanskrit pronunciation and melodies. Mukunda was a professional musician, so we decided that Yamuna and Mukunda would lead us daily in band practice. We would learn the words and create catchy melodies to several prayers and mantras and build a repertoire.

Third thing we discussed: let’s get right to work on this cover for the International Times and maybe write some articles for it to let everyone know we’re in town.

Fourth: were going to need an official permit to have street sankirtan. Mukunda was our man for this kind of thing, and he said he would start looking into it. He will also pursue getting ISKCON legalized as a charitable institution in the UK. For that, he needed proper apparel, so Janaki would shop for a bowler hat, brolly (umbrella), overcoat, and a nice scarf.

Fifth: we needed to buy some electric hot plates so the girls can cook upstairs, minimizing use of the kitchen downstairs, where the Kohlis eat meat, fish, and eggs.

We missed Swamiji terribly, even though it had been less than a month since we’d left him. But he had said that he would be always with us, and we felt his presence. We could easily imagine him in the room, laughing at our escapades, like Gurudas doing his Balfour imitation: “That’s a bit of a sticky wicket, eh, Mr. Das?” We sensed the Swami listening to our reports and guiding us with his wisdom. We saw in our minds his mobile facial features: mock-serious while he’s forming a joke, head-back hilarity at the punch line—then back to deep concern with Yamuna’s sore throat or baby Saraswati’s colic.

On September 20, both Mukunda and Gurudas got letters from the Swami. We crowded around, soaking in every word, as they read them aloud. Swamiji notes that there are nearly half a million Indians in London and that we should gain their cooperation. He also notes that there may be many old churches “not being properly used,” and we should try to get one: people will be attracted by nice temple arrangements. He goes on to inquire if the $1,655 has arrived (not yet) and, to inspire us with transcendental competition, he informs us that new ISKCON temples have opened in West Virginia, Hawaii, Seattle, and Vancouver. (Hint: Why you have not yet got anything?)

**\* \* \***

The nicks in our skulls started to heal, and we rubbed mineral oil into the skin on our heads to make them shine. The girls bought lengths of cotton and dyed them yellow. Yellow had become the de facto color for householders since the early days in San Francisco, when Kirtanananda had tried to dye his dhotis saffron with the saffron spice used in Indian cooking. The dhotis had come out yellow instead, so the Swami had told him to give them to the householders, because “they can wear yellow.” The girls also bought us three boys snappy matching turtleneck sweaters in orange—the closest they could find to yellow.

So we had these long yellow bed sheets. But how to tie them on? We tried combinations of folds and knots and tucks and laughed till tears came. Finally, Mr. Kohli advised us, and we stood before the ladies in our daffodil garb. If these bald-headed clowns in canary-yellow robes don’t catch peoples’ eyes, what will?
