Add the brightly colored Nehru jackets we had brought from Gargamuni’s shop in SF, the orange sweaters, the big red beads, and the white tilak smeared well up onto our bald shining heads—and who are these guys?

We started going to the Arts Lab on Wednesday and Friday nights. The place was dark, with many different events going on simultaneously in the many rooms, but gradually more and more young hippies started to join in our kirtans and listen to our brief and simple lectures.

**\* \* \***

“OK, the truck’s running, let’s get this outfit loaded up!”

Mukunda said, “Just a second, Shyamasundar. Lemme get some more plastic around this mridanga—it’s raining pretty hard.”

Yamuna said, “Here Goo-Goo, you carry the prasadam. Be careful! Hold it flat, it’ll spill.”

Janaki said, “Wait a sec, I forgot my shawl—it’s really freezing in the back of that truck—you comfy blokes in front with your heater...”

One by one we pile into the tiny truck. Sometimes it starts with the key, sometimes it doesn’t—in which case, I glide down the hill a bit and jerk-start it. On really cold nights, someone (usually Gurudas) has to take the hand crank, insert it into a hole in the front of the truck, engage it, and while I turn the key and pump the gas—in neutral, one foot on the brake—he turns the crank hard to his right: one, two, cough, three times—clank'. It backfires, and the steel rod whacks his hand hard. Yee-ouch! “One more time,

Gurudas. You can do it!” Finally, the bloody thing starts, and we roll off toward London, four bodies packed in back with food, garlands, instruments, and literature, if we have any. I drive. Malati (Saraswati bundled in her lap) sits in a bucket seat up front. The heater is on full blast, windshield wipers going flappity-flap, as we crawl up Herne Hill, coast down to Walworth Road, motor all the way to the Elephant and Castle roundabout, go north across the Thames at Waterloo Bridge, through Aldwych, turn left on narrow cobbled Drury Lane, past the Royal Opera House, and in less than an hour we re there: The Arts Lab, 182, Drury Lane.

Drury Lane, where Shakespeare had lived, is in the heart of. Covent Garden. This is medieval London, with cobblestone alleyways—small buildings packed tight, gargoyles, fountains, and little through traffic. Now it was London’s wholesale fruit, vegetable, and flower market. Almost every building was a warehouse, abandoned at night but opened at dawn to a brisk wholesale trade-dray horses pulling wagons, men pushing wheeled carts heaped with cauliflowers.

As we pass through Covent Garden, it doesn’t take much on a dark autumn night to imagine Jack the Ripper, Murders of the Rue Morgue, Jekyll & Hyde, and Sherlock Holmes. Widely separated streetlights throw dim pools of light on tavern shingles with coats of arms and names like Coach & Horses, The Globe, and the Cittie of Yorke. Only thing missing is the London fog, which they say doesn’t happen now since they’ve banned coal fires.

I drop everyone at the Arts Lab door and go off to find a parking spot—easy, streets are empty this time of night. I enter the bright hallway—Jim Haynes points to the left and yells, “You’re upstairs tonight!” I pull apart dark velour curtains and see the Krishnas are already spreading out, setting up for kirtan—kind of dark in here—I hope Gurudas or one of the girls speaks tonight, I’m not so good at this. Hey, there’s Tim and Dick and his girlfriend (what’s-her-name). This is the third time they’ve come to chant with us. It’s great. We’re attracting some English kids.

Yamuna made the deadline with her mantra calligraphy page, and on September 20, 1968, just three weeks after we’d arrived in the UK, 50,000 people saw the cover of IT #40, which looked fantastic: The maha-mantra centered on the 11" x 14" cover in bold, l"-high purple Devanagari script, with rows of mantras scrolled sideways down both sides, a big yellow diamond behind it all and bright yellow borders. In bold type at bottom right was “COVER BY YAMUNA DASI (see page 17).”

Page 17 of that issue carried this explanation:

MANTRAM MEANINGS:

These mantrams on the cover are transcendental sound vibrations.

Transcending what?

The Wheel of material life i.e. birth, death, disease and old age.

How? Chanting these mantram, thus purifying our senses, which were originally Krishna-conscious but are now covered over by the illusionary energy of material nature.

A mantra is made up of a glorifying word (generic) and one or more seed words, a name of the Lord (specific). For example, Hare (generic), meaning glory to the energy potency of KRISHNA—the Lord (specific seed word), meaning the all-attractive.

The generic words glorify the name, fame, passtimes and activities of the Lord. Since God or Krishna is so kind, he has many names and we of all nations, countries and villages have taken to at least one. Because the sun is called “sol” in Spanish and “sun” in English doesn’t mean it is a different sun. Similarly, as there are many names of God it is the same all-pervading supreme personality for all.

Following a detailed description of English word equivalents and meanings for the Hare Krishna mantra, and four other mantras, Yamuna wrote:

Please come and join us at the Arts Lab at 7 pm Wednesday and Friday nights, where we chant and dance in ecstasy. When we chant we are at once on the spiritual plane. Come and bring your friends. HARE KRISHNA.

We soon learned how to move around London on double-decker buses and the tube and started separating to perform our individual services. The girls usually stayed behind at Herne Hill to do what they had to do (Malati had a three-month-old baby), and on most days the three men went into town together, went to the IT or to hustle some new lead—or I’d drop Gurudas or Mukunda off somewhere, and we’d meet up later at the house. I bought some 2" x 2" poles at the timber merchant, got a gray-green canvas tarp from the ironmonger, and fashioned a canopy over the bed of the pickup—at least the precious passengers and cargo wouldn’t get so wet now. Some days we all went over to Hyde Park Corner to have sankirtan. We had been told that Hyde Park Corner was the one place in the Kingdom that every citizen was free to say or do anything they liked.

My redwood shipment from California arrived at King George V docks, and Tim, one of the new English boys from the Arts Lab, helped me haul it back and stack it behind the Kohlis’ house.

Malati: Practically speaking, our husbands would disappear every day and come back late to report on progress. Sometimes, if they were gone, we women took the train somewhere. At that point we were taking care of the house, the baby, and the cooking, and then we had programs. Engagements, we called them. These became increasingly frequent—almost every night. For us ladies, what I remember most is waiting for our men to come home and tell us what was happening and what was going to happen that night.

Mr. Kohli arranged an engagement for us at the Hindu Centre in Golders Green, a North London suburb. This was the first time we interacted with a crowd of Indian people. Most of them were new to the UK themselves, “refugees” from recent upheavals in Kenya and Uganda. Many of the Indian families were already more British than the British—some had even anglicized their last names, and Shah had become Shaw, Kapoor, Cooper. But most retained some allegiance to their Hindu gods and were pious at heart. We became their instant pets and were invited night after night to their homes for ten-course spicy Indian dinners and kirtan and a short talk: this is our mission here in London. Please help us. We’d roll back to Herne Hill completely stuffed, groaning, “Why did we do this again?”

But the Indian community donated small amounts of money, fed us, and otherwise helped a lot. And I took small jobs repairing their bathrooms, building walls and additions to their homes, paid at the rate of ten shillings an hour. One pound sterling equaled, roughly, $2.50 American. Twenty shillings equaled a pound, so I was getting paid about $1.25 an hour. (There were twelve pennies to the shilling; a crown was five shillings... Took us a while to sort this out.)

And some of the Indians we met really lifted our comedic spirits. Prem Sayal became our good friend, and we loved him very much. Prem was a flamboyant bhajan singer, who would glissade his fingers up and down the harmonium keyboard, throw his head back, black hair flying, eyes closed, and hit a high “Krishnaaaaa!” then drop to his back on the floor, swooning in feigned ecstasy. About once a day I’d say, “Hey, Gurudas, do Prem Sayal!” Gurudas would grab his imaginary harmonium, roll his eyes back, yell “Krishna!” and fall backward while we howled with laughter.

Mukunda and I met a guy who promoted rock dances, and we went out to his pad in Notting Hill Gate. We told him about the Mantra Rock dance we’d held in San Francisco, and that we were now in London, a band of professional musicians who led public chanting using instruments like drums and cymbals. He went for it and booked our first big gig at the Roundhouse for Friday, October 4,1968. Located at Chalk Farm in Camden town, the Roundhouse was an old train switching house, where they used to run the locomotives inside and spin them around. Now it had become Londons equivalent to San Franciscos Fillmore or Avalon Ballroom, and thousands of hippies gathered there on weekends to hear the latest bands.

Our band didn’t have a name yet, so the Roundhouse advertisement in IT’s issue #41 described our act as “mantra chanting” in small type below headliners “The Fugs” and a band called “Spooky Tooth.” The Fugs, from New York City, had popularized the Hare Krishna mantra in 1968, with a big-selling album called Tenderness Junction. Their 3:19-minute version of the mantra, with harmonium and sitar, presented as sort of a spoof, was in fact beautifully done. Mukunda and Gurudas knew the Fugs’s leader, Tuli Kupferberg, from their Lower East Side days.

We were pretty nervous—obviously amateurs—but we led off the show with kirtan, and hundreds of hippies chanted Hare Krishna in that huge dark space. Great acoustics. It went pretty well. We learned a lot. First thing: we needed a name. That was pretty easy, and by unanimous vote we became “The Radha Krishna Temple.” And we needed more instruments. And we should move around more on stage, yell more “Haribols!” We needed more props, like incense, big pictures of Krishna and the Swami, to juice up our act.

**\* \* \***

The October 4 issue of IT, #41, included on page 18, in its “Dear IT” letters to the editor, a letter from Michael Eaves of 555a Finchley Road, N.W.3, with reference to Yamuna’s calligraphy on the cover of issue #40:

And the Sanscrit words on the front page, sure brilliant, ‘Hare Krishna’ means glory to Krishna the popular God, an incarnated avatar of Vishnu the preserver. But please, please a million times, Hinduism is a complex and extensive subject. ‘Bring your friends—we chant and dance at the Arts Lab’—admission only 2s/6d for instant mysticism—I feel sick, how would any of the many educated Hindus in this country or the 225 million in India feel about this nauseating travesty some naive stupid charlatan has concocted?

Fighting words! I sit down at Herne Hill and write a reply, which IT prints in issue #42 (October 18, 1968):

Dear IT,

I have read Michael Eaves’ letter in IT of October 4. He states that Krishna is ‘an incarnated avatar of Vishnu.’ The Vedic science of God is indeed complex, but its first teaching, as we learn from Krishna Himself in Bhagavad-gita, ch. 10, viii and xxi, is that “I am the source of everything: from Me the entire creation flows... Of the Adityas I am Vishnu.” In other words, Vishnu and all other demi-gods are in fact emanations FROM Krishna.

We too are concerned about “concocted travesties” and “instant mysticism” being perpetrated by so-called Yogis. The Vedas prescribe two tests for a bonafide spiritual master, first, that he be able to trace his disciplic succession back through time to Krishna, or God, himself, and second, that he preaches only from authorized scripture. Swami A.C. Bhaktivedanta, who has popularized this Hare Krishna Mantra in the western world, meets both of the requirements in full. Swamiji is a transparent medium, through whom we discern the Absolute truth, unchanged, as it is revealed 240 million years ago by Krishna Himself, to Brahma, who spoke it to Narada, who repeated it to Vyasa, and so on, VERBATIM, down through time to Sri Srimad Bhaktivedanta Goswami Maharaj, who, as the living representative of this Brahma

Sampradaya, rather than “some naive stupid charlatan,” is in fact an Embodiment of Truth itself.

We are seeking a permanent temple site somewhere in central London to be open to everyone, free, free, free, 24 hours a day, for chanting and dancing, feasting, and lecturing from Bhagavad-gita. Swamiji will come as soon as we are situated. We are urging ANYONE who is sincerely interested in spiritual advancement to chant Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare, Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare, either alone, with friends, or with us at the Arts Lab on Wednesday and Friday evenings, or at our home, 80 Herne Hill, S.E.24, any Monday at 7 pm.

Your servant in the service of Lord Sri Krishna, Shyamasundar das Adhikary, phone 274-2970

In this same issue there was another letter to the editor, again complaining about Yamuna’s Sanskrit cover—this one from a guy in India:

The Editor,

Who ever (nom-de-plume “Devi Dasi”) did your Sept 20-Oct 3 issue cover, you can be assured they were not a deva-nagare” scrip-writing Hindu. The errors were so profuse and glaring that it makes LAMBIKIKI YOGA, the Yoga of Mantram writing, into some kind of kindergarten exercise. As a Yogi I protest! Another cover please, this time by someone who can use a quill and knows enough that all mantras must be proceeded by the “PRANAVA” invocation “OM” or "AUM" or else they are ineffective. Surely the YAMUNA DEVl DASI’s are already consigned to the metaphysical JUMNA (River in Lower Consciousness) where she is a Devadasu (Temple prostitute). Yours in sincere protest,

SWAMI GITANANANDA, KG, OSJ,

Acharya (Guru) of Yoga Jivana Satsangh, Mahant (Bishop) of the Brighu Order.

12, Rampart Row, Bombay 1, India

Whoa! More fighting words. This time Mukunda takes up his sword and comes to the rescue, as he “humbly submits the following information” in IT’s issue #43:

Yamuna Devi Dasi, the calligrapher, has to her credit many respectable works demonstrating her expert ability in scripts that range all the way from Rotunda to Chinese, not to speak of Devanagari, the Sanskrit alphabet. Her script on the cover is an intentional variation or characterization if you will, of Devanagari, with the specific aim of captivating the readers attention, and drawing him to the explanation page, where the Sanskrit is accurately transcribed into English letters.

After a long, six-paragraph explanation of transcendental sound vibration and aural reception—“the medyum iznhat tthe mesij’ ”-and using scriptural evidence, Mukunda really nails this guy to the wall:

As regards your final statement in which you have utilized an International publication to label my good sister as a “Devadasu (temple prostitute),” I can only request you to consider the words of Lord Krishna in the Bhagavad-gita, XII, 13 & 14, “One who is not envious, but is a kindly friend to all creatures, who does not think himself a proprietor, who is free from false ego, and equal both in happiness and distress, always satisfied, and engaged in devotional service with determination, and who is compact in mind and intelligence with Me, he is very dear to Me.”

Mukunda, you silver-tongued smoothie.

**\* \* \***

We keep going over to Chartered Bank to check on the Swami’s money transfer.

“Howdy, Mr. Balfour. How are ya doin’?”

“Good day to you, sir.”

“You can call me Sam.”

“No, Mr. Speerstra will be quite all right.”

These Englishmen—so hard to get to know.

The $1,655 arrived, finally, in the first week of October, six weeks after it had been sent. I got a letter from the Swami asking me to immediately transfer it back to New York—he needed it to pay for the first printing of his Bhagavad-gita. I wanted to ask Balfour, “What kind of a nut-job bank do you run here, anyway, you Limey scoundrel?” But I probably said something like, “Look here, Balfour, old chap, we wouldn’t want it to take six weeks again, would we?”

Chidananda writes to me from San Francisco and sadly reports that the elaborate deity throne that had taken me so many months to build was gone. Vandals, he said, broke into the garage at 685 Ashbury Street and took the whole thing away. Krishna gives and Krishna takes away. Never mind.

On October 13, Mukunda gets a letter from the Swami: “In the meantime, I have received one letter from Ginsberg, and he writes to say that he has written to some friend in England, to help you and one of his friends he names, Mr. Tom Driberg, M.P., and he has promised to help you, and sent letter to Mr. Ginsberg. So you will contact him. He is a prominent member of Parliament, Mr. Tom Driberg, M.P. Mr. Ginsberg says that he can help you in all matters of official and legal implications. So try to meet him as soon as possible, and do the needful.”

We’d been reading about Tom Driberg, MP, in the newspapers. He was born to aristocracy as the Baron of Bradwell and had become a radical and outspoken political figure—avowed communist and homosexual. But we really needed a contact within the government to help us with licenses and permits. Mukunda tracked down Tom Dribergs secretary, who told him that Tom was presently at St. Bartholomew’s Hospital recovering from an eye operation.

We went over to the hospital, Yamuna bearing a plate of Indian sweets, found his room number and rushed down the hall and into his room, anxious to meet this famous man. And there was this old, sick-looking guy in a white hospital gown, sitting in a chair, head tilted back like he’s asleep, but twitching all over. Yamuna shoves the plate of sweets in his face and loudly announces, “Mister Driberg, we have so much wanted to meet you!”

In his book Miracle On Second Avenue Mukunda describes what happened next: “The man’s eyes flew up and he began shaking uncontrollably as if he were having a seizure of some kind. He fumbled for a cord, which hung beside the bed, and repeatedly pushed a red button. We could hear the buzz of an alarm at the nurse’s station out in the hallway.”

Suddenly, a deep voice behind us said, “I'm Driberg.” We wheeled around and Tom, not as old as we’d thought, was lying in bed with an eyepatch on his face. Driberg was cool as a cucumber, un fazed by the squad of startled nurses who came in to help the freaked-out old man back into his bed. Driberg liked us; we were obviously free-thinking spirits like him. He was to play a major role in helping Mukunda cut through government red tape in the months to come. (After his death in 1976, Driberg was accused of being a KGB spy, a colleague of Kim Philby, but nothing was ever proven.)

**\* \* \***

The Beatles’ “Hey Jude” was the #1 single in the UK in September 1968. We were always reading British music magazines and underground newspapers, to see what was going on and how to take advantage of local events to spread Krishna consciousness. One day in early October, we saw in Melody Maker magazine the most amazing full-page ad. Beneath the caption, “This man has talent...” sat a young man wearing a hat, suit, and tie, playing a guitar and singing into a floor mike, a big drum on his back that kept time with his arm movements. He was a one-man band. Around him on the floor were a trombone, a trumpet, and a flugelhorn, along with a messy stack of “how-to” books for writers, artists, and musicians and a half-full wineglass at arm’s reach atop a boxy, reel-to-reel tape recorder. The text below the photo read:

One day he sang his songs to a tape recorder (borrowed from the man next door). In his neatest handwriting he wrote an explanatory note (giving his name and address) and, remembering to enclose a picture of himself, sent the tape, letter, and photograph to apple music, 94 Baker Street, London, W.l. If you were thinking of doing the same thingyourself—do it now! This man now owns a Bentley!

The Beatles were looking for new talent! We made a quick plan: we would record a demo tape and personally deliver it to Apple HQ. We spent three days practicing together as a band, until we had our sound pretty tight. Sitting in a circle around the Grundig reel-to-reel in the main room at Herne Hill, we recorded a rousing, five-min-ute “Hare Krishna Mantra” kirtan and a four-minute version of “Govinda jai jai.”

The next day, we piled in the truck and drove down to 3 Savile Row, the Beatles’ new headquarters in the poshest part of town, right behind the Chartered Bank on Regent Street. We were scared to death, but this was it: our big chance to break through to the Beatles. They were asking for talent, so here we were, live and in full costume. Chris O’Dell, a young American girl who worked in the Apple offices, describes what happened, in her book, Miss O’Dell:

Peter \[Asher\] and I were returning from lunch one fine autumn day... Parked at the curb right outside Apple’s front door was a clunky flatbed truck with wood posts and a red canopy. Flowers and bells decorated the top of the canopy, and psychedelic prints of an Indian guru-type guy playing a flute and backlit by a halo were stapled on the wooden posts. Half a dozen men and women dressed in long, flowing saffron robes stood on the sidewalk smiling at passersby.... Peter pulled in his head, turtle-like, his chin fell to his chest, and he kept his eyes fastened to the sidewalk as he scurried right past the truck and into the safety of Apple.

My reaction to the carnival-like scene outside the Apple office was the complete opposite of Peter’s. Fascinated by the colorful outfits, the white paint on their noses, the red dots on their foreheads, and the men’s shaved heads, I took my time walking past the truck.

Squirming in his chair, Peter opened his appointment book. “Someone named Sam is to drop off an audition tape. Why do these people need to meet with me? Who are these people?”

“Hello,” I said to the two men standing in the reception area, “I’m Chris O’Dell, Peter Asher’s personal assistant.”

“Hello,” said the man with the little blond ponytail in a very polite, businesslike tone, “I’m Shyamasundar das.”

“And I’m Mukunda das,” said the man with the little black ponytail.

“Well, how did it go?” I asked Peter after I’d escorted them back down the stairs and out the door.

“They were actually quite pleasant. They want to make a record... I think I’ll let George know about this meeting. He’ll know what to do.”

Little did we know, but the seed had been planted. Later that day, Chris took a note from Peter Asher describing our interview over to George Harrison at Abbey Road Studios, where the Beatles were working on their White Album, due for release in late November.

“Did you meet them?” George asked.

“Just briefly. They seemed really nice.”

I'd like to meet them. Maybe we can set up an appointment in the next few weeks.”

A couple of weeks later, we got a standard, boilerplate rejection letter from Peter Asher: thank you for sending your very nice demo tape. Unfortunately we are unable at this time... blah, blah, blah.

Never mind, we thought, this is just the first step. Lets keep pushing. We must meet George. Now that we knew Chris—a fellow American, after all—we planned a new strategy: we would show up at Apple on a regular basis, even if we didn’t get past the bobby at the front door, and drop off gifts for Chris O’Dell—an apple pie, flower garlands, a copy of Easy Journey to Other Planets... We also dropped off a toy red apple with legs, “Hare Krishna!” painted around the side, that you wound up and it danced. We were convinced that this was Krishna’s playful sport, and it was just a matter of time till we met George and all our dreams would come true.

We met the owners of the Yin-Yang macrobiotic restaurant, just off Bayswater Road, and they also invited us to have kirtan there one night a week, on Tuesdays from 7 to 9 p.m. (We got a good meal out of it, too.) So now both Arts Lab and Yin-Yang became our rehearsal stages. Mukunda and Yamuna composed the melodies together, and Yamuna researched the Sanskrit words, listened to Swamiji’s tapes, and wrote down the mantras so we could memorize them. Mukunda was the only one of us who could play the mridanga, and, as a concert pianist, he trained Yamuna on harmonium. Gurudas became an expert kartal player, making them sizzle just like the Swami did, but throwing in occasional jazz riffs. One set of kartals was enough for any band. Malati usually shook her tambourine, and Janaki clapped her hands, jumped, danced, and often shouted “Haribol!” to increase the excitement. Janaki also strummed a tamboura borrowed on occasion from Pandit-ji.

Pandit-ji. In the course of his travels about London Mukunda had befriended an Indian music teacher named Pandit Trika. Pandit-ji was an expert on the sitar and a number of other Indian instruments, and he often attended our kirtans. He was humble to the point of self-effacing, super gentle, and noble, very much like Ravi Shankar. (Mukunda’s imitation of Pandit Trika had us rolling on the floor: “Uhhhh, yesss, too much di di da da, more it should be da di di da dhan,” almost falling asleep he’s so relaxed.)

One time, back in San Francisco, Mukunda had played a record of some weird Indian stringed instrument, a dilruba he called it, and the eerie sound had haunted me. I had had some training on the violin when I was ten or twelve, so I asked Pandit Trika if he could get me a dilruba. He said he couldn’t, but he had an esraj, a similar stringed instrument, played with a bow like a violin but with the base held in your lap and the fingerboard held upright, like a cello. So I bought it from him and he taught me the basics: four main metal strings and fifteen sympathetic strings. Nice. You could slide your fingers up and down the frets and bend the sound (called the meend, in Indian music). We bought a little pick-up mike and, if we were onstage somewhere, we’d plug the esraj into a house amplifier. It added exotic resonance and a little variety to our band.

We did mostly Hare Krishna mantra, of course, using the two melodies (the regular and morning) taught to us by Swamiji. We also learned the Vande ’ham prayer, which we called “Prayer to the Spiritual Master.” We did “Govinda Jai Jai,” and later, after Yamuna taught us the opening verses to the Brahma Samhita, we cooked up a song called “Govindam” (later shortened to “Govinda”). Mukunda worked out some catchy melodies, and that was it: our repertoire.

Practicing. Getting ready.

After a month or six weeks wandering all over London, we could see that to start a vibrant, important temple here, in the style of San Francisco, would not be easy. We couldn’t just rent a cheap storefront in some remote suburb and expect to attract potential devotees. The hippies—young devotee material—were scattered around the city. There was no Haight-Ashbury or Lower East Side, no neighborhoods packed with hippies. Places like the Arts Lab were successful because they were in central London, where people often traveled anyway to shop or for entertainment. Regular Londoners still had a sense of “going downtown.” The Indian community urged us to open a temple in the suburbs with their help, but Swamiji wanted us to attract English boys and girls. After many discussions, we agreed unanimously: The Swami’s temple has to be in the center of London, as close to Piccadilly as possible, like a magnet attracting iron particles.

Swamiji’s letters, like this one to Shivananda (who had gone to Berlin), dated October 10,1968, spurred us on:

So the boys and girls from San Francisco who are in London, they are doing their best but till now they have not been able to find out a house on rent. I am surprised that in Germany you could find out a place without any difficulty, and why there are so many difficulties in London? So that, in spite of there being a strong group of 6 persons, headed by Mukunda and Shyamasundar, they could not find out a house in London, whereas you alone have already opened ISKCON RADHA KRISHNA TEMPLE in Berlin.

The Swami made sure we saw this letter, enclosing a copy of it in his letter to me of the same date: “You will be glad to know that Shivananda has already opened our ISKCON Radha Krishna Temple in Berlin. The copy of the letter which I am sending just now to him is also enclosed herewith, please find. I do not know why there is so much difficulty in finding out a London house on rent.”

Pressure from the Swami. Almost every day, at least one of us would be out jotting down the phone numbers from “To Let” signs and looking at empty buildings in central London. But downtown London was just so huge, and these places were so expensive that we kept drawing blanks.

Living out in Herne Hill, we spent much of our time traveling back and forth to central London, so when Gurudas announced one day that he had met Nigel Samuel, the owner of a building in the heart of town, and that Nigel had said we might be able to move into that building, we were elated. “Way t go, Gurudas!” 22 Betterton Street, four stories high, with a basement, served as the offices for the International Times newspaper, was around the corner from the Arts Lab in Covent Garden, and a ten-minute walk from Piccadilly. By October 1, the IT people had moved to 27 Endell Street, a few blocks away, and now the building was empty. Well, not exactly empty. IT still kept an office in the basement, and piles of their unsold back issues filled the ground floor. Nigel had an office on the fourth floor, and Michael X and his London branch of the Black Liberation Army had moved into the second and third floors.

All six of us and the baby immediately went to see Nigel at his posh penthouse apartment off Tottenham Court Road (the Samuels were one of the wealthiest families in the UK). We made our plea. It was after dark—the sun had gone over the yardarm, as they say—and Nigel was feeling no pain. Whether it was that or our heartfelt presentation, Nigel sympathized with us completely and said he would ask Michael X to leave and we could have the place, rent-free. He wanted to keep his office on the top floor, but we could have the bottom three floors. Gurudas was our liaison with Michael X; he had known some of the Black Power people in Manhattan, so he went down to Betterton Street every day to hurry them along, make sure they got out of the place. After several frustrating days, Gurudas said, “Maybe if we all go down and talk to Michael X, they’ll leave."

Gurudas is a big fellow. He stands on the sidewalk on Betterton Street in his heavy overcoat, getting into his New York tough-guy act, poking Michael X in the chest and shouting “You gotta get outta there, mon!” (Michael is from Trinidad.) We huddle behind Gurudas as his backup team, like maybe Malati s got a gun in her diaper bag. Whatever, it worked. The Black Liberation Army vacated, and next day we started moving into 22 Betterton Street. By the end of October 1968, we re living in the heart of London. (Michael X was convicted of murder in 1972; in 1975 he was executed by hanging in Port of Spain’s Royal Jail.)

The Betterton Street building was basically a warehouse. Each floor was just one big room, maybe fifty by fifty feet square, and the only toilet was off the stairwell between the ground and second floors. Nigel seldom came to his office on the fourth floor, so Gurudas and Yamuna moved in up there. Mukunda and Janaki occupied the third floor. We kept the second floor as our temple and common room, and I crashed on the floor there at night in a sleeping bag. On the ground floor, I stacked the old ITs along one wall and drove the Fordson through the delivery door to park it inside overnight. The baby’s feeding times were at odd hours, so to avoid disturbing the others, Malati built a “bedroom” for herself and Saraswati, to one side of the parked truck—she found a discarded cardboard refrigerator crate on the street, hauled it in, carved an opening into one side, laid down blankets, and that’s where they slept at night. One morning, I came down to see that an inch of snow had blown under the garage door overnight, so I bought an electric heater to place inside the cardboard crate (husband and provider, after all). I was always worried the blankets would catch fire in that tiny space. The rest of the building we heated with small paraffin (kerosene) stoves. They smelled pretty bad and did little to dispel the now-constant late-autumn chill. We kept lots of incense burning to cover the pervasive odor of petroleum distillates.

There were no bathing facilities in the old warehouse, so at first we used the Camden Town Public Baths around the corner, on Shaftsbury Avenue—a five-minute walk away. There, you had your own cubicle, which came with a huge, porcelain bathtub. The bloke who worked there would fill it to the brim with very hot water. You got thirty minutes and a towel for a shilling. Soap, if you forgot to bring your own, was an extra thrupence. After a few weeks of this running back and forth with wet heads in late-fall London, I built a tiny stall shower out of plywood and plastic sheets behind Malati s bedroom box in the garage. I installed a small electric geyser to heat just enough water for very brief dawn ablutions for six very quick-stepping people. We set up some electric hotplates next to the shower so the girls could cook: porridge for breakfast and mostly rice and dal for other meals.

On our trips to and from the public baths, we had noticed a big billboard above the Shaftsbury Theater advertising the Broadway show Hair!, which had opened the first week of October. “American Tribal Love-Rock Musical Smash!” the sign said. Someone told us that at the end of the show the entire cast gathers on stage to chant

Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna  
Krishna Krishna, Hare, Hare  
Hare Rama, Hare Rama  
Rama, Rama, Hare Hare  
Love, love, love, love  
Love, love, love, love  
Drop out, drop out, drop out, drop out  
Be in, be in, be in, be in  
Beads, flowers, freedom, happiness  
Beads, flowers, freedom, happiness  
Beads, flowers, freedom, happiness

So we started going over to the Shaftsbury Theatre in full costume at 10:30 p.m., just when the show let out. We’d hold sankirtan in front of the theater and ask for donations. As people came out, high from the show, they gave generous amounts to our happy cause. Even the cops thought we were part of the show and didn’t hassle us.

The IT office in the basement at Betterton was still being used, and they had Gestetner mimeograph machines down there: Vrrooom! Vrrooom! Vrrooom! all day long. It was literally an underground press. On the second floor, Yamuna set up a nice altar with the little Jagannath deities, and we settled into our routine of rising early, chanting, and reading. Now we had a space to bring guests, so the boys and girls we met at the Arts Lab began to come over, and gradually some of them moved in or slept on the floor from time to time. The girls went out every day to buy veggies at the adjacent, bustling Covent Garden marketplace, often getting free food and flowers from sympathetic tradesmen.

Malati: I was the cook and did everybody’s laundry—I had this big basket on wheels, so I had Saraswati in her pram in front and this basket thing on wheels behind, and I would go to the local laundromat. I was always worried that if the Swami came he’d have to go to that public bath.

Never mind. It was our home base and we were in the heart of the action. From Betterton Street we could easily fan out all over London, look for new premises, and quickly get things done. We met each night, catching up on the day’s activities and laughing a lot. We wanted to get the Swami over here immediately, but we needed to get things just right for him first. He was like the king to us, so he should come to England and be treated royally. It was slow going, but we were having fun on this great adventure to create a world-class Krishna conscious center for the Swami.

**\* \* \***

One of us gets a letter from the Swami at least once a week, and on November 8, the Swami wrote:

My Dear Yamuna, Mukunda, and Shyamasundar,

Please accept my blessings. I have duly received your letters dated Nov. 1, Nov. 2, and Oct. 25, respectively, and I have noted the contents carefully. I am confident that you are all trying your best so there is no question of doing things hastily. You can execute your respective duties peacefully and I am confident that you will be successful.

I understand from Yamuna’s letter that one English boy has given you rent-free a four story small building for living accommodations. This is all Krishna’s Grace.

And another one, to me, dated November 12:

I can understand also that because you could not start a temple in London you are feeling little disappointed, but there is no cause for such disappointment. I can understand that you are doing very well there even though we have not got our own temple. The pictures which Malati has sent me are nice, very very nice. From the pictures I can understand how sincerely you are working there. So even we could not establish a temple, there our preaching work is not in vain. So there is no cause of disappointment—you go on with your work as you are doing and everything will come successful in due course.

Mukunda, Gurudas, and I canvass the downtown area, looking for empty buildings. In his bowler hat and brolly, Mukunda pursues ISKCON registration and a sankirtan permit, and tries to open channels to the Archbishop of Canterbury. I hustle publicity and gigs for our band. We run short of money, so Gurudas builds a darkroom in his upstairs closet and then runs around town taking photos, trying to sell them to magazines and newspapers. Mukunda tunes pianos and gets a part-time job on Denmark Street, London’s Tin Pan Alley, where he transcribes musical notation from tapes. I remodel bathrooms for Hindus in the suburbs, and soon learn about the UK’s 240-volt electrical system when I get blown across a room.

At the end of October, Brahmananda sends us a few rush copies of the Swami’s Bhagavad-gita As It Is, newly printed by MacMillan Publishing. At last, we have the words of Krishna Himself in our hands. We devour the Gita, reading it alone or in class together, drinking in His words:

9.1 Sri Krishna said: My dear Arjuna, since you are never envious of Me, I shall impart to you this most confidential knowledge and realization, knowing which you shall be relieved of the miseries of material existence.

9.2 This knowledge is the king of education, the most secret of all secrets. It is the purest knowledge, and because it gives direct perception of the self by realization, it is the perfection of religion. It is everlasting, and it is joyfully performed.

Chapter 10, “Opulence of the Absolute,” became one of our favorite chapters. There Krishna describes His own wonders and glories. One morning, the six of us ended our morning class with verse 41: “Know that all opulent, beautiful, and glorious creations spring from but a spark of My splendor.”

Yamuna started it with a low chuckling. When pressed, she shook her head, “No, let’s not go there.” But she couldn’t suppress her chuckles and soon broke into a throaty laugh.

“Come on, Yamuna, let’s have it! What’s so funny?” She looked at me and said, “Do you remember those sock hops at Leslie Junior High and that song by the Four Aces? ‘Love is a...’” Mukunda gathered his breath, threw back his head, and crooned in a high voice, “Lo-o-ove is a maany-splennn-dooored thing.” On cue, the rest of us joined the chorus:

It’s the A-pril rose  
That only grows in the earr-ly spring,  
Love is nature’s way of giving  
A reason to be living,  
The gol-den crown—that makes a man—a kiinNNNG!

We’re all on the floor now, rolling with laughter—and I couldn’t get that song out of my head the rest of the day.

I drove around London, going here and there, thinking about things the Swami had told us, like in his letter to me dated November 24: “To the sincere devotee this ocean of maya is easily crossed by the cool breeze of Krishna’s' Grace. So this is our business, to submit to Krishna’s desire to have us back with Him in the eternal sky. And when we are determined in this way we become transcendental to the so-called hardships of place and circumstance.” Sacrifice your life for Krishna and escape the pangs of birth, death, disease, and old age. Yeah! And you can’t really say that our life here in London was a sacrifice. Sure, it was a bit lean, but we were having a ball.

Dick and his girlfriend, Pamela, visit us at Betterton Street with their friend Tim and a new boy, Colin Jury. Dick and Pamela get jobs cleaning flats and kick in a couple of pounds a week. Another fellow named Jimmy Doody starts hanging out and asking questions about Krishna. Jimmy has invented a light show device, a little projector that throws patterns on the wall using slides of colored oils compressed between glass. He asked if he could call his company “Krishna Lights,” and we said, sure, why not? His large ads for Krishna Lights start appearing in almost every issue of IT, with pictures of Krishna or the Pancha Tattva. These new kids have heavy Limey accents and idioms, and we have a hard time understanding them at first, like “kit” means clothes and “a bit o’ kip” is a nap. One boy can’t pronounce my name and calls me “Sammy-shoe.”

We attend occasional meetings at the Hindu Centre, and sometimes we go to Indians’ homes for dinner. We know something about Indian culture from the Swami, like calmer manners and shows of respect, but now we are learning first-hand how to socialize and mix freely with Indian people. Recent African politics—like Idi Amin’s crackdown in Uganda—had forced many of these families to flee to England. Most of the immigrant men were working menial civil service jobs or running small retail businesses, but in true Indian style, they were all frugal and totally family-oriented. They would save their incomes, put down payments on small suburban homes or flats, bring their relatives over, get them jobs, and soon take over whole neighborhoods. Southall was one such area. We sincerely liked many of these Indian people, but most of them had fixed ideas about their Hindu religion and considered Krishna just one god among many. They were more interested in feeding us lavish meals in return for our “blessings” and showing us off to their children: Just see, our religion is so good that even these white Europeans are taking it up.

Malati: Saraswati was crying a lot at one point, and one of the Indian ladies told me that she was hungry, but I told her no, she’s always nursing. But the Indian lady, who had far more experience than I did, showed me how to crush grains and mix them with milk, use a bottle, and that took care of that.

Issue #45 of IT came out on November 29,1968, with a four-color, double-paged feature put together by Gurudas. “Part One: Parana Bhagwata Bhakta Prahlad” was a series of twenty cartoon panels that had been mailed to us by Goursundar and Govinda dasi, illustrating the story of Prahlad Maharaj. Gurudas wrote the text for each panel, and Yamuna scribed the title with her perfect calligraphy. “Part Two” would appear in a later issue. Fifty thousand IT readers saw this ancient story from Srimad-Bhagavatam.

This event takes place long before the dawn of our modern recorded history. In each of the four Vedic ages, a particular method for spiritual advancement is foremost. The age in which this incident takes place is an age in which the average lifespan is 1000 years. Austerities and penances constituted the path of realizing the self and freeing the self from material bondage. The story opens on one of the Vaikuntha (spiritual) planets, as we see.

Twenty panels later, in the final panel, Prahlad is sitting in the courtyard, his mother in the background pleading with his evil father, Hiranyakasipu: “Prahlad is given poisoned food, his mother protests in vain. But Lord Krishna nullifies its effect, and once again Prahlad is saved from his fathers devices. To be continued.” The same IT issue advertises an upcoming rock concert at the Roundhouse, Chalk Farm, for Saturday, December 14,10:30 p.m. until dawn, starring Principal Edwards Magic Theatre. Second billing is Sitar Master Pandit Trika, and

RADHA KRISHNA TEMPLE

leading Vedic Mantra chanting

This was our second big gig at the Roundhouse, and the first show with our new name, Radha Krishna Temple. We had learned a lot about show business in the past couple of months. As the opening act, our plan was to gather the audience around a ceremonial bonfire in the center of the huge hall and have a kirtan. Show biz. Before the show, on a metal sheet surrounded by bricks, we laid out a three-foot-high mound of firewood on the dance floor and soaked it with kerosene. We sat in a circle around the stacked wood, hundreds of people seated around us on the floor, and started to chant: Hare Krishna, Hare Krish—I threw a match, and Whooosh! Flames shot high. The crowd stood quickly, then went wild, chanting and dancing with abandon, pressing closer to see the flames—closer they came, forcing us almost into the fire, our clothes starting to smoke. I’m looking at Gurudas, Now whatta we do? Just then a guy runs over with a bucket of water and starts dousing the flames just in time, saving us from certain immolation. A full brigade of London firemen enter the hall a few minutes later, pretty much ending the kirtan. Just another Saturday night rave-up at the Roundhouse.

The Beatles’ White Album came out in late November and instantly becomes the #l-selling album in the world, maintaining and deepening the Fab Four’s grip on global consciousness. We listen to “Revolution” over and over: “Don’t you know it’s gonna be alright, alright.” We hear George sing “While My Guitar Gently Weeps,” and “Little Piggies,” his cutting commentary on straight society:

Everywhere there’s lots of piggies  
Living piggy lives  
You can see them out for dinner  
With their piggy wives  
Clutching forks and knives to eat their bacon.

Back in August 1967, when George Harrison had toured Haight-Ashbury, he apparently met members of the Hell’s Angels and offhandedly said to them, “Hey, if you’re ever in London, drop by and say hello,” maybe never thinking they’d take him up on it. Well, I guess they did, because in early December, George dictated a memo to his staff:

MEMO

FROM: George Harrison

TO: Everybody at Apple

4th December 1968

Hell’s Angels will be in London within the next week, on the way to straighten out Czechoslovakia. There will be twelve in number complete with black leather jackets and motorcycles. They will undoubtedly arrive at Apple and I have heard they may try to make full use of Apple’s facilities. They may look as though they are going to do you in but are very straight and do good things, so don’t fear them or up-tight them. Try to assist them without neglecting your Apple business and without letting them take control of Savile Row.

On December 10 I got a phone call at Betterton Street: “Hey, it’s me, Rock Scully. How’s it goin’, Sambo? Listen, I’m out here at Heathrow with Danny and some of the guys from the Haight. We don’t have a place to stay. Can you put us up?”

“Sure, man! How many of you are there?”

“Were eight. Keseys here, Peter Coyote, some of the Angels-you got enough room?”

“Yeah, we can manage if you don’t mind crashing on the floor. How’re you getting into town?”

“Can you pick us up?”

“I’ve got a small pickup—yeah, I think you can all squeeze in, unless you’ve got a lot of luggage.”

“No, we’re traveling light, just our saddlebags, a few backpacks.” “It’ll take me an hour or so to get out there.”

“Cool. We’ll wait for you out in front of the terminal.”

Our friends from San Francisco, here in London! The gang is really excited. I say, “Look, we’ve got all these people coming, we need lots of food, maybe borrow some blankets from the Arts Lab. What do you think?” Everyone goes into high gear to get things ready for our illustrious guests.

They’ve been traveling all night, are hung over, had some hassles with customs, so they’re a little surly when they see me drive up in a midget red pickup truck. I guess they thought the whole world would welcome them with open arms, these intrepid pioneers of the psychedelic age: Rock Scully is here with Danny Rifkin, comanager of the Grateful Dead; Ken Kesey; Peter Coyote; Tumbleweed and Frisco Pete from the Hell’s Angels; a couple of hippie chicks, Sue Swanson and Connie. Rock has conjunctivitis, and his eyes are swollen and red.

Rock Scully: Yeah, we expected George to just open his castle and put us all up. You’d think he would have had eight rooms apiece for us—or at least a limo.

Seven bodies pile in the back with their gear. I look in the rearview mirror at the scowling faces of Californians not dressed for London’s December cold, tired but maybe happy to be here. Rock jumps in front and tells me on the way into town, “Tumbleweed and Frisco Pete brought their Harleys on the plane, but customs won’t release ’em till someone pays the air-freight bill.”

“Jeez, Rock. We don’t have that kind of money.”

“No, no, it’s OK. We re gonna call Apple. They’ll straighten it out.” Richard DiLello, who worked for Apple at the time, writes about this incident in his book, The Longest Cocktail Party:

“Press Office?”

“Yes?”

“Air freight arrivals, Heathrow Airport here, sir. We have two Harley-Davidson motorcycles which are ready for collection. However, the shipping costs must be paid before we can release them.”

“Just a minute, please. Who are they for?”

“Well, all it says here is Hell’s Angels, care of Apple, 3

Savile Row. Are they a group of yours?”

“Not exactly, but how much are the shipping costs?” “Two hundred and fifty pounds.”

“Hmm. Well, hold onto them until someone comes to claim them, and please, I beg you, make sure nothing happens to them. No scratches, please.”

Gurudas greets this wild-and-wooly bunch at the front door to Betterton Street and politely asks Frisco Pete to take off his boots before he goes up the stairs. Pete complies, but we almost wish he hadn’t—the smell of his days-old socks almost gags us, and much incense is lit. The girls feed everyone with bountiful prasadam. Tumbleweed asks, “Where are the forks?” and Malati tells him he had better wash some of that grease off his fingers because he has to eat with his hands. Frisco Pete can’t believe these ’taters. Peter Coyote uses our phone to call a friend in London and then splits. Mukunda and Danny catch up on Haight-Ashbury gossip, and Gurudas stays up most of the night talking philosophy with Ken Kesey. In his book, By His Example, Gurudas describes their conversation: “Then Kesey gave the Swami a great compliment: ‘Your guru is the heaviest.’ Translated, that means: ‘The deepest, most natural, determined, noncompromising, real, not-afraid-to-say-what-he-thinks, brightest star in the honorific galaxy of living saints in the universe.’”

I yak with Rock for hours about old times. I’m not sure how much sleep anyone got that night—people clomping up and down stairs to get to the toilet or outside for a cigarette—but by morning the SF gang is more than ready to leave. I take Tumbleweed and Pete out to Heathrow to pick up their Harleys, which have been released. They will be busy the next few days organizing a British branch of the Hell’s Angels.

Rock Scully: Well, they were looking to straighten them out, because there was a bunch of guys that had kind of whitewashed the backs of their jackets saying “Hells Angels” and calling themselves Hells Angels, and so Pete wanted to go check that out and see if there was something there that was righteous enough to actually start a chapter.

Three days later I get another call from Rock. He says they’re all going to a pre-Christmas party at Apple that afternoon, hoping to meet the Beatles. I say, “You mind if I tag along?” He says, “No, come on. I’ll see you down there.”

As I head out the door from Betterton Street, I’m scared out of my mind. On my way to meet the most popular people in the world, butterflies in my stomach, thinking: “No, no, it’s OK. Krishna will guide you.” It’s a short walk, maybe twenty minutes, to Savile Row—cross Piccadilly, up Regent Street—good, the rain stopped—Rock said four o’clock, four thirty—running a little late. But those guys’ll be there all afternoon—is my tilak OK?—Hells Angels, Ken Kesey, Grateful Dead guys—jeez, the Beatles won’t even see me—if they re even there. Stop! Let it go: Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna. Hurry, hurry, make the light, slap, slap, shoulda worn proper shoes, not flipflops—rain-crap all over my dhoti. There it is: Number 3, Savile Row.

A dozen girls, some with cameras, are hanging around the short stairway up to Apple Corps. One girl giggles and says “Hare Krishna!” A black-suited, big-shouldered bobby says, “Stop!” as I reach for the door.

“But sir, my friends are in there, you know, the group from California—the motorcycle guys. I have an appointment.”

Struggling not to laugh at this baldheaded nutcase Yank in his bed sheet, the bobby says, “Run along now, laddy.” The guy’s got a nice enough smile. I guess he’s just doing his job.

Squeals behind me announce the silent arrival of a white Rolls-Royce, and Big Shoulders hops down the stairs to open the rear door. Yoko Ono, knowing and looking cool in shades, steps out, sees me, smiles, and says softly, “You must be one of George’s friends. Come in.” Flashbulbs pop behind us.

Upstairs, we enter a big lounge with armchairs, sofas, small tables and lamps, a Christmas tree winking in one corner. The place is crowded with about fifty people drinking and talking—rock stars, elegant women, Carnaby Street hippies, guys in suits, and our motley San Francisco crew spread out among them. I greet Rock, then take a seat at the far end of the room away from two doors at the opposite end, behind which everyone says the Beatles are having a meeting and will be out shortly. Hours pass. No Beatles.

Finally, one by one, Paul, John, and Ringo each stick their head out of one of the doors and then bolt for the exit, not pausing to speak to anyone. A few minutes later, George pokes his head out too, and those famous, intense dark eyes scan the room and light on me. Before anyone can react, George shoots out the door, crosses the room, and comes straight at me, grinning. “Hare Krishna! Where have you been? I’ve been waiting to meet you!”

I love his accent. George is dressed in a loose, flowered shirt with ruffled neckline, and I’m in my dark-blue Nehru jacket, too tight at the collar, with drops of indigo dye running in the sweat down my back.

George sits down and we start yakking a mile a minute, as if we’re old friends meeting after a long time. Most people in the room are stunned, and some come over to gawk silently while we shoot the breeze. Others continue to mill around, drinks in hand, trying to look cool. Rather than nervous, I feel marvelously fluent, chosen, and wonderfully happy.

Motioning around the room, he says, “Do you know these people?”

“Yeah, I know some of these guys—old friends from San Francisco. I used to hang out with them and drop acid. Then I met the Swami. Now we’ve got a temple there in Haight-Ashbury, maybe a hundred devotees, and most are ex-freaks like me.” George, digging the story, telling me about his trip to the Haight, laughing.

He tells me some things he’s learned from Ravi and Yogananda and Maharishi. Telling how he often listens to the Happening record of Bhaktivedanta Swami leading the Hare Krishna mantra, and describing an incident that happened to him a year before, when a light plane he was on suddenly lost altitude and started to crash and he shouted “Hare Krishna!” at the top of his lungs until the plane leveled out just before hitting the ground.

I tell him how the six of us devotees, the Swami’s disciples-most of us friends since childhood—have come to London to spread the word about Krishna, that we live together in a warehouse in Covent Garden. How we’ve given up intoxicants of all kinds, even tea, and eat no meat, eggs, or fish. We don’t gamble and have given up sex (he winces) except for getting children—I have a child—but we get Krishna in return, and we’re feeling the great benefits of this surrender: the long-lasting pleasure that’s better than any drug. I tell him I’m a carpenter, doing odd jobs to get along; another devotee is a photographer; one tunes pianos.

“But what I don’t get is, why just Krishna? I mean, you’ve got Shiva, Ganesh, Brahma—they’re all the same, aren’t they? Why don’t you chant ‘Hare Shiva’ or something?”

“Yeah, well, you’ve read Bhagavad-gita, the Song of God? That’s Krishna speaking it, right, to His friend Arjuna? Krishna is Bhagavan. ‘Krishnas tu bhagavan svayam.’ He’s the Big Guy-he uses “I am” and “come to Me” and “this is Mine”—and He’s a person, but the supreme person. He's Bhagavan. Bhagavan means someone who has six opulences in full. I mean, you’re George Harrison-you’re famous, you’re rich, you’re, yes, you’re very beautiful, but God has all these opulences completely (ticking them off on my fingers). He has all wealth, all fame, all knowledge, all power, He’s more beautiful than anyone—and at the same time He is all-renounced: He doesn’t give a hoot about any of it. Such a person, full in everything, is therefore very attractive to us. He attracts us like no other. That’s Krishna. His name means the all-attractive supreme person. So we like to go to the top guy, Krishna, and call on Him, try to get something going with Him.”

Bombshell time. George, soaking it in, seems frozen for a moment, little tics and flutters of his eyes as the pieces whirr and settle and lock into place. I’m impressed. He’s so quick to understand things, goes quickly to the core.

“I get it,” he says.

I’m on a roll now, talking about my spiritual master, Bhakti-vedanta Swami, how he comes down in an ages-old line of disciplic succession, teaching what his master taught him and so on back through time, that what we’re learning is nothing new or made up but the real scoop about Krishna, sanatan dharma, the ancient science of God realization, as it is.

(Shaking off the little thoughts that, wow, this guy, my big hero, so handsome and magnetic—he’s actually spellbound and listening to me?)

“Remember in the Bhagavad-gita where Krishna expands Himself and shows Arjuna all the universes? You know—like that cover on the Jimi Hendrix album. Well, all these infinite universes are just expansions of Krishna’s energy, His effulgence, called the brah-majyoti. I know some of the great masters prefer to connect with this' brahmajyoti, this cosmic consciousness—they call it “become one with God” or “merge with the void”—and that’s all right, that’s one path to God consciousness. But it’s called ‘the impersonal approach’ and it’s very difficult to attain in this modern age of chaos.”

“Kali-yuga.”

“Right. Anyway, because were so fallen nowadays, it’s recommended that we just take the shortcut and go right to Krishna by chanting His name. That’s called ‘the personal approach.’ Personal or impersonal, these are the two approaches to God—it’s just easier to go personal. Just like now. Which do I prefer: meeting you here, talking with you, sharing some words, some emotions, some laughs—or sitting in my pad, stoned on your music? Hey, your musics great too, but I’d rather be here now, yakking with you.”

“Yeah. Shyam-sunda, you know, I feel like I’ve always known you somehow, maybe from a life before, like maybe we were brothers or something... If you’re free, we’ll talk again Sunday, at my place.”

I meet his eyes and see the ancient flame. He smiles with those huge teeth, and there’s nothing more to say. With quick efficient movements he scrawls a map to his place on a napkin, flicks his beautiful hair as he looks around, leaps up, waves farewell to everyone, and leaves. The whole room sparkles; his space smells like incense, French tobacco, and good soap.

Derek Taylor describes this day, December 13, 1968, as “Black Friday,” in his book, As Time Goes By:

I helped to spread the word that Apple was willing to help and thought maybe it was no use trying to blame anyone else for Black Friday, the day at Apple when Hell’s Angels, the managers of the Grateful Dead, a homeless family of seven from California, the Beach Boys, Hare Krishna, two transcendental meditators from Rishikesh, Mary Hopkin, George Harrison, John Lennon, Yoko Ono, a German television producer, and Tambi Muttu all arrived at once needing cups of tea and conversation. It is over, Black Friday, it is over, and I am still alive, and recall few details, few indeed.

Rock Scully: Apple didn’t put much food out, so there was a lot of drinking and everyone was really hungry, and people were getting pissed off. Hours of waiting. And finally, what happened, the Angels had brought one of those stupid English bikers and they were getting pushy and loud and ended up knocking somebody down those steep stairs, and Ringo was sitting there, and this faux English biker comes rolling down the stairs and Ringo goes, Whoa, what’s going on here? It got out of hand.

When I returned to Betterton Street I was surprisingly calm, relieved that the wait was over. The others pumped me for information, and I shared with them everything that had happened that afternoon from the cop at the front door to the ruckus on the stairs leaving Apple. George is such a nice guy, I told them over and over. “I’m not kidding, it was like talking with my brother. He’s a Krishna devotee, one of us. You’re gonna love him.” I was so thankful that I’d been chosen for this very special service, but my inner glee was tempered by the feeling that this was, after all, just Krishna’s plan unfolding—no big deal. Meeting George felt so normal.

Malati: I remember Shyamasundar coming up the stairs at Betterton Street saying he had met George and he’s a really nice guy. He said it so matter-of-factly. Usually he got really excited by these things; he’d rub his hands together and squeal like a coyote. But he was so low-key, casual.

**\* \* \***

On Sunday, December 15, I make a solo trip out to Kinfauns, George’s psychedelic, ranch-style house near Esher, in Surrey, pulling up in my red pickup in a cloud of oil smoke. George introduces me to Pattie—I get the feeling he wants to run all this new Krishna stuff by her too, see what she thinks. What a stunner she is. And so soft and quiet. Refined. We small-talk for a while and then get right into it. He fires one question after another—he’s so hungry for philosophy.

“Purpose of life?” I say, “That’s an easy one. Animals—they all have four purposes in their lives: eating, sleeping, mating, and defending themselves. As humans we share these four purposes— propensities, the Swami calls them—with animals. Sure, we have better eating arrangements, we build palaces for sleeping, we think of a thousand ways to have sex, and we have atom bombs to defend. But that’s still animal life. As humans we have a fifth propensity: to know who I am, where I came from, and where I’m going. If we don’t use this ability while we’re in our human body, were no better than animals. So the purpose of human life is to seek out who I am.”

George says, “Yeah, I’ve been sort of feeling that too the last few years. I want to know what it’s all about. You know, it’s like meeting you and hearing about Krishna and the Swami has come at the perfect time in my life. A lot of changes are going down. All these bits and pieces running around in my head—the pieces of the puzzle seem to fit into the big picture, the way you’re saying it.”

“But it’s not just me. I’m just a messenger. I don’t really know very much, but I do know the guy who knows everything. That’s the thing about Bhaktivedanta Swami. He can answer any question perfectly. He’s also a messenger, delivering what’s called ‘absolute knowledge’—but it’s logical, so it’s also absolute science. Everything I say about it can be proved. There’re no loose ends. Go ahead, ask me anything.”

“OK, so what do I find when I find out who I am, where I came from, and where I’m going?”

“Krishna—or Jehovah or Allah or The Great Spirit—whatever you want to call Him—is God, who created us, an infinite number of tiny individual spirit souls who inhabit His creation in an infinite number of individual bodies. Krishna is a person, and just like sparks are very small quantities of the big fire from which they came, so we share Krishna’s personal attributes: we sense with five senses, we think, we laugh, we work, we love. Krishna is the supreme enjoyer, and we are His enjoyed. If we forget this relationship and instead want to enjoy something on our own—we have free will, just like Krishna—Krishna is so kind that He creates this material world for us, and he’ll give us the body of a pig if we want to eat more, or a rabbit if we want more sex. Like that.

“So when I wake up and realize that I am someone to be enjoyed by Krishna, that I have an original, individual, loving relationship with Krishna as His servant, friend, parent, or even His lover, then I know where I’m going: I’m going to use this little lifetime to nourish my relationship, revive my love for God, feel God love me in return, and go back home, back to Godhead. This is called self-realization.”

George picks up on this, “Or like Vivekananda says, ‘Each soul is divine; the goal is to manifest that divinity.’”

Pattie says: “You do it by chanting, others may do it by meditating or something—different kinds of yoga?”

“Yeah, right. Yoga means ‘union’ in Sanskrit. We get the English ‘yoke’ from yoga. Yoga means you practice some discipline to unite yourself with God. Meditation, raja yoga, hatha yoga, so many yogas, but they’re very difficult to perform in this day and age, so we like to practice bhakti yoga, the yoga of loving devotional service. Bhakti means unalloyed love. We fan the flames of love of God by chanting His name all the time, reading about Him, talking about Him, eating His food, hanging out with others who are trying to remember Him. That way, you gradually become Krishna conscious. And what you feel in this state of consciousness, wow! Confidence, no fear, bliss, ecstasy—all the good stuff.”

George is laughing. His knowing look says he’s felt the bliss I’m describing and wants to feel it some more.

Pattie quietly moves off to the kitchen to whip us up a snack. George stays right with it, one question after another. He puts on a couple of his favorite records: Bob Dylan, Lenny Bruce. I can see he wants a spiritual friend, someone he can talk with about deep matters—he’s a one-on-one kinda guy, and prefers to deal with one person rather than a group. We talk for hours—about my checkered past, what led me to the Swami. We talk about India, Hinduism, the Beatles, his psychedelic refrigerator, his intense desire to perfect his life. He says that being a Beatle has proved to him that there is some much greater magic out there, and as a Beatle he now has the chance and freedom to seek out the highest truth and to tell others about it. This is an opportunity he’s earned after a million lifetimes, and he doesn’t want to waste it. He feels the burden placed on him, especially after seeing Haight-Ashbury, to set an example for others who are hanging on his every word. He’s alone, and hungry for spiritual companionship.

“Hey, George, you’ve gotta meet the rest of my gang, the devotees here in London. Mukunda is a jazz musician. Gurudas is a laugh a minute. Yamuna sings like Janis Joplin. Really, can you come over to our temple in the warehouse one of these days when you’re in London? It’s not much, but the girls are great cooks, and we can have a roaring chant and feel the bliss.”

In our first meetings, George was so familiar and at ease that it seemed like we were taking up a conversation we’d started yesterday. He was full of energy and inquisitive, and we understood each other immediately. It was a kinship of the soul. We both felt such deep pleasure in conversing—even my shoulders tingled with glee. He said, “I’ve only felt this way with two other people in my life: Ravi and Bob Dylan.” (To be regarded in such august company... This was happening way too quick, like fast-forwarding the movie of my life.)

To be assigned this special role by Krishna’s design meant a new and very great responsibility, and helping guide George in Krishna consciousness now became a duty that would drive my life. The sudden stewardship, being one of George’s siksha gurus—a peer who advises on spiritual life—quadrupled my enthusiasm to help Swamiji build his London center. Everything I did from that moment on, every decision, every act, was based not only on “Will this please the Swami?” but now also on “Will this please George?”

Swamiji wrote me on December 21,1968:

My Dear Shyamasundar,

It is understood from your letter that Mr. George Harrison has a little sympathy for our movement and if Krishna is actually satisfied on him surely he will be able to join with us in pushing on the Sankirtana Movement throughout the world. Somehow or other the Beatles have become the cynosure of the neighboring European countries and America also... During the British period in India, anything which was trademarked, “made in London” was sold very quickly, so try to make your London Yatra \[mission\] equally important so that when the young boys and girls of England and America combine together in Sankirtana Party it will be a great revolution in the world.

**\* \* \***

In the mid-November issue (#44) of IT, Jim Haynes and Jack Moore from the Arts Lab had announced an “Alchemical Wedding”—some kind of vague happening to be held in early December. The theme was to be “The Revolution Is Over and We Have Won.” Alchemical Wedding comes from the medieval concept of mixing mercury with iron to make gold. John Lennon and Yoko Ono were hanging out at the Arts Lab—you’d see their white Rolls-Royce parked out front— and they agreed to take part in Jim and Jack’s happening. The two of them, it was decided, would enter a big white bag onstage and lie down in it. That was the whole show. They rented prestigious Royal Albert Hall for the event, and the date was set for the night of December 18. Jim asked us if we could also be part of the show, to see what might happen. We said sure, we’ll be there.

On December 16, a messenger came to the girls at Betterton Street and said, “Yoko Ono wants to see you over at the Arts Lab.” Malati walked over, and Yoko asked her if the Krishnas could prepare a huge amount of prasadam for the 4,000 guests expected at Albert Hall. “Yes, but we have no pots,” Malati said, and she made a shopping list and gave it to Yoko. Later that day, the Rolls pulled up in front of 22 Betterton, and the chauffeur unloaded half a dozen huge pots, several bags of semolina, and crates of butter.

By 8 p.m., Albert Hall was packed with people. Author Lee Eli Harris describes the night of the Alchemical Wedding in his story, “A Footsoldier’s Tale”:

The hour had come, the crowd buzzed with excitement, but nothing happened. The audience started to fidget in their seats, there was an air of anxiety about... The silence became long and deep and seemed to engulf us. Then, almost out of nowhere, someone banged a drum, there was an ecstatic cry of joy, the Krishna devotees began to chant, and I found myself swept off my feet by the momentum. The space in front of the stage filled with swaying, swirling bodies high on the energy created out of silence. This was the culmination of what The Human Family was about. Theatre in its highest form was an act of communal magic that could transcend earthbound reality.

Janaki, shy and unassertive Janaki, broke the ice. She ran from the wings onto the stage and started banging her kartals. Then Mukunda whumped the drum and we all rushed out to help her. We jumped from the stage, danced and chanted in front of it, then Janaki led the charge up and down the aisles as hundreds left their seats to join us. These were not just hippies; these were mostly middle- and upper-class Londoners who had paid a pound apiece to see for themselves the strange new things happening in their city. On and on the kirtan roared, while John and Yoko lay silent in their bag onstage. Announcements that few could hear came over the PA—something about safety laws and severe punishment—and ultimately, policemen forced everyone back to their seats. Devotees served out hundreds of scoops of halvah in the hallway on little napkins. The news was out: a new age of freedom and enlightenment was being born, and the Krishnas were at the heart of it. We were the only thing that happened at the happening.

Well, one other thing happened. A woman removed her blouse and bra and looked rather normal sitting in the fourteenth row next to her husband in his white turtleneck sweater and horn-rimmed glasses. Gurudas, with his eye ever open for good shots, snapped photos of the odd couple and ran over to the Daily Mirror in time to meet their midnight deadline. Next morning’s headline read, “Naked Rave-Up at the Albert Hall.” He made a quick fifty pounds in Krishna’s service.

Gurudas made another quick fifty quid a few days later. The condemned building next door to the Arts Lab had been taken over by squatters, hippies camping there illegally. There was a fire-we heard the alarms and ran up the street to see the commotion. One girl was stranded on a window ledge three stories up, flames and smoke visible behind her. The firemen below stretched out a big blanket and beckoned her to jump. Gurudas whipped out his Nikon and snapped her mid-air, looks of wonder on everyone’s upturned faces. That evening’s front-page photo of the day was by Gurudas, our man on the spot.

We must have seemed rather strange to these Brits, with their long tradition of reserve, their stiff upper lips and rational, pragmatic philosophy. We gave off a frontier, cowboy energy: exuberant Americans with only a bright future ahead. We were so enthusiastic—maybe because we knew the deed was already done by Krishna—and were just enjoying devotional service in the time gap between now and a foregone conclusion. We watched Krishna sweep away obstacles— how easily He did it!—and everything seemed so easy, predestined.

We didn’t have a temple yet, but we knew it was only a matter of time. The Swami had backed off, no longer chiding us for being slow now that he saw our strategy. In fact, he says to Janaki in a letter of December 9: “I think also that it is good sign that Krishna is delaying to give us a nice London temple because when it finally will come it may be the most ideal temple for our purposes. So none of you be discouraged but go on with your engagements and everything will come automatically very nice.”

Of all of us, maybe Janaki is having the hardest time adjusting. In the same letter to her, Swamiji writes: “Actually, I always think of you as my naughty daughter. So far as you writing you are lonely I think that there is no need for this. Such sentiments of wishing for me to be there is nice but a girl is meant for living with her husband and not her father. And you have specially nice, beautiful husband and not only that but he is devotee also, so you are very fortunate. So continue to faithfully help your husband in executing the mission of his life to spread Krishna Consciousness and surely Krishna will bless you and grant you all happiness. This is my blessing upon the both of you.”

Through these letters, we felt that Swamiji was always with us. We talked with him in our heads, gazed inwardly at his beautiful physical form, heard him as he gave solace and answered our questions, like, “What do we do now?” We wrote back and forth constantly, exchanging minute details, and he guided us in every matter.

“I understand Malati is having some rheumatic condition, and it is causing pain and numbness. The best thing is to take hot baths, and massage with camphor oil, and if it is too much painful, use Sloan’s liniment. Best thing is to consult with some expert physician. If there is any Ayurvedic shop let me know and I shall recommend some medicine. The best thing is to chant Hare Krishna loudly.”

Malati: None of us were there for our personal glory or recognition. That was the unique thing about that period: every day, all day, whatever personal things were going on, we were united together to make this mission succeed for the Swami. And we weren’t intimidated, nor did we intimidate others, because we were there as the Swami’s ambassadors, to give something.

1968 was drawing to a close. Only four months had passed since the Swamis commandos had stormed the shores of the British Isles, but already the Hare Krishna mantra was being heard all over London, and there were several new English devotees. We were the darlings of the huge Indian community. Something about Krishna was featured in every issue of the International Times, the voice of the hippies. We had a central home base, jobs, a network of friends and well-wishers. Best of all, as per our original desire to come to the UK, we had met the Beatles and, more, had become their friends.

The Swami s hunt for another big rhino was well underway. 1969: This will be the Year!

### 2

1969 came in blustery and dark in London. I’m not sure whose idea it was, but we decided it was time to throw a party. We had a place now. 22 Better ton Street was big enough to invite people ove over, and we had1atnetwork of friends and acquaintances, most whom didn’t know each other, so we thought it time for everyone to meet and make a plan about how to formally organize and push forward the Swami’s London mission. We called it a “Board Meeting.”

Gurudas informed the Swami about this plan, and in late December, Swamiji sent us a tape of his speech addressing “members of the board.” We scheduled our party for Saturday afternoon, January 4, to give everyone a chance to recover from the holidays. We sent out printed announcements, unsure of how many, if any, would come. The invitation read, “Guess Who’s Coming to Dinner?” and included a list of about a hundred people we either knew or wanted to know in the London area. Jeez, I think we even put Prince Charles’s name on it.

We started getting a few RSVP’s: Tom Driberg, MP, would be there; Barry Miles from IT; a monk from Blackfriars College in Oxford named Simon Tugwell... We rented fancy cutlery and plates and even champagne glasses and real napkins. We cleaned and decorated the place, and the girls made a fabulous, multicourse dinner. And then we waited to see who might show up. George sent a telegram saying he couldn’t make it, but to our great surprise, many others did come: lords and ladies, big names in Britain They came to see who else might be there. Tom Driberg, MP, was delighted that one of our new devotees, Colin, was from his constituency of Barking, East London.
