We schmoozed, showed an eight-minute film of the Swami on a walk, gave our own brief talks about how КС was sweeping the globe, and otherwise kept everyone entertained

Malati: In those days there was this spirit of adventure, curiosity among upper-class people to see what was happening with the hip young people’s scene. My duty at that event was to stand at the bottom of the staircase and inform sir and madam so-and-so they had to take off their high heels and £200 handmade Italian loafers.

In our main temple room at Betterton Street, over fifty people sat on the coarse, sisal floor covering or stood, listening to the Swami address them: “Ladies and gentlemen: Please accept my greetings for the happy new year of 1969, and please accept the blessings of Sri Krishna, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, for your kindly participating in this happy meeting of Krishna consciousness.”

We watched each of these selected, highly intelligent persons in the room sink into various degrees of concentration, listening for fifteen minutes to our mysterious master’s deep, convincing voice:

Our conditioned life of material existence is due to dirty things in our heart. Originally we are all Krishna conscious living beings, but because of our long association with matter, we have been transmigrating from one form of body to another, suffering in the cycle of birth and death in various species of life (numbering 8,400,000). Thus we are being subjected to various material pangs, headed by birth, old age, disease, and death

Against these perpetual problems human society is struggling fruitlessly in many ways. The materialists trying to solve the problems through scientific knowlеdgе education, philosophy, ethics, literature, and so on. The salvationists are trying to solve the problems by putting forth various ways of discerning matter from spirit. And some people are trying to overcome birth and death through mystic yoga.

We are, however, misled at present by leaders who have very little connection with God, or Krishna... Our process is transcendentally colorful and full of transcendental pleasure. We prosecute Krishna consciousness through singing, dancing, eating, and hearing philosophy received through the authorized disciplic succession of spiritual masters coming down from Krishna Himself. Therefore Krishna consciousness gives us complete spiritual success, without our undergoing any artificial change of our natural instincts.

I thought, Wow, this is really heavy stuff. It must be making these people nervous. But then Swamiji ends the philosophy part, and makes his plea:

But to assemble and sit together we require a place for congregation... Therefore I fervently appeal to you all present in this meeting to extend your cooperation for successful execution of this great movement. My disciples in London have very eagerly asked me to visit there, and I am also very anxious to see you all. Thanking you once more. That is all.

The seeds were sown. Some of these people would play major roles helping us to obtain government permissions in the UK—like Lord Fenner Brockway, who probably came because he thought Prince Charles might be there. Lord Brockway was born near Calcutta and was an early advocate of Indian independence. He was a founding member of the “War On Want” and “Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament” and, like Driberg, a prominent outlaw in British politics.

The January 1 issue of IT, the “Know Thyself” issue, has an amazing photo on page 4 of our two Hell’s Angels friends mounted on their 1200cc Harleys: Pete is snarling and looks like he’s removing his right eye. More of their friends have just arrived from Frisco, and the Angels’ presence, like ours, is being felt like a shockwave through this normally staid city. IT reports:

They swagger about like cowboys in their genuine Levis, saddle boots, and assorted head gear, rambling on about their “choppers,” their love of the wide open spaces back home, and the rip-roaring crazy excitement which seems to emanate from San Francisco.

Below this photo is an article about the December 18 Alchemical Wedding:

Then Hare and the Krishnas came forth, playing their bells, bald and robed vegetarian loveliness; one could not dislike them, even in their plunging toward discorporation.

On page 14 of the same issue, John Peel, Britain’s most famous DJ and rock commentator, writes in his weekly column, “Perfumed Garden”:

And they wonder why their churches are empty. Listen to the followers of the Hare Krishna movement or whatever their official title is (The International Society for Krishna Consciousness, Ltd., 22, Betterton Street, WC2, actually). Who knows whether they’re right or wrong, but they have visited the jaundiced Peel and each of them radiates more love and joy than 100 bus-loads of Archbishops could envisage and that in itself is rightness of a sort.

There’s a lot of talk about Krishna on the local scene.

On January 8, George phoned. “Shyam-sundar? Hare Krishna! I’m over here at Twickenham and I was wondering, can you come out here right away?”

“What is that, George? Say again?”

“Well, we’re making this ‘Let It Be’ him out here, and I want you to come over and explain Krishna, the Krishna thing, to the boys.”

“OK.”

“It’s called Twickenham Studios. You’ll find it in your A-Z.

We’re on stage two. Come as soon as you can.”

Jesus. I hung up and ran up the stairs, shouting to the others. “Looks like I’m going to meet the Beatles!” I got into my outfit: yellow dhoti and electric-blue Nehru jacket, with the big red beads.

Twickenham is about twelve miles southwest of London. Looks like it’ll take about an hour to get there. I’m nervous as can be. Preaching to the Beatles? Swamiji, Krishna, you gotta help me now. Thinking all the way out there what I might say, finally just surrendering my mind: Krishna, whatever you want to say to these guys, just let it flow from this stupid tongue. Whatever. Even if I blow it, it doesn’t really matter, does it? This is devotional service. I’m not responsible for the result. Just try. Let it be. Thank you, Swamiji, for this rare fun. You are the master planner of all things—I’m really gonna talk to the Beatles!—How incredible!

I lose my way a few times, but I finally find the right street— and immediately, I’m caught in a traffic jam. About a half a block away, I can see a huge crowd of people, mostly young women, blocking the street; there are bobbies everywhere, and traffic is at a standstill. Just then I spot a tiny parking spot under a tree and squeeze in with my truck. How am I going to get inside? George had said, “Come over.” He didn’t tell me how to get past gates and cops. It’s bedlam—girls screaming “John!” “Paul!” “George!” and swooning, holding up “Fab-Four-I-Love-You!” signs as I squeeze past, trying to be invisible.

The big iron gates are still closed as I work my way toward them. Just then, they start to swing open and I see a sleek white Rolls-Royce approaching behind me. Like the Red Sea parting, I crouch down beside the left side of the car and duck-walk through the gates with it. Out of the corner of my right eye I see Yoko in the back seat smiling down at me—we’re through! Then I stand up tall, as if I belong here. I’m in.

Jeez, where do I go? Stage two? I feel naked in my devotee robes and shaved head; this huge place, with its many streets and buildings, is so suddenly quiet after the pandemonium outside. I put on my brave face and walk boldly forward—and see a bunch of Roman warriors coming at me, laughing and talking about football—and yes, there’s Christopher Lee in his Dracula outfit, bloodstains on his white tuxedo shirt. This is a movie lot, and dancers and stuntmen and actors in period dress are strolling around between scenes. I’m just another weirdo. I see the parked Rolls and the door to stage two. Behind it, I hear guitar music. There’s a flashing red light above the door: “No Entry.”

Around the end of ’68, the first part of ’69, we were blissfully unaware of the major changes going on in George Harrison’s lifeproblems with Paul and John, the imminent breakup of the Beatles, maybe a crisis in George’s spiritual convictions, and frustration with making his own songs heard. We were far away from his world. I hadn’t listened to a radio since we’d arrived in the UK four months earlier. I hardly knew what George was talking about whenever he mentioned the rock-and-roll business.

I push open the heavy door and enter. It’s dark, cavernous. Drum kits and sound equipment are massed in one corner of the huge space. It takes a minute for my eyes to adjust, but then I see George sitting under spotlights with his guitar and yakking with Paul, who’s standing beside him. George has an angry crease between his shadowed eyes, obviously upset about something. I hear harsh words—and then he looks up and sees me. He’s too intense to deal with me right now, I see, but he says,

“Hare Krishna!” into a mike, then returns to the squabble he’s having over notes in a riff. John stands with his guitar in front of another mike, looking bored, Yoko beside him and seated on a folding chair with her arm around his legs. Ringo is perched above them at his drums. Wheeled cameras and wires and cables are all over the place. I tiptoe around to the darkest wall and sit down on the floor, a fly on the wall, and start chanting the mantra on my big red beads.

While George Martin walks around adjusting mikes and making suggestions, The Fab Four run through version after version of a song George has apparently just written called “I-Me-Mine.” George is serious to get it right, and Ringo’s on his side, but Paul and John are poking fun at the song—during a playback, John grabs Yoko and they waltz around the room to

All through the day, I me mine

I me mine, I me mine

All through the night, I me mine

I me mine, I me mine

Now they’re frightened of leaving it

Everyone’s weaving it

Coming on strong all the time

All through the day, I me mine.

The tension is high—old friends arguing interspersed with moments of laughter and relief. Finally, chairs are scooted back, someone shouts, “Two hours everyone!” and it looks like a lunch break. George hops down from the low stage and waves me over.

“We’re havin’ a little break, Shyamasundar. Can you come along? Not sure where we’re going, but they’ve got a room set up for us. The boys all want to hear what you’ve got to say.”

And out the door we go, my head reeling, praying to Krishna for help. In a loose pack we walk through mockups of castles and London city streets as George introduces me one by one to John,

Paul, Ringo, road manager Mal Evans, and some of the others. We go into a large furnished room, the door slams—and it’s just me and the Beatles. George seats me in the center of them at one end of the room, and the other four relax in armchairs, one in each corner.

“All right, what’s it all about then?” says John.

Somehow Krishna takes over my tongue. I start from the top: Krishna, the Supreme Person; Bhagavad-gita, the book; Bhaktivedanta Swami, the teacher. “I mean, in a way I’m no different from you guys. I’m your age, I’m looking for answers to life, I took LSD—there’s gotta be more to it than this. And it’s not easy. You can grope your way in the dark and, if you’re very lucky and after a lot of pain, you might find the light. But it’s so much easier, so much less suffering, and you don’t waste time, if you can find a spiritual master like I found the Swami, who just says, ‘Here. Here’s the light. Take it.’ Everyone would have to say that you guys are very, very lucky, but I consider myself even luckier than you, because I found a perfect teacher.

“You know all about karma, right?” I say, “and migration of the soul—how we get another body depending on the state of consciousness we develop in this body? So now all of you, you four guys, you’ve taken these very highly elevated births through so many previous austerities and pious acts. Krishna says, yad yad acharati sresthas tad tad evetaro janah: ‘Whatever action a great man performs, common men will follow.’ So now each of you has this golden chance to find the perfection of life and then lead everyone else up the path to self-realization.”

“So, what about music—as an expression of consciousness. How does that fit in?” asks Paul, sharp and really listening.

Good question. I reply that sound vibration links us to God. “We say ‘Krishna.’ His name is no different from Krishna himself. Sound vibration, music, involves hearing, and hearing is the most subtle of our senses. Sound goes through the ear and directly into our consciousness. Even in sleep the ear is alert. So sound is the most direct way of reaching Krishna. He hears us, singing His name, playing songs for Him, glorifying Him-and this gives Him great pleasure. You get His attention, and you exchange love with Him.

“Bhakti yoga, Krishna consciousness, doesn’t say, ‘Give up your life, your occupation.’ No. You can be a doctor, a mother, a bum, a Beatle—but be a doctor for Krishna, be a rock star for Krishna, dedicate everything to Krishna, for His pleasure. Take some fruits of your labors and offer them to Him. Have a nice wife and raise children for Krishna. Whatever homes, cars, and guitars you need for your work, that’s OK. Krishna consciousness is not a life of austerity and deprivation.”

Lennon is taking it all in. “What about peace? I mean you can’t have all these people runnin’ around in the world and killin’ each other, can ya?”

“The only real peace happens when the people of the world are truly glorifying God with pure hearts. If you love the father, naturally you love his children. We are all children of God, children of Krishna: people, cats, dogs, birds, ants. We don’t kill anything—that is real peace. If you kill a cow and eat its flesh, drink its blood, do you think the cow doesn’t suffer, that she is peaceful when Maxwell’s hammer comes down on her head? No. For peace to be widespread and real, you have to water the root, develop God consciousness. Then peace will automatically be there for all living creatures.”

I explain what George means by “I-Me-Mine”: “The living entity falsely identifies the body as the self and relates everything to ‘me’ and ‘my’ sense gratification. This illusion—I am this body, this is me, that is mine—is the cause of all suffering.” The boys had heard this already from Maharishi, but I could sense that George’s song was maybe referring to something more personal going on among them, so I steered away from this subject.

And on and on into the late afternoon, as time and again the Fab Four ignored the call to the set and plowed forward with more questions. We ate something, we laughed a lot, they made fun of my dress (“Hey, it’s the only way I could get your attention!”) They got personal when it came to wives and relationships, talked about India and Maharishi and the time George and John had chanted Hare Krishna all day on a Greek isle. (“It made me really high, it did!”) George got more relaxed, loosening up as the afternoon passed. As we left the building, George told John that the Krishnas don’t really have a place to live, so John said, “Well, I’m thinking about getting a big place out in the country—maybe you Krishnas can come out there and stay for a bit, y’know, and do some work around the place?”

I’m not sure what all transpired that day, but I think George was trying in his own way, by having me meet his mates, to calm the waters, throw some healing glue on the wounds that were starting to open among them. I saw that George was visibly more at ease afterward; perhaps his pals could discern that Krishna consciousness was for real, that they would not judge him now as having taken up with some loony new cult.

I know that John was much affected by our talks, and he would remain a lifelong friend of the Krishna devotees, even though he liked to poke fun at us too. Paul and Ringo learned a lot that afternoon in their own ways. I also learned a few lessons from them-about the terrible strain forced on a person by fame, and the temptation to obliterate the self in the face of so much pressure—and I saw how these wonderful, uniquely intelligent men were trying to cope with all of this, first by understanding it and then by acting on their understanding. How rare was this combination!

I returned to Betterton Street late that afternoon, and the Beatles went on to finish recording and filming “I-Me-Mine”. It took them forty-one takes. They also filmed two versions of “Hare Krishna Mantra,” in a clowning way. Not really knowing the words, Paul sang: “Harry Krishna, Harry Rama, Harry Radha” to an upbeat melody, and everyone was laughing. They called it “Harry Pinsker.” At the end of the song, Paul said, “On our way home, Johnny, were on our way back home.”

Four days later, George Harrison walked out of the “Let It Be” sessions, unable any longer to tolerate the tensions within the group, saying to his bandmates, “See you ’round the clubs.” Three days after that, on January 15, George returned to Twickenham, made up with the others, and they moved over to Apple Studios to finish “Let It Be.”

I wrote to Swamiji in Los Angeles that George was writing a song called, “Hear Me Lord,” and I sent him the lyrics, which George had sung for me:

Forgive me Lord, please

Those years when I ignored you

Forgive them Lord

Those that feel they can’t afford you.

Help me Lord, please

To rise above this dealing

Help me Lord, please

To love you with more feeling.

At both ends of the road

To the left and the right

Above and below us

Out and in—

There’s no place that you’re not in

Won’t you hear me, Lord?

The Swami replied: “I am so glad that Mr. Harrison is composing songs like ‘Lord which we so long ignored.’ He is very thoughtful. When we actually meet, I shall be able to give him thoughts about separation from Krishna, and they will be able to compose very attractive songs for public receptions. The public is in need of such songs, and if they are administered through nice agents like the Beatles, it will surely be a great success.”

On January 12 the Sunday Times featured a great photo of the seven London devotees, taken with a fish-eye lens that distorted our faces but at the same time created an appealing, otherworldly effect. Saraswati, now seven months old, is chewing on my beads in the photo. The article appeared in the widely read “Atticus” column, and was entitled “Atticus Feels the Benefit”:

HARE KRISHNA (pronounced Harry Kreeshna) is good for you. What it means, more or less, is ‘Take heed of Krishna.’ Innocuous, you may think. But say it sincerely and wonderful things can happen... The Krishna people also want to try their chants on tourists, derelicts, and businessmen. They’re quite prepared, they say, to go into action outside the Stock Exchange. It’s an intriguing idea, and what it might do for the economy, Krishna only knows.

This very positive article was picked up by the Associated Press and printed all over the world as “Krishna Chant Startles London.” Swamiji read it in the San Francisco Examiner, then wrote us a letter: “This picture is undoubtedly very unique because perhaps in the history of the world it is for the first time that Western boys and girls in the dress of pure Vaishnavas are chanting on the street of a very important quarter of the greatest city in the world, London... I am so much pleased.”

Now the Swami’s desire to come to London greatly increases: “Above all I am very much anxious to see a London temple established first. So please let me know by return mail if there is an immediate chance of my going to London. I do not mind any climatic condition there; I am quite fit, and whenever required, I am prepared to go there.”

And: “I am sitting here anxious to meet you as soon as possible. If you think that my presence will accelerate matters, I am prepared to go immediately.”

It’s all we can do to write back and say, “Swamiji, please don’t come just yet. We need to get everything prepared just right for you first.”

The January 17 issue of IT (#48) carried on the back page “part two” of the continuing saga of Prahlad Maharaj, and in eight more brightly colored panels we see Krishna emerge from the pillar as Nrsimhadev, half-man, half-lion, to slay Prahlad’s demon father.

IT #49, dated January 31, 1969, carries two announcements on its “What’s Happening” page: “Hare Krishna Chanting” is still on for Monday and Wednesday nights, 7 p.m., at the Arts Lab; “Hare Krishna Chanters” appear every Tuesday night at 6:45 at the Macrobiotic Restaurant. On the same page: Moby Grape is playing at the Roundhouse this weekend.

A friend in London gave us an article he clipped from the January 21, 1969, Times of India Illustrated Weekly, a newspaper read by millions in India. Headlined “The Krishna Bhaktas of San Francisco,” it’s a two-page spread with five photos:

Visiting the Krishna temple along with friends from India, was an interesting experience each time. My friends were amazed to see the Krishna devotees, who studied Sanscrit, listened to learned discourses on Lord Krishna, Srimad Bhagavatam, etc, and sang Krishna kir-tan. This was a scene truly reminiscent of Hardwar or Benares. We could see for ourselves that it was no fake, no religious rock ‘n’ roll... It looks as if the Krishna cult is going to be among the many other recent Indian influences on American ways.

Word about the Swami’s incredible mission was leaking back to his homeland.

The cold and gloomy London winter didn’t slow us down all that much. We were a close-knit family, with our beloved father/son Swamiji always cheering us on in his letters.

My Dear little mothers, Yamuna, Malati, Janaki,

Please accept my blessings. I lost my mother when I was only 14 years old. So I didn’t get much of my mother’s affection in my childhood. But in my old age, Krishna has given me so many young mothers to take care of me.

And:

In other words, I am just claiming this contribution as your pet child. My father was very much affectionate, and in my childhood, whatever I wanted from my father he would at once give me. One time he purchased for me one rifle, and so after taking it I demanded that he must give me another. My father denied ‘You already have one. Why do you ask me for another?’ So my argument was that I must have two rifles, one for each hand. Due to my obstinacy my father finally agreed. Later on when I was young and lost my father, I was very sorry to have lost such affectionate father, but by Krishna’s Grace, I have now many American fathers and mothers.

It was the girls who kept us going: Yamuna, Malati, Janaki. Their disciplined adherence to rising early and chanting and reading together on the rough, sisal matting before our little altar gave each day a new energy. Continuous letters from the Swami gave us everfresh hope, and although we worked as a well-oiled team, we each had our individual duties to keep spreading KC in London as fast as we could. And there was survival mode too: each of the men had to hustle to maintain a steady minimum cash flow to pay for food, kerosene and, in my case, petrol for the truck and nappies for the baby.

After the morning program, we’d bolt down our porridge with raisins (and bottles of English milk with real cream on top) and plan the day.

Mukunda: I have to work over on Denmark Street today, but I’m taking off early to get a mridanga lesson from Pandit Trika. Oh, and I’ve got to take that letter from Swamiji over to Constable O’Malley—try to speed up our sankirtan permit.

Shyamasundar: Hey, Mukunda, would you ask Panditji when I can come over for another esraj lesson? Gurudas, you want to go over with me to see that old morgue in Holborn today?

Gurudas: Yeah, but I’ve got to pick up a gallon of developer for my darkroom first.

Yamuna: When will everybody be back? We need to practice the Vande ’ham prayers some more. I’m gonna get them copied today so we can memorize them better. What, about five o’clock?

Mukunda: That sounds OK. But don’t forget we have kirtan at the Macrobiotic Restaurant tonight at 6:45—and we’ve gotta drive through rush hour.

Shyamasundar: Mukunda, one more time, please—do your Pandit Trika imitation!

Mukunda turns extremely calm, centered, and says with an Indian accent: “I am so much happy if you can just try this for me one time only, Mr Mukundaah”—wobbling his head from side to side—“Ga-ni-ga-dha—no, no, little slowww”—closing his eyes, he goes into an apparent trance, but with the big grin showing he gives it away while we crack up, rolling on the floor and laughing.

Shyamasundar: Gurudas, do Prem Sayal!

Gurudas pretends he is pumping a harmonium with left hand, “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishnaaa,” throws right hand in air, whips head back as if flipping long hair out of his eyes—“O Krishna! I LAWVyou!—Hare, Hare!” as he falls backward in a swoon.

Comedy as much as anything got us through those short, dark days. Mukunda, with his musical ear, is able to perfectly imitate these eccentric (to us) Englishmen and quaint (to us) Anglo-Indians.

Sometimes he would regale us with monologues from Lenny Bruce, like “The White-Collar Drunk”: “I’m from the FBI. You gonna give me 29 cents for a bottle a’wine, or am I gonna take you downtown?” Gurudas especially saw everything from its comic side and made slapstick jokes all the time. Buffoonery was Gurudas’s mood. I guess I was their audience, because I broke into laughter constantly in their company.

Janaki was having a harder time. In mid-January, she took the gold ring from Mukunda’s hand and mailed it to Swamiji in San Francisco. The Swami replied:

I thank you very much for your kind presentation, even sacrificing your husband s interest... Regarding your unstable condition of mind, I beg to request you to remember that you have all gone to London with a great mission and responsibility. Please do your duty combinedly without any disruption of peaceful attitudes amongst yourselves. If we show a little disturbance in our own camp that will not be very good example. Therefore, everyone should be forbearing, tolerant and cooperative. That is my special request to you all.

Malati: Saraswati was a very well-behaved baby. She adapted to the total chaos around her. It was chaos with a purpose. Her first word was “gayment,” “engagement”—the word she heard most often.

That old morgue. Gurudas and I went over to look at it, thinking maybe this empty building would make a good temple. But as we approached the address the estate agent had given us—down rainslick cobbled alleyways, pressed on all sides by gothic gray-brick buildings leaning at angles—we both thought the same thing: “The Murders in the Rue Morgue.” We inspected the freezing-cold premises in midday by flashlight and saw marble slabs where in the 1850s they had laid out the corpses. There were little troughs around the edges of these slabs to drain bodily fluids. Puffs of fog shot from our mouths as we started to chuckle, then hoot, and finally howl with laughter. How could this death-haunted place be a home for the Supreme Personality of Godhead and His gorgeous Consort? Besides, they wanted too much money for the place and there was almost no parking. We called the estate agent and told him politely, “No, thank you.”

The girls, more serious in their general behavior, laughed a lot too. It was always fun to tickle a laugh out of Yamuna when she looked so serious. Janaki’s stormy relationship with Mukunda often had us in stitches, as she would say something like, “Muki, I just ironed that shirt. Don’t you be playing drums and get it all sweaty. Yuck!”

Everything we did together had comic overtones. We needed ten pounds to pay for our sankirtan permit, but we couldn’t scrape together two crowns and a ha’penny among us. Mukunda had seen a stamp and coin shop over on High Holborn that had a sign in the window: “We buy autographs.” He told us about this place at a morning meeting.

Gurudas: Hey, I’ve got those two letters from Allen Ginsberg— you think they’d buy ’em?

Shyamasundar: But those are our letters of introduction to Driberg and Barry Miles.

Yamuna: Yeah, but we’ve already met those guys. Why do we still need the letters?

Shyamasundar: OK—and I’ve got that postcard George sent me in December. He signed it.

Mukunda: Hmmm, and I’ve got that letter from Ravi Shankar. Let’s take ’em over there, see what we can get for them.

We three men went over to Spink & Son, 69 Southampton Row, a few blocks from Betterton Street, near Russell Square, curious to learn about this money-for-autographs business. We got £20 for all four letters—and Krishna got us through another scrape.

In a letter to Gurudas and Yamuna, dated January 21, 1969, the Swami wrote: “I am so glad to understand that as soon as your finances are almost depleted, Krishna sees to everything. When we serve some mundane master, He gives us sufficient salary, so when we serve the Supreme Master, how it is possible that He will keep us fasting?”

On the afternoon of January 30, the Beatles gathered on the windy roof of Apple headquarters on Savile Row and gave their last public concert to the world. At George’s invitation, I stood in the wings and watched, pondering the Swami’s amazing appearance at this watershed moment in history, as “Let It Be” reverberated over the rooftops and congested traffic below.

February rolled in, and it was bitterly cold. We never knew from one day to the next whether we might have to leave Betterton Street-the place was for sale, after all—but we carried on as if there was no tomorrow. We missed Swamiji so much, and he would come over in a heartbeat, but we thought it best to postpone his coming until we had a secure address. The next best thing, we decided to call him on the phone, even though transatlantic calls meant a complex procedure with overseas operators and, for us they were expensive.

On February 4, we huddled around the phone at Betterton Street to finally hear the words “Bhaktivedanta here.” We took turns telling him our news (Yamuna crying so hard she could barely speak) and then introduced Swamiji to the new English devotees one by one. The Swami asked Colin if he had learned how to make chapatis—“Oh, yes, Swamiji!”—and at that point, I guess, the Swami knew we were making genuine progress. We said how much we missed him and that we would certainly call for him the moment we had a place. He asked about Mr. Harrison, and we told him we expected George for a special feast at the temple in three days, when he would be in London. The Swami praised us and told us not to worry, that Krishna was planning everything.

**\* \* \***

It’s like a Chinese fire drill, with devotees running around, getting ready for George’s Friday afternoon visit to our Betterton Street warehouse. The girls have been up all night, cooking. Gurudas and I have combed the flower stalls and dumpsters in nearby streets, returning with armloads of only slightly wilted marigolds, free-sias, and roses for garlands. The place smells like a cross between a Bombay curry house and Kew gardens. Despite the winter chill, we’ve got a couple of front windows wide open to listen for George’s approach through the cobbled alleys of Covent Garden.

Varooom! Vrrrr! Varoom! We race to the windows. There’s pony-tailed George stepping out of his black Porsche in jeans and a black, furry Afghan coat. He hears us shout, looks up, waves.

Up the steep stairs I lead him. I make the introductions, everyone shy, exhilarated, grinning—the ladies first: “George, meet my wife Malati.” (I’d already told him: formerly Melanie, hip, busy Frisco motorcycle chick, a dark-haired beauty—now vulnerable and blushingas she slips agarland around his neck.) “And this is our baby, Saraswati, almost eight months old. This is Yamuna.” (My friend since childhood, no-nonsense Joan, contralto voice like an angel.) “And this is her sister Janaki, and Janaki s husband, Mukunda, who’s my old college mate, the first of us to meet Bhaktivedanta Swami.” (Mike Grant, jazz pianist extraordinaire.) “And Gurudas, Yamuna’s husband.” (New York street-wise dreamer and SF flower child.)

George says to us, “I saw that photo of you lot in the Times last Sunday, with the baby chewing on Shyamasundar’s beads. It really impressed me. You looked so, well, you looked transcendental”

George is a natural. That’s the only word for him—unassuming, almost self-effacing, not judging us. He radiates fun and intrinsic spirituality. He has a deep interest to learn new things— and we’re hooked.

“Hey, come over here and see our altar, George!” We lead him in stocking feet across the room to get down on our knees and bow, hands and foreheads on the hard, sisal carpet. From the corner of my eye I see George bowing down too, without hesitation. We look up to see big color pictures of Radha and Krishna on the wall, photos of the Swami and his gurus lining a wooden shelf, incense sticks and burning candles, flowers everywhere, three little deities in tiny dazzling dresses.

“Who’s this, then?” asks George, chuckling and slightly perplexed.

“Lord Jagannath is an ancient form of Krishna,” I say. “We get our English word juggernaut (which means something huge and overpowering) from Him, His original form in the Jagannath temple in India is, like, eight feet tall—and He’s been worshiped there along with His brother Balaram and sister Subhadra for thousands of years. Now He’s in London. What do you think?”

“Really amazing. But it’s going to take a bit of something to get the sods in the bowler hats to go along with all of this. ‘You know something’s happening here, but you don’t know what it is...’”

We all join in the chorus, do you, Mr. Jones?”’

Everyone laughs. Gurudas says, “Yeah, can you imagine—we say ‘This is God’ and they all go, ‘Yeah, right.’ When the Swami said to us, ‘This is God,’ we accepted it immediately, but we were ready for it. That’s why we make it easy: ‘Just chant the Hare Krishna mantra.’ That’s all. The rest can come later.”

“We like to feel we are guests in Krishna’s home,” Malati adds. “And we’re happy when Krishna is happy, so we dress the deities nicely, offer them fresh flowers, incense, nice food. This way we are always thinking about Krishna’s pleasure and it helps us to become Krishna conscious.”

“These photos must be your gurus, then, starting with Swami Bhaktivedanta?”

“Yes, like I was explaining the other day, these saints are in our disciplic succession: BhaktivedantaSwami, his guru Bhaktisiddhanta Swami, his guru Bhaktivinode Thakur, like that, going back millions of years to Lord Brahma. In fact, our line is called the Brahma-sampradaya, the Brahma line. In India, that’s one of the main tests to see if a guru is for real: he can be traced down through one of the four existing disciplic lines from time immemorial.”

“You mean, like Yogananda was begat by Yukteshwar, who was begat by his guru, and all the way back—then you know it’s the real thing, right?” George starts talking about some stuff he’s learned fromAutobiography of a Yogi, and we tell him what a big effect that book has had on our lives too—how it opened our eyes to spiritual possibilities and convinced us to seek a master.

The girls come in bearing huge trays of food in little dishes. They place them one by one on the altar.

Yamuna says, “Now we offer this food to Krishna. He eats it first, and then we eat His remnants. This offered food is called prasadam, Krishna’s mercy.”

Then Mukunda beats his double-ended drum—ba-boom-boom—and says, “We’ll have a little kirtan while Krishna eats.” Janaki tosses a few pillows around, and we sit down, cross-legged. Ba-boom-boom. Mukunda stops, slides a box toward George and opens it. “D’you know how to play one of these?”

George exclaims, “A harmonium!” He lifts it out, starts pumping, tickling the keyboard with some Indian scales, a little shy, composing himself. I open another box and pull out a bow and a long-necked stringed instrument with a bulbous end. “What’s that?” he asks.

“This is an esraj,” I say, placing it in my lap and bowing through a few sliding notes. The others grab kartals and tentatively tinkle and crash out a one-two-three beat. Ba-boom-boom, the mridanga pounds; George on harmonium fingers the keys up and down and gets around the melody I’m laying down, and we’re off, Mukunda leading: “Hare Krishna... Hare Krishna... Krishna Krishna... Hare Hare... Hare Rama... Hare Rama... Rama Rama...”

We all watch George’s face, but his eyes are closed. He’s concentrating. It’s finally happening like we imagined a year—lifetimes— ago: George Harrison is playing and singing the Hare Krishna mantra with us. Our pleasure soars.

After five minutes, Mukunda signals to Yamuna, who takes the lead in pure Billie Holiday style, a half-octave lower than everybody else. George’s eyes flick wide open with surprise, and he smiles to hear her reach up and up for a note, her face glowing with devotion.

Minutes race by and the beat picks up to reach a final blazing crescendo. “Hare Hare!” Then silence. Slowly, all of us bow down while Yamuna mutters prayers.

Just as slowly, we sit back up, seeing each other with that stunned, after-kirtan look. George, flushed with effort and transcendental pleasure, wiping hair out of his face, says in a husky, faltering voice, “That was really divine.”

Gurudas breaks the spell. “Let’s eat!” And we gather around on the floor as the girls lay out plates and bowls and heap them with Indian specialties.

Just then, Saraswati crawls over, grabs a chapati off George’s plate, and holds it up to his face—“Why, thank you, Saraswati,” he says, unsure, but he takes a nibble. Malati grabs her, pulls her back to her lap, and everyone laughs.

George says, “That’s dal, right? I love it. Dal is my favorite. I could live on dal alone.” He shovels plate after plate of food into his mouth with his fingers, slurping, going hmmm, asking, “What is this?” He comments how he remembers these dishes from Rishikesh, how great it all is. “I can’t eat another...”

“Come on, George, just one more little samosa.”

“OK just one,” he laughs, holding his stomach, “No, no more. Agghhh!”

Were all sprawled out on the floor, stuffed, like little kids, giggling and high on prasadam.

“You guys have everything. lean see that. You’ve got great music, beautiful things all around you, great things to read and talk about, families, children—it’s like a whole way of life, isn’t it? With Krishna in it—a society of Krishnas. And the food, that prasadam'. But that’s it, I’ve got to go. I can hardly move. Is there someplace I can wash my hands? Listen, we’ve got to do this again. Can you all come out to my place one day soon?”

We gather at the open windows. It’s dark out now. We see George under the streetlight below. He looks up and hollers, “Haribol'” and starts walking down the street—the wrong way! We shout, “This way!” and sit quietly on the windowsills thinking about our new friend long after the sound of his shifting gears has faded away.

On the evening of February 4, George checked into University College Hospital to have his tonsils removed. Jimmy Doody got a letter from the Swami dated February 8: “Please convey my blessings to the others there. I was so glad to talk with them the other day on the telephone. I enjoyed hearing their voices, and I was happy that they were also very much pleased to hear me. I will be glad to hear if Mr. George Harrison attended the love feast ceremony yesterday as I was informed he would.”

**\* \* \***

By the middle of February, Mukunda and Gurudas finally secure official permission to hold sankirtan in designated public places, so we hit the streets and literally startle London. We’ve grown in number now with the addition of a half dozen English kids, and sankirtan is the perfect activity to get them tuned-up for devotional service. Mukunda has taught Colin to play mridanga, so we can go out in two shifts. We print flyers and distribute them. We chant and dance up and down Oxford Street, Piccadilly, Leicester Square, Tottenham Court Road—all the major pedestrian centers of this huge metropolis.

On page 15 of the February 28 issue of IT (#51) there’s a big photo of Mukunda, Gurudas, and I on sankirtan in Hanover Square, just off Oxford Circus, in the center of London. We are surrounded by large type that reads, “Beautiful Blue/Smiling Cowherd Boy/Steals Their Hearts.” Gurudas announces in the article below the photo:

Our spiritual teacher, Swami Bhaktivedanta, requested that we sing “Hare Krishna” in the streets of London, so on February 17, 1969, we had our first street chanting in Hanover Square and in front of Apple on Savile Row. From time to time you may pass a few happy people singing and dancing in the streets glorifying the all-attractive Krishna—

Praise the Lord  
with every breath  
with bell and horn  
with drum and timbrel  
with lute and harp  
with song and dance  
Praise the Lord.  
(Psalm 150)

Krishna Consciousness is not “Imported from India.” One person said, “This chant has blown my mind, and put the pieces back together again.” Everyone was nicely receptive here, as they are all over, and they genuinely wanted to know “Just what are we doing?” So while we sang, one or two of us told of the joys of Krishna Consciousness and the beautiful blue smiling cowherd boy Krishna stealing our hearts.

A policeman appeared and said be careful with obstructing, smiled for a second, and moved on. But, as always for us, the fruits of other people’s reactions are secondary to the fact that they themselves hear the jewel of Krishna s Holy name. JAI SRI KRISHNA.

George phoned and invited us to come out to his house in Esher on Sunday afternoon, February 23.

**\* \* \***

We got lucky with the weather that day. It was cold but clear, though, some clouds were building in the west. Ten devotees (ten and a half, with Saraswati) piled into my pickup along with our instruments and a ton of food and other gear, and drove out to George's place about twenty-five miles southwest of London, on the A3.

We held a meeting before we left. I was like George's filter, a guard dog between him and the devotees. My close friends, the original six, they were OK—I would trust them with anything—but we had new people with us now, Colin, Tim, Dick, and Pamela, and I was worried that someone might do something uncool or ask George for something or otherwise let their ego intrude. So I gave a little talk about how we should act around George, like only talk about Krishna and the Swami, watch your ego, be humble. We unanimously agreed that the one working rule in our relationship with George would be to never, ever ask George for anything. Just give to him, always.

George and Pattie are standing out front when we arrive (they must have heard our rig backfiring up their driveway). “This is my wife Pattie,” George says. Introductions are made, pranaming, all of us stunned by Pattie’s grace and beauty. After admiring the Day-Glo artwork decorating George’s house and the body of his Mini Cooper parked in front, we remove our muddy shoes and plunge into the house, shedding the stress of the journey with exuberance and cheer, everyone giggling. Malati whips out a couple of garlands from a plastic sack and drapes them over Georges and Patties heads.

A medium-sized black guy with a huge afro and a shy, gap-toothed grin strolls over in blue-and-white-striped bell-bottoms, and George says, “I’d like you guys to meet a good friend, Billy Preston—Billy, these are the ... the lot I was ... this is the Krishna temple, no, the Radha-Krishna temple.”

“Hey, fellow Americans. How ’ya doin’?”

George tells us that Billy is in town to play piano and organ on the Let It Be album, which the Beatles were still working on. Gurudas high-fives Billy, brothers him, and tells him about his days in the civil rights movement, marching with Martin Luther King.

Then baby Saraswati gets dumped in my arms as Pattie and the girls, yakking away, scoot off to the kitchen with shopping bags and armloads of precooked prasadam. Pattie’s making raita, a yogurt-cucumber-tomato dish she learned in India. The spacious living room is filled with incense smoke. We ooh and ahh at wall hangings and pictures spread around the living room, pictures of Krishna, Shiva,

Ganesh, and the goddess of music, Saraswati; photos of Yogananda, Sri Yukteshwar. And there’s the big photo of Bhaktivedanta Swami I gave George a couple of weeks ago. Gurudas points to some prints on the wall of dogs smoking cigars and playing poker around a green felt table. One dog is palming an ace. “Just see,” Gurudas says, “in the material world even the dogs are cheating.”

George puts on an afternoon raga by Ravi Shankar, and we kick back and get mellow. The girls return and relax on chairs and Rajasthani pillows scattered around the floor. Mukunda spots a big sit-down harmonium in the corner. It’s white and covered with psychedelic flowers, and he goes over to it and pumps out a tune—“Hey, this is pretty nice—a lot more sound than our little squeezebox.” George asks, “Shall we have a kirtan?” Hey, George, that’s like asking kids if they want ice cream. .

Everyone shifts around, grabbing instruments. Mukunda picks up the mridanga, starts tuning it—thwack! thwack! Billy sits behind the harmonium, goofs us with a couple of bars of ragtime and a Bach fugue. I unpack my esraj and rosin the bow. George rattles around at his guitar rack, then picks one up and plugs it into a small amp—blasting our ears with feedback as he dials it down. Then he sheepishly plucks a few chords and says, “This is a little song I’ve been working on. D’ya want to hear it?” (Yeah, yeah!) “It’s very simple. I call it ‘Something.’”

He clears his throat and feels his way into it slowly. “Something in the way she smiles, attracts me like no other lover—Something in the way she moooves me...” Were awestruck, of course, transported beyond space and time by George’s sweet words and sounds. We don’t know whether to look at him or just close our eyes and dream... Chuckling when it’s over, he says, “Do you think it’ll sell?” Everyone laughs. “Actually,” he says, “it’s about Krishna, but I can’t say that, can I?”

Someone clangs kartals, I sketch out our favorite melody on the esraj, and one by one—hand claps, drum, harmonium, guitar—Malati grabs a tambourine—we fuse into a wonderful, slow, melodic rendition of the Hare Krishna mantra. At a nod from George, Yamuna leads with her powerful voice. The tempo picks up, crashes to a frenzy, then slows again. We change lead singers, change melodies, some stand and dance. Billy’s all over the place, like a down-home gospel meet; George switches from guitar to sitar and back—on and on, hour after hour the ecstasy builds—until finally by mutual consent we crash out flat, trying to catch our wind and laughing our heads off.

Out of breath, sweat rolling down his glowing face, George says, “That’s it! We’ve got to make a record! The Radha Krishna Temple—I can see it now, the first Sanskrit song in the top ten.”

We eat a long, sumptuous prasadam feast, gossiping, joking, listening to records, and talking philosophy deep into the night. As we line up at the door to leave, everyone embracing and thanking everyone else, Janaki remembers, and she digs out a beautiful, hand-embroidered beadbag with chanting beads inside and hands it to George. “I forgot to give you this,” she says, and George takes it with both hands and thanks her deeply with his eyes.

“Are you sure you guys will be all right on the road? It’s kind of late, and it’s starting to snow out there.”

No problem, George. Good night, Pattie! A million thanks! Hare Krishna! Take care, Billy! Krishna will guide our chariot.

Only one “tyre” blew out on the way home. We pulled over to the shoulder. It was dark, snow gusting as cars swished past us on the A3. We managed without a flashlight to get the truck jacked up and the tire changed. Then the truck wouldn’t start. “OK, Gurudas, here’s the crank. You know what to do.”

While everyone else waited in the truck, Gurudas, in his New York overcoat, yellow skirts, and flip-flops, hunched over the front of the truck, snow flying by him in the headlights. Snarling, he jerked that damn crank handle around—and around. Suddenly the crank got caught in his dhoti just as the little motor fired and spun the crank—and four yards of yellow cloth were stripped from his body, flying up into the air like a giant flag waving in the wind.

Gurudas just stood there stunned, his bare white legs and knickers exposed in the high beams of passing motorists. We laughed so hard we couldn’t move for about five minutes. Finally, we put the old girl into gear and chugged off down the motorway at 30 mph in a cloud of blue smoke. Wait’ll George hears about this!

On March 4, a few days after our visit to Esher, George is interviewed at the Apple offices by David Wigg. The interview is aired on BBC Radio 1’s “Scene and Heard,” one of the most popular radio programs in Britain.

In India, they chant all the time. “На-re” calls upon the energy of Krishna. By making the repetition of that, Hare Krishna, if you say it long enough, you build up this identification. Whatever you truly identify with you become one with it. So it’s really a method of becoming one with God. It’s the same sort of thing as meditation. It has a quicker effect because music is a powerful force. “God likes me when I work but loves me when I sing”... The response is in the form of bliss.

These first meetings and talks with George were very special to us, of course, but as young disciples on a mission for our spiritual master in London, our busy lives went on without too much thought about George or what he was doing. We were driven by an intense desire to see Krishna, that’s all. We risked everything. And, yes, we began to see Krishna—Krishna’s magic—how He untied the knots, tore down the walls, provided everything we needed to spread His name. We saw our connection with George in this light as well: “Oh, you want to meet George Harrison, just to tell him about Me? OK, here he is.”

George liked our naivete, our otherworldliness, that we were different. What was so important to his regular friends—fame, money, beauty, getting loaded—didn’t interest us at all. We wanted only self-realization; we wanted Krishna. In many ways we were much like George: same age, same working-class background, similar education, same rock-and-roll music—and then LSD, and the search for life’s answers in mystic India. These were common denominators that deepened the attraction on both sides.

George liked it, too, that we didn’t misuse our relationship. We didn’t go screaming to the press or capitalize by going public with our growing friendship. If anything, George was always trying to drag us, reluctantly, into the limelight. He appreciated the simple, intimate, one-on-one, almost underground, nature of our association. He had his rock-and-roll friends and he had his transcendental friends, and he preferred to keep them separate. Quietly, privately, we met and exchanged rare words and emotions.

March 1969 was not a great month for George, actually. Police raided his Esher home on March 12, led by the notorious Sgt. Norman Pilcher, who had recently busted Mick Jagger, Eric Clapton, John Lennon, and Brian Jones. Pilcher came with nine other cops and a sniffer dog named Yogi. They claimed to find a small piece of hashish, and George claimed they planted it. The trial was held on March 31 at Esher and Walton Magistrates Court. George and Pattie were each fined £250. George was famously quoted as saying, “I’m a tidy man. I keep my socks in the sock drawer and stash in the stash box. It’s not mine.”

We knew about the sensationalism of British tabloids, so we pretty much ignored what we heard. George was our friend, a devotee of Krishna, and we didn’t judge what he might do in his private life. We were busy with sankirtan and spreading Krishna consciousness during every waking hour in an unfamiliar and sometimes hostile city, and at the same time working hard to keep a roof over our heads, feed the kid, keep the truck running, and maintain our spiritual practices.

**\* \* \***

We heard that Ravi Shankar was in London and would be giving a concert at Alumni Hall on Sunday, March 30. The concert would be followed by a reception and dinner at the home of Mr. S. S.

Dhawan, the Indian High Commissioner, in the heavily guarded diplomatic enclave at Kensington Palace Gardens. In our usual bold style, we decided to crash the party. Our twenty-year-old red truck, overflowing with strange-looking people, pulled up to the gate. A bobby came to my window. “We re going to the reception for Ravi Shankar,” I said. “Our Invitation is in the truck here somewhere.”

It was dark, and we shuffled stuff around in the crowded cab, the baby fussing. Finally, the copper said, “All right, I can see you’re dressed for the occasion,” and motioned us through. Once again, Krishna opened the gates for His reckless preachers.

We rolled up the circular driveway, past the Mercedes-Benzes and Bentleys, and stopped under the portico. Everyone jumped out and marched up to the front door as if we belonged there. I held the truck door open for the startled valet and told him, “Whatever you do, don’t kill the engine until you’re finished parking.” The butler whispered to Mukunda, “Whom shall I announce is calling?” Then, as we strode into the foyer, we heard the butler say very loudly, “Ladies and gentlemen, the International Society for Consciousness!”

A hush fell over the roomful of elegant guests clustered under the huge chandelier in their tuxedos and ballgowns, cocktails in their hands. Ravi wheeled around and then came to our rescue. “Oh, hi there! These are friends of mine from California.” And the party resumed.

We were the evening’s sensation. We had a good chat with Ravi and otherwise spread our Krishna message to some very special people that night. We became friends with the High Commissioner himself, who invited us back for a private dinner the following weekend.

**\* \* \***

Meanwhile, the Swami is bounding all over the place: San Francisco, Los Angeles, Hawaii, New York, Buffalo, Boston, Columbus, and the New Vrindaban ashram in West Virginia. We can’t keep up with him. He wants to come to London as soon as possible. But at the end of March 1969, we get hit with a bomb: Nigel has sold the Betterton Street building and we must leave the premises.

We hear clomping up the stairwell to the top floor. We hear, through the ceiling, sounds of furniture being moved. Nigel phones and informs us that new owners are moving into the building and that we need to find other accommodations. We’d known this place was for sale, but with so much on our plates and dreading the inevitable, we had postponed finding a more permanent place. Suddenly, it hits home. It’s every man for himself!

Through some of his connections Mukunda finds a place for himself and Janaki out in Hackney, a distant northern suburb. Gurudas and Yamuna find a room with the Sharma family in a South London suburb near Clapham Common. Malati and I scour the classifieds and find a garret for rent in a four-story house at 11 Balham Park Road, SW 12. We now live about three miles from Gurudas and Yamuna’s place, and thirteen miles from Hackney. In two short days, we’re spread out all over London.

For the first time since we’ve been in London, I’m depressed, like, for days on end. Seven months we’ve been here, and still no temple—no way we can bring Swamiji over to this mess. OK, we’ve made Hare Krishna a little popular in London, at least among the hippies. We have four or five boys and girls ready to become devotees. We’ve made friends with George Harrison and the Beatles, but that’s going really slow; we don’t want to spoil the relationship by going too fast. We re broke, spread out all over—we might as well be living in separate countries. Depressing!

Then I get a letter from my spiritual master:

My Dear Shyamasundar,

Please accept my blessings. I have received one letter from your devoted wife, Malati, dated April 2, 1969, and there is a new return address written on the envelope. I wish that you inform me as to the position of this new address. I also understand from Malati’s letter that you have been feeling some disturbance. Perhaps this is because there is difficulty there in finding a temple that you feel that you are not doing nicely, but this is not a fact. Your efforts there are especially nice and pleasing to me. Actually, Sankirtana Party is our main business, and even you do not find a temple there for some time it is not cause for any disturbance... and surely you will come out fully successful.

The Swami s letter helps a lot.

**\* \* \***

In early April, we received our first shipment of two thousand Back to Godhead magazines from America, for distribution in England. We paid 15€ apiece for them, and sold them on the street for three shillings—about 38€. BTG #23, the April 18 issue, contains some startling news:

PRABHUPADA

The word Prabhupada is a term of the utmost reverence in Vedic religious circles, and it signifies a great saint even among saints. The word actually has two meanings: first, one at whose feet (Pada) there are many Prabhus (a term meaning “master,” which the disciples of a Guru use in addressing each other). The second meaning is one who is always found at the Lotus Feet of Krishna (the Supreme Master).

In the line of disciplic succession through which Krishna Consciousness is conveyed to mankind, there have been a number of figures of such spiritual importance as to be called Prabhupada:

Srila Rupa Goswami Prabhupada executed the will of his Master, Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu, and therefore he and his associate Goswamis are called Prabhupada. Srila Bhakti Siddhanta Saraswati Goswami Thakur executed the will of Srila Bhaktivinode Thakur, and therefore he is also addressed as Prabhupada. Our Spiritual Master, Om Vishnupad 108 Sri Srimad Bhaktivedanta Swami Maharaj has, in the same way, executed the will of Srila Bhakti Siddhanta Saraswati Goswami Prabhupada in carrying the message of love of Krishna to the Western world, and therefore we American and European humble servants of His Divine Grace, from all the different centers of the Sankirtan Movement, have followed in the footsteps of Srila Rupa Goswami Prabhupada, and prefer to address His Grace our Spiritual Master as Prabhupada, and he has kindly said “Yes.”

Since late 1968, apparently, the Swami had preferred to be called “Prabhupada.” We hadn’t heard about this until April 1969. It took us a while to remember to call him Prabhupada (we pronounced it “Prabhupad”), but we gradually got used to it. More formally, you could also say “Srila Prabhupada,” which means something like “Revered Prabhupada.”

Personally, I was locked on to pleasing the Swami—Prabhupada— by pleasing George. I knew I had a special relationship with George. I wanted to protect that friendship, not allow the other devotees to jeopardize it with precipitate actions. George said many times, “If it’s anything to do with Krishna, I want to go through you first.” My main service had become keeping George happy and guiding him gradually into Krishna consciousness. But George was often traveling or otherwise preoccupied, so that service was going very slow, and I felt idle and indisposed.

At least spring had finally come, and for the first time since we’d been in England, the sun was out and warming that sodden isle. The Balham Park Road house had a nice garden with driveway access, so at least I had an open space to start carving Jagannath deities again. That would keep me busy and take my mind off not having a temple yet.

I go out to a barge-dismantling yard in Greenwich and find a twelve-foot beam of old-growth Douglas fir, salvaged from a barge built in British Columbia. Once again, it’s the perfect piece of ocean-borne wood for Lord Jagannath: the З'-wide by 2-thick dimensions will be perfect for three Jagannath murtis. The thing weighs a ton, but somehow Tim and I get it onto my truck and over to Balham, where we cut it into three sections. I build some sawhorses, and the carving begins. The depression drops away and, by Prabhupadas mercy, I am focused and ecstatic again. A huge lilac bush beside my workplace bursts into purple bloom, filling the air with a wonderful fragrance, and butterflies flutter by my face as Krishna, then Balaram, and then little Subhadra (she always gets made last) take their sublime shapes.

The devotees meet downtown for sankirtan as often as we can, and we still have our regular program at Yin-Yang restaurant on Tuesday nights. Yamuna and Gurudas live closest to me and Malati, so we visit them more often. Mukunda and Janaki are so far away, but we telephone each other almost every day. Now that Jagannath deities are being carved, we write to ask Prabhupada if we can stage a Ratha-yatra festival this summer; a huge Jagannath parade in London might be just the event to kick us back into gear. We need a rhino to chase for the Swami.

Meanwhile, Mukunda and Gurudas are running around looking at empty buildings and redundant churches; Mukunda is still trying to get ISKCON legalized in the UK and tuning pianos; and I’m still out in Balham carving deities in the yard and building a bathroom for Mr. Parikh. Prabhupada regularly sends Yamuna transliterations and tapes of prayers and songs, so she’s still working on our repertoire, and Malati is caring for little Saraswati. Not much going on, but the seeds are planted and spring sunshine will soon bring blossoms, we hope.

On May 12, I received an urgently awaited letter from Srila Prabhupada: “The Rathayatra may be fixed up from the 20th July to the 27th July.”

We choose July 30 in order to give ourselves as much time as possible—as it is, we’ll have little more than two months to prepare for this gigantic festival. Mukunda and Gurudas start working on parade permits. Yamuna takes charge of decorations for the rath car and the deities.

In my typical style, I had impulsively promised Prabhupada to build three gigantic rath cars, with each car being twice as big as any previous car. I cleared enough workspace in the yard, measured the width of the driveway entrance gate at 11 Balham Park Road, and designed the first car to be a few inches narrower, so that at least we could get it out onto the street.

The car dimensions, on paper, were something like 20' wide and 30' long. Where would the money come from to pay for the wood? Wood wasn’t cheap in England like it was in California. And who would build it with me? Despite these challenges, the project took on a life of its own. All the devotees, including the new English kids, plus sons of some of our Indian friends, pitched in. They raised money and came over to help whenever they could. Slowly this monster car took shape in the side yard of the Balham house. Using techniques we had developed for the rath cars in San Francisco, I doubled the size of everything. To save money, I bought used lumber off demolition sites and used two bolts where there should have been three.

I would call Mukunda and Gurudas and tell them how much money I needed for wood, bolts, or nails, and somehow they would deliver. We got way behind on time and human effort. I can’t remember working harder in my life. The behemoth grew and became the talk of the neighborhood. At one point, we could see that just finishing one car was nearly impossible, so I wrote to Prabhupada that we will have only one car this year, but it will be a very big car. And we’ll have three cars next year, for sure.

Prabhupada urged us on:

69-05-26

My Dear Shyamasundar,

Your anxiety for my health may not be increased, because I am keeping quite fit nowadays. There was a little backache in Boston for two or three days, but now I am walking in this hilly tract \[New Vrindaban\] up and down. So don’t be anxious. There are about 16 centers now, so we have to take care of at least 16 centers daily. Otherwise, how these branches can be conducted? Regarding Rathayatra, this must be done at any cost. I may go or not go; that is not important. But Rathayatra Festival, as you have already made program, somehow or other it must be done.

If Prabhupada can work so hard at age 73, why can’t we? We double our efforts.

On May 27, Prabhupada wrote to Yamuna about ivhy we decorate Krishna:

You have inquired about the Jagannatha Car. The crimson color with silver decorations is quite all right. We can decorate the cars very fascinatingly with gold, silver, and other shiny metallic embroidery work as far as possible. The idea is the more we decorate Krishna, Who is non-different from His car also, the more we become decorated indirectly. We are compared as the shadow of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and as it is stated in the Bible also, man is made after God. We understand from the scriptures that Krishna has His Vigraha, or Spiritual Body, exactly like a man who has two hands, two legs and all similar features. If you decorate your face, you do not see directly how your face has become beautiful, but when you see the reflection of your face in the mirror, then indirectly you can see the beauty. Therefore, by serving Krishna directly the result of the service indirectly comes to us.

The simple bottom deck of the rath car measured 15' wide by 20' long. 2" x 8" pine timbers ran lengthwise, nailed to 2 x 4s crossing beneath. I pulled a wheel out of a wrecked lorry in a junkyard and welded one side of it to the center of a steel axle; the other side of the car’s wheel was bolted to the deck to allow the front axle to swivel. Two ten-foot wood 4" x 4"s were slipped over this axle like a yoke, extending out and connected by a crossbar. That allowed us to crudely steer the thing. I found four 5'-diameter spoked wagon wheels at a scrapyard. They were rather old, but they seemed sound, and using them would save a lot of time. The lower deck and chassis, now mounted on wheels, slowly grew above the hedges in Balham.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupadas biggest concern for his London devotees, as expressed in every letter he wrote to us during this period, was that we live together. Time after time he said, “Now you try to assemble together in the same spirit as you were doing, temple or no temple,” and, “So try your best immediately to live together. I am very much eager to see that you are again living together.” Krishna must have heard the pleas of his intimate devotee, Srila Prabhupada, because we finally got a break.

On the fifth day of June, Gurudas was standing in the queue at Chartered Bank on Regent Street, managing his six-pound account, when a well-dressed Britisher approached him and said, “You’re a Hare Krishna, aren’t you?”

“Yes. We’re here establishing our new center in London.”

“You don’t by chance happen to know someone named Sam? He’s a Krishna bloke—lived in my building in San Francisco?”

“You must mean Shyamasundar. Yes, he’s here with me now, in London.”

It turned out that this man was the same David Cocking who had lived in Peggy’s apartments on Ashbury Street. I used to collect his rent every month—the same guy who told me about Chartered Bank. And what’s more (Krishna’s magic!) he was now an estate agent working for a big firm in downtown London.

And it just so happened that his company had an empty five-story building with a basement at 7 Bury Place, just two short blocks from the British Museum and fifty paces from Oxford Street, the main thoroughfare for this greatest city in the world. The Bury Place building was the former quarters of the Trinitarian Bible Society, distributors of the Good Word since 1831. О Krishna, You’ve done it again!

Gurudas followed David to his office near Regent Street, and obtained a set of keys. Then we all met at 7 Bury Place and toured the premises. Afterward, we gathered outside in front of the door we would come to know so well.

"Yamuna: Looks perfect! О Krishna, thank You! Prabhupada will love it!

Malati: Yeah, but no elevator. We’ll have to get used to walking up and down six flights—my legs are killing me already!

Janaki: And how ’bout all those old books we found up there? The Love of God, Peace With God—seems like this place was meant for us.

Shyamasundar: Yeah, but it needs a lot of work! That front room is just too small to hold many people. But I think if we take out that one wall and maybe the false ceiling, make it higher, it could work.

Yamuna: Yes, much, much work. Cleaning, too. Looks like nobody’s been in this place for years.

Shyamasundar: But we’ve gotta have this place, right? All agreed?

All: Of course! No question! Haribol\\

Mukunda: How much is it a week, did you say, Gurudas?

Gurudas: Well, since David knows us from Frisco, I think he’s put in a good word for us with his partners. There’s some kind of a legal thing going on and the place is in limbo. David said it’ll probably take years to sort out. So they want 35 pounds a week plus three months’ deposit and some kind of letter from someone to guarantee the rent if we default.

George had previously offered us Apple as a guarantor if we found a place, so that part was good. Now we needed cash. A month’s rent would cost us £140—about $350 US—so three months would come to about $1050. On June 19, Mukunda sent Prabhupada a telegram asking him if we could borrow $1025. On June 21, Prabhupada wrote to Yamuna: “The day before yesterday I received one cable from Mukunda asking for a loan of $1,025.00 for depositing three months’ rent on the house. I have advised New York to cable the money immediately, and I hope Mukunda has already received the money and the transaction is nicely terminated... If arrangement is made to call me to London I will go there as first preference.”

Money was so scarce in those days that Prabhupada had to borrow from Peter to pay Paul, shifting resources around for London, his number-one priority. He wrote to Satsvarupa in Boston: “Yesterday I received one telegram from London to send them by cable $1,025.00. They require this for their house there. This money was to be for a down payment on purchasing an IBM machine for our books, and therefore I now need some money. If you have got some extra money, you can help me.”

Gurudas and Mukunda finalized negotiations on the building, got a letter from Apple to guarantee the lease, and got the down payment money from Prabhupada. We scrounged enough for the first week’s rent and we were in. Before the ink was dry on the lease papers we each sent a letter to the Swami, and he replied to each of us with glowing praise and ideas to help us. To me he wrote:

“With great joy I understand that at last you have secured one nice house which can accommodate twenty or thirty people. I have immediately asked Trivikrama das Brahmacari of Buffalo to go there, and he is sending Mukunda one telegram confirming this. I am also advising Bibhavati and Ishan das to go there. Next I shall try to find out some other brahmacaris to go there as you require immediately some men for your great endeavor. You write to say you are too much strained in various ways, but by your such activities, Krishna will be very much pleased. I think because Krishna is pleased, therefore you have got at last a very nice place... Regarding the altar, the design which you have submitted is nice, but I wish to add that underneath the Jagannathas a throne of Radha-Krishna should be there. I am enclosing herewith a picture of the throne, and on the back side is my idea of an altar design.”

What the Swami referred to as “too much strained in various ways”: I was building this giant rath car out in Balham almost single-handedly, and now I was dashing back and forth to London for demolition work at Bury Place. With Dick, Tim, and Colin helping, we knocked out the ground-floor interior wall to extend the main room the full length of the building. But there still wouldn’t be space enough to embrace the design I’d made, the one based on the Ajanta cave temple. The ceiling had to go.

I went to the local ironmongers and bought two big sledgehammers, some wrecking bars, and a big crosscut saw. There were no other tools for it. Without thinking much, we tore into the false ceiling and brought it down piece by piece, cutting out joists, old timbers, planking, lath and plaster—the whole thing came crashing down in a few days. We looked around the scene of total destruction: Yes, it’s big enough. The ground-floor room now had a fourteen-foot ceiling and, except for a narrow passageway and staircase on one side, it ran the full width and length of the building. Mukunda came into the newly-demolished room wearing his three-piece suit. He was on his way to the Borough of Camden Council offices to look into what was called a “planning permit.” Brushing plaster dust from his immaculate shoulders, he said, “Jeez, you guys, we haven’t got permission to do any of this yet!”

When we see what a huge job it is to get permits and build a fancy temple room, we write to Prabhupada again, telling him that it may take some time before we’re ready for him. He writes back: “It doesn’t matter when I go there. In the meantime, you give your full attention to decorating the temple... Make everything slow but sure. To do things hastily and incorrectly is not good. There is a proverb in Bengali sabure mawaphale. This means that all valuable nuts like almonds, macadamias, walnuts, coconuts, etc. all take a long time to fructify. Anything valuable takes a little time to come into existence. Therefore there is no harm in waiting for the best thing. But everything is well that ends well: That should be the principle.”

We kept the doors closed and tried to be as quiet as possible, but we were making an obvious impact on the neighborhood. We were OK with the neighbor on one side of the building—a pub facing onto New Oxford Street that did most of its business at night— but the other side was residential, with people crowded into council flats the full length of the street. 7 Bury Place emitted late-night banging of sledgehammers, the furious buzz of Skil saws and drills, the thump-thump-crash! of big timbers and chunks of ceiling collapsing—and whenever we did open the door, huge clouds of plaster dust flew out. We would stack debris inside the front door, then late at night haul it out to my truck. The next morning, I would drive it to an inner-city waste disposal depot, where they weighed the truck before and after dumping. With this coming and going at all hours and the constant noise, the neighbors became agitated.

So Mukunda, Gurudas, and the ladies with the baby would go around the neighborhood in good clothes, knock on doors, smile all over and act cute, and try to soothe ruffled feathers. It was all very civilized. After all, this was Bloomsbury, for centuries the home of British erudition (British Museum, University of London), religion (St. George’s Church, built by Nicholas Hawksmoor in 1731) and theology (the Swedenborg Society was across the street, the original Theosophical Society was just down the road). Charles

Darwin, Charles Dickens, Virginia Woolf, and William Butler Yeats had once strolled down three-block Bury Place.

The neighbors probably sicced them on us, but one day, we got a visit from the building inspectors, a Mr. Savage and a Mr. Black. Savage and Black were quite civil about everything, but they informed us we would have to cease work until we had the proper permits. I was steamed and went with Mukunda to see their boss, a Mr. Wallace. We warned him that with the ceiling torn down, the building was now unsafe. We also told him about all the good work we were doing, like reforming drug addicts (there was a photo of his long-haired son on his desk) and maybe we hit a nerve, because he softened a bit and said, “Well, if you put in two stout I-beams thusly”—and he drew us a little picture—“you can continue. But mind, you must apply for planning permission.”

The price of the two 50' x 6" steel I-beams was way beyond our means. On a whim, I walked over to Mick Jagger’s office in the Fitzrovia district, a few blocks away. Mick wasn’t there, so I left a note for him with his secretary. When I called back later, she said, “Yes, Mick has approved your request for £50, and you can come by and pick up the check.”

When the truck arrived with the I-beams, we had to figure out how to get these huge items into the building. Finally, we removed the 6'-square plate-glass front window and swung the beams by crane into the temple room. Traffic was blocked for hours in narrow Bury Place.

We used the tiny car jack from my truck to lift the end of each beam six inches at a time. Then we would slide a block in under it, lift the other end six inches, block it, and slowly, days later, the steel I-beams rose into place, fourteen feet from the floor along both sides of the room.

Meanwhile, back in Balham: the rectangular 15' x 20' lower deck is finished and, now mounted on wheels, stands about three feet off the ground. To create a sense of largeness, the upper deck will be six feet up from the lower deck and much bigger at 30' x

19'4"—not a full twenty feet because it has to fit through the gate. We run twenty-four 4 x 4s and 6 x 6s up and on the diagonal from the bottom deck and start framing the second deck. Photos of the rath car in Puri, India, showed little porches jutting out on each side, so we copy this, and the upper deck takes on an octagonal shape. From my fourth-floor window, it looks like a giant cookie cutter, slowly growing up toward my outstretched hands.

So my schedule went like this: work all day on the rath car in Balham, drive to London at dark, work all night at Bury Place (despite the ruling to stop work), then drive back to Balham at dawn to sleep a few hours. This went on day after day through June and July 1969. There was one functional bathroom at Bury Place, so Tim, Colin, and a few other boys sneaked onto the premises and lived there, hiding their sleeping bags and toothbrushes during the day. Some nights, if I was too exhausted to drive back to Balham, I crashed there as well. During that time, we allowed an English boy named Robert to move in. Robert seemed OK at first—highly intelligent—and we knew about his ongoing situation with meds and psychiatric treatments, but when he kept going off about UFOs and started wearing bed sheets wrapped around his naked body, we asked him to leave. He could visit, we told him, but no more living in the temple. Trivikram came over from the States and, along with Ted Berk, Mukunda’s poet friend from New York, also moved in and gave much needed help. Ishan, Bibhavati dasi, and their young baby came over from Canada, at Prabhupada s request, and they, too, took up clandestine residence at Bury Place.

Bibhavati told us about her meeting with John and Yoko three weeks earlier in Montreal at the famous "bed-in” at the Queen Elizabeth Hotel. Krishna devotees joined a host of celebrities in room 1742 and led boisterous kirtans. The resulting mantralike Lennon song, “Give Peace a Chance,” reflects their presence. The last verse of the song references John and Yoko, Timothy Leary, Rosemary, Tommy Smothers, Bobby Dylan, Tommy Cooper, Derek Taylor, Norman Mailer, Allen Ginsberg, and “Hare
