Krishna, Hare Krishna.” Prabhupada wrote to us about it as well: “You will be glad to know that Mr. John Lennon had an interview with Bibhavati, and a nice article was published in the Montreal Star in which it is understood that he also is interested in our Krishna Consciousness Movement.”

One mid-June day, George Harrison phones me from his vacation villa in Sardinia and says, “I’ve written this little ditty, I wanted to sing it to you—see what you think.” Then he sings “Here Comes the Sun” to me over the telephone. I’m speechless. “Do you think people will like it?” he asks.

“George, it’s wonderful.”

“You know, it’s about Krishna coming into my life, but I’ve got to disguise that a bit.”

Then I tell him about our new temple in Bloomsbury, but Derek Taylor has already called him regarding Apple being our guarantor, so he just says, “I can hardly wait to see it.”

Mukunda and Gurudas are furiously pounding the pavement to get planning permission for Bury Place from Camden Council and a Ratha-yatra parade permit from Scotland Yard. Also, they are trying to raise money to satisfy my unending thirst for wood and bolts out in Balham (“Hey, Mukunda, I need a hundred more six-inch carriage bolts with nuts, and two hundred one-inch washers!”) What’s more, we need money to pay the Bury Place rent plus the rent on our individual apartments. And we have to maintain our sankirtan program on the streets and distribute BTGs. Where did we get the energy? We got it from Krishna, that’s for sure, and knowing that Prabhupada would be coming soon. We’ve got a place now, and soon Ratha-yatra will display Lord Krishna’s glories to all of London.

The rath car’s second deck, with all of its angles and miter cuts, takes a long while to build. Shortages of manpower and money slow the work. Malati keeps the workforce supplied with food and drink during the increasingly warm days, and we toil till dark, then dash off to London to work some more.

And then in the middle of all this, Chris O’Dell calls me one day from Apple and says, “John Lennon would like you to carve his piano.” What? “Like, carve his piano.” So I go over to Savile Row, and John shows me his upright white piano sitting against the wall in Apple Studios, in the basement of the building. He says, “Just carve Krishna into that popup sheet-music rack. It’s made of solid wood. Can you do it?” I envision Gopal Krishna holding His little flute and say, “Sure, John, no problem.” (Rath car to build, Bury Place to build—but this is John Lennon, after all.)

I appreciated like never before the magic of Krishna’s energy, realizing how Krishna must endow the Swami with his relentless energy. Swamiji has told us that Krishna gives His devotees the transcendental power to perform inhuman feats in their desire for devotional service. OK, then, Krishna, here’s the deal: I want to please my spiritual master, and there’s so much work to do, so You may help me or not help me, but without Your help I will surely fail, and Srila Prabhupada will not be happy.

I start spending a few hours every few days in the Apple Studios, carving on the piano. One day, I look up and there’s Mick Jagger looking over my shoulder. “What’s this, then?” he asks.

I say, “I’m carving Krishna into John’s piano.”

“Brilliant. I hope we won’t be disturbing you. We’ll be doing a bit of rehearsing.”

I look around and watch as the rest of the Rolling Stones stride into the room and start setting up their instruments. A gang of roadies and technicians begins adjusting the amplifiers and sound equipment. It turns out they’re training guitarist Mick Taylor to replace Brian Jones—it was big headlines when Jones left the band on June 9. The band hasn’t performed in public for about two years, and now they’ve scheduled a free concert in Hyde Park on July 5. The rehearsal is intense. Day after day, week after week, I dig the Rolling Stones, live and loud—jeez, they’re in the same room!

Time, time, time is on my side, yes it is!

And while I chip and carve on John’s white piano, I consider how closely my life over the past five years has been intertwined with Rolling Stones’ songs—from the break with my parents’ world (“I’m free to do what I want any old time”), to my drug years (“I can’t get no satis-fack-shun”), the Owsley DMT trip (“Surprise, surprise, I could see it in your eyes”)... The Stones were part of my life with Melanie; she was my Down-home Girl (“Lord, I swear that the perfume you wear was made out of turnip greens”), and when she rescued me from the redwood tree, “She comes in colors everywhere; she’s a rainbow.” As I looked for a spiritual master, Mick Jagger sang these words: I’m goin’ home/ you can make it if you try/ it’s the singer not the song/ congratula-shuns. And a new one I hadn’t heard before: “You can’t always get what you want/ but if you try sometimes well you just might find/ you get what you need.” The lyrics rang in my ears while little Krishna with His peacock feather and flute in His baby hand emerged from fine-grained mahogany.

Now I’m working almost twenty hours a day on three projects and without a lot of physical help. The slowdown at Bury Place, decreed by the Camden Council, is almost welcome. But the rath car—omigod!—how did it get so big? We kind of forgot we had to roll the thing about twelve miles just to get it to the starting point at Marble Arch. Will it even move?

But how these events have brought us together. Swamiji’s team just gets'er done! Working so hard on so many fronts we scarcely have time for group sadhana—reading or chanting together. Our work, our devotional service, becomes our sadhana. Besides, we still live in separate places—in Hackney and Clapham and Balham—and much of each day is spent traveling. Driving back and forth to Balham, I dwell on a concept Prabhupada recently sent to Mukunda in a letter:

A bona fide Spiritual Master is never a conditioned soul. There are three kinds of liberated persons. They are called 1) sadhan siddha, 2) kripa siddha, and 3) nitya siddha. Sadhan siddha means one who has attained perfection by executing the regulative principles of devotional service. Kripa siddha means one who has attained perfection by the special mercy of Krishna and the Spiritual Master, and nitya siddha means one who was never contaminated. The symptom of nitya siddha is that from the beginning of his life he is attached to Krishna, and he is never tired of rendering service to Krishna. But when one is actually on the siddha or liberated platform there is no such distinction as to who is sadhan, kripa, or nitya siddha. When one is siddha, there is no distinction what is what. Just like when the river water glides down to the Atlantic Ocean nobody can distinguish which portion was the Hudson River or some other river. Neither is there any necessity to make any such distinction. Actually, every living entity is eternally uncontaminated, siddha, although he may be in the material touch. This is the version of the Vedas. Asanga ayam purusha—the living entity is uncontaminated. Just like when there is a drop of oil in water you can immediately distinguish the oil from the water, and the water never mixes with the oil. Similarly, a living entity, although in material contact, is always siddha, distinct from the matter.

Amazing stuff.

I have to work at Bury Place on the evening of June 25, but the other devotees travel for a kirtan and speaking engagement at Oxford University, about sixty miles from Bloomsbury. Their program is well received by the students, and this news really tickles Prabhupada, who grew up hearing about the glories of Oxford. I am very much encouraged to learn that your kirtana is going to be held at Oxford University, which is the most important university in the world. At least in India, if somebody is a graduate of Oxford University, he is very highly respected, and immediately he gets some good job in the governments service. So if your Sankirtana Program becomes recognized by the Oxonians, it will be a great credit for you.”

London’s summer of 1969 was similar to Haight-Ashbury’s 1967 Summer of Love: young hippies were flocking to the city, hanging around Piccadilly Circus, Portobello Road, Notting Hill, and the Arts Lab. Parliament Hill out in Hampstead Heath, and Primrose Hill on the north side of Regents Park, became, that summer, like our Hippie Hill in San Francisco, as thousands of young people slept in the parks and met on the streets to celebrate their new hippie freedoms.

June was a blaze of activity for our small group, but July would be even hotter. On July 1, John and Yoko crash his Austin Maxi car in the Scottish highlands and are taken to Lawson Memorial Hospital in Sutherland for facial stitches, remaining there five days. On July 7, George rings me late in the afternoon and says: “Shyamasundar, I’m over here at Abbey Road Studios. Were making our Beatles album here, but John’s gotten into a car crash and he’s going to be late, so we’ve got a few hours free in the studio. Can you all come over right away? Let’s make a record together. Hare Krishna!”

George was growing evangelistic, almost messianic, as he experienced more and more of Krishna’s magic working in his life. He felt he had “a lot of work to do, trying to get a message through”—and he liked his surprises. These were impulsive days, and “do your thing” was the anthem of the times. George appreciated that we were quick to respond, always prepared and mobile, if Krishna was involved. He and I almost never planned anything together; everything was spontaneous. He’d say, “Let’s do it!” and we would be ready. (In later years, we often joked about Georges “Bat Light”: whenever we saw his signal in the sky over Gotham, off we’d go in an instant.)

Abbey Road Studios, in St. John’s Wood—maybe fifteen minutes’ drive from Bury Place—was not famous, yet—that would happen in about ten weeks’ time with the release of the Beatles’ Abbey Road album. But even now we had to shuffle through a mob of fans and a cordon of police to get into the place. We were directed into a huge room filled with sound gear. There was a glass-fronted control booth off to the left. Paul McCartney was there too—he was going to be spinning the dials tonight, producing the thing from the booth.

We’ve brought everyone: the six of us, the new British devotees, and some American devotees who are passing through town—maybe thirteen of us. None of us have ever done this sort of thing before, so it takes a while before we calm down and start organizing the session. Chris O’Dell is there with her mother, who’s visiting from the States; and there’s Mal Evans and some of the other Apple staff.

Mukunda sits down behind a grand piano, and he and George work out the key and melody we’ll use. The rest of us are trying on earphones, unbalanced by the different dimension they create. Our faces are red, hesitant, a bit embarrassed to perform like this. Our eyes dart back and forth from the microphones to George, who jokes around, doing his “Springtime for Hitler” Mel Brooks imitations to help us relax.

Chris O’Dell describes the scene in her book, Miss O’Dell:

We watched in almost prayerful silence, for it was such a beautiful sight in that small studio with all the Krishna oranges and yellows mixed with the red and purple flowers they were holding and an overall feeling of peace and tranquility.. . At that moment Shyamasundar walked through the door connecting the studio and control room, his beads swinging back and forth on his neck.

“Chris! Why don’t you come and sing with us, bring your mother with you!”

One of the Krishnas wrote down the words to the chant so my mother could follow along, and that little act of grace gave her the confidence to step into the circle gathered around several microphones.

We make a few false starts. On the first go-around, Yamuna takes the lead, but one voice alone sounds too weak against the many-voiced chorus. Then Yamuna and Mukunda, our two best singers, take the lead together on the second cut, but Mukunda’s drum—he’s the only one who knows how to play it well—is overbearing so close to the mike. We try it again, this time with Yamuna and me in duet. This seems to work OK, my weaker voice harmonizing in and around Yamuna’s strong contralto. George, fresh from his holiday in Sardinia, shifts from harmonium to guitar and back, then settles behind his psychedelic sit-down harmonium. Microphones and wires are moved around and balances are struck as Paul feeds us information from the control booth. I’m on amplified esraj, Gurudas and a couple of others with their penetrating kartals are moved back. We’re spread out all over the floor. The chorus is assembled behind some mikes far in the back. There were only eight tracks in those days, so we have to get it right early on. George is restless now, businesslike, the pro. He wants to finish it off. One take, two takes... Into the third take we know this is the one. The maha-mantra leaps off our instruments and tongues and grows and gains momentum and weight and spiritual depth as it whirls in a three-minute, thirty-second package to the end: Bongggg! as Malati hits the gong.

George runs over to the control booth to hear the replay. Chris again:

I looked up at George in the control room, his hair pulled back behind his ears, listening intently to the music, smiling and nodding to the beat. I get it, I thought, as the voices of my mother and all the Krishnas enfolded me, this is why he chants. It was a new kind of high... It was a feeling of peace, and I wondered at the fact that you can feel physically and spiritually changed just by repeating certain words. There was a point of freedom where there was no effort at all, no criticism or judgment, just the sound generated from deep inside, like a flame warmed us from the inside out.

That’s it. “Hare Krishna Mantra” is in the can. George and Paul go off to work on their Abbey Road album, and we go home, wondering what that was all about or if anything would come of it. We later heard that George went back and dubbed in some guitar tracks, mixed and fooled around with the tapes—and God knows what kind of politics he had to play to get our record produced on the Apple label.

A couple of weeks later, George sneaks us into Abbey Road to record the record’s B-side. We do the namah om prayers, which we call “Prayers to the Spiritual Masters.” This time there were only a few of us—we didn’t need a lot of people for a chorus. Yamuna puts tilak on everyone’s foreheads, even on the brows of the sound engineers. I strike the melody with my esraj, and Yamuna leads us in a haunting version of the song we’d practiced a hundred times. We get it right in two takes and are out of the studio in a couple of hours.

Meanwhile, Mukunda has obtained our Ratha-yatra parade permit from Scotland Yard, so on July 27, we’re set for a 1:30 p.m. departure from Marble Arch, just by Speakers’ Corner in Hyde Park. The parade will wind south down Park Lane, left on Piccadilly Road, one mile east to Piccadilly Circus, then down Haymarket Street to Pall Mall, then left and into Trafalgar Square—about two and a half miles in total.

Back in Balham, the upper deck of the rath car was taking shape, but what about the huge crown? We wanted it to be as close to forty feet as possible, but at that height it wouldn’t pass below the wires that crossed the roads en route from Balham to Marble Arch. We decided to run four twelve-foot 2" x 8" struts vertically from the lower deck, extending through and about six feet above the top deck, to anchor the crown’s wooden framework, which we would build separately, dismantle, and reassemble at the Marble Arch site the morning of the parade.

IT’s July 18 issue (#60), page 7, has a photo of the Ratha-yatra in Puri, India, with millions of people swarming around the giant car and, under the headline “GODONWHEELS,” this announcement:

NIRVANA, Hare (PNS): At 1.30pm, Sunday 27h July at Speakers’ Corner Marble Arch, will commence the world’s largest parade known as the Holy Car Festival of Lord Jagannath... The London version sponsored by the Hare Krishna Movement will be an almost exact replica of the Indian parade, with immense, aspiring, hand-pulled wooden car, complete with dazzling crimson canopy, live elephant, feasting, singing, dancing. Five to ten thousand Indian nationals and Englishmen are expected to attend the event.... The Car itself, built in London by English and American members of the Society, will have eight five-foot wooden wheels, and it will be hand-drawn by participants. Two 38-yard lengths of rope attached to the front of the car will provide ample gripping space for pulling... Further information can be obtained by contacting the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, 7 Bury Place, London WC1.”

Back at Bury Place, the old ceiling was now down and hauled away. Despite Mr. Wallace’s stricture not to work beyond “making the place safe” pending the Council’s planning permission, we kept working, knowing that the sooner we finished, the sooner Prabhupada would come to us. The new ceiling plan called for twenty huge, arching crossbeams. To anchor these heavy arches, we first had to build a horizontal support structure along the walls. Once again we went to the barge-dismantlers to find twenty-four 6' x 2' x Г columns for uprights to hold two massive 3' x Г x 50' single-span beams of beautiful, knot-free, old-growth Douglas fir. The arches would be footed atop these long beams. We had to remove the front window again to swing the wooden beams inside with a crane and a system of winches—along with the brute strength of fired-up young devotees.

On July 25, George was interviewed by the popular Kenny Everett for his radio show on BBC-1. George played Kenny our new “Hare Krishna Mantra” record, and then said to millions of listeners: “If anybody happens to be walking from Hyde Park down

Park Lane along towards Piccadilly and down Trafalgar Square at 1:30 on Sunday they’ll be in good company, because God, he’ll be there, Lord Jagannath, and Krishna, and all the gang, Balaram, and they’re all gonna be there, at that million-dollar bash, starts 1:30 blah blah—great chanting—and fun, fun.”

Countdown to Ratha-yatra.

On the top deck of the rath car we built a stout 4' by 8' platform for the deities. We also built an eighteen-inch-high picket fence with railings around the upper deck, leaving openings for little porches. We painted the fence white, but we wouldn’t have time to paint anything else, so the rath car had a raw-wood look.

We designed the rath car’s crown to be assembled in four sections in roughly a tapering square, twenty-five feet high. The bottom section will be 15' x 15' at the base, tapering to 10' x 10' over a height of ten feet. Each of the next three sections is 10' by 10' square and five feet high. Four twenty-foot lengths of 1" x 6" boards, fastened on one end to 2" x 8" uprights, rose to form the corners of the crown. We preassembled all these pieces, and drilled bolt holes in what we hoped were the right places. The idea was that we would assemble this unwieldy construction onsite at Marble Arch in the few hours before the parade and cover it with Yamuna’s incredible crimson satin canopy.

Yamuna, with minimal help from other ladies, had been on a sewing marathon. The canopy would be twenty-five feet tall and, at the bottom, sixty feet around, tapering to a forty-foot circumference at the top end. She had to stitch her canopy in the shape of a giant sleeve to slip over the entire wooden framework. At the base of this sleeve, she had to allow for three nine-foot-high openings that would part like curtains on three sides of the deities. For the front of the canopy she fashioned a gorgeous 5' x 15' dark-blue satin banner with each letter of the maha-mantra cut from bright yellow cloth and sewn meticulously into place. Around every edge of the canopy—and horizontally around it at five-foot intervals, she sewed a metallic gold two-inch fringe.

July 27, Ratha-yatra day. It’s still quite cold at 4 a.m. when Mukunda, Gurudas, and Ted Berk join me in Balham to start moving the rath car to Marble Arch. With no clear idea how we were really going to do this, and depending solely on Krishna, we hooked a ten-foot chain between my little Fordson and the massive wood-framed juggernaut. All the wood and materials we’d need to assemble the crown were stacked on the lower deck. The plan was that I would pull with the truck and the three guys would walk behind and push. It was still dark as we squeezed through the gate and started off down Balham Park Road.

To avoid the main roads as much as possible, we have to take a back route from Balham, so it’s about twelve miles to Marble Arch. Sunday, so not much traffic. Very slowly we creep up Balham Park Road, cut north to Bolingbrooke Grove, go right on Battersea Rise, go left on Latchmere Road, three miles to cross the Thames at Battersea Bridge. Whew. More men join us along the way, but its a tough go. When we get to a corner, we have to turn the yoke by hand with everyone pushing one wheel or the other, angling back and forth until we’ve made the ninety degrees. Kind residents and passers-by—not that many so early on a Sunday morning—lend a hand or cheer us on. Cobblestones on some of the side streets make the cart jerk and tremble in godawful ways—I am worried the thing will fall apart. Bits and pieces do fall off here and there, but we throw them on the cart to repair later.

Then it’s four miles north on Warwick Road to Shepherd’s Bush, hang a right on Bayswater Road, and another two miles along Hyde Park to the staging area at Marble Arch. Most streets are narrow, and we take up three-quarters of even the larger boulevards. One guy is usually out front directing cars around us. The truck’s tiny four-cylinder motor strains, a plume of black smoke pouring from the exhaust pipe.

I clearly remember one scene: the sun is just coming up behind us on a beautiful summer day, and we’re going over a small hill-I’m over the rise, leaning out my window, looking back—and I see this giant wooden scaffold ponderously emerge over the rise. Slowly it comes forward, and then I see three plumes of sunlit steam shooting above the horizon in rhythm to the grunting of the three men pushing. Then their faces appear, eyes closed, totally focused on pushing this massive thing up and over the top. When we got to flat ground, I stopped the truck so I could get out on the pavement and roll with laughter: three bald-headed men with bright-red faces huffing and puffing to push this gigantic boulder up a hill on a trip to nowhere, just for their love of God.

Six hours later, around 10 a.m., we arrived at Marble Arch, where the parade will start.

Poet Ted Berk—later initiated by Prabhupada as Rama Tanu das—described that day in an article he wrote for issue #30 of Back to Godhead:

It is the backyard of an ordinary English neighborhood. But soon the red-headed five-year-old who lives next door begins to repeat after us as we fix oversized wooden carriage wheels to the frame... ‘Hare Kr-ishna’... smiling, feeling the devotion, asking his mother if he can join our procession. Sometimes Prabhu Malati sends down orange drink and prasad^ and Indian boys stop after their day’s work to paint a wheel or sand the frame. To work directly for Krishna is so irresistible that it is not work at all! Such a large noble creation being pushed through the dawn-light streets, first in the European world, this car bearing the ever brightly smiling Personality of Godhead. Then after six hours journey over the historic bridges we arrive at Hyde Park.

We get there and it’s total chaos. At least there are now many men to help, and piece by piece we assemble the twenty-five-foot crown from bottom to top, hanging from the flimsy structure to tighten the last bolts. The huge bundle of red satin is passed up, and Yamuna yells out instructions as we somehow fit the canopy over the top of the swaying crown, fighting vertigo and miscalculation, little by little pulling it down tight, taking it off again to adjust, redo, and at last it’s done and it looks fantastic.

We attach two hundred-foot lengths of one-inch rope to the front of the car so people can pull it. I run back out to Balham to pick up Malati and Saraswati—and the Jagannath deities—and bring them to Marble Arch. The deities are escorted to their seats on their car and garlanded. The sound system’s going, BTG magazines are being sold, a huge crowd of “Indian nationals and Englishmen” have gathered, more decorations are going up, and we catch up to Ted Berk’s narrative:

We arrive at Hyde Park and humbly lay the garlanded flowers at the Lotus Feet of the Deities, the frame swaying as we place the ladder against it to staple the red and yellow satin, the Hare Krishna banner, and the other bunting of this most happy of vehicles. People from everywhere gather about to take pictures, other banners of Indian communities being carried in, east and west singing to Lord Krishna. Small children and gray-haired grandmothers with tilak all wanting to pull the rope that will send dancing the grand Lord of the Universe. Then the conch is sounded (she who was laid so nicely upon the shore just for this service of being sounded for the Lord) and with the transcendental broom sweeping away the material dust, we dance towards Trafalgar Square, the street resounding with “Hari Bol! All Glories to Lord Jagannath!”

The rath car jerks forward slowly as thousands pull and shout, then gains momentum. We pull it a hundred feet or so, then it lurches to the right —and collapses—the right front wheel broken off at the axle. No one can believe it. After all those miles of rough travel just to get it here.

And on top of that, the elephant never showed up.

The cops tell us we’ll have to move this beached whale quickly-it’s blocking traffic on one of London’s busiest streets. How can we move it? We’ll have to dismantle the whole thing, bolt by bolt. I’m already exhausted, about to collapse myself. I say to Trivikram, “Buddy, you’re going to have to take care of this, please.” An ex-Navy man, Trivikram is good at giving orders; I give him my toolbox, and he gathers a team and takes charge. The wood will be taken to Bury Place. I drive back out to Balham and sleep for two days straight.

Meanwhile, at the parade starting point, devotees remove the deities from their fallen coach and bundle them onto hastily assembled palanquins, and two thousand celebrants march and dance down the parade route all the way to Trafalgar Square. Despite the car’s failure, it was a great Ratha-yatra afternoon at the base of Nelson’s Column. Next day the papers report the event in a positive way, not mentioning that it caused one of the worst traffic jams in London history.

Prabhupada was shocked to hear news of the collapsing car, but he saw the whole event from a higher perspective. In a letter to me dated August 13, my merciful master wrote:

When I first heard that the wheels of the car collapsed it was so great a shock to me that the whole day I was sick and morose, and now after receiving this letter and your pictures I am very much enlivened. The car was on the road and the photo is taken with a multitude of people. That is your success and as a result of this collapse you have got now the sympathy of the Indian community as well as all the people of London ... I think we may take it as Krishna’s Grace that the difficulty took place at the very beginning of the procession and not later on when the Ratha was in a more populated area. So do not feel discouraged at all. By Krishna’s Plan everything will come out for the best.

June and July were the busiest months to date for letters from Prabhupada to his London disciples. During those two months, he wrote us a total of twenty-two long letters, guiding every aspect of our accelerating affairs. This is almost twice the number of letters of any previous month. He wrote us everything from major decisions like, “I want to establish at least 108 centers, and at least one dozen centers in England,” to small stuff like “yellowish marble or white marble should be used” on the new altar at Bury Place. (Little did we know that it would be almost six months before the altar is finally built.)

On July 31, Prabhupada sent chanting beads to our earliest English devotees, Colin, Tim, and Dick. They became Kulashekar, Tirthapada, and Digvijaya. Many English boys and girls were attracted by the Ratha-yatra, and almost daily new aspirants moved into Bury Place.

Ratha-yatra was another rhino in the bag, and whole herds of rhinos were in our sights.

George called to inform us that our record would be released as a 45-rpm single, with “Hare Krishna Mantra” on the А-side and “Prayers to the Spiritual Masters” on the B-side. It would come out sometime in late August. Apple agreed to let us insert in each record jacket a two-sided transcription of Swami s spoken introduction to the Happening album, titled “On Chanting the Hare Krishna Mantra.” The Apple PR people got to work. In early August, we dressed up, took along our props and our Jagannath deities, and went to Clive Arrowsmith s portrait studio to be photographed in various poses as The Radha Krishna Temple. A video was shot with George and the Krishna devotees singing the mantra on the rooftop at Savile Row—George with his hair tied back and wearing an overcoat, cowboy boots, and a huge grin on his face. Mukunda negotiated our contract with Apple, and we received the standard performer’s royalty of 10% net on record sales. Another 10% was assigned to composers, so we listed “Mukunda das Adhikary” as the composer on both songs. Ads started appearing in music magazines. The countdown toward the singles release was on.

At Bury Place, soundproofing the temple room was becoming a big concern. Prabhupada cautioned by letter that “soundproofing is a very expensive enterprise,” and he asked if we had the money for it. But somehow we would have to reduce the sound of loud kirtans or the authorities would close us down for sure.

I design all the columns, beams and redwood walls to stand five inches out from the existing walls, with double sheets of fiberglass insulation between. Even the one door into the temple room becomes six inches thick, filled with soundproofing material. The windows are double paned to reduce noise. All this takes so much time.

It takes us many days to build sturdy columns along the walls and install the fifty-foot wooden beams on top of them. Now we re ready to start on the twenty arches. These reach from the eight-foot level on one wall, arc up to the center of the fourteen-foot ceiling, then come down again on the other wall, twenty feet away. The arches are made of 2" x 12" wooden planks, twelve inches wide where they leave the wall and tapering to six inches wide at the room’s center. We use deck planks scrounged from the rath car for these. Each arch must be spliced together from six planks to create the arc: six pieces cut to extreme precision, each piece dovetailed and glued to the next without metal fasteners, matching the color and woodgrain to appear a seamless arch. Each arch takes two or three days to create; multiplied by twenty, building them will be the single-most time-consuming activity of the temples construction. By the end August we’ve only finished two arches. Mukunda informs us that Camden Council planning permission may take many more weeks.

On August 11, John Lennon and Yoko Oko moved into their Tittenhurst Park estate in Sunninghill, near Ascot, Berkshire, forty miles from London. The Beatles were busy laying down final tracks on their latest album, Abbey Road. On August 18,1 called George at his home and told him that a few of us were working at Bury

Place. He said the Beatles were going to be recording that night at Abbey Road, “Why don’t you swing by after work?”

So at about ten o’clock, Gurudas and I and a new boy named David Naismith stopped by Abbey Road and wandered into the studio where the Beatles were recording “Carry That Weight.” (“Boy, you’re going to carry that weight... a long time.”) They were recording in room 43, a smaller room—not the big studio where we had recorded “Hare Krishna Mantra” a few weeks before. This room had skylights in the ceiling, maybe twenty feet up. We stood in the shadows, quietly appreciating some of the best music ever made.

Suddenly: Crackle-Crash-Boom! And a flailing figure came crashing through the skylight, a pink sari tearing from his body. He landed loudly on the floor right in front of John, who stopped singing and looked down at this groaning naked man. Then he said softly into the mike: “Must be one of yours, George.” It was Robert, the crazy guy from Bury Place. We bundled him off quickly, laughing so hard we could scarcely move, and sent him back out into the night. When we returned, the Beatles are playing on as if nothing had happened.

Later that night, during a break, I’m chatting with George and John, and George mentions again how we're struggling to build the downtown London temple while living scattered in different suburbs. John says, “Well, I just moved into a new place in the country. Lots of work to fix the place up. Why don’t your lot move out to Tittenhurst Park and do the place up for me?”

He describes the servants’ quarters, a building about a hundred yards from the main house, and proposes we live there. Wow. We need to talk about this, but it sounds perfect. Krishna comes through again!

On August 20, the Beatles record their last songs together-George’s “Here Comes the Sun” and Paul’s “The End”:

And in the end

The love you take

Is equal to the love

You make.

On August 22, the Fab Four gather at Tittenhurst Park and walk around, posing here and there for their final photo shoot. One of the most famous of these shots is the four of them standing shoulder to shoulder in the doorway of the “Gallery,” George’s black cowboy hat perched atop the marble bust of a Roman senator by the door. (In ten days’ time, this building will become a Hare Krishna temple.)

George calls me that night and says he has arranged for us to look at the place. A guy named Dan and his wife Jill are the caretakers and will show us around. So Mukunda, Gurudas, and I go out to Tittenhurst the next morning. We get lost several times en route, but eventually find the estate and walk the property with Dan, asking him all the relevant questions, like,

“Do the toilets work?”

“Yes.”

“Are there buses and trains to London?”

“Yes.”

“There re about fifteen of us. Is that OK?”

“Hey, that’s between you and John, but he said you were all welcome.”

“Where do we buy food?”

“Sunninghill and Ascot have shops, a few kilometers down the road.”

“OK, looks perfect, Dan. Tell John we’ll start moving our stuff in a couple of days.”

In the meantime, Prabhupada has made arrangements to visit his new ISKCON center in Hamburg, Germany, and he leaves Los Angeles on August 23. He probably flies right over us around 4 a.m. on August 25.

We start moving to Tittenhurst on August 27. We had agreed to try out this new living arrangement for a few days, see how it goes, before inviting Srila Prabhupada. I write to Prabhupada in Hamburg that we are moving to John Lennon’s place, but I leave it open-ended as to if or when he should come.

The “Hare Krishna Mantra” 45-rpm single record was scheduled for release on August 29,1969. On August 28, the Apple publicity machine put on a fantastic party for the record launch, and that afternoon, about a hundred press members gathered on the elegant lawns and under elaborate marquees at The Wood, an expansive, Tudor-style estate at 16 Sydenham Hill Road, in South London, near the Crystal Palace. It was announced that George Harrison would personally attend to introduce his new spiritual friends to the world. Reporters and photographers arrived at 1 p.m. and were handed press packets. After formal talks from a podium in the main house by Derek and George, the guests walked out into the gardens for an afternoon of prasadam, chanting, and interviews. The record was played over and over from an outdoor sound system.

George was a veteran and so in his element—he’d been in the spotlight for nearly a decade—but today he shined with new vigor: these are my special friends, they have something important to tell you, please listen, and know that I am one of them. The press took this very seriously, and the flashbulbs popped. Old news in a few days perhaps, but tomorrow’s headlines:

I’m completely at ease now. I know everything’s all right and getting better. I just get happier and happier. You have control of your mind, rather than your mind having control of you. The higher your consciousness goes, the more you are able to see, and you are able to stop things like temper. It’s helped me; it can only get better for me... The thing they have in common is to get back to God, and to get consciousness. With the Krishna consciousness people, there are probably more people I can identify with, because these are younger people, and they’ve been through different scenes like we all have. I never stop chanting the Krishna mantra. I chant for about three-quarters of an hour in the mornings.

And this article appeared a few days later in New York’s Village Voice:

Harrison said it \[Krishna consciousness\] changed his life and now things can only get better... Here we have holy people who—though easy to laugh at—are obviously sincere. They are reasoning through more than most of us. For thousands of years men have searched for THE TRUTH. And what’s the result? A press party—reporters scrambling to ace each other out over some words on the subject of transcendental experience from a pop star. To push a product. Must everything wind up on the marketplace?

After the party, George, Pattie, John, and Ringo traveled by plane to the Isle of Wight, five miles off the south English coast, to meet Bob Dylan, who was to perform at the Isle of Wight Rock Festival from August 29-31. During the show’s intermissions, George convinced the organizers to play “Hare Krishna Mantra” over the sound system again and again.

The next day, our record was released to rave reviews in every paper in town, drawing the attention of millions of people. “Hare Krishna Mantra” promptly sold 70,000 copies—the highest first-day sales of any non-Beatle Apple single to date. There were advertisements for the Radha Krishna Temple’s new record posted up and down escalators in tube stations all over London, and more on the sides of hundreds of London’s red double-decker buses.

Another major rhinoceros reclines at the lotus feet of Srila Prabhupada.

The record continued to sell at a brisk pace, quickly moving into the Top 100. Mukunda or Gurudas stopped by the Apple offices almost every day to see the latest sales reports—and to collect our royalty checks. We got an advance from Apple Records, and pretty good amounts of cash (for us) started to roll in. We sent Prabhupada partial repayment of the money he’d loaned us for the Bury Place rent deposit. He responded, “I am glad that you have already sent to New York part of the payment of $1,025. It doesn’t matter that you could not pay all at a time. You can pay the balance \[later\].”

IT’s August 31, 1969 issue (#63) was full of good stuff. Pages 3 and 5 are George’s interview entitled “You Can’t Even Say What It Is,” which takes up where he’d left off in his IT interview from August 1967, exactly two years ago:

The first thing in my life is music, but now I just want to sing songs that give me some benefit, which is like—your cosmic chants! I’ve come to understand that music should be employed really for the benefit of God-perception, like chanting, that sort of thing. In the 1967 interview with IT, it started by saying—if you could get a few words that just say everything and get them together in one sentence, and just say that all the time—it’s like the Hare Krishna mantra, that’s setting up—just say words of God, and just repeating them over and over and over—the repetition itself has great effect, and by saying the words of God then you build up those vibrations to try and identify with that—and with him.

And on page 24 we found Ted Berk’s exuberant article “Krishna! Krishna! Let Me Sing Your Name”:

WHEN I LEAP into the air... dance and dance for Krishna... Absolute Person... Supreme Being... Blackish in Colour bathed in late afternoon sun flame... Agni demigod of Fire who Adores Him also... and Brahma The Creator and Shiva of the Forest Austerities all watching from the Heavens... as we whirl madly... love full for our Lord... Now here in Central London by the Grace of Krishna, a new, opulent, extraordinary, International Radha Krishna Temple will soon be open to our Friends (the 19th such Temple in the Western world since the introduction of the Hare Krishna movement into the United States by His Divine Grace A C Bhaktivedanta Swami who at the age of 72 came to the United States from India most lovingly and almost penniless upon the express instructions of his Spiritual Master) We hope that with your help and blessings, this Temple and Center of Study of Vedic Literature such as the Divine Bhagavad-gita will soon be dedicated for daily chanting, dancing, learning, devotional service, and feasting with us upon subtle vegetarian delicacies. If you can join us in any way, with love, labour, building materials, words, or contributions, please telephone our International Society for Krishna Consciousness at 01-243-0394. MAY YOU BE FILLED WITH BLISS ALWAYS!

**\* \* \***

Over a four-day period at the end August, beginning of September, we shifted to Tittenhurst Park, dividing up available quarters and getting used to the living arrangements. Tittenhurst Park is seventy-two acres of cultivated English gardens set in the posh county of Berkshire. The main house, a Georgian mansion, was built in 1737. In the late nineteenth century it had been owned by Thomas Lowinsky, owner of Deccan coal mines in India and a member of the Royal Horticultural Society. It was he who created the outstanding gardens on the property. He sold the place to Peter Cadbury, heir to the Cadbury chocolate fortune, who sold it to John for £145,000. (Later, before moving to New York in 1973, Lennon will sell the estate to Ringo Starr.) The drive from Bury Place to Tittenhurst is about thirty-five miles; when the traffic was congested, the drive could take almost two hours. You’d take the A30 past Runnymede (where the Magna Carta was signed in 1215) to Virginia Water, turn right on London Road at ^eggar s Bush, another one and a half miles, then turn left into the gates at John’s place. Tittenhurst sits in the heart of blue blood country, just a couple of miles from the Royal Raceway at Ascot.

It seemed that John had a serious desire to see a bit more what this Krishna thing was about—his pal George was certainly keen on it—so his attitude was, “Let’s have a look at it up close.” Both men scrutinized us when we were with them—especially myself and the other more senior devotees among us—perhaps judging how confident, happy, and secure we might be with our Krishna philosophy and way of life. They repeatedly told us, “We must meet your Swami.” The deal at Tittenhurst was room and board in exchange for work.

The main house at Tittenhurst was big, and John wanted the whole interior painted white. He also wanted the kitchen expanded and brick walls removed for his recording studio. The inside construction work was directed by Yoko's ex-husband from New York, Dan Cox. He was a pretty nice guy, and we got on well with him. The whole situation was like a honeymoon—high summer and the livin’ was easy. Dan opened an unlimited charge account for us at the greengrocer in Ascot, so I would drive Malati and the girls down there a couple of times a week to buy food (at twice the price of London shops). This went on until Yoko saw how much we were spending and put a limit on our purchases.

The grounds were impressive. Over the centuries, previous owners had developed more than fifty acres of amazing gardens, with plants and trees from all over the world. The estate had been abandoned for some months before John moved in, and the grounds were now overgrown. The head gardener, Mr. MacDougal, mobilized devotees to whack brush, clear paths, and trim and plant trees and shrubs. My favorite spot in the gardens was a remote stand of six huge California redwoods, maybe two hundred years old and already four or five feet in diameter. After morning programs I would make my way through the foggy dawn to this isolated place to chant my rounds, and for an hour at least I was back on the California coast I missed so much.

Then the bell for porridge and hot milk would ring and I would return to eat breakfast, then dash off to London. My not-so-trusty red pickup was the only vehicle we had, and most mornings,

I ferried several men to work at Bury Place, coming back late at night. Sometimes we stayed over at Bury Place wrapped in sleeping bags. The other devotees soon learned to use the local taxi, bus, and train systems.

Behind the main house at Tittenhurst is a large Victorian hall-some called it the gallery, others the conservatory—where once owners had hung their art collections. Rembrandts and Turners had watched over classical music recitals while lords and ladies of the empire sipped iced tea and pink gin. Now it was an empty shell. We asked Dan if we could fix it up for our “chapel.” He relayed the question to John and Yoko, and they agreed.

OK, then. Now we have a temple and can invite Srila Prabhupada to come and live with us! It’s been exactly one year since we’ve seen him. We haven’t yet received official permission to use Bury Place as a house of worship, but were pretty confident that the permission will come soon. Mukunda sends a telegram to the Swami in Hamburg, inviting him to England.

Prabhupada responds on September 5: “Now it is decided that I shall be starting on the 11th of September, Thursday, by flight 234, Lufthansa Airlines, starting at 12:15 Hamburg time, and reaching London at 13:40. So you can make the necessary arrangements.”

We need to get ready, everybody—Prabhupada’s coming in a week! Mukunda and Gurudas rush to London to organize public speaking programs and publicity.

Besides the six of us and Saraswati, we have invited all the initiated and aspiring British devotees, plus a few recently-arrived American devotees, to live and work with us out at John’s place. Looking back, that may not have been the right thing to do, as a couple of these persons turned out to be just a hair short of the lunatic fringe, but I was working every day at Bury Place, and with everything we had going on, we were short-handed as it was. We had a temple to build. We were recording artists and had to appear in public to promote the new record. We had to prepare for Prabhupada’s arrival. Plus, we had to renovate John’s somewhat run-down estate.

We occupied the servants’ quarters, a two-story brick building from Victorian times. This building was divided into four separate units, one room down and one room up, connected by stairs. There was a tiny bathroom tucked under the staircase of each. The rooms were about 30' x 30' square. Malati and I shared the end unit with an English couple, David and Denise (later Devadatta and Draupadi), who also had a child. We fixed up the next apartment for Prabhupada. Yamuna and Gurudas and Janaki and Mukunda were on the other side of Prabhupada, and the fourth apartment was occupied by unmarried female devotees. The unmarried men, the brahmacharis, lived in the adjacent, dilapidated stables with their stall-like doors. The stables and servants’ quarters were surrounded by a wooden picket fence.

The gallery, our new temple, was a hundred feet down the driveway. It had Corinthian columns and a huge fireplace, and stood directly across from the main house. We set up an altar on the far wall, with paintings of Radha and Krishna and photos of our spiritual lineage. Next to that, we modified an existing podium into an upholstered vyasasan, an elevated throne, for Prabhupada to lecture from when he arrived. We held formal kirtan programs there in the early mornings and evenings, but it seemed there was always the sound of chanting, kartals, drums, and conch shells emanating from that building and echoing across the grounds. Must have driven John and Yoko nuts.

After the first week or so, we didn’t see much of our famous landlords. While we painted the main rooms and kitchen, they were apparently asleep upstairs. We would hear John’s piano late into the night. It was a two-shift arrangement, which seemed to suit everyone. And their guests came and went. On September 1, Bob Dylan walked up the driveway—“How ya doin’, Bob?”—and quickly disappeared into the main house. His car was gone the next morning.

On Sunday, September 7, we invited George and Pattie, with John and Yoko, to a picnic in our new temple in the gallery. The girls whipped up a multicourse lunch, and we had a kirtan and sat around on the cool tile floor eating and getting to know each other. Janaki taught John how to play kartals; Saraswati, now walking, charmed everybody; Gurudas ran around and took black-and-white photos, although it made John and Yoko a little uptight. Everyone loved the prasadam, and all agreed it was a perfect day.

Things are moving rapidly now. On September 7, Prabhupada wrote to a disciple in America: This Thursday morning I will be going to London, and most probably by the beginning of November, I will have returned to the USA.”

Now one thing is certain: finally, after a year of heartbreaking separation, Prabhupada is coming!

### 3

Since we’d seen him in Montreal a year ago, Srila Prabhupada had traveled to New York, New Vrindaban, San Francisco, Seattle, Santa Fe, and Los Angeles, where he stayed from November 1968 to February 1969, declaring Los Angeles to be his world headquarters. After L.A. he went to Hawaii, San Francisco, New York, Buffalo, Boston, Columbus, New Vrindaban, and then back to L.A., then to San Francisco for Ratha-yatra, then back to L.A., and now he was in Hamburg, Germany.

It had been a year of great longing for us, yearning to see Prabhupada and to hear him speak, feel his glance, the refreshment and joy of his personal company. One thing we all felt: by this one year of extreme devotional service, our individual relationships with our spiritual master had moved to a higher level. Krishna had somehow worked through us to accelerate the spread of His name throughout Great Britain; through us, his instruments, Prabhupadas great desire to propagate Krishna consciousness in England was being fulfilled at last. We had missed him terribly. And he had missed us, too.

**\* \* \***

September 11, 1969, Lufthansa flight 234 arrives from Hamburg at 13:40. It’s 75°F at Heathrow. The sliding gates part, and in he strides among us, laughing and touching our heads as we bow, garlands piled around his shoulders. Like thirsty refugees in from the desert, we drink in his presence—Srila Prabhupada, our glorious spiritual master.

For the Swami’s arrival at Heathrow, we’d called on the Apple publicity machine, and they’d booked the VIP room in the international arrivals area. More than forty reporters are waiting to interview Prabhupada. After the press conference, John Lennon’s white Rolls-Royce, driven by his chauffeur, Eddy, will carry Prabhupada to Tittenhurst.

We don’t get to exchange many words before Prabhupada is rushed into the VIP room to meet the press.

“Swami, why have you come to England?” asks the BBC guy.

“I have come here to teach you what you have forgotten: love of God.”

The reporters fire question after question, and Prabhupada fields them all with complete poise. He is kinglike in his first few minutes in Britain—a different, more regal Prabhupada than we have seen before. When airport officials finally hurry us out, Prabhupada takes a moment to smile at each of us in turn, like, “I love you so much.” (Or the look he gives me: “How did I do?”) Then he pinches my daughter’s cheek and says, “So, everything is all right, Miss Saraswati?”

Our emotions overflow. We feel deep relief in his presence, realizing how feeble our efforts to serve compare to his, yet at the same time, we feel pride and an urgency to report to him the fruits of our struggle, to bask in his appreciation. Were chanting and dancing around, dazzled and tongue-tied and full of joy as he gets into the back seat of the limo and drives off, alone.

Purushottam, who flew from Germany with the Swami as his personal aide, is standing there, confused. We gather our stuff as fast as we can and hope like anything that Eddy knows to take him out to John’s place.

The timing is perfect. The Swami’s movement has a strong foothold in Britain; Krishna’s name is being heard daily—over the radio, on the telly, in newspapers, and in periodicals—by millions of people throughout the land. Swamiji landed in the UK to greet his old disciples and meet his many new ones, and to deliver what I considered his most powerful interview yet to the world press-then be whisked off in a Rolls-Royce with a liveried driver to one of the poshest mansions in England, the guest of one of the most famous people in the world. Prabhupadas five-story temple in the heart of London is under lavish construction, and money is flowing in.

Eight months ago, the Swami had written us, “You have mentioned a very encouraging invitation that my visit there will be the biggest event in London since the time of the Roman Invasion. Actually this will be so. This time there is no question of invasion, but this time, if England is prepared, they will receive something sublime which they cannot produce in their country, neither in Manchester, Glasgow, nor Edinburgh.”

Our dreams of that big event are realized: Prabhupada arrives in Great Britain like a king...

But gets dropped in John’s driveway like a transient stranger. When we finally pull into the Tittenhurst grounds, the Swami is standing in the parking lot with his luggage, as if welcoming us to his new place. We quickly get him set up in his quarters, and about a dozen devotees sit around in his room, catching up.

Prabhupada relaxes against cushions behind his low desk and fires questions at us. Were bubbling over with our good news. Every time we tell him something about the London yatra he tells us something about Los Angeles, Hawaii, Germany, or the new books being printed. It’s a briefing, a catching up with the increased dimensions of Prabhupada’s exploding movement.

We’ve never seen the Swami looking so good. His beauty dazzles our eyes. And were struck by how gracefully he has settled into these radical new surroundings, as if only moments had passed since we’d last met. We are overwhelmed with relief and the sense that we’ve done the right thing, a job well done, and a conviction that Krishna is real and actively helping us, that right now we are the most special people in creation and that right here is better than heaven—waves of pleasure flow through our bodies. How glorious is our spiritual master.

Then we hear George’s Porsche roaring into the driveway below—all the windows are open on this warm, late-summer evening—and a few minutes later John, Yoko, and George filter into the back of the room. No one sees them at first—we’re so involved with the special being who is glowing before us. But suddenly Prabhupada’s eyes open wide as his attention shifts to the back of the room—and I turn, jump up, and beckon our guests forward. I can see they’re a little shy, even nervous. They sit a few feet apart on the floor across from Prabhupada’s low table. The rest of the room is crammed with devotees. I sit next to Prabhupada, up front, in order to work his big reel-to-reel Uher tape recorder. Introductions are made, and George indicates it’s OK to turn on the tape recorder. Prabhupada is relaxed and beaming, and he starts right in:

“Yes, there is a verse in Bhagavad-gita: yad yad acharati shresthas. The idea is that anything which is, I mean to say, accepted by the leading persons, ordinary persons will follow them. So by the grace of God, Krishna, you are leaders. Thousands of young men follow you. They like you. So if you give them something actually nice, the face of the earth will change.”

Watching Prabhupada in action again! Hearing the deepest, most perfect knowledge flow like nectar from his amazing, articulate mouth. As always, his instinct and timing are flawless—he is so sensitive, so in tune with whomever he converses, that words fall into line like melodies.

“So this Krishna consciousness movement is not any sectarian religious movement. It is a science. So we put this philosophy to your judgment, and if you judge it scrutinizingly with all your intellects, and you’ll find it sublime. And if you find it sublime, then why not put it to the world? You are also anxious to bring some peace to the world. I have read sometimes your statements.”

I pipe up, “Yes, Swamiji, John and Yoko recently made a very popular record called Give Peace A Chance, which is chanted like a mantra over and over.” Prabhupada doesn’t miss a lick.

“But we must know the process. Krishna says in Bhagavad-gita that people can be peaceful by learning three things: that 'Krishna is the enjoyer of all sacrifices, all austerities,’ that ‘Everything belongs to Krishna,’ and ‘Krishna is the real friend to all living entities.’ Just like your activities. Your artistic songs, they have become popular because you have undergone some austerities. You have come to the perfection. So Krishna is claiming, ‘The result of your austerities should come to Me.’ Then you’ll be satisfied, peaceful. And Krishna says, ‘I am the Supreme Lord of every place.’ We come here in the kingdom of God empty-handed; we go empty-handed. So what is the benefit to say, ‘This is mine’ and fight over it? And Krishna is so kind friend He does not live apart from you like some friend in the material existence. He lives inside of you and inside of all living creatures. He is so kind friend. So where is the question of harming someone else if Krishna lives there too? So if these three things are understood clearly, then he becomes peaceful. This is the peace formula. You have read Bhagavad-gita?”

John says, “I’ve read bits of the Bhagavad-gita. I don’t know which version it was. There are so many different translations.”

“Yes, but basic teaching of Bhagavad-gita is this world is not meant for happiness; this is a place of miseries. Everything is temporary. Nature will not allow you to stay here; you have to leave. Nature is so cruel. Just like in America, President Kennedy, he was thought to be the most fortunate man, happy man, young man, was elected President, nice wife, children, honored all over the world”-he snaps his fingers—“within a second, finished. Now where is he? Is he happy or is he distressed? Nobody can say. But Krishna says, mam upetya: ‘If anyone comes to Me,’ mam upetya tu kaunteya, punar janma na vidyate, ‘he does not come back again to this miserable condition.’ So this is the greatest benediction to the human society, to train them to go back to Krishna. So, you are after something nice. Is my proposal unreasonable?” He chuckles, and says, “You are all intelligent boys and girls. Try to understand this.”

Another question from John, “How does all this relate to music?” “Ah, you have got a very good talent, music. Na vidya sangita uttamam. The Vedic mantras are all through music. Sama Veda. Sama Veda is full of music. Samagah. Samagah means the followers of Sama Veda. Gayanti. They are always in music. Through musical vibration they are approaching the Supreme. So Vedic mantras are sung. This whole Bhagavad-gita, Srimad-Bhagavatam, can be sung very nicely. Simply by hearing the vibration, people will be benefited.”

Long pause. Everyone stares, breathless, as Srila Prabhupada pulls over a little harmonium, unclips the bellows, and begins to pump and, eyes closed, explore an exotic, haunting melody—none of us has ever seen him do this before in front of nondevotee guests. He breaks into a Sanskrit song—verses from the Seventh Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam:

matir na krisbne paratah svato va  
mitho ’bhipadyeta grha-vratanam  
adanta-gobhir vishatam tamisram  
punah punas charvita-charvananam

He sings for about five minutes, deep in ecstasy. Suddenly, Prabhupada seems to remember were there and stops. Opening his eyes, his smile huge and radiant, he says, “Simply by transcendental vibration of sound everything can be achieved.” Long pause again. We’re all in outer space. “What kind of philosophy you are following? May I ask?”

“Following?” John asks.

Yoko interjects, “We don’t follow anything. We are just living.”

Then George says, “We’ve done meditation. Or I do my meditation. I have a mantra meditation.”

Prabhupada: This is also mantra.

John: It’s not the song, though.

George: No, no, it’s chanting.

John: We heard it from Maharishi—a mantra each.

Prabhupada: His mantra is not public.

George: Not out loud. No.

“No. It’s a secret,” John says, and he laughs.

Prabhupada then tells a story about Ramanujacharya.“Ramanuj-acharya was a great acharya of this Vaishnava sampradaya. His spiritual master gave him one mantra: ‘My dear boy, you chant this mantra silently. Nobody can hear. It is very ...’ So then Ramanuja asked, ‘What is the effect of this mantra?’ He said, ‘By chanting this mantra, by meditation, you’ll get liberation.’ So he immediately went out and in a big public meeting, and he said that ‘You chant this mantra. You will all be liberated.’ (laughter) So he came back to his spiritual master, and his spiritual master was very angry that ‘I told you that you should chant silently.’ Ramanuja said, ‘Yes, I have committed offense. So whatever punishment you like, you can give me. But because you told me that this mantra will liberate, I have given it publicly. Let everyone hear and be liberated. Let me go to hell. I don’t mind. I have disobeyed your order. Let me go to hell. I am prepared. But by chanting this mantra, if anyone is liberated, let it be publicly distributed.’ His spiritual master embraced him and said, ‘You are greater than me!’

“You see? So if a mantra has so much power, why it should be secret? It should be distributed. People are suffering. So Chaitanya Mahaprabhu said, ‘Chant this Hare Krishna mantra. Anyone who will hear... even the birds and beasts will hear and they will be liberated.’ That’s all.”

I’m thinking Swamiji, this is so perfect and to the point: Krishna consciousness made clear. How does he do it? This beautiful old sage, so laid-back, exotic, and not attached to worldly affairs, his perceptions and reasoning ringing like a bell.

John shifts the topic a little: “If all mantras are just the name of God... Whether it’s a secret mantra or an open mantra, it’s all the name of God. So it doesn’t really make much difference, does it, which one you sing?”

“No,” Prabhupada says. “Just like in drug shop they sell all medicines for curing disease. But still, you have to take doctors prescription to take a particular type of medicine. Otherwise they will not supply you. If you go to a drug shop and you say, ‘I am diseased. You give me any medicine,’ the druggist will ask you, ‘Where is your prescription?’ So similarly, in this Kali-yuga age, this Hare Krishna mantra is recommended in the shastras. Our great stalwart, Chaitanya Mahaprabhu—we consider Him the incarnation of Krishna—He preached this. Therefore our principle is we should follow the footprints of great authorities. So it is very secret. Then how to get that secret thing? Mahajano yena gatah sa panthah: you simply follow great personalities, how they have achieved success. Imam rajarshayo viduh. Mantra cannot be manufactured. Mantra must be from the original Supreme Absolute, coming down through disciplic succession, like a channel.”

Now an argumentative mood develops—these are important people, and they’re hearing someone say that his way is the only way (wait a minute, we’ve heard that before!) and they’re skittish, defensive, but at the same time genuinely want to know what Prabhupada is teaching.

Yoko: If the mantra itself has such power, does it matter where you receive it, where you take it?

Prabhupada: Yes, yes. That is a fact. The example is given, just like milk. Milk is nutritious. That’s a fact. Everyone knows. But if the milk is touched by the tongue of a serpent, it is no more nutritious; it is poison.

John: But, you see, Maharishi said exactly the same thing about his mantra is coming from the Vedic, with seemingly as much authority as you, and he was probably right. So how... It’s like having too many fruits on a plate and you can’t only eat two of them.

Prabhupada: No. If mantra is coming down in that way, then it has potency; the potency is there.

John: But Hare Krishna is the best one.

Prabhupada: Yes.

Yoko: Well, if Hare Krishna is the best one, what is the reason why we have to bother to even say anything else other than this one?

Prabhupada: You don’t require to bother to say anything else. We say that Hare Krishna mantra is sufficient for one’s perfection.

George: Surely, isn’t it like flowers? If somebody may prefer roses and somebody may like carnations better... Isn’t it really a matter for the devotee, that one person may find Hare Krishna is more beneficial to his spiritual progress and yet for somebody else, some other mantra may be more beneficial? Isn’t it like just a matter of taste? Like judging a flower? They’re all flowers, but some people may like one better than the other.

Prabhupada: But still, there is distinction. The rose flower is considered better than simply a flower without any flavor.

Yoko: So a flower that has flavor is better than—?

Prabhupada: If you want a yellow flower—never mind whether there is any scent or any flavor or not—so that flower is there. It is for you. That’s all. But if anyone wants rose flower, Krishna gives him rose flower. But when you make a comparative study which one is better, then rose will be considered better. \[Prabhupada chuckles.\]

Yoko: But you said if the milk goes through a serpent’s mouth it will produce poison and, for instance, the Catholic Church and all those churches first probably had good words, and now it’s deteriorating. Now, how would you decide, really, what brahmins are always in the pure state that you speak of that they would never turn into serpents?

Prabhupada: For that you have to become a serious student.

Yoko: Well, I mean, what do you mean by “serious student”? Does it depend on knowledge? I mean, the final judgment that you make?

Prabhupada: Everything depends on knowledge. Without knowledge, how can we make progress? Serious student means to acquire knowledge.

John: Yeah, we’ve gotta go around. Yoko never met Maharishi. We’re asking advice of how to, you know, how to stop. You can go on forever. I know people that have been wandering around for years seeking gurus and spiritual teachers. I mean, it’s doing them all quite well, but—

Ah, now we’re at the crux of the matter, and just as I’m thinking this, Prabhupada signals to a devotee to bring prasadam. Then he looks back at John, who says, “I mean, we can only judge on a material level by looking at your disciples and looking at other people’s disciples and looking at ourselves, you know. And, of course, if there are thirty disciples, seven of them look fairly spiritual, another ten look okay, and the others just look as though they’re having trouble. You know. So there’s no... We still have to keep sifting through like sand to see who has got the best answers, or—

Prabhupada interrupts him. “Try to understand this. You ask how to find out the best authority. To answer this question: Krishna is best authority. There is no doubt. Maharishi takes also Krishna’s book and Aurobindo takes Krishna’s book, Vivekananda takes Krishna’s book, Dr. Radhakrishnan takes Krishna’s book. So Krishna is authority. Shankaracarya also says, ‘Krishna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead.’ He says this very word in his commentary. So authority means one who has accepted Krishna as the Supreme Lord. Then he is authority.”

Yoko: But about the knowledge, I’m a bit worried about it. If you have to, you know, learn Sanskrit and all that, and that’s the only way to get enlightenment... I mean, what do you do about people who are not skillful in learning languages and things like that?

Prabhupada: Just like through channel you will understand very easily. You send some money order to your friend. So from which channel he’ll receive? He’ll receive through the post office, not through any other channel. So if the mailman delivers it, you are confident, “Yes, the money has come.” So why you give the importance to the mailman? Because he is representative of the post office. Similarly, Krishna is the original authority. So Krishna’s representative is also the authority. And who is Krishna’s representative? One who is a devotee of Krishna. So therefore the devotee of Krishna is authority, at least of Bhagavad-gita. So you have to receive through the devotee of Krishna about Bhagavad-gita. This is common sense.

George: I’m not saying Krishna isn’t the Supreme. I believe that. But we didn’t really have an understanding as to the identity of Krishna.

Prabhupada: That’s all right. If you believe Krishna is the Supreme Lord, if that is your version, then you have to see who are most addicted to Krishna directly. For argument’s sake \[and he gestures around the room\], these people are twenty-four hours chanting ‘Krishna,’ and another person who says not a single word about Krishna, how he can be representative of Krishna, who does not utter even the name of Krishna? If Krishna is accepted as authority, therefore those who are directly addicted to Krishna, they are authorities.

That’s it. Everyone is whipped, wilted by the intensity of the exchange. George, John, and Yoko shift their legs and butts around, groan from sitting so long on the floor. Prabhupada leans back, ever-fresh and beaming, and calls for kirtan. I hand George kartals, motion for him to lead—he’s shy, but his face lights up-and he closes his eyes and begins singing Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna...

After nearly two hours with Prabhupada, John and Yoko pranam and walk slowly out, silent with their thoughts. George slides over close to Prabhupada and excuses himself. He has to leave for Liverpool to visit his mom in hospital, he says, but he’ll see Prabhupada when he comes back. Prabhupada commiserates about his mother, beams and, as George bows, pats him on the head. George leaves looking very happy.

Then Prabhupada turns to us. “So, now what is the program?”

Mukunda says, “Srila Prabhupada, we’ve set up a series of lectures at Conway Hall—that’s right in the center of London—six of them, and you’re speaking at Camden Town Hall on September 15.”

Gurudas adds, “And there are some engagements in Indian people’s homes, and we have some radio and TV shows, the BBC...”

Prabhupada turns to me. “Shyamasundar, the temple building, Bury Place—what is the position?”

“Well, Prabhupada, we’ve had some delays due to building permissions, but the work is going on daily. I’ve got a lot of helpers now. I would say it’s about 70 percent finished.”

“That is all right. At least for time being we are well situated here. Try to finish as soon as possible.”

Then Yamuna speaks. “We’ve built up a nice temple here, Swamiji, in the old art gallery. We have classes every day, and you can speak there mornings and nights. We can’t invite a lot of people or John and Yoko may get angry with us...”

We talk about cooking arrangements, and baby Saraswati, and the beautiful grounds for his morning walks. Prabhupada makes certain that his children’s needs are satisfied. Then he expounds on the British Empire—how a few Englishmen were clever enough to take over his country and make it the crown jewel of their empire and thus support palaces like Tittenhurst. “But one thing,” he says, “these English have given their language, so I am able to come to you.” When someone mentions that John bought Tittenhurst from the heirs to the Cadbury fortune, Prabhupada says, “Oh, this Cadbury, yes. We have this chocolate in India. Just see, our money has paid for this house.”

We talk about the “Hare Krishna Mantra” record and how it is rapidly climbing the charts, explaining to Prabhupada what a “chart” is.

“And how much money is coming from this?” he asks.

Mukunda explains that we are receiving 10 percent of the net record price as performer fees and another 10 percent for composer fees, and that he has listed himself as composer of both songs in order to get the extra 10 percent. Prabhupada is loving it, beaming with a father’s pride that his children have done well in the world. With Mukunda, he calculates our profits. “And how much is the record price?”

“Each record sells for about $1 retail, so the net is about 50C ."

“So, twenty percent? And how many records?”

“About 80,000 so far, but it’s only been out about two weeks and sales are increasing daily.”

“So, say, twenty percent of $40,000 equals to $8,000—oh! It is a good business: simply sing something, chant Hare Krishna, and so many people are giving you money.”

Gurudas says, “Yes, and now also we are invited to so many clubs and concert halls to chant, and they pay us for that. We are called ‘Radha Krishna Temple,’ just like a famous band of musicians. Just like tonight, they are showing us chanting the “Hare Krishna Mantra” record tonight on Top of the Pops—that’s a BBC television show watched by millions of English people.”

We discuss upcoming initiation ceremonies for the new English devotees: David (who will become Dhananjaya), Pamela (who will become Pritha devi), and a couple, Dave and Denise (who will become Devadatta and Draupadi). We discuss the work we are doing for John: stripping walls and carpets in the main house prior to painting everything white, chopping weeds and planting trees in the garden. We linger, drinking in Prabhupada’s presence, until Purushottam comes in and informs Prabhupada that his prasadam is getting cold.

“All right,” he says, finally, “you go now, take your prasadam. We will meet tomorrow.”

A week earlier, we had gone to the ВВС-TV studios in London to videotape a pantomime of Radha Krishna Temple singing the Hare Krishna mantra, lip-syncing the words to our record. Top of the Pops broadcast every Thursday night for thirty minutes and, with twelve million viewers, it was the most-watched show in Britain. We didn’t have a TV set, but on the very night Prabhupada arrived in England, the Radha Krishna Temple appeared on Top of the Pops as the third of seven acts. On the same show were Crosby, Stills, & Nash doing “Marrakesh Express” and Bobby Gentry doing “I'll Never Fall In Love Again.” George watched the show and told us later that it was one of the highlights of his life to be sitting at home and watching the Krishnas on Top of the Pops. He got a kick out of converting a gang of outlandish-looking monks into rock stars.

Early the next morning, September 12, Prabhupada dives into his new surroundings. Walking the magnificent grounds, through prized rhododendron thickets still in bloom, he points his cane at flowers and trees and things around us and makes flowing philosophical statements like, “Just see all these statues of so-called famous men. Now they are covered with weeds, and no one remembers their names.” The donkey John keeps in a meadow also becomes a topic for Prabhupada’s wise and often hilarious comments.

That first morning in the gallery-temple room, Prabhupada delivers an energetic lecture from Srimad-Bhagavatam (5.5.1), and he tells us some stories from his travels:

When I was in Los Angeles, there is a freeway. So eight lines of cars running in seventy miles speed this way, and eight lines running cars on the opposite side. And unfortunately one day we had one car which was running at thirty-five miles only, and our Goursundar was driving, (chuckling) Immediately he was arrested by the police. Not exactly arrested—stopped. That means you cannot run your car in this way, thirty-five miles speed. So now from impartial point of view, if we study why people are running in this way and that way, what is the ultimate goal? If we calculate with very cool head, the ultimate goal is sense gratification. That’s all. I have seen in the Times Square in New York, there are so many advertisements for sense gratification. Advertising, ‘Here you will have nice girls. Come on,’ like that. Freely written and some naked picture. And so many theaters. The whole idea is sense gratification.

And the point: “Whatever Krishna has offered me, that is sufficient. No more. Then our problem of sense gratification is solved.”

After breakfast, we go to Prabhupada’s room and pore through the morning papers, looking for articles about his arrival. We find articles in the Daily Sketch (“Enter His Divine Grace Abhay Charan B. Swami”); the Sun (“Happiness Is Hare Krishna”); and the Daily Mirror, which has a photo of Saraswati with her tiny arms in the air, greeting Prabhupada.

The Daily Telegraph runs an article (“Hindu Temple Protests”) about our problems at Bury Place with the Camden Council: “Conversion of office premises in Bloomsbury into a Hindu temple is being investigated by the Ministry of Public Buildings and Works. If their planning application is not granted, it will cost them a lot of money.”

Mukunda explains to Prabhupada how behind-the-scenes pressure by Tom Driberg, MP, plus the subtle effect of public opinion from our newfound fame, are gradually overcoming the neighbors’ protests, and that he is pretty sure we’ll get the permission, but it may take another month or so.

This morning, Prabhupada wants to see Bury Place, so we arrange John’s Rolls and driver, and go to town in style. Mukunda, Gurudas, and I are in the back seat, with the beloved object of all of our desires and efforts, Srila Prabhupada, seated in front, on his first expedition into London.

We take the A3 into the city, approaching Westminster Bridge from the south. One of the biggest thrills of my life was seeing the look on Prabhupada’s face as we crossed the river Thames-his saucer-eyes look: his eyebrows shot up, his eyes opened wide, mouth set in a big “O,” as he turned his head to us and said, “It is just like a small canal!”

Mukunda explains that the tide is out. “When the tide is in, Prabhupada, the Thames looks much bigger.” And just then, Big Ben and the Houses of Parliament come up on our left. As we point these out, Prabhupada seems almost reverent, impressed, maybe harkening back to his youth when these faraway buildings were the center of the world to him.

“And that’s Westminster Abbey.”

“Oh, is this where we shall meet Archbishop of Canterbury?” He becomes thoughtful, then says, “We have so many temples in India, thousands of temples, much bigger than this.”

As we swing around on Victoria Street and pass Buckingham Palace, he says, “Even in Calcutta, so many maharajas, big industrialists, they have big palaces like this.” Winding our way up Pall Mall, we point out Whitehall, 10 Downing Street, and Trafalgar Square.

“Prabhupada, our Ratha-yatra parade finished here. Thousands of people chanted Hare Krishna in this -”
