“And who is that?” he asks as he cranes his neck and looks up out of the window.

“That’s Admiral Nelson on top.”

We can tell Prabhupada is thrilled and delighted with this voyage of discovery; he is seeing the great, imagined monuments of his childhood for the very first time.

“There’s the National Gallery, Srila Prabhupada, where they keep England’s great art, and this is Piccadilly Circus. There’s our Chartered Bank building, Regent Street, and the Beatles office is just behind. This is Leicester Square, Shaftsbury Avenue, Oxford Circus ... This is Oxford Street, the busiest street in London. We have daily sankirtan and sell BTGs along this street. Just ahead is the British museum. And this is Bury Place.”

I hold my breath as Prabhupada walks into the unfinished temple room. Two stories high now, solid wood up to about eye-level, two broad, fifty-foot wooden beams run down both walls. I tell him they will serve as the base for towering arches and a cedar-plank ceiling. Sawdust and plaster dust cover the floor, there are scraps of wood underfoot. It’s so cold in here you can see your breath. I show him how the walls will be soundproofed, even the door, and how we have mounted two horizontal steel beams that support the place. He touches different structures with his cane, asks questions, makes comments. Gurudas snaps away with his Nikon. My big worry all along has been that I made this job too elegant, too time-consuming and difficult, replicating the feel of a genuine Indian temple. But Prabhupada eases my anxiety when he expresses his admiration for the design and work. “This is the best temple we have seen. Now you just go on and finish it. It is very nice.” Whew!

One young boy, who is already working here when we arrive, hands us the latest issue of the International Times—just out this morning—and points to page thirteen, where there is a full-page ad from Apple for its latest hit record, “Hare Krishna Mantra”, by Radha Krishna Temple. The Jagannath deities are prominently displayed behind our band, and Srila Prabhupadas portrait occupies the bottom half of the page. The look of endearment when his eyes glance up from the paper, and the long moment of silence—I had to clear my throat to keep from choking up. Finally he says, “Yes, now let us look upstairs.”

Not only was “Hare Krishna Mantra” now a big hit in the UK, it was slowly climbing the charts in Holland, Germany, France, Sweden, Australia (where it was #4), South Africa, and Japan. It was even the number-one seller in Yugoslavia, a communist nation controlled by Russia.

**\* \* \***

With help from the Indian community, the devotees had organized an evening program for Sunday, September 15, at the Camden Town Hall, opposite King’s Cross Station in downtown London. Eddy will drive Prabhupada from John’s place and take the scenic route through five-thousand-acre Windsor Great Park, with its herds of antlered red deer grazing beneath giant oaks from the time of William the Conqueror. Prabhupada knows English history, and he enjoys a gentle nostalgia as we transit this pastoral scene, the towers of Windsor Castle touched by the setting sun.

At his first public meeting in London, Prabhupada is cheered by the nine hundred British and Indian people seated in the huge hall. He explains to them what a mahatma, great soul, is:

You have seen the great President of United States, Mr. Kennedy. He was supposed to be the happiest man in the world, but within a second he was kicked out, immediately. In our country Mahatma Gandhi was a very popular leader, but in a second he was removed from the field. Mahatma means broader-minded. They are not, I mean to say, short-minded, that “I am this,” “I am that”-“I am Hindu,” “I am Muslim,” “I am Christian,” “I am Indian,” “I am German, I am Englishman —no. Mahatma is sarvo-padhi-vinirmuktam. He is freed from all designation.. . Our real identity, real constitutional position, is that we are part and parcel of Krishna, or God. Therefore you can understand what is your duty. Just like this hand is part and parcel of your body. Now, what is the duty of this hand? To serve the body, that’s all. The living example you can see, that I have got now branches, twenty branches in Europe and America. They are neither Hindus nor Indian. In our Society, International Society for Krishna Consciousness, perhaps I am the only Indian. But why? How they are taking it? Because it is there, Krishna consciousness is there, within you, within me, within everyone. Simply we have to invoke it. That’s all. And that invoking process is the sankirtan movement. Ceto-darpana-marjanam.

Many of the Indian families we had met over the past year were there at Camden Town Hall that night, getting their first glimpse of Srila Prabhupada. As usual, he knocked them out. Now they began to understand the disciplined fervor of this general's London army, he was the real thing. We sincerely liked these people from India, but found that many Hindus had fixed ideas about their religion, and considered Krishna to be just one god among many. Now, after hearing Prabhupada and seeing him so regal, so self-assured, so authentic and powerful, we sensed an awakening among them that they should take his movement seriously, maybe change their beliefs.

The next night, September 16, a BBC-2 TV crew came out to Tittenhurst to film some background for Late Night Line-Up, the most popular late-night show in Britain. We followed them back to their studios in Shepherd’s Bush, where Prabhupada and some devotees were interviewed by suave host Tony Bilbow late into the night.

Tony asks Prabhupada, “Do you have a concept of hell in your religion?”

“Yes, London is hell. Of course, it is a very great credit to the English people to have established such a great civilization in such a climate.”

Gurudas, picking up on the Swami’s playful mood, says, “And if you chant ‘Queen Elizabeth, Queen Elizabeth, Queen Elizabeth,’ over and over again, you’ll get disgusted.” (Three days later, Late Night Line-Up will feature the Beatles in a one-hour “Abbey Road Special.”)

Meanwhile, Apple record sales were booming. Prabhupada wrote to Tamai Krishna on September 19: “The Hare Krishna record is selling very nicely. Yesterday, it sold 5,000 copies, and this week it is on the chronological list as #20. They say next week it will come to be #3, and after that it may come to #1. So they are very much hopeful of this record.”

The Beatles’ Abbey Road album, including George’s songs, “Something” and “Here Comes the Sun,” was released on September 26. Abbey Road would become one of the biggest-selling records of all times. Prabhupada doesn’t know much about this and doesn’t really care. His disciples have told him that these Beatles are very important and influential people, world leaders, but he is actually oblivious to all of that. His mind and being are fixed only on Krishna, on glorifying Him, spreading His divine message. Prabhupada sees everyone equally, both the pauper and the billionaire:

These moneyed men and women have to be very cautiously dealt with in spiritual life. We have to sometimes deal with them on account of preaching work; otherwise

Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu has strictly restricted to mix with them for the Krishna Conscious people. But we get instruction from Rupa Goswami that whatever opportunity is favorable for pushing on Krishna Consciousness we should accept.

We were instant rock stars, even giving autographs to screaming fans. With our hit record, our Radha Krishna Temple band was in great demand. Mukunda and Gurudas handled all these show-biz affairs. At first, Apple would call and say, “So-and-so wants you to play at their club. Here’s the phone number.” Later, Apple hooked us up with Red Door Agency, who booked the RKT at nightclubs, concert halls, and posh private parties all over London. We performed in Manchester, Birmingham, and other cities in the UK, and eventually we played all over Europe. Whoever showed up the night we were performing was in the band. Sometimes there were five or six of us, sometimes twenty. Radha Krishna Temple was the worlds only floating band.

On the night of Sunday, September 28, just a couple of weeks after Prabhupada’s arrival, RKT headlined at the Marquee Club on Wardour Street in Soho, at the time the most famous club in London, regularly featuring acts like the Rolling Stones, Led Zeppelin, The Who, and Jimi Hendrix. What a scene. The night we were there, the dark, smoky nightclub was crowded with London’s jet set, drunk and yelling—and we had to settle them down, ask for silence. We start off with pliiiing, pliiiing, pliiiing (the tamboura); then one pair of kartals quietly ting, ting, tll-linngg; then Mukunda’s mridanga—kadawoomp kadawhoomp; and Kulashekar’s mridanga: Ba-boom-boom\*. Then Yamuna’s celestial voice: “Namah от vishnu padaya, krishna presthaya ...” My amplified esraj picks up the melody. As we sail through “Prayers to the Spiritual Masters,” we can’t believe it. The crowd actually sits still, mesmerized and tranquil. We segue into the maha-mantra, more kartals, more oomph, and soon the crowd is clapping along with us, singing in response. A long, loud kirtan follows—and the crowd loves it.

On our way out of the Marquee Club, we run into Tom Driberg, MP, on the sidewalk. He’s totally drunk. He tries to give us all his money, turning his pockets inside out, and, worried for his safety, we drive him home.

In the mornings in his room at Tittenhurst, Prabhupada loves to hear our stories about our nights out chanting in public and getting paid for it.

“So, how much they have paid you?”

“We made £100 for Krishna last night, Srila Prabhupada.”

But he cautions us, too, not to take our young unmarried monks to nightclub engagements.

On October 2, Radha Krishna Temple is again featured on Top of the Pops, coming in at #12 this week. We’re the fourth of seven acts on the show. Other acts this time include Creedence Clearwater Revival with “Bad Moon Rising” (#1) and The Hollies (“He Ain’t Heavy, He’s My Brother.”)

The presenter that night is the famous disc jockey Tony Blackbum. This time, the BBC shows a video of Radha Krishna Temple performing sankirtan on Oxford Street with the “Hare Krishna Mantra” dubbed over, like a predecessor to today’s music videos.

By October 4, the “Hare Krishna Mantra” is the eleventh most-heard record in the UK, up from #19 the week before. #4 that week is “A Boy Named Sue,” by Johnny Cash; #9 is Bob Dylan’s “Lay Lady Lay.” In the top 100 we’re ahead of Smokey Robinson and the Miracles (“I Second That Emotion”) and the Rolling Stones (“Honky Tonk Women.”) “Hare Krishna Mantra” stays on the charts for nine weeks—the first Sanskrit song in history to make the Top Twenty.

We keep asking George “What about releasing our record in the United States?” He is very upset about this—apparently, some sort of internal politics (maybe Catholic sentiments?) prevents EMI and Capital Records from promoting “Hare Krishna Mantra” in

America. But with this one single record, George Harrison has done more to spread the maha-mantra farther and faster than anyone else in history so far.

**\* \* \***

At Tittenhurst, after a long night of playing the clubs or working at Bury Place, we relished taking morning walks with Srila Prabhupada. It was warm the whole month of September—in the mid 70s—and there’d only been a few showers all month, so the garden was flourishing.

In San Francisco I had only gotten to hang out with Swamiji a few hours a week. Now I was with him every day. I would drive back to Tittenhurst after working late at Bury Place, and on the next morning’s walk Prabhupada would say, “I heard you drive in last night. It was about 11:40.”

We walk on the wide, crushed limestone path through spacious lawns still silver with dew, then around the fountain and statue of Diana the huntress. The tap, tap, tap of his cane on the path, the weak morning autumn sun hitting brilliantly colored crowns of beech and chestnut trees, “Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare”— Prabhupada stops, leans over to look at a small sign stuck in the ground and asks, “What is this?”

“These trees are called Cedrus atlantica, ‘Glauca Pendula’”— blue Atlas cedars from the mountains of Morocco. They call them ‘weeping cedars.’”

“Weeping? What is that?”

“Like crying.”

Prabhupada laughs and says, “Yes, they are weeping that ‘We have to stand in this cold place for a hundred years.’”

“Srila Prabhupada, you told that English reporter that London is so cold it is like living in hell.”

“Yes, they do not know. We have got choice. Even you have a big mansion and wear costly clothes, still you have to suffer in this cold place. The devotee of Krishna, he does not mind—if Krishna is here, I am always in a heavenly place...”

Prabhupada sees a freshly dug hole with a pile of soil beside it and says, “Just see, digging and piling. This is their whole life-digging and piling. I will dig holes and pile dirt and go away. Next life I will dig so many holes and pile. And they are thinking this is life.”

“Prabhupada, how is it that a person like me, from such a degraded background, can become a devotee?”

“Because you are intelligent.”

Walking, chanting japa, the Swami stops now and then to smell a flower, touch the grass with his cane, look around. He says, “Why this grass is green, that grass is yellow? (pause) You do not know? It is because the roots are not attached. Similarly, when we detach ourselves from Krishna, then we also dry up.”

“Srila Prabhupada, what was that song you were singing in your room this morning?”

“That song is about Lord Chaitanya Mahaprabhu. He would rise, and He would go out at this time of morning, when the sun has risen but is not yet in the sky, and He would dance in the morning, like this.” Prabhupada lifts his cane in the air and sways from side to side a couple of times, laughing.

Tap, tap, tap as we cross over the quaint, arched bridge. Then he asks me, “So, the temple work is going on nicely?”

“Yes, Prabhupada. The arches are about half completed. Now we’re building a scaffold to start putting them up. Yesterday I bought all the cedar wood to construct the ceiling, to set over the arches once they are in place. Tirthapada is a good carpenter. He’s helping a lot.”

We make the turn back toward John’s large, silent house. “So, what is the program today?” Prabhupada asks (as only Prabhupada can, with his unspoken innuendos like, “You’ve done a great job, but where is the action?” “I want to preach in London, so how much longer are you going to keep me in this place?” “Why aren’t you working harder?”).

Gurudas says, “Well, your first program at Conway Hall is next Sunday—October 6—and tomorrow night the Sharma family has invited us for kirtan at their home in Clapham. Yamuna and I stayed with the Sharmas for a couple of months. They are very nice people. But I can cancel if...”

“No, no, we shall go. These Indian people, they have been misled, that is all. Now they are seeing the Western boys and girls taking it up. They will help us.”

Waiting by the gate in the picket fence that surrounds the servants’ quarters are several devotees, who bow as Prabhupada strolls up with his gang. He hands Purushottam his cane while Janaki kneels down to help him off with his wellies. “Prabhupada, your cheeks are so rosy this morning!” she says.

“Ah, look, there is Miss Saraswati!”

“Pa-pa-pad!”

Saraswati rises from her obeisances, raises her little arms in the air, and runs toward him, then stops, hangs her head, shy, thinking maybe she’s overstepped her bounds. Prabhupada leans down, takes her hand, and they walk together into his building and slowly up the stairs, chatting like old friends.

In a closet next to his room, Prabhupada had set up small Radha-Krishna Deities. He’s been teaching Yamuna and others the art of Deity worship. Purushottam offers breakfast to the Deities with an arati ceremony, ringing the bell, while the Swami sits joking around with Saraswati. Suddenly Saraswati rises to leave, and Prabhupada tosses a marigold at her that bounces off the back of her head. She picks up the flower, brings it back and, arm extended, she stuffs it into Prabhupada’s mouth. Just then I walk in—and crack up. Prabhupada has that saucer-eyed look, an orange flower filling his mouth. I hand the kid her favorite toy, a small Gopal doll she had dropped on the stairs, and say, “Saraswati, show Prabhupada your new doll, Baby Krishna.”

Saraswati hands Prabhupada her little doll, and he admires it with an “Oooooh!” She turns to me for my approval, and while she’s looking away, the Swami tucks the doll behind his back and says, “Saraswati, where is Krishna?”

She looks around his desk, under the desk, makes a circuit around the room, and is about to cry, then points to Prabhupada, and says, “Pa-pa-pad has Krishna!”

Her little hands dive around in the fold of his dhoti and behind his back while Prabhupada shakes with hilarity. Then he reaches back and brings out the doll. Saraswati grabs it from him, shrieks, and runs out of the room, leaving us speechless were laughing so hard.

**\* \* \***

I came back to Tittenhurst late one night, and Malati told me that Saraswati had almost died that day.

What?!

She tells me that she had noticed Saraswati lying on the floor, blue and not breathing, so she ran to find Jill (John’s caretaker). They leaped into Jill’s little Austin and drove like crazy to Ascot, stopping at the first sign that said “Doctor.” Malati snagged her sari on the gearshift getting out, so she was practically undressed as she rushed frantically into the office—“Help! My baby’s not breathing!” She was chanting Hare Krishna so loud, she said, that the provincial doctor had to tell her to shut up so he could hear the baby’s heart.

Malati: Then all of a sudden he turned her over and she projectile vomited and then started to cry. I was pretty happy at that point just to hear her crying. So I asked the doctor what had happened? He says, "Madam, sometimes they do this.” Nobody was around-we were stretched pretty thin. So my point is that stuff like this was going on in the background, these little incidents.

Prabhupada busied himself at Tittenhurst with singing and playing harmonium while Purushottam softly rang the kartals for him. Yamuna would sit at the base of his stairs and weep as she listened. One day, Prabhupada sensed her presence and called out, “Yamuna, you are there? Come!”

Yamuna shakes, as if emerging from a dream, runs upstairs, wiping her eyes, enters the room, bows down, and sits.

“Do you enjoy my kirtan?”

“Oh, yes, Srila Prabhupada, very much.”

“These are prayers of Narottam das Thakur. This sound comes directly from the spiritual platform. And there is no need of understanding the language, Bengali language. It is just like a thunder-burst. Everyone can hear the sound of thunder; there is no misunderstanding. Similarly, these songs are above the material platform, and they crack like thunder within your heart. Why don’t you come here every day during my chanting? I will teach you.”

“That would be wonderful, Oh, thank you so much... Srila Prabhupada, what is your favorite bhajan?”

“What is yours?”

“Lord Chaitanya’s 'Shikshastakam prayers.”

“My favorite is ‘Hari Hari Biphale’.” Then Prabhupada finds the tune on his harmonium and recites the translation of the Bengali song as he plays: “O Lord Hari, I have spent my life uselessly. Although I have taken this rare human birth, I have not worshiped Radha and Krishna, and so I have knowingly drunk poison.”

He stops playing for a moment, and says quietly, “There is so much depth of meaning.”

Prabhupada’s disciple Jayapataka mailed the Swami a clipping from his hometown Calcutta newspaper, the Amrita Bazar Patrika (the Sunday, September 21 issue), headlined “Krishna Consciousness Movement in America.” Now Prabhupada has Purushottam read it out to the few of us gathered in his room:

In every town and village My Name will be sung by all creatures. This divine prediction of Sri Krishna Chaitanya is 483 years later coming to pass. In New York City, Boston, San Francisco, Los Angeles, Canada, London, Paris, and Germany, a group of stalwart workers are constantly engaged in fulfilling the prophecy of Sriman Mahaprabhu.

Their leader and preceptor, who has instilled the fire of devotion into the hearts of these young missionaries, is the revered Tridandi Bhaktivedanta Swami.... The energetic Swami has touched off a major “Spiritual Revolution” in the West. Swamiji is a true “Jet Age” Paribrajaka. All who come in contact with the 74 years young holy man are amazed at his wit, humor, scholarship, and humble but firm nature.

We poke some fun: “Oh, Prabhupada, you have made a revolution’. Humble but firm!” and he laughs. You can see he deeply enjoys the stir he is causing on his home turf in India.

Prabhupada speaks in the temple at Tittenhurst some mornings from the Fifth Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam, on the theme of sense gratification. Who knows, maybe he intuits that we might be lured by the opulent surroundings here. He says that life is meant for austerity, tapasya, and that we must follow regulative principles in order to understand “I am spirit soul, Brahman.” When 1 understand that I am not this body, that is called self-realization, he says. After self-realization, there are activities. This is where Bhagavadgita begins: by instructing the activities of a self-realized person.

For the self-realized person there are two paths to liberation: one for the yogis and one for the bhaktas, or devotees of Krishna. There are still many yogis in India.

They are trying to make perfection of the yoga practice, and they look like young men, say, twenty-five years, thirty years old. But they are seven hundred years old, three hundred years old. They are yogis. They have practiced yoga. Still, they consider they are not perfect. The perfection will be when a yogi at his sweet will can leave this body and can go to any planet he likes. Not only within this material universe, but also even, if he likes, he can go to the spiritual planet also. That is yoga.

But Krishna recommends the path of bhakti, devotional service: Just surrender unto Me, serve Me, and come to Me—why waste time on the difficult yoga process, which may take seven hundred years to perfect? “A spirit soul, being part and parcel of the Supreme Lord, he is by nature very powerful. We do not know how much spiritual power we have got that is being suppressed by the material covering.”

Prabhupada often uses the example of the sun planet. The yogi may see sunshine and wish to go to its source, the sun planet. After so much time he may qualify to go there, and if he becomes even more qualified, he may meet the proprietor of this place, the demigod Surya. But why waste time in this way? Why not go directly to meet Krishna, who has created the sun planet and so many millions of other planets? “So our material knowledge, material science, our material endeavor is very, very insignificant to understand the Absolute Truth. It is almost impossible. You cannot understand even one planet, and what to speak of the Supreme.”

Another zinger: one morning after Prabhupada’s lecture, a devotee asks him, “Did Krishna invent Radha for His own pleasure?”

“Not invent,” Prabhupada responds. “Krishna has nothing to invent. Invent means what was not in Him. For Krishna, everything is already there and then it is manifested. His energy is manifested. Radha is always manifest, constantly, but because we want to say that Krishna is the source of everything, we sometimes say created. If Krishna is transcendental whole spirit, similarly Radha, or His expansion, is eternally manifested ... So all of Krishna’s activities, they are manifestations of His spiritual internal potency. Do you understand this?”

No, but we’re trying.

**\* \* \***

By now we re a pretty big crew: the original six are now about twenty, with more people arriving every day. Besides the new English devotees, devotees from America and Germany are flocking to join the fun of living with the Beatles (despite the rumor spreading around ISKCON that we had gone to London, met the Beatles, and fallen into maya). We have to send out letters to all the temples to stop the flow: “Don’t come, please!” Living with John and Yoko is a delicate business. Mukunda, Gurudas, and I are hyperaware how much damage could be done by uncool, thoughtless acts wrought by even well-intentioned devotees. Our English devotees were young, they were coming along, but we had to keep our hands on the reins of this horse, not sure what will happen if we let go.

After morning programs in the gallery-temple, we discussed the work to be done that day, cautioning the devotees, time after time, to be cool, don’t disturb the main house, don’t wander the grounds, stay on our side of the line. This was a time of great stress, especially for Gurudas, who remained behind most days to supervise. We were in the home of one of the world’s most famous people, and the devotees were young and impetuous—we scarcely knew some of the new ones coming from abroad—and, let’s face it, several were loose cannons, to say the least.

As the weeks passed, John and Yoko came into the temple from time to time, especially on feast days, and chanted with us with apparent gusto. But gradually we didn’t see much of them anymore, and after a month or so, except for the work instructions Yoko occasionally delivered, we didn’t see them at all. Looking back, I often think about Prabhupada’s first conversation with John and Yoko, especially his example of President Kennedy and his reference to addiction—how so-called material happiness can be snatched from us in an instant, and either we are addicts of the material world or addicts of Krishna; we have a choice.

While we painted the main rooms and kitchen, it seemed that John and Yoko were hidden away upstairs, asleep. People came and went. One day Dan introduced us to a new guest from Yoko’s crowd in New York City named Alexander Trocchi. I had read Trocchi’s book, Cains Book, a few years earlier, and knew from it that he was an outspoken junkie. William Burroughs, another well-known heroin addict, also showed up at Tittenhurst. Mukunda and I started to get an idea what was going on in the main house.

Some of John’s earlier songs had hinted at his own drug addiction, songs like “Happiness Is a Warm Gun” (“I need a fix cause I’m going down”) and “Me And My Monkey” (“The deeper you go, the higher you fly”), but who was I to judge such things, myself a former drug addict? During our stay at Tittenhurst John wrote—and on September 30 he recorded—his famous single “Cold Turkey,” a raw depiction of heroin withdrawal. We now knew we were living in a very black and white world: heroin in the main house, Srila Prabhupada’s light beaming from the servants’ quarters.

Ranchor Prime describes this period in his book When the Sun Shines:

The two contrasting figures, John and Prabhupada, were both only passing through Tittenhurst, and though they would share its cedar groves they would hardly meet. John, when he was not away recording his solo music with Yoko, ranged through his mansion’s empty rooms, while Prabhupada lived quietly in the cottage or walked among the trees with his disciples in the early mornings.

Most days, Trivikram led a few devotees by bus and train into downtown London for sankirtan on Oxford Street, where they would also sell BTGs and pass out fliers for the upcoming Conway Hall lectures. Prabhupada was very pleased to hear that they were bringing in $25 to $50 a day.

**\* \* \***

On Monday, October 6, we staged the first of Prabhupada’s six public lectures at Conway Hall, 25 Red Lion Square, in Holborn, not far from the Bury Place temple. We had booked the Hall for the evenings of October 6, 15, 20, and 29, and November 10 and 17.

Our original idea was to keep Swamiji with us as long as possible whether we had a temple or not by arranging plenty of speaking engagements. We knew he enjoyed speaking to high-class English audiences. We had heard that Prabhupada’s godbrothers back in the ’30s and ’40s had spoken in London halls like this—maybe even at Conway Hall—so it seemed the perfect venue.

In San Francisco, the Swami had been a curiosity among curious people; in London, he was at home with the English ways, a gentleman among gentlemen, and with his head held high, he was a force to be reckoned with. His intelligence would be appreciated in Conway Hall, where cultured gentlemen had been speaking since 1919. Conway Hall was the historic home of the Humanist Society and Unitarianism, and A. C. Bhaktivedanta Swami would join the ranks of great intellectual leaders who had spoken here: in 1920 Ivor Tuckett spoke on “Mysticism and the Way Out”; in 1922 Bertrand Russell’s theme was “Free Thought and Propaganda”; in 1930 Julian Huxley lectured on “Science, Religion, and Human Nature.”

We too selected themes for each of Prabhupada’s lectures, themes like, “Teachings of the Vedas,” “Self-Realization,” “Sankirtan: Chanting the Glories of God,” “You Are Not This Body,” “The Song of God,” and “The Golden Avatar.” The stage setup was simple—Prabhupada designed it himself: at stage left, a table with small Radha-Krishna Deities; stage center, Yamuna with harmonium; stage right, the Swami seated on a table in front of the lectern; and devotees seated wherever they could find room. We created a ten-foot banner declaring the maha-mantra—I drew the letters on yellow felt, and the girls cut them out and sewed them on purple velvet. This banner dominated the wall behind Prabhupada.

At Conway Hall, Srila Prabhupada is animated and precise, more than we have seen him at other public gatherings. He gets right into it:

Ladies and gentlemen, today’s subject matter is the teachings of the Vedas. What are the Vedas? The Sanskrit verbal root of veda can be interpreted variously, but the purport is finally one. Veda means knowledge. Any knowledge you accept is veda, for the teachings of the Vedas are the original knowledge. In the conditioned state, our knowledge is subjected to many deficiencies.

He discusses the four deficiencies inherent in being human:

One: we make mistakes—“to err is human.” Two: we are prone to illusion. Maya means “that which is not.” Three: we have the propensity to cheat. Four: our senses are imperfect.

But the Vedas are not compilations of human knowledge. Vedic knowledge comes from the spiritual world, from Lord Krishna. Another name for the Vedas is shruti. Shruti refers to that knowledge which is acquired by hearing. It is not experimental knowledge. Just like you may ask, “Who is my father?” Only your mother can tell you with complete authority. So shruti is your mother.

In simple, concise strokes, Srila Prabhupada paints the picture of knowledge, from the tiniest ant to beyond the farthest star, telling us that everything we possibly want to know can be found in Srimad-Bhagavatam, the summary of all Vedic knowledge.

This historical first lecture at Conway Hall later became the introduction to Prabhupadas book Sri Ishopanisad. That night, Gurudas took a famous profile photo of the Swami, his eyes closed and finger raised in the air, which has been the logo of Back to Godhead magazine to this day. Prabhupada was truly the great sage in action.

Two days later, on October 8, George Harrison is again interviewed by David Wigg for his “Scene and Heard” program on BBC Radio 1:

David: George, how did you come to record “Hare Krishna”? George: Because the people from the Radha Krishna Temple were over here since about a year. And I got to know a couple of them, because they were in and out of the Apple office. And I’ve known about the Hare Krishna mantra anyway for a number of years. Originally, the spiritual master made a record in America, which didn’t really sell well. And apparently the people ran off with the money. But I got to know about it. And also in India, you know, they chant those sort of things all over the place. The thing about the word hare is the word that calls upon the energy that’s around from the Lord. Whichever Lord you like, really. But in this case it happens to be Krishna, which is like the words that Christ said became the Christian Bible. And the words that Krishna said became the sort of Hindu Bible called the Bhagavad-gita. So it’s just by merely the repetition of that. It’s the same if you were just to go round chanting “Christ, Christ, Christ, Christ.” If you say it long enough, then you build up this identification. Whatever you identify with, you become one with it. So it’s really a method of becoming one with God... It’s just another process. It’s really the same sort of thing as meditation, but this is the thing: it has more effect, I think. Or quicker effect, because music is such a powerful force. And it’s like God likes me when I work, but loves me when I sing. (George chuckles)

David: George, and what about these rules? Do you support all these things that the Krishna movement supports? I mean, for example, no alcohol and drugs, vegetarian food, no sex unless you’re going to have children, right?

George: Yeah, illicit. No illicit sex. There are members of this Radha Krishna Temple who are married and now have children. So all that means, you know, not raving around and knocking off everybody. You know, because that becomes a bit undisciplined. Because all those emotions like that, lust and greed, are emotions that have got to be curbed. I believe in being a vegetarian, because meat’s one of the worst things anybody can eat.

Thataboy, George, sock it to ’em.

On October 10, we got the best news of all: planning permission has been granted by the Camden Borough Council, legalizing Bury Place as a public house of worship. Prabhupada wrote to a devotee in Boston about it: “One good news is that our temple which was being checked by the planning commission for possession has now been released and we are now able to possess it. So our temple at 7 Bury Place is now fixed up.”

Well, not quite fixed up. Fifteen of the twenty huge ceiling arches are complete, and one or two a day are being set into place. There’s still maybe a month of work to be done at Bury Place, and Prabhupada was getting impatient. He wanted to be in London, where the action was.

**\* \* \***

IT #66 came out on October 10. On page 2 they’d printed a wonderful photo of Prabhupada, taken by Gurudas. Prabhupada is sitting erect on a bench in the VIP lounge at Heathrow, his caped shoulders spread wide, one hand on his cane, the other in his beadbag; his youthful face alert and grinning. The caption below the photo reads: “His Divine Grace Acharya A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami, founder of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness is now in London for a series of twelve chants and lectures at the Conway Hall in Holborn. The first of these lectures will be on 6th, 15th, and 20th Oct at 7:30 pm. For further information ring the Radha Krishna Temple 242-0394.”

On the afternoon of October 12, a few of us flew to Amsterdam to appear that evening at the Concertgebouw, Holland’s premier concert venue since 1888. Radha Krishna Temple will be the main act, with a little-known band called Deep Purple as the second billing. Deep Purple goes onstage first, whipping two thousand people into a frenzy with their new brand of heavy metal rock: loud, bass, repetitive chords, shouted lyrics. During “Smoke On The Water,” dry ice machines threw fog all over the stage. When the smoke cleared, guitarist Ritchie Blackmore smashed his guitar on the amplifiers and floor, and pieces flew out into the audience, rousing them to a fever pitch.

It was a tough act to follow. We lit dozens of sticks of incense and carried them out with us in fire buckets filled with sand that we’d found backstage. As the house lights came up, we were sitting there in a cloud of blue incense smoke, waiting for quiet. Janaki slowly strummed an amplified tamboura to set the mood, and after a few minutes, the crowd picked up on our serenity. Mukunda gave a short talk on the maha-mantra, promising them they’d get high if they chanted with us. “First we will chant, then you respond. We hope you will enjoy a special transcendental ecstasy.” They did. They loved it so much they wouldn’t let us leave. Twice we had to chant the Hare Krishna mantra in long encores until the Concertgebouw managers made us stop.

When we told Prabhupada about the Concertgebouw show the next morning, he asked, “Why you did not take me along?”

On the evening of October 15, Prabhupada gave his second lecture, “Self-Realization,” at Conway Hall. Attendance had fallen-maybe only a hundred people show up—but Prabhupada gave it his full shot, delivering an erudite, almost pedantic examination of the self, as if the hall were filled with PhDs.

When we speak of self, there are two kinds of self, according to Vedic literatures. The exact word in Sanskrit, the self, is atma. And there is another word—Paramatma. Atma and Paramatma. Atma is the individual soul; Paramatma is the Supersoul. There are other atmas: a mahatma is one who has developed his little soul to the stage of understanding

Paramatma; a duratma is very far from understanding the self at all.

Prabhupada used the example of the sun again, this time to describe the three stages of self-realization: Brahman, Paramatma, and Bhagavan. He spent most of his lecture describing Brahman realization, or the understanding that “all is one,” just like sunshine in England is the same as sunshine in America or India.

‘Now I am in the light, sunshine; my business is finished,’ that is Brahman realization. But that is not final. By Brahman realization, you are in the light, that’s a fact. You are in illumination, you are in temperature, that’s a fact. But if you are satisfied with such temperature and light, then you remain there. And if you are fortunate enough to make further progress, that is Paramatma—to realize the Supersoul in everyone’s heart: in your heart, in my heart, everywhere, all-pervading... The Supersoul is within every atom.

Recognizing that the Supersoul exhibits intelligence and thus personality, the seeker for self-realization will probe further in order to find the source of this intelligence: the Supreme Personality, Krishna.

We should not be satisfied either living in the Brahman platform or Paramatma realization platform. We must make farther progress and go to the platform of Bhagavan, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and engage ourselves in His loving transcendental service. Then our life will be successful. This is our Krishna consciousness movement.

On October 17, reporters from London’s major newspaper the Daily Sketch came out to Tittenhurst and photographed the devotees having lunch. Several photos and a short article appeared the next day: “Krishna People Dine Out at John and Yoko’s Place.” The article wasn’t bad, but it only served to agitate what was already becoming an untenable situation.

Yoko had been rushed away from Tittenhurst by ambulance on the evening of October 8. We knew she was pregnant and feared the worst: she had had a miscarriage. We gathered in the temple room and prayed for her. The next evening she returned by ambulance, and early on the morning of October 10, Mukunda and I received a summons from the main house that Yoko wanted to see us in the kitchen. It was still dark as we ran over from the morning program in the temple. Yoko was sitting at the kitchen table in a black gown eating cold lobster with a tiny fork. There was a golf ball-sized chunk of Nepalese black hash by her plate. Trouble was brewing. She started in by criticizing our work, our noise (we could hear the kartals from the kitchen), and letting it be known how much she would like to see us out of there. We got the message.

A few days later, at about 10 p.m., Dan came to Prabhupadas quarters and asked him to come over to the main house. John and Yoko wanted to have a private meeting with him. Could he please come alone? Prabhupada explained that it is traditional that a Swami cannot meet alone with a woman, and that he would have to bring his assistant, Purushottam. Reluctantly, John and Yoko agreed.

We saw Prabhupada walk over there, and everybody kind of stayed awake and watched out the windows. After midnight, I finally fell asleep. The next morning, we pestered Purushottam—“What happened over there?”—but Purushottam was kind of close-mouthed about it. But one thing he said was that John and Yoko wanted Srila Prabhupada to “zap” them, maybe touch them somehow and give them instant liberation. Yes, we had once thought like that too, that a mystic swami could just do or say something that would give you special powers. So we could imagine the scene—how Prabhupada must have patiently explained that liberation doesn’t just happen, that to get Krishna requires great sacrifice, reciprocation, and practice. On the walk that morning Prabhupada didn’t talk much about his meeting the night before, but he did say, “It is better we leave this place. Shyamasundar, you must increase the work.”

John and Yoko were such incredible people, so open and generous. They had welcomed Srila Prabhupada and his growing band of British devotees into their luxurious home, given us a glorious temple, food, magnificent surroundings. To their great credit, the Lennons tolerated so much from us—our noise, our shoddy work ethic, and our occasionally intrusive behavior. We were deeply grateful to our famous hosts, but the writing was on the wall: it was time to go.

I was working as fast as humanly possible at Bury Place. The arches were nearly all in place. The long 1" x 4" cedar planks were soaking in tubs of water, so they would bend and conform over the arches to make a ceiling. Electric wiring was being run for lights and power. In the meantime, Mukunda started looking for an interim apartment in London so Prabhupada could leave Tittenhurst as soon as possible.

**\* \* \***

On his morning walks, Prabhupada would often stop to chat with Frank MacDougal, the head gardener, who lived in a small cottage on the grounds. They would sometimes compare their symptoms of old age, Prabhupada trumping Frank by still having all of his teeth. Frank mentioned that he and his wife were having a problem with a ghost in their place. “What does Your Grace think about that?” Later that morning, Prabhupada sent a team of devotees over to Frank s two-hundred-year-old garden house, where they held a raucous kirtan and chased the ghost away.

My old red Fordson pickup had finally quit, after performing its devotional duties so graciously until its dying gasp. I gave it to Frank, who said he might be able to fix it. The last I saw of that truck it was sitting on blocks in the brambles behind Frank s ghost-busted home.

On October 24, Srila Prabhupada gave a program at the College of Law in Moorgate, in the oldest part of London, near the Inns of Court in High Holborn, home to British jurisprudence for centuries. Of course, his theme was law: the laws of material nature vs. the laws of God. These were young law students, and Prabhupada really laid it on. No matter what adjustments you make, he told them, you cannot escape birth, death, disease, and old age. This is natures law. So why don’t you follow Krishnas law and be happy now and forever? The students were fascinated and joined in the kirtan, everyone dancing.

The next day, October 25, Prabhupada wrote to Tamai Krishna in Los Angeles:

Yesterday we had a meeting in a law college and all the boys and girls joined us in chanting and dancing. But I think Mukunda is little bit strained managing all the affairs. The most important thing before us immediately is to organize a nice Sankirtana Party here in London for daily work, because there is as good potency of preaching work in London as there is in Los Angeles. But I do not know if it will be possible for you to come here for some days. Mukunda likes your assistance for coordinating all these opportunities. So please consider these points, and if you think that your absence in the West Coast will not cause any mismanagement, then I would advise you for some days to come here.

It’s true that Mukunda was burning out—we were all burning out. We needed help. Prabhupada decided to enlist aid from the Indian community. For that purpose, we booked a hall at the English-Speaking Union for October 27, where Prabhupada would speak to London’s Indian leaders. He sent them a personal invitation:

As you know that my mission is to spread Krishna Consciousness in the Western part of the World, I wish to establish one permanent Sri Sri Radha Krishna Temple in

London. In this connection I beg to invite you in a meeting as follows for the purpose. Please join and encourage me. Meeting on Monday the 27th day of October 1969 at 7:30 p.m. Place: English speaking union Main Luncheon Room.

37 Charles Street, W-1, near Berkeley Square.

One of Prabhupada’s biggest desires was to spread Krishna consciousness in Oxford. Oxford had long been the British home of Indology and Sanskrit studies, and to him, Oxford was the center of civilized education in the world. In letter after letter, he asked us to start something in Oxford. “I shall request you that your next attempt shall be to establish a center in Oxford,” and “I shall request you again to establish another branch in Oxford as soon as possible.”

So it was quite a feather in Mukunda’s cap when he arranged a lecture for Srila Prabhupada at Oxford Town Hall for the evening of October 30. Oxford is about fifty miles northwest of Tittenhurst, and as we pulled into this medieval town and began to roll past the famous colleges—Balliol, Corpus Christi, Magdalen, and the Bodleian Library—a mood of reverence came over Prabhupada. How small these buildings are, he said, considering their worldwide fame.

The huge town hall stood at the corner of High Street and St. Aldate’s. As we entered the brightly lit, all-white auditorium and saw that it was packed with four hundred students, we knew this would be a highlight of Prabhupada’s visit. He sat on a vyasasan placed center stage, well up from his audience and surrounded by his devotees. You could sense an eager thirst for knowledge in the air. And Prabhupada knocked them out, speaking basic philosophy but with verse after verse in Sanskrit.

When kirtan started, it was so ecstatic that every person in the hall stood and danced. Mukunda’s passionate moods were normally well hidden by his calm demeanor, but even Mukunda, in a rare moment of abandon, ran over to the keyboard for the huge pipe organ that dominates the hall and began to play. This organ was built in 1897 by Father Willis, England’s greatest organ builder, and it has dozens of huge pipes that literally cover the back of Oxford Town Hall. The sound is enormous. On this night, instead of a Bach fugue, the hall filled to resounding with chords of the maha-mantra. It was overwhelming.

After the kirtan, many students came forward to meet Prabhupada, and he was beaming all over the place. For those who could see, he was the classic Oxford don, professor emeritus of Absolute Spiritual Science—and to me, the world’s smartest man.

Prabhupada reported the meeting to his disciples in Boston: “Yesterday we had a very successful meeting at Oxford at the Town Hall. About 350 boys, girls, old men, ladies and gentlemen participated, and we made them all dance and chant with us, every one. After the meeting, many boys and gentlemen came to congratulate me. Out of them, one was an Egyptian gentleman.”

The next day, Mukunda found a three-room flat at Farley Court in Allsop Place, where Baker Street meets Marylebone Road, across from The London Planetarium and Madame Tussaud’s Wax Museum and about a fifteen-minute drive from Bury Place.

**\* \* \***

Prabhupada leaves Tittenhurst and moves to Farley Court on November3. Over the next few days, now that we have planning permission, the unmarried devotees who had been living at Tittenhurst gradually squeeze into Bury Place, while married couples find scattered rooms around London. Malati finds a little basement room around the corner from Bury Place, near Russell Square—she’ll stay there with the baby, and I’ll sleep where I fall, in the temple. Mukunda and Janaki also find a place nearby. Gurudas and Yamuna stay at Bury Place to supervise the young devotees. We’re spread out again, but at least we now have a center, and although we don’t see Prabhupada much, we know he’s not far away. We double our efforts to get the temple ready for him.

The twenty arches are now in place, and we start laying more than a hundred pliant 1" x 4" cedar planks over them, fitting them tightly to each other one by one and, hunched in the tiny crawlspace above, nailing them to the wooden arches. I ask Prabhupada if I should just abandon my fancy ideas and make something plainer, just to be finished with it, but he says, “No, what you have started you must finish. It is a good design. You just go a little faster.”

A squad of devotees readies Prabhupada’s room at Bury Place, which is just above the temple room. They clean, paint, repair the natural gas heater that’s mounted inside the old fireplace; they lay an oriental rug and hang dark-blue curtains from his windows that look below onto Bury Place; they replace plumbing and the toilet in his tiny bathroom. When his room is ready, Srila Prabhupada comes over from Farley Court to Bury Place on some days to write letters, translate, or to meet with people. But the construction noise is disturbing—we’re constantly pounding nails on the temple ceiling, which is just below his feet—so we urge him to stay away until the place is finished. Hah! It’s like trying to hold back the Ganges with a Ping-Pong paddle, and he comes anyway. “The noise is not a bother,” he says, “it is a blessing.”

Meanwhile, Malati and other ladies fix up the basement kitchen and “dining” room. Both are very small, maybe 15' x 15'. Everyone’s shoes are kept in a pile at the bottom of the basement stairs, where in shifts dozens of devotees and guests squeeze together on the cold cement floor to eat. Behind them, under the sidewalk on Bury Place, is a dank storage room, where we keep books, magazines, wood, and food supplies.

Malati: To ventilate the kitchen, we opened the door that led out to a deep well that separated the back wall of our building from the next building. It was like a dungeon. There were shoes—some-times a hundred shoes in a pile—you couldn’t walk.

The last touch before building the vyasasan and altar is to cut and mount the beloved redwood planks I had had shipped from California. These will cover six inches of fiberglass insulation material and form the interior walls of the temple room. We use marine copper nails, at eight pence a nail, because they look so cool against the redwood. The place is more buttoned up each day, and muffled by the soundproofing. It’s quiet in here—from the street you could hardly hear the kirtan. But the room gets hot quickly, so we install a small, twelve-inch ventilating fan at the back of the room (we have to break through the buildings outer brick wall to do it). Still, the room will remain stuffy when filled with floodlights, candles, incense, and the heat of dancing devotees. Prabhupada inspects our work daily, subtly putting the squeeze on us to finish.

Gurudas makes arrangements for Radha Krishna Temple to appear on TopPop, the Dutch equivalent to Top of the Pops. This time Prabhupada is not to be held back, and in early November, ten devotees are on a plane with the Swami to Amsterdam. This is the first time most of us have traveled with Prabhupada, and we relish every moment of it, our spirits leaping at this wild adventure with our Pirate Leader.

Gurudas had called TopPops producer, Rien van Wijk, and insisted that the Swami be allowed to speak, or we wouldn’t do the show. Van Wijk met us at Schiphol airport, and took us in a private bus to the studios. We spent hours waiting in an underground bunker for show time, praying Prabhupada would be comfortable and, although we had little control over the situation, doing everything we could to keep him happy. They fussed over us with makeup, and provided a nice armchair for the Swami. Prabhupada would be given thirty seconds to speak following the “Hare Krishna Mantra.”

Finally, we sat onstage in our usual Radha Krishna Temple ensemble, waiting for the cameras to roll for a practice run. We were to mime playing our instruments and lip-sync the mantra (“That means moving our lips, Prabhupada, pretend that we’re singing the words”) while they played the Apple record.

George’s opening slide guitar notes drone over the speakers, and Yamuna’s low voice is barely into “Ha-re Krish-n-a” when six girls in miniskirts appear below the stage and start dancing the

Watusi, the latest dance craze. Then the dry ice machine cuts in and envelopes us in fog. Then the strobe lights come on.. .Whoa! “Wait a minute!” Gurudas shouts. Prabhupada is relaxed in his overstuffed Dutch armchair over there, a bemused look on his lotus face. “We can’t have this, Rien!”

And Gurudas negotiates a compromise: the wardrobe department finds some colored madras sheets and we drape them over the girls, teach them the Swami two-step, unplug the strobe lights, allow the dry ice but stand, so the fog reaches only to our knees— and the show goes on. At the end of our song, Malati bangs the fake gong they’ve found in the props department and Prabhupada comes in on cue and delivers a perfect, thirty-second Krishna conscious capsule—and it’s a wrap. Afterward, the TopPop crew members exclaim that it was one of the best shows they’ve ever done.

Yet the long, stressful day is not over, and we go almost ballistic when our tickets get screwed up at the airport, the flight is delayed, and Prabhupada has to go through customs on his own. But in the many photos taken by Gurudas that day, you can see the calm confidence and pleasure in Srila Prabhupada’s face as he enjoys a day out with his crazy children. We arrive in London very late that night. Prabhupada, the first swami to make the Hit Parade in recorded history.

The next morning, Mukunda brings a message over from Farley Court: Prabhupada wants to leave London in little more than a month—by December 20—and we must finish everything before he goes.

We notify the Camden Council inspectors that we are finished with basic construction at Bury Place, and we schedule a final sound test with them. The inspectors arrive with a handheld meter that measures decibel levels. We assemble five or six devotees in the temple room and, in the presence of the two inspectors, we start banging mridangas and kartals, chanting at the top of our lungs. The inspectors leave the room with their little gizmo. As soon as the door shuts, we go on pantomiming a full-on kirtan without actually striking the drums or cymbals, dropping our voices to a whisper. We see them walk up and down the sidewalk outside as we jump around, silently whooping and hollering. A few moments later they return and we re back to full blast. “Congratulations,” they say. “A good bit o’ work. We couldn’t hear a thing”—and they sign off on our final sound permit.

Mr. French, who lived in the basement flat next door, was the most vociferous opponent to our planning permission. He petitioned authorities for a protest meeting at 7 Bury Place. A few of us, along with our solicitor, met with two members of the Camden Council and Mr. French and his solicitor (a former Olympic fencing champ). We pulled the same gag. Whenever French and his team were in the temple room, kirtan would be going full blast, and the moment they left, the devotees would go silent and pantomime the kirtan. When we met in the office upstairs afterward, Mr. French was livid. “It’s a bloody trick!” he yelled, while the lawyers tried to calm him. But Mr. French had had enough. He ran to the office door, yanked it open—the doorknob came off in his hand and he bowled over backwards, sprawling on the floor, his face beet-red. “Mr. French!” his solicitor said. “Just when the going gets tough, you bolt in the crunch!” For days after, we took turns reliving this scene to hilarious laughter.

Tamal Krishna arrived from Los Angeles on November 11. We were relieved to see him—a strong, senior devotee who could manage all these brahmacharis, shape them up for sankirtan and distributing BTGs and Prabhupada’s precious books. By now, thousands of magazines and hundreds of books—mostly Bhagavad-gitas and Teachings of Lord Chaitanya—were piled in the cellar beneath the sidewalk at Bury Place. We had just been too busy and disorganized to distribute them. Tamal took charge quickly, and he soon had teams of devotees out on sankirtan day and night every day of the week.

When we met the Swami in early 1967, his live lectures and letters to us, his conversations with us, and a few printed lectures in BTG magazine were basically our only sources of knowledge about Krishna consciousness. We did have his three-volume set of Srimad-Bhagavatam, First Canto, but these books were hard to read, printed in India at a time when Swamiji was still polishing his English. And anyway, there were not many copies of them to pass around. What we did have was the person Bhagavatam teaching us directly, and we had our devotional work and chanting, and we discussed Krishna a lot, and these seemed enough—more than we could absorb, really.

By late autumn 1968, we had Prabhupadas Bhagavad-gita As It Is in our hands, and this book, at last, gave us a solid footing in the philosophy. We read a few verses every day, enabled by Krishnas own words to understand most of what Prabhupada had been saying, and we began to use this understanding to preach to others. Bhagavadgita was book knowledge enough for the bred-up pioneers.

While the Swami was with us in San Francisco, we knew he was summarizing Sri Chaitanya Charitamrita to form a book he called Teachings of Lord Chaitanya, but by the time a few hundred copies of this book arrived on British shores in June of 1969, we scarcely had time to read it.

During the spring of 1969, we kind of knew that Swamiji was writing a book called Krishna because he’d mentioned it in a letter to Gurudas, but we didn’t know much about it. Nor, I must admit, did we much care. It was the job of other godbrothers—devotees like Satsvarupa, Brahmananda, Rayarama, Adwaita—to print books. Our job was overwhelmingly clear: build a world-class center for the Swami in London.

But on July 15, 1969, Prabhupada had sent me a letter: “I have prepared a nice book, Krishna, and I want to print it in a deluxe edition. If some of your friends finance this publication, it will be a unique presentation to the world. This will contain Krishna’s life from the beginning of His Appearance to His Disappearance from this mortal world. It will contain all of His activities throughout. It is full of philosophy, instructions, transcendental pastimes and artistic pictures.” OK, sounds great—and I promptly forgot about it. Preparing for Ratha-yatra and building Bury Place consumed every waking moment.

As for Prabhupada’s reference to my “friends” financing his new book, I had made a strict policy never to ask George for anything. One of the main reasons we were accepted by George and the Beatles was that we never asked them for anything, separating us from the thousands of outstretched hands around them.

I wrote back to Prabhupada that George had recently offered to put up $3,000 to buy marble for the new altar at Bury Place. But I must have also written that I thought George would probably print this Krishna Book (he seemed to be in the mood these days), because in his next letter to me, dated July 31, Prabhupada wrote: “I am very pleased to note that you have made such a nice recording of Hare Krishna, which may be released by the Beatles Company. Also, it is very encouraging to learn that you think Mr. George Harrison will be happy to print our Krishna Book. This will be a very great service to the mankind at large, if he can be of assistance in this connection. We are giving a unique contribution with this book; a book which tells of the activities of God. There is no other book that has been authoritatively presented to the Western World up till this time which has given so high a contribution.” \[emphasis mine\]

Wow, a pretty heavy statement. Knowing my penchant for flowery propaganda, Prabhupada often sold his ideas to me by using a kind of show-biz language. But I was always doing stuff like this, sticking my foot in my mouth. I didn’t have a clue whether George would print this book or not. I just liked to please the Swami, tell him big things that might or might not come true, almost daring myself—and Krishna—to do the impossible and make them happen.

August 1969 was an intense month of work at Bury Place. Prabhupada even sent detailed plans for the throne, down to the color of the marble, months before the throne would actually be built. So the whole subject of the Krishna Book appeared to have died down—but not for the Swami. Again, on August 31, he wrote: “In your last letter you informed me that George Harrison may be interested to publish my book, Krishna, so I am carrying with me the manuscript. I want to print this book with many pictures, and the pictures are also ready in Los Angeles and Boston. So if possible, you can talk further with him about this publication, and if he does so, it will be very nice for him as well as for us.”

This one flew completely over my head. I didn’t even consider asking George to print this book. Jeez, the record just came out, he’s just done an all-day press conference with us to create major publicity, and he’s promised the marble for Bury Place—how can I even think of asking him for something more?

Two and a half months go by. Srila Prabhupada has since come to the UK and is now living at Farley Court. He comes to Bury Place every day to work in his room above the construction site, despite muffled, sporadic hammering and Skil saws buzzing beneath his feet.

One day, Prabhupada sends down word that he wants to see me upstairs. I lay down my tools and run up to see him, arriving out of breath. “Are we making too much noise, Srila Prabhupada?”

“No, no, noise means there is progress.” I start to relax.

“Achaa, one thing. You will be seeing George Harrison today, regarding marble?”

“Yes, I’m meeting George and his friend David Wynne at the place where they sell the marble. Mr. Wynne is what they call the Sculptor Laureate of England. He’s designing the new British coins, and right now he’s carving a bust of Prince Charles.”

“Oh, so he is very expert. When you will be back?”

“So he will help us pick out the best marble, and they will give a good price to him. You said in your letter, white or golden, right? After we select the marble, we’re going over to David Wynne’s house in Wimbledon for dinner—that’s pretty far from here, so I don’t know. It may be very late.”

Prabhupada has this big pile of pages, prints of full-color paintings, on his desk, and he starts rustling through them. He holds up a page—the picture of Vasudeva carrying newborn Krishna across the river while lightning bolts streak in the sky. (OK, I remember hearing the story.) Then he holds up others, one by one, telling me briefly the story behind each painting. (My mind’s still on the work downstairs, the deadline—and the paintings are nice, brightly colored, but seem rather amateurish to me.) “And this is the Kaliya demon,” he says. “This demon lived in Yamuna River and has threatened Krishna, and He has defeated, just see, dancing on the heads.” (I’m thinking back to Bible-school summers in Salem, Oregon, and the pictures of Jesus making one loaf into many, Moses parting the Red Sea. This is fairytale stuff. I don’t get it.) “And this one,” he says. (Ahh, there’s Krishna and Balarama jumping off a mountain. I kinda dig that one, like, we have nothing to fear in Krishna’s hands.)

Prabhupada goes on and on, showing me the illustrations, explaining what’s happening in each. I’m thinking: Prabhupada is so into this. He’s blissed out looking at Krishna’s childhood adventures. Adventures, yes, that’s what these paintings are about—we hadn’t heard much of this stuff before. I’m starting to get it. Look at him—he looks like a child reading Alice in Wonderland. He’s smiling, laughing, sometimes in awe as his eyebrows lift: “Just see!” he says, and he holds up the picture of a fight scene where Krishna is slaying some demon—the guy’s head is flying off!—and I’m starting to think this is pretty cool stuff.

“So long it is sitting there and we cannot publish,” Prabhupada says.

By now I kind of expect what he will say next: “We have got quote from Dai Nippon, $19,000. I want you to ask George to print this Krishna Book for me.”

I’m quiet. He can read the perplexed look on my face, the hesitancy.

“But Prabhupada, up to now we have good relations with George because we never ask him for anything. Hundreds of people are after him every day, saying, ‘Give me this, give me that.’ Because we never ask him, he is always willing to give us something. How can I do this? It may spoil everything—that is my worry.”

Prabhupada chuckles. “You do not worry. You just ask. That will be a favor for me. I shall take the—how you say?—blame. You tell him like this: ‘My spiritual master has requested you,’ like that. Krishna will help you, you will see.” He hands me a sheaf of paintings, “Just show these to Mr. Harrison.”

I bow and head back downstairs, my head spinning—and lose myself in work until the hour approaches and I must head out to meet George. It’s an hour-and-a-half drive out to Gerald Culliford Ltd., Britain’s prestigious purveyor of fine marble, in Kingston-Upon-Thames in Surrey, where I’m meeting George and David at three o’clock. The whole way I’m trying to block out what Prabhupada has asked me to do. Surely Krishna will take control—there’s really nothing to worry about—but my stomach is in butterflies and I’m worried almost sick.

George is in London a lot these days, helping Ringo with his new album, so he’s not under a lot of pressure. In fact, today George seems to be in very fine spirits. We don’t get to do stuff together often, just the two of us, George free of the restraints of being a Beatle and me out of my work clothes and in devotee mode, feeling lighter in a dhoti and flip-flops. David Wynne impresses me as a groovy guy, very aristocratic (hey, he patters with the Prince while he sits for him hours at a time). David has that quick, subtle English wit, and he and George are throwing one-liners back and forth that have me in stitches. David says he is the man who introduced George to the Maharishi a couple of years back. He strikes me as a very spiritual person, and I take to him immediately and we start having fun.

I hand him a little sketch I’ve made, the general temple layout, with altar design and dimensions, and I describe the temple room: mostly reddish-brown wood with probably a blue or purple carpet. “The Deities will be dressed in intricate, bright colors, so the marble shouldn’t be too busy—our eyes should focus on the deities’ faces.

And the Swami wants the altar dimensions to be something like 40" by 50", and the color of the marble should be white or golden.” George is running around the place like a little boy.

George could buy this whole place with a snap of his fingers, so his cautious bargaining with the salesman impresses me. We all kind of agree on gold as the main color, so when we see the travertine marble from Siena, Italy, we know. These are such expensive slabs—and this marble so rare—that we have to modify my sketches to fit what is available. Finally, we choose two pieces of three-inch-thick Siena. "We are knocked out by the Norwegian blue marble too—actually a kind of feldspathic granite called labradorite—blackish-blue with fluorescent blue flashes as the light moves over it, sort of like opal. So we choose this material for the stairs leading up to the altar. The pieces are bigger than what Prabhupada had suggested, but we have been struck by the incredible beauty of these stones.

Wow. Dark blue and gold, a perfect combo. Culliford s tells us they will cut and edge the pieces, polish them, and deliver them to 7 Bury Place in a few days. As we re leaving, George sees a big one-and-a-half-ton block of white South African marble and buys it for David as a gift. Wynne will later carve it into a sculpture called Gaia, which now sits in Abbey Gardens in the Isles of Scilly.

As we cross the parking lot to our cars, the skies begin to darken, heavy clouds roll in, and the wind blows dust in our eyes. We are ecstatic.

David lived at “Rushmere,” a converted farmhouse on Wimbledon Common in South London. He had a warm and welcoming home, and his wife, Gillian, and two young sons, Ed and Roly, were super people. The lightness of the occasion, and the delicious aromas of a fine vegetarian dinner, took my mind completely away from the task before me: asking George to fund Krishna Book. After several courses, we were still yakking away while a storm blustered outside. Heavy rain was sluicing past dark dining room windows, and thunder rumbled in the distance. A Brandenburg

Concerto played softly on a phonograph somewhere. Then dessert and coffee came and I knew it was about time.

As Gillian started clearing the dishes—it’s now or never, the party’s over, it’s a long drive back through the night—I looked across the table to George, and said, “George, Prabhupada wanted me to ask you something.” I hand the pictures to him across the table. “He’s been working for a year or so on this big book called Krishna— you’ve seen all those nice paintings by devotees in his other books? Well, this book has dozens of paintings like these, and it’s all about Krishna, His birth, His childhood, all the demons He kills, all that. Anyway, he wanted me to ask you if you’d print it for him.”

There, it’s out. As I’m explaining all this, I see his face start to change from happy-go-lucky dinnertime friends and patter about football, to serious, then to angry, and I think, “Oh, I’ve screwed this thing up forever now.”

A brilliant white-blue flash comes through the windows— КАВВООООООООООМММ!!! The huge house shudders—and the lights go out.

A lightning bolt hit the house!

Total dark and silence for maybe ten seconds. I can hear everyone breathing.

Suddenly, the lights come back on. Bach starts up again in the living room. Everyone’s oohing and ahhing. I look at George, expecting the worst—but a husky laugh and he’s got this huge grin on his face. “Well, uh, how much is it, then?”

I am swept by waves of relief—were all laughing and buzzing: we’ve seen Krishna’s magic together.

“Well, about $19,000.”

George says he has to be in London tomorrow—at Abbey Road studios, working with Ringo—so he’ll stop by Bury Place and talk it over with Prabhupada.

I drive my rented car back to London through the late-night storm—driving anywhere in London at night is a venture into the unknown—but tonight wheels find the path without effort. I’m on top of the world. I’ve just pleased my spiritual master in a very big way. Another rhinoceros. It doesn’t get any better than this. But will George really do it?

Shortly after midnight I pull in front of Bury Place and see that the light’s still on behind the blue curtains in Prabhupada’s room upstairs. He’s still here; he hasn’t gone back to Farley Court. I park, unlock the door, quietly go up the stairs, and timidly knock on Prabhupada’s door. “Yes, come in,” he says.

“So, what is the news?” I bow, and before I can even sit up I’m blurting out, “He says he’ll print Krishna Book!” As I give my excited report about both marble and book, I’m beaming all over, but Prabhupada is just smiling and calm, even after I tell him about the lightning hitting the house. Still, there’s that special gleam in his eye I’m beginning to recognize: he’s especially happy. “And George says he’ll be in London tomorrow, and he’ll stop by to see you about it.”

As I head out the door, Prabhupada says to my back, “You see, I told you Krishna would help you.”

Next day, George pops into Bury Place and hurries up the stairs to meet me in front of Prabhupada’s door, saying he’s really nervous, “more than going on the Ed Sullivan Show or meeting Elvis.” George bows down, and when he rises, Prabhupada greets him so heartily it’s like watching two ancient friends reunited, taking up where they left off long ago. The Swami hands George a sheaf of colored prints and says, “Just see. The devotees have worked so hard to paint so many pictures of Krishna’s pastimes, all big, big paintings. But here we have reduced into prints. I wanted the best quality printing work, so cost is a little more, but the general public must see Krishna nicely.”

“Yes, these are very nice... Oh, I like this one with Krishna and Radha in a boat. And what is this?” asks George.

“This is Trinavarta demon, in the whirlwind.”

“Hmm, kinda looks like me, doesn’t it?”

A few days later, November 19, Americans “landed” on the moon for the second time. Malati tells the story:

That Farley Court was a furnished flat. There was a small black-and-white TV. Carpeting everywhere, elevator. I was going every day to cook for him. I walked up to the door one day and the door was slightly ajar, and I had this feeling of apprehension. You couldn’t hear footsteps on the carpet, but I heard, “Malati, is that you?” Somehow, Prabhupada knew I was there. He said, “Come in. We’re going to watch TV. They say they are going to land on the moon today. Let us watch the fun.” So I went into the kitchen and started preparing food. Then I went in to watch the landing. Swamiji turned and said, “They did not go.” Purushottam was visibly upset, and he said, “But we just saw it!” Prabhupada said again, “They did not go”—so matter-of-fact, the way he said it. Then he said, “Turn off the idiot box. It has made idiots of us all.”

The November weather had started out mild, but by mid-month it had become windy, with thunderstorms and heavy rain. Winter was coming. Prabhupada had consulted Vedic astrological calendars and set December 14 as the most auspicious date to install the deities. On November 20, he moved into his upstairs room at Bury Place, urging us to finish the work so he could get on to other matters.

The temple room ceiling and walls were now complete. The next project was the vyasasan. I liked the idea that a great sage should sit on a lotus when speaking, so I start building a half-lotus growing out from the right wall, just in front of the Deity curtain and a few feet from the altar. Space was at a premium at Bury Place, so I had to make Prabhupada’s seat as small as possible, but at the same time, it had to be impressive.

I built three half-circles from eight-inch-thick wood with radii of 3'8", 3'10", and 4', with the smallest half-circle on the bottom—stacking them so that the eight-inch-thick tiers tapered up and out. The top half-circle was overlaid by a radial “flower,” like the petals of a lotus. Twenty-five 2" x 6" redwood boards cut to V-shapes and fitted tightly together flared out like a flower, supporting a four-inch-thick, 4 foot diameter red velvet-covered foam pad, where His Divine Grace would sit. Another four-foot round red velvet backrest with gold fringe fastened to the wall behind his seated form. Around each of the three tiers I wrapped an eightinch-wide strip of 1/8" polished brass, so the whole thing looked like a prop on the set of Star Trek.

**\* \* \***

Growing up in Calcutta during the British Raj, Prabhupada had been exposed to the Anglican Church and their Christian philosophy. He had attended Scottish Churches College, where Bible studies were mandatory. The Archbishop of Canterbury is for Anglicans like the Pope is for Catholics. The Swami would sometimes quote the Archbishop’s famous line, “You want the kingdom of God without God,” considering him a fellow traveler in his mission to spread peace in the world. From the moment we started for London in August 1968, Srila Prabhupada had urged us to contact the Archbishop of Canterbury and to set up a meeting between them. He once wrote to Yamuna, “Of various engagements in London when I go there, the most important is seeing the Archbishop of Canterbury.”

Mukunda was our ace at grooming bureaucrats to help us, so it was he who took on the task, through letters and the British old-boy system, to contact Michael Ramsey, the 100th Archbishop of Canterbury. When it was announced in the spring of 1969 that the Anglicans were going to sell “redundant” churches, Prabhupada wrote letter after letter to Mukunda and applied his gentle pressure: “Regarding my meeting with the Archbishop of Canterbury, please arrange for this.”

On June 21, Srila Prabhupada had sent Mukunda a very long letter addressed to the Archbishop, requesting that George Harrison personally deliver it to “His Divine Grace.” In the letter, Prabhupada introduced himself, his movement, and the basic theology of Krishna consciousness, saying, “The world is gradually declining in God consciousness... and because Your Holiness is the Head of a great religious sect, I would be very pleased to meet with you, and perhaps chalk out some program for helping to alleviate the present Godless situation.”

When Prabhupada had at last arrived in England, he had asked almost daily, “And what about my meeting with Archbishop of Canterbury?” Mukunda really tried, but the Archbishop evaded every effort. Finally, as a second choice, Mukunda arranged a meeting with the Archdeacon of Westminster, Edward Carpenter, in his chambers at Westminster Abbey. The Archdeacon and his wife, Lilian, were well-known supporters of the interfaith movement—the Dalai Lama called Lilian his “English mother.” So a few of us put on our best duds and drove to Westminster Abbey with Prabhupada. We walked into a room obviously filled with Christian piety and good will and were introduced to Carpenter, his wife, and Baron Donald Soper, a fiery Methodist ecclesiastic who often preached at Speakers’ Corner in Hyde Park. We were hoping Prabhupada would raise the issue of redundant churches, but instead he said, “It’s very rare to find a real Christian.”

So a kind of argument followed, with Baron Soper insisting that he knew many “good people” who were also atheists. Trivikram was with us, and he kind of dominated the argument, quoting the Vedas and strongly defining what it means to be a real Christian—while Prabhupada quietly observed. The meeting started off on the wrong foot and just kind of petered out. The Archdeacon congratulated the Swami on his good work, glanced briefly at one of our books, showed us one of his books—and we were ushered out the door.

Very little of substance came from meeting these Christian leaders, and Prabhupada sort of backed off on his insistence to meet the Archbishop of Canterbury, saying, “These men will not help us.”

**\* \* \***

The “Hare Krishna Mantra” record is still selling well—over 150,000 copies by now—and we bought a fifteen-seat Bedford van with our Apple royalties. On November 20, six devotees, led by Mukunda, Gurudas, and Tamal Krishna, drove the newly-purchased van to Germany for a four-day Radha Krishna Temple concert tour, booked for us by Red Door Agency.
