They played at the Star Club in Hamburg’s red-light district, made famous by the Beatles a few years earlier, and at several other clubs around West Germany, bringing home about $1,000 in cash and a lot of wild stories.

On November 28 and 29, there was a light snow in London, and Prabhupada walked around Regent’s Park in the mornings in his waterproof wellies, commenting on and making jokes about snow, cold, and ice. This is mind-blowing stuff for English people, seeing a bundled-up Swami with saffron cloth peeking from beneath his overcoat, followed by a gaggle of white youngsters in robes, one of them holding an umbrella oyer the Swami’s head and tuned to his every word. It was something totally new, as bizarre and outrageous as seeing a pack of two-headed aliens strolling down the sidewalk or waiting for the light to change. The low temperature on November 30 was 24°F.

The deadline was approaching. First of December and we’re just two weeks away from the Big Day. Everyone is busy to the extreme; no one is sleeping and the lights are on 24/7. Yamuna and some girls are preparing Lord Jagannath’s clothes and decorations for the altar; Malati and others are organizing the basement kitchen; others are repairing and expanding living and office quarters on the upper floors. Brahmacharis sleep in shifts, head to toe, in the attic in a crawlspace with a three-foot ceiling—one shift is out on afternoon sankirtan on Oxford Street, and another goes out at night to Leicester Square and Piccadilly Circus. There’s one toilet and one shower in the whole place, and always a long line in front of them. The stairwell from ground floor to fifth floor is narrow and steep; it’s practically a two-lane highway of devotees moving up and down. Some learn how to brace one hand on the rail and just jump down from floor to floor. All of this activity revolves around Prabhupada’s schedule, as he sits like a king in his room. No yelling or stomping en route past his room; pounding nails in the temple room only during daylight hours; quiet as possible between 10 p.m. and dawn.

Prabhupada loves it. He’s seeing and hearing his devotees buzzing around him like bees twenty-four hours a day. He’s happy, but also firmly resolved that we must have everything ready for the deity installation in two weeks.

Add to this mayhem Prabhupadas public speaking engagements and the concert commitments by the now-famous Radha Krishna Temple mantra rock band. Attendance at Conway Hall lectures has fallen way off, so we cancel the remaining November shows, but Prabhupada speaks at Queens College in Regents Park on the night of December 3. The Radha Krishna Temple performs at A Heads Gathering concert on the night of December 5 before a thousand rock fans at Hammersmith Town Hall. We’ve spent a lot of money lately, and rely on these paid concert gigs to keep the construction going.

We would drive back down the motorways after some late-night performance, ten of us in the van—from Gloucester or Worcester or Manchester—yakking with excitement. “How much did we make tonight?”

“How many Back to Godheads did we sell, Digvijaya?”

“Did you see that drunk guy in front, dancing?”

“What about that announcer, Gurudas, the guy with the Midlands accent!”

“Couldn’t understand a word he said.”

And slowly fall asleep, thinking about our spiritual master, who was waiting for us back at Bury Place. One night, while Mukunda was driving, I nodded out in the passenger seat. Suddenly, I jerked awake and looked over: Mukunda’s eyes were closed and he looked so peaceful, even in sleep his hands glued to the wheel—fences and fields flashed by in the headlights—we’re heading off the motorway at 70 mph! I lunged for the steering wheel as he bolted awake and corrected our course. “Mukunda, I think I’d better drive for a while.”

**\* \* \***

The vyasasan is completed and Culliford’s has delivered the marble. Now the altar. This will be the central, primary structure around which the rest of the temple is built. The last of the California redwood is laid vertically across the back wall, framing the altar. We build up a platform of beams to support the heavy marble. The deities will stand on a piece of golden Siena marble 72" x 30". A second slab measuring 52" x 20" is placed in front of that for offerings. Three dark-blue marble stairs lead up to the altar: A 52" x 12" x 5" slab for the bottom step, then a 48" x 12" x 7" slab for the second riser, then a 44" x 12" x 8" slab for the third stair, which tucks in nicely below the Siena marble platform. Once in place, the marble looks amazing—much bigger than what Prabhupada had originally described. Never mind, what is, is. We are surrendered to whatever Krishna throws our way and realize we have no control over this thing. It’s just unfolding.

And what will go on the altar? We have no Deities to install! Of course, we can just install the three Jagannath deities, but Prabhupada specifically wants Radha-Krishna Deities—we are the Radha Krishna Temple, after all. Six months ago, on June 12, Prabhupada had written me a letter: “Regarding the altar, the design which you have submitted is nice, but I wish to add that underneath the Jagannathas a throne of Radha-Krishna should be there. I am enclosing herewith a picture of the throne, and on the back side is my idea of an altar design.”

Malati: We still didn't grasp what Deity worship meant, but we were attracted to the idea. Yamuna was most clued in on that one. We didn’t really know what to think. Mukunda called around—somehow, everyone knew about our quest for Deities, the

Indian community especially. You couldn’t buy them anywhere in London; no one had even ever seen them, not even at the Hindu center. None of us had yet been to India. And we didn’t yet know that temple worship was important.

Nowadays you can buy mangos in London that were picked yesterday in Maharashtra. But late-’60s London was totally different. There were few Indian goods in London yet, certainly no Deities or worship paraphernalia. There was no way to get these things easily, except to write letters to India, then go through the long process of transferring money, dealing with Indian and British banking restrictions, and then wait weeks for a cargo ship to arrive. Prabhupada wrote letter after letter for more than a year to Mr. Birla and other big industrialists in India, to a friend named Shyamlal, to the Mullik family in Calcutta, and to his godbrothers: “Please send large-size Radha-Krishna deities, I will pay.” On November 16, the Swami wrote to his friend Hitsaranji, in Calcutta: “With further reference to my letter dated November 1, 1969,1 beg to inform you that immediately I want one pair of Radha-Krishna Deities in London, by the quickest transport, maybe by air cargo, then it will be very kind of you. Kindly treat this as urgent.”

But no one responded to Prabhupada’s urgent plea.

A woman named Shyama Mataji had given us a set of ten-inch brass Radha-Krishna Deities. Yamuna kept them carefully in a box. But these were quite small, way too unimpressive in physical stature to install on that huge marble altar. Mukunda had a set of eighteen-inch wooden Deities that had been carved by Goursundar in Hawaii, but these were also deemed too small for the grand theme of the London temple. We actually sent these Deities with the RKT team on their German concert tour to install in the Hamburg temple.

I kept thinking, “Hey, getting Deities is not my department. I’m just building their home. You guys find the Deities—Krishna will send them. Prabhupada is here, so certainly Deities will come.”

Temple room and vyasasan were complete, and the altar was nearly finished. Radha and Krishna had to appear somehow—we all felt this. The magic that dominated our whirlwind of extreme devotional effort had to prevail.

And so it did. Mr. Goyal, secretary of the East London Hindu Centre, phoned on December 2 to inform Swamiji that their organization had just received a set of large marble Radha-Krishna Deities from Jaipur—would he be interested in seeing them? Prabhupada immediately assembled his SWAT team, and we drove out to Mr. Goyal’s house in Stratford, ten miles east of Bloomsbury.

Mr. Goyal met us at the door with two other officers of the Hindu Centre, who touched Prabhupadas feet, and then we all crammed into Goyal’s tiny parlor and sat down. The mood was jolly, and reverent. Prabhupada was in his Mr. Charming mode. “So you are mostly Gujarati ladies and gentlemen?”

“Yes,” Mr. Goyal said, “my family is from Surat. But most of us have been in London for almost ten years.”

The men jabbered back and forth in Hindi—we didn’t have a clue what was going on. Suddenly, two men got up, went into another room, and returned with a large wooden crate. We moved the coffee table out of the way, and they opened the lid. It was Radharani! We fell to the floor, the room so small we were practically on top of each other. Prabhupada remained seated, closed his eyes, made a pranam, and said a little prayer.

She is nearly four feet tall, sculpted of pure white Makrana marble. She looks like a Greek goddess. Her eyebrows, lips, sari, and jewels were painted by a fine artist. The men continue yakking in Hindi, and Mr. Goyal points out a tiny break on Radharani’s left hand, the one that holds the lotus flower, at the tip of Her little finger, almost hidden from view.

Prabhupada says in English, dead-pan, “Never mind, our Shyamasundar can repair and, Yamuna, you can paint?” Then back to Hindi. Then the two men bring in a second crate and open the lid to reveal a magnificent Krishna. Again we hit the floor. Prabhupada seems to ignore these worshipful statues and, taking his cue, we settle down and appear disinterested, trying to keep our eyes off these Perfect Beings.

Then Prabhupada switches to English again. “This, our temple at Bury Place—you have seen it? The work is nearly completed and very soon I shall require to install the Radha-Krishna Deity. I have got about twenty Krishna consciousness centers now all over the world, and these Western boys and girls, they are taking to worshiping Radha and Krishna very nicely. There is no compromise with our Vedic standard. Actually, I am old man and not much accustomed to this cold-weather climate, and I am also due to leave back to Los Angeles to install Deity in our new temple there costing more than two lakhs, dollars.”

Prabhupada switches back to Hindi for a while, then looks at us and says, “Tamal Krishna, you can lift up Krishna? Just see how heavy.”

Tamal grasps Krishna, top and bottom, and lifts Him a few inches. “Not so heavy, Srila Prabhupada.”

“Radharaman, you can assist. Just, you can take out to the van now.”

Tamal Krishna and Radharaman disappear out the door, each carrying one end of Krishna in His box. Srila Prabhupada turns to the startled Indians as if nothing has happened and says to me, “Now, Shyamasundar, you and Mukunda, you can take Radharani. Go on. She is not as heavy.”

When we get to the van with Radha, we look back, and there’s Yamuna and Prabhupada coming out the door, making small talk with the puzzled Hindu Centre guys, something about how honored we’ll be if you come to our installation on the 14th. Then Mukunda opens the passenger door for the Swami, we all climb in, and off we go. A successful lightning raid.

I’m driving, and we drive down the street a few blocks, and Prabhupada motions to me to pull over into an empty parking space. The van stops, Prabhupada turns around, instructs Tamal to open the two wooden crates, and with great reverence, folds his hands and recites prayers from the Brahma Samhita: govindam adi-purusam tam aham bhajami... “Krishna is so kind,” Prabhupada says, “that He has come like this.”

I pull back out into traffic. After a long silence, Mukunda says from the back of the van, “Prabhupada, I think you have kidnapped Krishna!”

Prabhupada chuckles. “Yes. Once when I was in India, I presented one idea to my bank manager. He did not like. He presented a counterscheme. But I argued with him and foiled his scheme. So he said to me, ‘Mr. De, you should have been a politician.’”

Another thing Prabhupada says on the trip back to Bury Place: “Krishna has played a great trick. Just like when Krishna’s mother tried to tie Him with ropes because He is such a rascal, stealing the butter—and by His magic power He makes the rope too short. So Mother Yasoda brings more rope, still it is too short. Like that, another trick has been played. Krishna wanted to come here, so this chip on Radharani’s finger is just another Krishna’s trick. And we have captured Radha and Krishna.”

“Srila Prabhupada, you are always doing the impossible. You are the greatest magician!” Tamal Krishna says.

“I am not magician. My only credit is that I am presenting Krishna as He is. The magic will act as soon as you become a pure devotee of Krishna. The magic will be done by Krishna, not by me or you.”

It’s a great relief for all. Krishna’s magical appearance accelerates our enthusiasm, and we double our efforts. No one in our ISKCON world has yet seen such large, beautiful, important Deities. They are taken to an upstairs room. I call David Wynne, who comes by and expertlyrepairs Radha’s little finger.

On December 12, Prabhupada writes again to his friend Hitsaranji: “You need not send the Murtis by air cargo to London because some devotee here has contributed a Radha-Krishna Murti pair imported from Jaipur. It is 42" high and very nice. So Krishna has saved us in this point because we are going to have our opening ceremony on the 14th December through the 21st of December.”

As the grand finale of our construction work, we need to build a canopy or hood over the Radha-Krishna Deities. Prabhupada had told me that the altar should be fifty-seven inches high “including canopy.” There’s not enough space to set three huge Jagannath deities alongside Radha and Krishna, so, like in San Francisco, we run a 2" x 12" wooden plank across the backwall, sixty inches above the altar, for the Jagannath murtis to stand.

With the marble in place, it’s December 13, one day to deadline, and we still have that canopy to build. Prabhupada had also told me that “the front pillars are three on each side, and the whole throne is supported by four lion claws.” So I run around London’s antique stores to find Victorian wooden Doric columns that are about eight inches in diameter. I find four. They’re six feet long but can be trimmed to fit. No lion claws, though; the columns are more like elephant legs, but they’ll have to do.

While I work on this, devotees coat every square inch of wood in the place with varnish. Carpet installers come in the morning and lay a gorgeous, royal purple, wall-to-wall carpet, donated by George. Pictures of Krishna and our spiritual masters are hung on the still-sticky walls. Eyes stinging from varnish fumes, we string a curtain rod across the temple to create a Deity room, and Yamuna hangs elegant dark-blue curtains. We mount four floodlights to illuminate the altar. The simple, four-sided canopy is fashioned from two-inch wooden poles and aluminum conduit pipe, then fitted with purple silk cloth with a gold fringe. On the eve of the installation, I’m so tired I can barely see. There’s no time to drill holes through wood and marble to anchor the columns with bolts, so the canopy will have to remain free-standing on the marble slab. At about 4 a.m. on December 14, I stagger upstairs to sleep, remarking on my way out the door that the place looks like the hull of a Viking ship turned upside-down.

I slept for the next twenty hours, so I missed the ceremony, but I later heard what happened: London weather archives for

December 1969 report that every day that month was overcast and cold except for December 14, which was sunny and mild. The devotees had sent out hundreds of fancy invitations. The Apple press office once again had waved their magic wand, and dozens of reporters descended on Bury Place to record this major event, including a full BBC crew with portable floodlights and three TV cameras. Apple sent over a florist, who arranged a thousand out-of-season flowers around the temple. The place was packed and, with all the bodies and floodlights, very hot.

Prabhupada came in shortly after noon, built a small fire, which added to the heat, and conducted a lavish Deity installation ceremony, ladling ghee, yogurt, milk, and honey over Sri Sri Radha and Krishna before They were taken behind the curtains to Their new altar-home. Prabhupada went behind the curtain with them to direct Gurudas, Mukunda, and Yamuna in how the Deities should be placed. Lifting the heavy marble murtis up onto the altar, someone hit the left front column with an elbow—and it collapsed! The whole canopy crashed down! Prabhupada leaped up on the steps, grasped the heavy canopy, raised it, and held it, his eyes blazing like half-man, half-lion. He whispered harshly, “Take it out! Outside!”

Gurudas beckoned Tamal and Dhananjaya through, the curtain, and together they dismantled the canopy, hauling it in pieces through the crowded temple outside to the sidewalk. The loudly chanting audience was oblivious to all this, perhaps thinking it was part of the ceremony. Yamuna dressed and decorated the Deities, tidied up, and the curtains were thrown open to reveal Their magnificent Lordships: Balaram, Subhadra, and Lord Jagannath on top, and Krishna and Radha below, resplendent.

Gurudas: What I remember most was seeing Prabhupada behind that curtain. Prabhupada was so strong and sturdy, and his eyes blazed brightly. His hands were like talons, his feet like pillars, and he appeared like a lion!

Srila Prabhupada named the Deities Radha-Londonishvara, meaning “the Supreme Lord of London.” He regarded Them as his personal Deities, the first Deities of Radha and Krishna to accept his service. Radha-Londonishvara remained Swamiji’s favorite Deities for the rest of his life.

Late that December fourteenth night, I awoke and saw the light under Prabhupada’s door. I timidly knocked, wiping sleep from my eyes. “Ohhh,” Prabhupada said, when I walked in and bowed down. Seeing the contrition on my face—“Prabhupada, I’m so sorry”—he said, with deep compassion, “Never mind. Radha and Krishna did not want this canopy over Their heads—to be separated from Lord Jagannath.”

The next morning, returning from his usual walk around Russell Square, Prabhupada pointed with his cane to a blank area on the wall beside the front door and said to Mukunda, “You will mount a brass plaque here, and it shall say, ‘This temple was constructed with great labor and effort by Shyamasundar das Adhikary.’” Can you imagine such love, such mercy?

Prabhupada stayed at Bury Place for seven more days, leaving for Boston from Heathrow on December 21. During this period, there was a continuous celebration in the temple room, and nonstop prasadam was served to the public. Prabhupada instructed Yamuna in Deity worship and made ready to leave us while we tried to prepare for a world without him. On the last day, he distributed articles of his clothing, and he spent time in the temple alone with Radha-Londonishvara. The devotees lined up on the sidewalk in front of Bury Place, full of love and lamentation to see him off. Prabhupada said, “Simply practice what I have taught you, and your life will be perfect.” Then he waved to everyone, got into the car, and someone drove him to the airport.

### 4

The January 1970 issue of the International Times featured a list of predictions for the New Year—among them, on page 5:

In every town and village the chanting of Hare Krishna will be heard... In this age of Kali the glorification of the Lord by chanting his holy name is the only means of liberation.

Prophecy made by Lord Sri Chaitanya Mahaprabhu 1486-1534 A.D.

Although the “Hare Krishna Mantra” record had dropped out of the Top 100 and record-related engagements had pretty much dried up, we were now taking the mantra to the streets. We all felt the heat of Sri Chaitanya’s prediction: seeds of the maha-mantra had recently blown to every corner of the globe, and Britain was abuzz with “What is this chanting? The Beatles do it, so it must be good.” So street sankirtan—spreading Chaitanya’s message in the human marketplace—became the main activity for thirty-one devotees now living in cramped quarters at Bury Place, plus a dozen householders spread out in flats and bedsits around the city. The afternoon team patrolled a two-mile beat up Oxford Street from Tottenham Court Road to Hyde Park Corner and back. Another team— mostly householders—worked the nearby nighttime amusement crowd—theatre- and cinema-goers in Piccadilly, Leicester Square, and Shaftsbury Avenue. Bury Place became a clearinghouse for continuous sankirtan: eat, sleep, maybe change robes, grab some BTGs, and then back out on the street.

Krishna consciousness was expanding like a forest fire in America too, and from the USA, devotees were fanning out all over the globe, going to Germany, France, Australia, Japan. The growing team of devotees responsible for publishing Prabhupada’s books— typists, editors, Sanskrit experts, artists, layout and design people— were cranking out new books, published in many thousands of copies, like Sri Ishopanisad, Easy Journey to Other Planets, and Krishna Consciousness: The Topmost Yoga System.

Prabhupada was gone from London, and there was a deep hole in our hearts. But now the Deities lived with us at Bury Place, and Their wonderful presence gradually filled the vacuum we felt and created a new dimension in our lives. It was Their home now, and our lives became focused on pleasing Them. Radha and Krishna’s routine became our routine. We performed early morning, midday, and evening arati, with offerings of food and flowers; and we regularly chanted and studied Vedic scriptures in the temple. When the Deities rested the temple was quiet; when They were awake, the place echoed with purposeful activity.

Prabhupada had created so much in just three months: a big temple in the heart of London, home for a magnificent manifestation of Radha and Krishna; “Hare Krishna” was now a household phrase throughout the country; there were ample books to help us understand the philosophy and purpose that governed our lives; and most importantly, there were many new, bright-faced British devotees.

People from all parts of the UK and Europe descended on the temple to see what was going on. Many soon became devotees and were given names like Sarvamangala, Navaswati, Vrindabanishwari, Mahavishnu, Tribhuvanath, Ranchor, Bhaja Hari, Vraja Hari, Nara Hari, Smara Hari, and Jai Hari—a lot of guys named Hari. Sometimes total strangers followed us back to the temple from sankirtan and asked to join. We shaved the men up, got the girls into saris, trained them quickly, and our sankirtan parties swelled with new recruits. Despite our appeals to stay away, devotees from other ISKCON centers flocked to London to join in the excitement. Thirty, then forty, devotees crammed into Bury Place. We were still a family, but now a very large family, centered on the Deities’ schedule and street sankirtan. These devotees were from all walks of life—strangers one day, bunkmates the next, but with our one common spiritual focus, so many disparate personalities lived in remarkable harmony. To help Tamal and Trivikram manage the troops, Gurudas and Yamuna stayed at Bury Place.

Malati: We were not self-centered but Prabhupada-centered, and even though each faced personal challenges, emotional ups and downs, we never let that derail us from collectively going forward to finish the job. On that level there was just no bickering, no quarrel, no distraction. I don’t think I’ve ever experienced it since, teamwork like that, but having so much fun doing everything.

The householders were again scattered around London, facing dreaded concerns (rent, tube fare, childcare costs) that Prabhupada s whirlwind visit had lifted for a while. Malati and I and Saraswati moved to a dreary, institutional-gray room in Golders Green, ten tube stations from Bury Place. One day, we returned home to find our entire room and its contents covered with a thick coat of ash, almost impossible to clean. Seems we had left the kerosene heater on, and when the fuel was spent the wick burnt to ash and the fan sent it all over the place.

In Golders Green, Saraswati was rushed to the hospital twice: first, after she stuck her finger into a 240-volt wall socket, and again when she swallowed a bottle of iron pills. We shared a bathroom with other tenants, and one woman upstairs was particularly upset about our daily bathing.

Malati: This lady got angry and said, we take a bath once a week and you take one every day, and she pushed me down the stairs and I actually got a big gash here, on my arm.

When Prabhupada was in our midst, it was like being on a safe island: everything ran perfectly, and there was no anxiety. With him gone, it was a struggle to keep the feeling that Krishna would take care of us.

And after the huge Bury Place construction effort, I was bored and out of sorts. I needed to build something. So at Golders Green, I started crafting a miniature, two-foot temple, a smaller version of the stolen temple throne I’d built in San Francisco. This little temple was also very intricate, modeled after temples in Bhubaneshwar in Orissa, South India. Hundreds of small, varicolored, exotic-wood laminations with step-carved edges slowly tapered up to a balsawood amalaka, the carved round disc that represents the sun lotus. Day after day, I crouched under our room’s single, dim bulb, building this thing while we ran out of money.

In that same issue of IT that carried Chaitanya’s New Years prediction I submitted a want ad. It was printed on page 28:

SHYAMASUNDER, 7, Bury Place, WC1,242-0394. Architect, Carpenter, Builder. Six far-out West Coast Head shops & London Krishna Temple. Requires similar challenge. Has all tools and contacts for inexpensive materials.

No one replied.

It’s January 1970. Three of the Beatles are making solo records: George is playing backup on Ringo’s first solo album (Sentimental Journey)-, Paul is recording McCartney, due for an April release; John and Yoko are recording Instant Karma (“and we all shine on, like the moon and the stars and the sun”).

George’s money to pay for Krsna Book gets delayed after a series of clerical errors and, maybe, Beatles manager Allen Klein’s shenanigans. I catch up to George one day at Olympic Studios and—trying not to bitch and moan—mention the problem. He says he’ll get right on it. My service as liaison to George continues, but we don’t talk often as he is busy in the studio. Meanwhile, George is looking for a new home. He tells me his Kinfauns house is too small, too unprotected, and besides, he’s moving into a new, expanded version of himself and more appropriate quarters are required.

Throughout January Malati and I go out on sankirtan in the freezing cold, mostly at night, pushing the pram with a bundled-up Saraswati on board. This is something new for us, hustling people on the sidewalk and selling them magazines. I’m not very good at it, but Malati is a whiz. Sometimes I work on that little temple in the Golders Green flat. Sometimes I hang out at Bury Place all day, gaze at the Deities for hours, keep a hand in things—but my heart’s not really in it. Where’s a rhino?

We know someday soon we will make our second record as a follow-up release to “Hare Krishna Mantra,” so once in a while Yamuna calls a band practice and we rehearse our song from the Brahma Samhita. I miss Prabhupada so much—and the action, the deadlines.

Meanwhile, George finds a near-derelict, hundred-room mansion on acres of overgrown gardens, in Oxfordshire, near Henley-on-Thames, called Friar Park. George asks if I could move out there, bring a few devotees, and help him with landscaping and fixing the place up. “But I’ll have to ask Prabhupada if it’s all right, George. I mean, I’d love to do it, no problem. We could whip the place into shape pretty fast. But I have to ask him first, OK?”

So on February 3 I write to Srila Prabhupada my innermost thoughts: I’m really a useless idiot. I even screwed up getting the money for your Krsna Book. George wants me to supervise work at his new monastery in Oxfordshire. I’m lax and unoccupied. Should I stay in London, go out to George’s place, or just go off and start a new center somewhere else?

George phones on the night of February 6: “The studio is open tomorrow. Are you guys ready to record?”

You know us by now, George. We’re always ready.

Next afternoon, at Trident Studios, in a small alleyway in Soho just a block from Piccadilly Circus, we lay down the first tracks of what will become the “Govinda” 45-rpm single. Yamuna’s husky, golden, bell-like voice leads the prayer, and the rest of us join the chorus: Govindam adi purusham, tamaham bhajami: “Let me worship Govinda, the original Person.” George plays guitar, Mukunda an electric organ, and Kulashekar thumps the mridanga behind a glass partition. There’s a separate mike on my esraj, and Gurudas and his penetrating kartals are moved to the back of the room. Klaus Voorman is on bass guitar, and Ringo pops in to lay down some drum riffs.

After the session, George and I walk a few blocks through rush-hour traffic to the Apple offices in Savile Row. As we wait for the light at Regent Street, George looks at me and says, “There’s a first: more people are lookin’ at you than are lookin’ at me—I love it!” He seems lost in reflection for a moment, then quietly says, “You know, some days I wish I could get on one of those double-decker buses and just ride around London with no one hassling me.”

George Martin decides our song needs more depth—like “Sgt. Pepper’s Lonely Hearts Club Band”—so he makes arrangements for another session at Trident for the night of February 10, bringing in twenty members of the London Philharmonic. Martin likes the harp sound, so puts the harpist in a sound booth and records her celestial playing on a separate track. He also likes the sound of Harivilas’s oudh with its Middle Eastern twang. Violins and cellos join to create the incredible symphonic sound that surrounds our rendition of the Brahma Samhita, the oldest poem in the world.

On February 14, I receive the anxiously awaited letter, an amazing, comprehensive letter, from Srila Prabhupada. It resolves everything.

“Your service is appreciated not only by me, but all other members of the Society. Now everyone has heard how strenuously you induced George Harrison to contribute for our Krsna book.”

Puffed-up, egoistic karmi that I am, Prabhupada has boosted my self-esteem.

“The delay of payment was not due to your so-called ‘negligence,’ but it was due to the tricks of the lawyer, Mr. Klein. Anyway, now we have got the money, let us forget the delay.”

Whew! The money had finally gotten there. I’m off the hook. The anxiety caused to Prabhupada by that money delay was a continual source of guilt and worry.

“I wish to print a few thousands of a small booklet regarding Krsna book, with a few pictures, giving a short description of Krsna on the cover page”:

Krsna: The Supreme rich, powerful, famous and beautiful Personality of Godhead without any material attachment is now available in book form (400 pages reading matter and 52 colorful illustrations) in first-class hardbound and wood-free paper printed. Those who have heard the “Hare Krishna Mantra” record as well as “Govindam” will do well by procuring this deluxe book and keep at home as a great treasure. Try to understand the basic principles of the great movement “Krsna Consciousness.”

Prabhupada the promo man. He is expert in everything else; now I’m seeing our master as a modern-day advertising genius. Yes, I dig it! Advertising with a message, preaching Krishna consciousness in a way everyone understands. Not only convincing the public, but sales, man—sales are what it’s all about.

“I think if George writes a small Foreword to Krsna Book, then we can include it along with other description in the pamphlet... So I shall be glad to hear from you how you like this idea.”

In other words, your next assignment is to persuade George to write a foreword for Krsna Book.

“I am glad that your press release propaganda is going very nicely and I shall request you to include the names of our books and publications as far as possible. When such press releases are printed, send me the copies regularly. It will be a great advantage for us as well as for the people in general if the press reporters can ventilate the idea of one God, one religion, one Scripture, one Mantra, and one people throughout the whole world... For you this engagement of press propaganda, I think, is the best occupation.”

Bringing me into the deal, showing me how to do it, Mr. Showman himself.

Then he answered my biggest question:

Regarding your staying in London, you must do it by all means and utilize the facilities offered by George s cooperation. Regarding Georges request to supervise the work in his monastery, I think you should help him because he is our good friend and by that way you shall be able to utilize his telegraph machine, etc. This is a good plan; I completely approve of this.

I hope this meets you in good health.

Your ever well-wisher, ACBS

Srila Prabhupada, the ultimate expert on every subject, was becoming ever more dear to us as he guided our individual services by letter. Each devotee had a particular talent or ambition to serve, and Prabhupada was able to recognize and guide each of us as if we alone were very special to him.

On the night of February 16,1 sent Prabhupada a copy of the “Govinda” 45-rpm record by express mail to Los Angeles, along with this note:

Dearest Prabhupada,

Please accept my humble obeisance, though I am so unworthy to accept crumbs from the plates of your devotees. Please find enclosed the first copy of “Govinda,” which you have taught us, and which we, quite badly, have rendered for the glorification of your Guru Maharaj in the world at large...

Yamuna, myself, Mukunda, and Janaki are singing, with everyone joining on the chorus. George plays guitar,

Mukunda plays organ, a new boy, Hampur (an Armenian boy) plays “oudh” (an Arabian stringed instrument), I play esraj, Gurudas and I play kartals and there is an orchestra of 6 violins, 6 violas, 2 double bass, 2 tubas, 3 cellos, and one harp. Please accept this record as our humble offering unto your divine Lotus Feet...

I will be with George all day Thursday walking on the grounds of his new monastery to assess the work he wants me to supervise, and I shall discuss the Foreword with him. I’m pretty sure he will do it. Shall I ask him to say anything in particular?

I have just come from the cutting room where this record was made, it is late night in Trafalgar Square Post Office, so I shall write a longer letter tomorrow to clarify some points.

Your servant & friend forever, Shyamasundar

We heard that when the “Govinda” record was played for him, Prabhupada wept tears of joy and announced that it should be played at the beginning of morning services in every ISKCON temple, forever. He later wrote me, “The record is so nice that I am playing it at least once in a day and it is giving me transcendental pleasure with tears in my eyes. I am sure this record will be the first-class 'hit' as already opined by the experts.”

George had arranged to meet me at Friar Park on February 19, saying on the phone, “be sure to bring your raincoat and wellies. So I went out by train from Londons Paddington station, and he picked me up at the Henley-on-Thames station. A few minutes later we entered Friar Park. I couldn’t believe what I was seeing: vast acres of once-proud gardens, forests, lakes—quaint little buildings everywhere, and a castle.

George says, “Yeah, it was built back in the 1890s by a rich bac elor named Sir Francis Crisp. He left it to the Catholic Church, you know, that weird order with Jesus ripping open his chest and his heart is bleeding all over the place? Anyway, now there’s only a couple of nuns and an old padre, Friar Tuck or something, living here, so they have to sell.”

“How big are the grounds, George?”

“About thirty-five acres or so, but that’s the great part: this Sir Francis, he was a bit like Lewis Carroll, you know, Alice In Wonderland— or Walt Disney—it’s like Disneyland, really, a theme park, the Japanese garden, the maze—even a Matterhorn, like Disney has. There’s a manmade river flowing under the property, and you can take a boat through all these famous grottos and caves.”

“Wow, are you gonna buy it?”

“Yeah, I think so. I wanted you to come out and take a look. I was out here a few days back and took the tour. The main house is enormous; it has this jolly-friar theme. There’re funny statues all over the place, and little signs, like, ‘The speech of flowers excels the flowers of speech,’ or ‘Shadows we are, and shadows we depart’—what do you call them? Clever little sayings—”

“Homilies?”

“Yeah, like homilies, like songs, really, about the devil, about friendship, life—it’s really great. I put a big poster of four-armed Vishnu in the empty fireplace, and the nuns were really freaked out, so I told them, ‘Well, if God is unlimited, then He can appear in any form, whichever way He likes to appear. This is one way. He’s called Vishnu.’ I mean, these churches, they say, ‘No, you’re not going to see Him. He’s way above you. Just believe what we tell you and shut up.’ Shook them up a bit, but you know, they’re not bad people, just uninformed. That’s why no one joins up anymore. The place is too big. They can’t pay the light bill. And the grounds are a mess, all grown over with thorn bushes and ivy. If I get the place, I was hoping some of you could live out here and help me clean it up a bit.”

It’s raining. We get sopping wet, but walk around every part of the property, every room in the house, getting more and more excited with each discovery. We see some of those little signs George mentioned—“Don’t Keep Off the Grass” or, by the lake, “Herons Will Be Prosecuted.” There’s a rococo room, where the private parts on all the cupids are painted over by the recent inhabitants. The vast central hall is made entirely of dark, close-grained oak, hand-carved filigree, with stained glass windows. In the Anglo-Saxon chapel George says, “This’d make a nice little temple.” And Vishnu still stands in the huge stone fireplace.

**\* \* \***

The London action started heating up again right after the “Govinda” recording sessions. For more than a year we’ve had our police permit to perform public sankirtan, but the legal terms of our permit restrict us to public squares and assigned locations—and certainly don’t allow us to wander wherever we’d like on the main thoroughfares. The police had been overlooking our happy sidewalk parades-maybe we’re good for tourism, symbols of a liberal London—but in the winter of 1970, they began to crack down. Devotees got arrested constantly. Almost every day Mukunda had to don his three-piece suit and bowler hat and, brolly in hand, go to the Old Bailey to bail out devotees who had been arrested the previous night.

Tamal Krishna, Trivikram, Gurudas, Mukunda, and I got together and developed a strategy: we would do a blatant sankirtan in Leicester Square on the night of February 17, and when the paddy wagons arrived, the following would happen: Malati with baby in pram would walk away (no point having the kid stay overnight in jail); Mondakini and the other single women would fall on their knees and beg for mercy from the stalwart bobbies; and some of the men would pretend to nurse arms and legs, hinting at police brutality. Gurudas in mufti would stay some distance from the group and, using both his cameras, would shoot two rolls of film to record these drastic events.

It happened just as we planned, and several devotees were hauled off to jail. Gurudas’s photos appeared in newspapers the next day, sending a strong signal to the police. Henceforth we did reduce some of our more outrageous tactics, though, like Ishan’s loud trumpet and wearing “Chant Hare Krishna!” sandwich boards, and police harassment tapered off as we came to some sort of unspoken understanding.

A photo of ten sankirtan devotees, arms raised in surrender, appeared on page 5 of the International Times (issue #74), along with the following article:

KRISHNA BUSTED!

The Hare Krishna Group were arrested for obstruction in Coventry St., W1 on the evening of Tuesday, 17 Feb. They were moving along the edge of the pavement, singing & chanting the holy names of God, when 2 policemen asked them to stop singing and move on. The Hare Krishna Group explained that they could not stop chanting as it is the foundation of their religion. They asked the policemen to understand that it is part of the Sam Kirtan movement that devotees should purify themselves by worshiping publicly and that this was important in gaining disciples. They pointed out that the Salvation Army is allowed to hold meetings in the street &C requested the freedom to practice their religion. They bowed down to make a plea to their God, but unfortunately at this point they were arrested.

Shortly after hearing about this, Prabhupada wrote to us, advising how far we should take this thing:

Regarding civil-disobedience movement, I do not think there is need of it. Our people have been arrested on the charge of blocking the pedestrian path; so if some friends give evidence that we did not actually block the way, then the case will certainly be dismissed as you said that the members o£ the public were in sympathy with us. Our people are still going with Sankirtana Party, so government does not prohibit us so far Sankirtana is concerned. Therefore, civil-disobedience on this ground would be unnecessary.

Srila Prabhupada was a brilliant lawyer. He weighed evidence and made logical legal conclusions. (I would see Prabhupada’s lawyerly expertise prevail again and again as ISKCON became more and more encumbered by legal situations in the coming years.)

On February 16, 1970, another letter arrived from the Swami, this time addressed to George and thanking him for Krsna Book:

Please note that every farthing of this money will be employed in the service of the Lord, and the Lord is so kind and grateful that He will bestow upon you benediction at least ten times more than that you have done for Him. It does not, however, mean that Lord Krsna is like a business man, and He bestows benediction when He is profited by our service. The Lord is full in Himself; He does not require our service, but if we render service unto Him in love and devotion, such action enriches our very existence.

In the same letter Prabhupada planted the words that would eventually emerge as George’s own words in his foreword to Krsna Book:

Please try to understand the philosophy of Krsna Consciousness in a nutshell: Every living entity has a dormant propensity to love somebody other who is very excellent in his opinion. Somebody loves some other either on account of riches, power, popularity, beauty, knowledge, or renunciation. But this loving propensity for somebody else is fundamentally meant for the supremely rich, powerful, popular, beautiful, wise, and unattached Lord Krsna. Everyone is hankering after loving the Supreme Personality of Godhead, but none of them has the right information. By Krsna Consciousness movement we want to broadcast this information that if anyone reposes his loving propensity upon Krsna, he will immediately feel full satisfaction, as much as he feels full satisfaction by supplying food in the stomach. Please try to understand this simple philosophy by critical analysis, and I hope by the grace of Krsna you will be a great servant of His in fulfilling His desire that He may be known by His Holy Name in every village and every city all over the world, and thus the people will become happy.

I informed George that Prabhupada has suggested he write a foreword or introduction to Krishna Book, and George more or less said no, he didn’t like to write such things. The pressure is on, and I’m angling now, trying to ask him again in a very nice way. “George, this would really please the Swami,” with some of, “He wants you to do it,” along with, “How about if I rough it out for you? What do you think?”

Prabhupada keeps after me on this matter:

The suggestions \[for George’s foreword\] I may repeat again, that every living entity has a dormant propensity of love for somebody else. That is exhibited not only in human society, but also in the animal society. That love is exhibited primarily in five kinds of relationship—especially as master and servant, as friend and friend, as parents and children, and as lover and beloved. This stock of love in every living entity is dormant eternal love for Krsna, but because the living entity has forgotten Krsna since a very, very long time, even before this creation was manifested, therefore all of us are misplacing that dormant love in a perverted way... As soon as we revive that love of Krsna in any one of the five primary relationships, immediately we become happy.

Now the pressure is really on. Maybe if I write something up myself, based on Srila Prabhupada’s suggestions, then give it to George...

**\* \* \***

Our second Radha Krishna Temple single, “Govinda,” was released on the Apple label on March 6, 1970. It was another Sanskrit chartbuster. By March 28 it had peaked at #23 in the charts, and it stayed in the Top 100 for eight weeks. George Martin’s wall of sound and Yamuna’s profound contralto made “Govinda” a record you just had to hear over and over. George told Yamuna, “With a voice like yours, I could make you a famous rock star—I’m serious. Do you want to do it?” Yamuna replied, “Give me a few days to think about it.” I tried to talk her into it. But later she told him, “No, thank you, George, but that’s not the life for me.”

On March 11, George was interviewed for BBC Radio 1 by Johnny Moran. Millions of people heard his words.

JM: George, what would you say has influenced you most, musically?

GH: Just like the Hare Krishna mantra, that’s really a spiritual song, the purpose is to give people a bit of relief from the normal pressures. But you don’t have to understand that sort of music or lyric because it’s in the actual sound that it produces a certain magic. It’s like singing hallelujah, hallelujah—if you really sang that for an hour, you’d really feel good. And I’d like to get into more of that sort of music, because you can benefit from it, like physically and mentally, and that actually does you some good rather than bebop and having a nice time.

JM: Well, it’s obviously through your interest in Indian music that you became associated with the Radha Krishna Temple.

GH: Also with the experience of meditation, and having experienced that I could understand really what this Krishna movement is about. And also it's a much more appealing path to God, that is to sing and dance and eat, which is really what their thing is all about-just to eat nice food and to dance a lot and to sing a lot. And they sing the name of God, which is ‘Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare/ Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama’-you know, you’ve all heard that one. Also, their new record now, I think is so much nicer.

JM: What’s the meaning of the new one?

GH: Govinda is also a name of Krishna and Krishna is the name of God, because God is unlimited and so He can have as many names, you know, like Allah, all those names: all God. And it’s the same God, all you listeners, it’s not like... we haven’t got a different one. It’s the same one.

George and Pattie moved into Friar Park on March 12 and, a few days later, so did we.

**\* \* \***

“Chaaarge! Take that, you commie pinko bastards!” Whoosh! Another gush of high-octane flame blasts from George s hands and zaps a clump of unsuspecting ivy.

“Bonzai! Treacherous nonbelievers!” I yell, and Whoosh. A patch of brambles goes up in smoke.

The WWII flame-throwers are George’s idea; I don't know where he got them, but it’s a good idea and makes short work оf clearing the seemingly endless acres of wild blackberry bushes and ivy that blanket Friar Park. My feeble attempts with hoe and machete just weren’t doing the job.

Three married couples—Malati and me, Devadatta and Draupadi, and Murari and Lilavati—have moved to Friar Park. Among us we have four very young children. Devadatta comes from a farming background, and Murari has experience in construction. The house is immense—more than a hundred rooms if you count those in the outbuildings. Being late winter and stripped of furnishings, it’s also cold, dark, and gloomy, and with a knight in medieval armor on the landing between floors, right out of a haunted-house story.

We take up “residence”—blankets and quilts on the floor—in one upstairs wing; George and Pattie are camped out in sleeping bags somewhere in another wing. We ask and get permission from George to set up a Krishna temple in the mock Anglo-Saxon chapel off the main hall, on ground level. With Gothic stained-glass' windows and a high, vaulted ceiling, the place is perfect (as long as you ignore the walls, which are covered in elegant, brocade-stamped cow leather). 1 finish building the fancy little Deity house I’d started in Golders Green, fashion an altar, and we install our own holy trinity, three small Jagannath deities, in this once strictly Christian domain. Every morning at 7, our drum, hartals, and Hare Krishna chanting boom and echo through the house. No one complains. Sometimes George and/or Pattie join us for early morning or evening kirtan; at other times George goes into the temple at night and chants there by himself. Pattie describes this time in her book, Wonderful Tonight:

I don’t know if it was to fulfill his spiritual dream or as a means of providing cheap labor, or a combination of the two, but shortly after we moved into the house George decided to invite three Hare Krishna families to live with us. The idea is that the men would do gardening and the women would look after us and cook. I wasn’t sure of this arrangement but George thought it would be wonderful: we would chant together and there would be good vibes in the house... He had become quite involved with the Hare Krishnas.

To begin with, it was fine. They were a mixture of English and Americans, they were young, they had children, and they all dressed in orange. They lived on the top floor of rhe house and every morning at seven they would start cooking pungent-smelling food. Then they would take over the dining room for their chanting; I joined them sometimes, but I began to feel that what I considered my home had been taken over. And while they may have been spiritual and belonged to a spiritual group, at the end of the day they were just people. And as they were big fans of the Beatles and George, they were quite nice to me, particularly Syamasundasa, the head of this Hare Krishna chapter. George liked having them there—and what he wanted counted more than what I wanted.

On March 19, twelve million viewers watched Radha Krishna Temple on BBC’s Top of the Pops singing their new smash hit, “Govinda.” Top of the Pops was now a one-hour show, and we huddled around the TV in Georges bedroom and watched the Beatles do “Let It Be,” then Simon & Garfunkel with “Bridge Over Troubled Waters.” We were the ninth act on the twelve-act show, with Tony Blackburn introducing “The Radha Krishna Temple!” All of us whooped and hollered, and George was over the moon.

Fourteen devotees are assembled onstage, clean and radiant, in matching saffron robes. Yamuna, in purple sari, is seated in the front row with a microphone in her right hand, her left hand balancing Prabhupada’s prominent photo. To her left is Yogeshvara on harmonium, to her right I’m sitting playing esraj, and beside me is Gurudas in his yellow pilot’s cap playing kartals. There are eight devotees seated behind us, and Dhananjaya and Mondakini dance, one of them on each side of the stage. On a separate stage, with cameras cutting in and out, are nine young ladies in colorful miniskirts (Pan’s People) doing the Swami two-step, arms raised in the air.

RKT is followed by The Band, which does “Rag Mama Rag,” and then Andy Williams.

We settled into a routine: the men worked outside every day (spring was upon the Chiltern Hills and the land had turned a luscious green) while the women cooked, chatted with Pattie, and took care of the kids. Phil Spector had been hired to mix the final version of the Let It Be album at Abbey Road Studios, so George was often in London until the wee hours. Some mornings, he’d drop into the temple, have a little kirtan, maybe walk around the grounds with us a bit to inspect the work, then go off to his sleeping bag and crash, not to be seen again for a few days.

On the morning of March 23, I told George I had to go into London and would be at Bury Place all day. He replied, “OK, I have to be at Abbey Road today. Maybe I’ll pop by and say hello.”

Later that day, George did pop his head in the office door at Bury Place—winded from the four-story dash upstairs from his illegally-parked Porsche. He walked in just as I was hanging up the phone.

“Hare Krishna, Shyamasundar! In the neighborhood. Thought I’d-why the glum look?”

“Jeez, George. I just got off the phone with a guy at the Paris temple. What a hassle. The cops are giving the devotees a real bad time on the streets over there, you know—jail and all that stuff.”

“Not like England, is it? I mean, here you’ve been on the telly, Hare Krishna on Top of the Pops, lots of publicity—people know who you are.”

“Yeah, but over there, lots of devotees are joining up, that’s all right, but they’re practically sleeping on top of each other in a hotel room about an hour from central Paris. They have no money. It’s like the people over there just don’t know anything about us.”

George grinned, and said, “Well, why don’t we go over there and tell ’em? I mean, I’m one of the Fab Four. They’ll listen to me, won’t they?”

“Well, yeah...” Not going to take this too far. He’s laughing now, so I’m not sure if he’s serious.

“We’ll have a press conference, get all the reporters to come: ‘Look here, Krishna is God, you are His servants, so why not help out?’ You know, all that bit,” George says.

And he did it. He picked up the phone, called Peter Brown at Apple and, in minutes, arranged the whole thing for tomorrow night—a press conference in Paris with George Harrison, first Beatle to return to France since Les Beatles played six sold-out shows at the Olympia Theater in February of ’64.

A few of the French devotees joined us in the brilliant Grand Salon at Maxim’s Restaurant on Rue Royale, fanciest place in Paris—Baroque furnishings, chandeliers, hordes of reporters and paparazzi from all over Europe mingling with the formally-dressed creme of French society. It’s a Tuesday night and raining in Paris, but a huge crowd gathers, waiting for Beatle George. An hour passes, the large room roars with gossip and bonhomie.

“Hey, isn’t that Brigitte Bardot?”

“Who’s that guy, Umapati?”

“That’s the famous French singer Georges Moustaki.” Hund reds of elegant people mill around, greeting each other, excited to be at this historic event.

Ignored are the six or seven Hare Krishnas huddled on a banquette by the far wall. Madame Nhu and Madame Sukarno (former first ladies of South Vietnam and Indonesia, respectively, and both exiled to France) sit at a little glass table to my right, a white poodle in Madame Sukarno’s lap. Two-year-old Saraswati capers around my knees—sees the little dog and makes a grab for it. The dog yips and leaps, the first ladies yip and leap, the table upends, and champagne and canapes splash all over the Madames’ five-thousand-dollar Chanel gowns. It’s total chaos.

Then a hush falls as George appears behind us and softly says, “Hare Krishna.” The room takes on a strobe effect as flashbulbs pop. One gorgeous gal grabs George by the arm and says through all the clamor, “Hello, George.”

“Hello, Frangoise.”

“I heard you were here,” she says, “so I came to see you. But I can only stay for a minute. I’m on my way somewhere.” (Umapati whispers that she’s Francoise Hardy, a French pop star—and I see a little more than a glimmer of recognition in George’s eyes.)

Suridas, a handsome French devotee with little Sartre-style granny glasses, stands and tries to quiet the room. The reporters are trying to out-yell each other with questions for George, like, “Why are you here?” “Are the Beatles breaking up?” With that resigned French look of “What can I do?” on his face, Suridas turns back to us with his arms outstretched, baffled.

Then Kulashekar bangs the mridanga a few times and Gurudas clangs his hartals. Yamuna begins to pump the harmonium, and leads us into the Hare Krishna mantra. We sing as loud as we can, and slowly, the crowd quiets as we dance and whirl under the chandeliers.

Meanwhile, Maxim’s house manager hastens over to Gurudas and me and we make a quick plan.

Loud shouting resumes as we grab Suridas, make our way to a slightly stunned George, and guide him into a small storage room for tablecloths and linens, finally closing the door against the flashbulbs and writhing bodies. There are two doors, one at each side of the small room.

“How you holding up, Shyamsundar? Welcome to Beatle-mania.”

“Whew! A lotta reporters out there, George. What we’re gonna do: you sit here behind this little table. Serge, that’s Suridas, he’ll translate, here on your right. I’m here on your left, and Gurudas will stand by that door and let three reporters in at a time—they’ll sit there, on the other side of the table. The most important newspapers will come in Erst, Le Monde, Herald Tribune, France-Soir, five minutes for each group—then they go out this other door, and three new ones come in. All right with you?”

After a lot of shouting and pushing, three reporters are seated in the little room and the door is shut.

Reporter: Are the Beatles breaking up?

GH: This isn’t about the Beaties at ail, this is about Krishna.

Reporter: Are the other Beatles coming to Paris?

GH (nervous now, raising his voice over the growing din of people trying to break down the doors): The Hare Krishna people, the International Society for Krishna Consciousness, is looking for a new temple here in Paris and they need your—

Reporter: Why is your hair so long now?

GH (pointing at me): I think their hair is more interesting.

Woman reporter (impeccably coiffed, outshouts the others): I’m from Elie magazine. What about this unlawful sex. You mean, they don’t cheat on their wives?”

GH: If they’re not married, they don’t have sex at all.

She gasps in horror.

CRASH! They’re breaking down the doors! One door splinters off its hinges, the other bends inward in a U-shape. I look around wildly for an exit. There’s no way out! Over my shoulder, I see a small door in the wall behind us, a couple of feet off the floor—I open it. It’s a laundry chute, gleaming aluminum, one floor down to a pile of white linens.

“George! Through here. Hurry!”

Whoosh, and George goes down like a slide at the playground. I look over behind me to see both doors shattered and lying on the floor, Gurudas fighting a losing rearguard battle with twenty wild journalists who have stormed the room, before I too plunge down the chute...

... to land in a big soft pile of white tablecloths, a half-dozen astonished laundry maids frozen in disbelief. I’m right behind George, who is hotfooting it through steaming pipes and laundry machines. Behind me the first reporters hit the chute—I’m on my way, racing behind George, out the basement door to the street behind Maxim’s.

A black Citroen taxi sits idling in the cold night, parking lights on. I whip open the back door and George jumps in. “Allez!” I say, “Tout de suite, Hotel George Cinq!” The driver says, “Non, monsieur, cest occupe! Occupe!” just then, the mob hits us. I jump out, George locks the doors. I begin pushing paparazzi away from the cab, but now they’re lying down on the pavement in front of the cab, so I’m dragging them out of the way as, at last, the driver realizes he had better just get out of there. He flashes his headlights, floors it, and careens off down the foggy street past Place de L’Opera.

Later, we relax and laugh in George’s fancy suite. Were laughing so much that tears are running down our cheeks. “Did you see that reporter who went over backwards in his chair!”

“Yeah, and the other guys jumping over him?”

“Did you see Gurudas slam the guy with the big TV camera against the fireplace-Whack!”

Were too excited to drink much strawberry citron or munch on the Camembert slices brought up to us by room service. George says, “It was like the chase scene from A Hard Days Night!” and we crack up yet again.

But were all a bit sad that not much got accomplished. George’s mission, to inspire Parisians to find the Krishnas a temple space, has largely failed. George, lying on his huge kingsize bed, says, “Well, at least now they know I’m with you.”

Sometime after midnight we make our way by Metro to the tiny hotel room far away from central Paris, where maybe twenty devotees squeeze together to sleep on the floor. Just before dawn, I open my eyes to a terrible headache; I’m so groggy I can barely move my limbs. Everyone else is still deep in sleep—and I smell the toxic chemical smoke of burning plastic in the dark room. Rising, I find what’s left of a melted plastic colander someone left on the water heater to dry, stumble to open the one window in the place, and yell at everyone to wake up. Several devotees roll over and grumble, but some are unconscious and difficult to rouse. A half-hour more and maybe we’d have all been dead.

For Prabhupada’s pioneers, it was just another hard day’s night.

Umapati later sent us news clippings. Not much was said about Krishna; the news was mostly about what George looked like now, his beard and long hair, who was there and what they were wearing. One article said, “Fran^oise Hardy came in her new maxi skirt, but was so horrified by the chanting that she left after only five minutes.”

Prabhupada wrote, “I have also received the newspaper cutting in which the picture of George has come out and it appears to be very nice because he has taken to chanting Hare Krsna Mantra along with you. The heading ‘An Anti-Alcoholic Beatle at Maxims’— does it mean that George has given up alcohol?”

**\* \* \***

George is such an amazing guy. His intelligence and sensitivity hit you immediately. He’s always right there. He sizes you up in a second, so keen are his perceptions, and his response to you will be immediate but simple, unsophisticated. I don’t know if being the receiver of millions of peoples’ adoration has anything to do with it, but George exudes a special magnetism and confidence. He’s as much at home with the queen as he is chatting with Devadatta about hibiscus. When he hears something true, that’s it, he accepts it and fits it into the jigsaw-puzzle cosmology he has constructed, bit by bit, to understand how the universe works. Imagine what it must be like to have been separated in adolescence from a middle-class life, then isolated in a bubble and shot to the very peak of fame, wealth, power, and beauty for the rest of your days. He’s on the most powerful magical mystery tour that anyone could imagine. George has deeply examined, “Why me, Lord?” He understands the concepts of karma, rebirth, and using this lifespan to understand what it’s all about—and the importance of spreading such knowledge to others—because he has realized what he has understood.

George is writing songs, doing his own thing now, no Beatles stuff. He has surrounded himself with a group of practicing Hindu monks, who live under the same roof with him and who are similar in age and background. These monks are living their spiritual culture, and are disciplined and obviously happy, the real thing.

Looking back, I think that having us around, even if we didn’t see him all that often, helped nurture George’s fledgling convictions—he couldn’t help but hear the chanting day and night—and reinforced his great determination to make his spiritual voice heard after the Beatles split. In the privacy of his own home, George became one of the Krishna gang. He said in public interviews more than once, “Let’s face it. If you’re going to have to stand up and be counted, I figure I would rather be with these guys than with those guys over there.”

In muddy boots and rain-soaked jeans, George turns off the fuel feed to his flamethrower, blows a tendril of smoke from the end of the barrel, then points his finger at me and laughs: he’s not used to seeing me in pants—my only pair of too-short, brown corduroys. “Those trousers!” he says, howling with wicked glee as I aim my flamethrower at him and try to strike the igniter. “Let’s go up to the kitchen and have a cuppa.”

**\* \* \***

Chris O’Dell, from the Apple office, an American gal, soon moves into Friar Park to help George organize plans for his first solo album and maybe to keep Pattie company. Chris writes in her book:

At first we thought it would be a great adventure, and we were happy for George because now he’d have people to chant with whenever he felt like it, and they’d be a big help with the gardens. I was a little in awe of the Krishnas. From the moment they moved in, a saffron glow seemed to permeate the entire house. They created a temple in the former chapel, filling the entire room with candles and incense burners. I often went there to meditate. Shyamasundar and others would often join us in the kitchen for a cup of tea, thanking us with little prayerful bows, their orange robes flowing softly around them, before leaving to join George in the garden. Every morning we’d awake to find a prayer or spiritual reminder on the kitchen blackboard, reminding us of our earthly bondage and our essential spiritual nature.

Convincing George to write a foreword to Krsna Book becomes a saga in itself. Back in early March, George had finally decided he would do it; if I wrote it up first, then he could add or delete. We tossed the foreword back and forth—I’d write a draft, George would change it, we’d make another draft—over and over, with fewer changes each time. Finally, we had a version that George liked and he signed it, sent it over to Derek Taylor, and Apple released George s foreword to Krsna Book as an official Radha Krishna Temple press release dated March 31,1970.

Everybody is looking for KRSNA.

Some don’t realize that they are, but they are.

KRSNA is GOD, the Source of all that exists, the Cause of all that is, was, or ever will be.

As GOD is unlimited HE has many Names.

Allah-Buddha-Jehova-Rama: All are KRSNA, all are ONE. God is not abstract; He has both the impersonal and the personal aspects to His personality which is SUPREME, ETERNAL, BLISSFUL, and full of KNOWLEDGE. As a single drop of water has the same qualities as an ocean of water, so has our consciousness the qualities of GOD’S consciousness... but through our identification and attachment with material energy (physical body, sense pleasures, material possessions, ego, etc.) our true TRANSCENDENTAL CONSCIOUSNESS has been polluted, and like a dirty mirror it is unable to reflect a pure image.

With many lives our association with the TEMPORARY has grown. This impermanent body, a bag of bones and flesh, is mistaken for our true self, and we have accepted this temporary condition to be final.

Through all ages, great SAINTS have remained as living proof that this non-temporary, permanent state of GOD CONSCIOUSNESS can be revived in all living Souls. Each soul is potentially divine.

Krsna says in Bhagavad-gita: “Steady in the Self, being freed from all material contamination, the yogi achieves the highest perfectional stage of happiness in touch with the Supreme Consciousness.” (VI, 28)

YOGA (a scientific method for GOD (SELF) realization) is the process by which we purify our consciousness, stop further pollution, and arrive at the state of Perfection, full KNOWLEDGE, full BLISS.

If there’s a God, I want to see Him. It’s pointless to believe in something without proof, and Krsna Consciousness and meditation are methods where you can actually obtain GOD perception. You can actually see God, and hear Him, play with Him. It might sound crazy, but He is actually there, actually with you.

There are many yogic Paths—Raja, Jnana, Hatha, Kriya, Karma, Bhakti—which are all acclaimed by the MASTERS of each method.

SWAMI BHAKTIVEDANTA is as his title says, a BHAKTI Yogi following the path of DEVOTION. By serving GOD through each thought, word, and DEED, and by chanting HIS Holy Names, the devotee quickly develops God-consciousness. By chanting

Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna  
Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare  
Hare Rama, Hare Rama  
Rama Rama, Hare Hare

one inevitably arrives at KRSNA Consciousness. (The proof of the pudding is in the eating!)

I request that you take advantage of this book KRSNA, and enter into its understanding. I also request that you make an appointment to meet your God now, through the self-liberating process of YOGA (UNION) and GIVE PEACE A CHANCE.

All You need is Love (Krisna) Hari Bol.

George Harrison

I send George’s final version to Prabhupada on April 9. He writes back on April 12, “Please convey my thanks to him.”

But then George tells me he wants to change something. He wants to add a quote from Swami Vivekananda: “What right has a man to say he has a soul if he does not feel it, or that there is a God if he does not see Him?” So I write to Prabhupada again on April 11 about this proposed change:

It hurts me to see this change, quoting Vivekananda, but this is what George wants and, as we have had several arguments lately over spiritual questions, I’m afraid if we do not include this change it would seriously affect our relationship. He still thinks there is “God” beyond Krishna, that God having a “body” denotes impurity, and he is still unwilling to accept Bhagavad-gita as the Supreme Authority. He has been hurt by so many bogies that he is very much confused so that when I say that I speak from authority (meaning yourself), he is still doubting. I’m not sure I can ever convince him of Krishna’s supremacy, but if you can help by presenting some capsule proof in your letter as to how there can be nothing beyond the embodied Krishna, perhaps this would convince him ... Anyway, I am trying, by Krishna’s Grace.

Your ignorant servant, Shyamasundardas

On April 15 Prabhupada replies: “Regarding your further statement in the matter of George’s introduction, I think as he is not settled up in his conclusion, he wants to change it, either we should wait for his final decision or it may not be added. For the time being I am holding its publication.”

Now it gets complex. Most of my waking moments are consumed by thinking how to diplomatically get this intro completed by George without Vivekananda. We argue back and forth. Finally I use the example, “Well, if it were your book, George, would you like to have Paul McCartney glorified this way?” Yeah, he got it, and said OK, leave it out. So I write again to Prabhupada on April 20 that I have talked with George and there will be no further changes, no statement by Swami Vivekananda, adding, “We are continuing nicely here and actually it is very blissful to chant and work for Krishna in the sunshine &C open air, and at night to speak with George & chant with him. He is feeling the effects of chanting very nicely. His next record will be entitled “Chanting the Names of the Lord.” John was here the other day. Your worthless servant, Shyamasundar.”

Prabhupada replies on April 24:

Regarding George’s foreword, you write to say that certainly there will be no more changes. Do you mean that the changes which you have already suggested in your letter dated 11th April with quotation from Swami Vivekananda are to be added? We cannot add any quotation from Swami Vivekananda... This man (Vivekananda) has no understanding either of soul or of God and still he has posed himself as a Swami to mislead so many innocent persons.

You cannot learn music from a carpenter. If you want to learn music, then you have to approach an expert musician. So how he can learn about God from persons like Vivekananda who had no preliminary knowledge of the soul, even? George’s foreword as it was originally sent can be published with little editorial changes, but in no case can the quotation from Swami Vivekananda be placed in my book. You have already admitted in your letter dated 11th April that it hurts you to see this change, so please let me know clearly what you want me to do. If you think that George’s foreword will help the selling of the books, then it may be published as it was originally sent.

The foreword was published as originally sent, without Vivekananda. At Friar Park, the cavernous Victorian tile kitchen became the focal point of our daily lives. All activities centered on the kitchen—it was the only warm space in the house. An organ, drum set, and a couple of small amps were sitting around in there, and several guitars stood on a rack. Our schedules were vastly different. Terry Doran, George’s friend from his Liverpool days, was always around—it was his job to oversee the work at Friar Park. And most mornings we’d find Pattie and Chris in the kitchen too. But George was often traveling or in a recording studio all night somewhere, so he usually made only brief appearances in the early afternoons before again dashing off to London.

He had some big flags made to be run up a pole atop the highest tower at Friar Park. On days when he was troubled by something, we’d see a skull and crossbones flag—George’s pirate persona—but on most days we’d see a red OM on a white background flapping in the wind, meaning all was well at Friar Park.

Living in such close proximity, our vastly different lifestyles created some conflicts. Our early-morning noise—kartals, drums, conch shells, and lively singing—began to wear on the house’s other inhabitants, as did the smells from our spicy cooking and congestion at the washing machine and telephone. Four young children running around in all this space without close supervision were also bound to cause trouble. One day, I was felling some dead trees down by the lake. When I shut down the chainsaw I heard Saraswati screaming up by the fountain in front of the main house, maybe two hundred yards away. I ran up there and saw my daughter frantic, pointing at the twenty-foot circular pond. Two-year-old Dhruvananda was floating face down, absolutely still, in the two-feet of water! I jumped in and pulled him out. Then, laying him on his stomach on the lawn, I pounded him on the back until he spewed up water, algae, and snot. He was alive but white as a sheet. The ambulance arrived from Henley, and the kid survived to raise hell another day. Chris O’Dell describes this period in her book:

“What is wrong with them? They can’t even take care of their own children!” Pattie was thoroughly exasperated and ready to kick them out right then and there, but George wanted them to stay... What made it even worse was that George was spending more and more time with them. George walked around all day with his hand in his prayer bag, quietly repeating the words, “Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare.” He was disappearing into spiritual bliss while Pattie, Terry and I continued to gather in the kitchen for our nightly ritual of wine, gossip and belly laughs, telling stories about the Krishnas’ latest antics and trying not to worry too much about George. After a while, though, the laughter was forced... because we were trying to find a way to cope with the realization that George would rather be with the Krishnas than with us.

On April 9, Radha Krishna Temple appeared on Top of the Pops for the fourth time—a repeat of their March 19 “Govinda” show. We followed Simon & Garfunkel and were introduced by DJ Jimmy Savile as the fifth act on the show, just ahead of Stevie Wonder (“Never Had A Dream Come True”). “Let It Be” and Elvis’s “Don’t Cry Daddy” are near the top of the charts this week, but at #23 with a bullet, we’ve outsold Canned Heat and Juicy Lucy.

On April 10, 1970, Apple officially announced that the Beatles were splitting up, and the whole world was shaken by the news. It was like the day President Kennedy was killed or when Buddy Holly and the Big Bopper went down—the end of a glorious era.

On the same day, George Harrison gave an interview to BBC-1 for their religious TV series Fact or Fantasy? This segment was called “Prayer and Meditation.” George spoke for nearly twelve minutes about his spiritual beliefs, with particular emphasis on the Krishna movement. He makes only one mention of the Beatles in a statement that has become famous:

People will say I’m the Beatle who changed the most, but really that’s what I see life is about. The point is, unless you’re God conscious, then you have to change, because otherwise it’s a waste of time... And the whole thing is to change; try and make everything better and better.

At Friar Park, when the weather was too nasty to work outside, I scraped thick, blistered layers of old paint off the marvelous oak panels surrounding the fireplace in the kitchen. George is a Pisces, or as he was fond of saying, “a bit this way, a bit that way.” A man of opposites. And sometimes I’d catch him in a different mood. Affection and good humor so governed our relationship that this odd moment stands out in my mind: I’m in the kitchen at Friar Park, another foggy morning starting to light up outside; I’m up on the ladder scraping paint, when George drives in from an all-night session, grumps in through the back door, guitar case in hand, strained and exhausted. He walks to the base of the ladder and says, “You know, I’ve been thinking about this, and I don’t believe Krishna in His human form is the highest manifestation of God. Vivekananda says ...” (Whoa, so early in the morning, George?) “So I think Prabhupada’s got it all wrong. There’s something beyond Krishna.”

Trying to control myself: “Well, George, then why does Krishna say in Bhagavad-gita that there is nothing superior beyond this human form that Arjuna is seeing?”

“Prabhupada just doesn’t have a clue about it.”

That’s it. I’m off the ladder in a bound, fist clenched and ready to slug him. I push him back a step instead, then I pull away and start to march out.

“Hey, Shyamsundar!” I turn, fuming, my fist still balled. But he’s got a silly grin on his face and then we both have a laugh as he says, “Just checking.”

I write to Prabhupada, seeking guidance on how to convince George, when he gets into one of his moods, that Krishna is the Supreme Person. Prabhupada writes a very long letter in reply. Here’s the gist:

You write to say that George is still not convinced that there is not something beyond the form or body of Krishna. He is thinking that beyond Krishna there is an unmanifest God. This is quite natural for persons who try to understand God by their own endeavor.

God cannot be understood by such ascending process of speculation. Take for example that there was one Mr. John who was the great-grandfather of the great-grandfather of George.
