So if George wants to know about this Mr. John, his forefather, is it possible for him to know about Mr. John by speculation? He has to know about Mr. John by family history spoken by his father or grandfather. This is descending process. Similarly, God is the Supreme Father, so if anyone wants to know about God, he must try to understand Him through the history of creation. This is called Parampara system. This Bhagavad-gita is spoken by God Himself, and that is the best source of knowing about God. He cannot be known by our limited exercise of the senses. The difficulty of understanding God as person is that as soon as there is conception of person we think of God as a person like us. Therefore, in the Vedas, the personality of God is described in different ways which are distinct from our personalities.

It is not very difficult to understand that a big business man is sitting in his room and thousands of workers are engaged in different activities under his direction. So practically the business man’s brain is working, but when we see the business man alone in the room we see that he is not working. So we have to understand God from authoritative sources, otherwise how we can understand such vast subject matter delineating about God?

So George’s question that there is something beyond Krishna is answered by Krishna in the Bhagavad-gita that there is nothing superior beyond Himself.

Hey, this is exactly what I told him.

On April 23 the RKT performed again on Top of the Pops, for the fifth and last time. It was a repeat video from the previous two shows. The Moody Blues appeared first on the show, with their song “Question”:

I’m looking for someone to change my life.

I’m looking for a miracle in my life.

And if you could see what it’s done to me

To lose the love I knew

Could safely lead me to

The land that I once knew.

To learn as we grow old

The secrets of our souls.

Next up were The Who with “The Seeker”:

I asked bobby dylan

I asked the beatles

I asked timothy leary

But he couldn’t help me either

I’m lookin for me

You’re lookin for you

Were lookin at each other and we don’t know what to do

The young people of 1970 were looking desperately for the answers to life’s mysteries. We sang “Govinda” at the end of the show. Maybe a few souls out there got the message.

The April 24 edition of Life magazine came out, with Jim Lovell of Apollo 13 on the cover and a two-page article called, “The Beatles Decide to Let It Be—Apart.” There are four photos, one of each Beatle with his new solo passion: Paul with his children on his farm in Scotland, John with Yoko on their peace campaign, Ringo on the set of his new film, and George, surrounded by nine devotees having kirtan on the lawn at Sydenham. Now the world knew.

On April 28, George flew to New York City for business meetings and to hang out with his pal Bob Dylan for a few days. Derek Taylor accompanied George, and in his book As Time Goes By, describes the trip:

George doesn’t drink a lot and today he had a bag of Krishna beads made and presented by Shyam Sundar-Das, one of the London Krishna temple who was presently engaged on carpentry work at Friar Park, George’s Gothic revival pile at Henley on Thames in Oxfordshire where another Krishna devotee, Deva Data (from Westmoreland) was putting the thirty-acre garden straight... What with chanting the maha mantra—Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare, Hare Rama, Hare Rama, Rama Rama, Hare Hare—and counting the beads, there wasn’t going to be much call for whiskey on this flight... George saw the New York Krishna devotees dancing and chanting on Sixth Avenue and it felt like home.

When asked in an interview, on May 1, for New York’s WABC-FM radio, if there was animosity between Yoko Ono and Paul’s wife, Linda, George said, “Ah, I don’t know. I don’t think about it, you know. I refuse to be a part of any hassles like that. You know, Hare Krishna, Hare Krishna, Krishna Krishna, Hare Hare. And it’ll all be OK, you know.”

Springtime arrived at Friar Park and I wandered the grounds half-dizzy from the lush beauty I saw in the English countryside. Occasionally, when I was chopping brush, I’d come across some partially buried epigram carved on a stone or a rusty, old plaque stamped with words of wisdom. If George was around, I’d run to the house to tell him about it. He’d usually drop what he was doing and come racing to see it.

Our weeks of hacking and burning and chainsaw work had scarcely made a dent in the overgrown foliage, so we decided to concentrate on the “alpine gardens” surrounding the “Matterhorn,” not far from the manor house. Back in the 1890s, Sir Francis Crisp had hauled hundreds of tons of limestone to Friar Park, by river barge and mule, to build a replica of the Matterhorn so that he could gaze at the Swiss Alps from his master bedroom. The mountain stood many stories high, and had a door on the back side. You opened the door, switched on the lights, and descended a flight of stairs to the dock by a stream. You could get into a boat and row underground through replicas of famous caves, like the Blue Grotto of Capri or Kentucky’s Mammoth Caves, and exit about five hundred feet downstream into daylight, on a lake.

Pulling weeds up on the Matterhorn, some days I would look out and see George sitting on a wall or on the steps by the house, and I’d walk over and sit with him. Sometimes a point of philosophy was troubling him, so he’d ask me things like, “I was reading in Bhagavad-gita something about devotees—that even if they do something bad, even then, if they are Krishna conscious, doing something for Krishna, they’ll be OK. What does that mean?”

I was shy to preach to George, first because I didn’t want to come off as a preachy guy—I wanted him to think of me as someone very much like him, just trying to understand what it was all about—and second, because I didn’t really know that much about Krishna myself. So I’d say something like, “Yeah, well Prabhupada explains that we’ve spent so many millions of lifetimes in the material world performing so many sinful activities that if we finally do get information about Krishna and start trying to serve Him, still, that service may be tinged by occasional fall-downs, old habits—but that’s OK, if you’re trying. Like you unplug an electric fan, the blades keep spinning a while.” On this occasion I ran into the house, brought back a Bhagavad-gita, and read a verse to him: “Even if one commits the most abominable action, if he is engaged in devotional service he is to be considered saintly because he is properly situated in his determination.”

Prabhupada would send hints in his letters to me, things to pass on to George, such as how to fit spiritual practice into his busy life-like in this letter of April 12: “Regarding how George may continue his meditation, that meditation may be done by keeping one picture of Krishna, keeping his mind concentrated on the picture of Krishna and softly chanting the Hare Krishna Mahamantra as long as he likes. That will complete both his meditation and Krishna Consciousness perfectly according to the last verse of the sixth chapter of Bhagavad-gita which says, ‘And of all yogis, he who always abides in Me with great faith, worshiping Me in transcendental loving service, is most intimately united with Me in Yoga, and is the highest of all.’”

Sometimes when I’d see George around the house or somewhere on the grounds he would have a guitar in his hands. He’d often play and sing a new tune, try it out on me, or he’d be working out a chord progression or some lyrics in his head. “What’s another word for ‘contaminated’?”

“‘Polluted’?”

“Yeah, polluted.”

Then he would sing, stretching his voice to impossible ranges until it cracked-and then giggling, those big teeth showing, and shaking his locks around. He told me he was going to start recording his own album in the last week in May.

One day, we sat on top of the newly-cleared Matterhorn, and George asked me, “D’ya think I should shave me head, you know, be initiated by Prabhupada like you lot? Don’t know if I could do that.”

Prabhupada very quickly answered George’s doubt in a letter: “I think George does not require to become my formal disciple because he is already more than my disciple. He has sympathy for my movement and I have all blessings for him.”

These intimate times were sweet but short-lived, as George went on his private way with a mission all his own: confident, competent, complete in himself, he roamed his halls and gardens. He rarely told me where he’d been or where he was going, but each meeting, each conversation we had, seemed to take up exactly where it left off, even if it was many days later.

Srila Prabhupada was becoming worried that our little group had isolated ourselves from the other devotees in London, and he was right: so much physical work, hacking brush and planting alpine flowers, started to blur the definition of devotional service, especially for Murari and Lilavati, who just don’t quite get why we are at Friar Park. We all occasionally went into London by train to see Radha-Londonishvara and go on sankirtan, and we carried on with kirtans in the chapel at Friar Park, but it was a constant battle for me to keep the others fired up. “Don’t you see? It’s a very great service to inspire George. He’s writing and recording songs about Krishna that will change the world, and he likes us around for inspiration. This is the best thing you could be doing right now. Don’t you remember Prabhupada’s letter?” And I’d quote it to them:

“I am so glad to learn that George has said, ‘I don’t want to make nonsense records any more.’ So when he says that he does not wish to produce nonsense this does not mean that he has to close his business. On the other hand, he will get greater opportunity for producing finest transcendental records which are still unknown to the world. This version of George I consider very valuable.”

But in the third week of May everyone got a break.

On May 20, the Beatles premiered their long-awaited film Let It Be to a capacity crowd in the thousand-seat London Pavilion theater in Piccadilly Circus, a short walk from Bury Place. Apple sent over tickets, and fifty devotees made Krishna’s presence felt at the celebrity-studded, red-carpet affair.

Hollywood Music Festival 1970 was touted as the British Woodstock. For two days, May 23-24, some of the biggest bands in the world would play for 70,000 people camped out on a hundredacre farm near Newcastle-under-Lyme in Staffordshire, 160 miles northwest of London. The Grateful Dead would be making their first UK appearance. The program also included Traffic, Black Sabbath, Ginger Baker’s Air Force, Family, Jose Feliciano, The

James Gang—and leading things off, Radha Krishna Temple. By now our band was formed of mostly English devotees, who opened the shows with rousing kirtans on the 23rd and 24th. After their performances, they walked around through the vast crowd of young seekers chatting and selling BTGs. The mostly-new devotees cut their teeth and then found their wings as they spread Krishna consciousness to the unwashed masses.

Mick Fleetwood of Fleetwood Mac was married to Pattie’s sister, Jenny Boyd, and the couple would often visit the kitchen at Friar Park, yakking into the night. Another visitor was Donovan, who had become sort of the English Bob Dylan, with hit records like Sunshine Superman and Hurdy-Gurdy Man. Donovan had accompanied the Beatles on their 1968 voyage to meet Maharishi in India, and he was a very spiritual person. In late May, Donovan requested that three or four devotees join him at a commencement party at his alma mater, Stowe School, in Buckinghamshire, where he would be the guest speaker and sing some songs. He sent his limo, and Malati, Saraswati, Jai Hari, and I climbed in. When we pulled into the driveway at Stowe, we could see this was no ordinary educational institution. Greek colonnades lined the front of the palatial buildings—it was obviously a school for children of nobility and means. We had a wonderful evening on the lawns, leading kirtan with Donovan, and the students loved seeing their own alumnus minstrel chanting Hare Krishna with his lovely voice.

My parents stopped over in London for two nights. They were on a vacation to Egypt to see the pyramids. I met them at their hotel near Heathrow, and brought them to Friar Park, where they toured the house and grounds and met George and Pattie. George was very cordial, and I finally sensed my parents’ pride that maybe, yes, their son had amounted to something after all.

On May 26, George Harrison started recording his album All Things Must Pass. Now we scarcely saw him at all.

We were into June now, and I mostly pulled weeds all day, smelled the newly opened blossoms, and suffered the sweet pangs of spring fever—slowing down and feeling bored without much to do. I was blissfully unaware of the tensions building in Georges life with his music, with Pattie and Eric Clapton, whatever. Sometimes I climbed the tower where the flag-of-the-day was flying—an om or a skull and crossbones, depending on Mr. Pisces' mood. From the tower I watched late-spring cumulus clouds make shadow plays across the rolling, checkerboard Chiltern Hills and dreamed of another rhino chase with Prabhupada, over the far horizon. What was I doing? Why was I at Friar Park? Why wasn’t I out with the other devotees spreading Krishna consciousness like I should be? I reasoned that I was there to be the part-time spiritual companion to one of the worlds great rock stars, working in the man’s garden. But was that important?

One morning at about 6 a.m., I’m eating some porridge and a Mercedes sedan roars up and parks outside the kitchen door. George, Billy Preston, and Klaus Voorman clump in, boisterous and full of energy from an all-night recording session for All Things Must Pass. Billy immediately sits down at the organ in the kitchen and thumps out this wild gospel melody. George and Klaus grab guitars and plug them into the little amps. They sing, “My Sweet Lord, oooh My Lord, Halle-lu-ya, Halle-lu-ya.” George breaks in with “На-re Krish-na, На-re Krish-na!” Pretty soon were all dancing around and singing at the top of our lungs, the rising sun bathing us in rosy golden light. “This one’s gonna be on the new album,” George says.

I realize yes, it’s important to stay here at Friar Park.

**\* \* \***

Meanwhile, Radha Krishna Temple was busy performing at summer rock festivals all over Europe. On Sunday, June 13, we played the Cambridge Free Festival on Coldham’s Common in Cambridge.

We performed with such showstoppers as Blonde On Blonde, Paul Brett & Sage, Comus, and Melton Constable (hey, who else did you expect to play for free?). On June 19, some devotees fly to Sweden to open the “Festival of the Midnight Sun,” at Mantorp racetrack, about 150 miles southwest of Stockholm. Lilashakti, one of three Swedish sisters—triplets, who had all become devotees—overcame her shyness and introduced Radha Krishna Temple to thousands of people. Chuck Berry, Elton John, and Canned Heat were among the other performers.

Throughout the month of June, George was recording his album All Things Must Pass. George told me he had so many songs that he had decided to make it a triple album. Except for “My Sweet Lord,” which George had run by me one morning in the kitchen, I had no idea what George was doing with the album, but I knew instinctively that his words would stamp the name of Krishna into the minds of millions of people around the world. My gut feeling was so what if the other devotees out here at Friar Park are antsy? By living here, being around George all the time, we are spreading Krishna consciousness in the best way—through the mass media. This is what Prabhupada had told me to do.

Years later, I saw the lyrics to “My Sweet Lord” written in George’s own hand in his autobiography, I-Me-Mine. There’s a “Hare Krishna!” postage stamp pasted in the right upper corner of the page, a stamp I had given him at Friar Park.

One song was called, “Beware of Darkness,” a tune George says, “was written at home in England during a period when I had some of my friends from the Radha Krishna Temple staying.” “Awaiting On You All” is the most obvious song emanating from our stay at Friar Park: “By chanting the names of the Lord and you’ll be free.” George explains in I-Me-Mine: “\[A\]nd the maha-mantra has been prescribed as the easiest and surest way for attaining God realization in this present age. (It can also rebuild rhe atoms in the body and make your life sublime!) Hari Bol.” The last track on the album, “Hear Me Lord,” is George’s pure plea for Krishna’s divine mercy:

Forgive me Lord, please

Those years when I ignored you

Forgive them Lord

Those that feel they can’t afford you.

Help me Lord, please

To rise above this dealing

Help me Lord, please

To love you with more feeling.

At both ends of the road

To the left and the right

Above and below us

Out and in—

There’s no place that you’re not in

Won’t you hear me, Lord?

By the summer of 1970, we started receiving letters from Prabhupada, such as this one:

Regarding the three couples staying with George, temporarily we may take advantage of it, but as soon as possible the devotees must live together and execute our Krishna Conscious business in right earnest. We shall not act anything which has no connection directly with Krishna Consciousness.

So the pressure was on. Yes, by living with George we were having some positive influence (maybe more than we knew), but we were young, full of idealistic energy and a thirst for action. We missed the other devotees, and Prabhupada now seemed to want us to return to London. George wasn’t home much, and our work efforts were puny compared to what was really needed to restore the house and grounds. The days dragged on. Another Ratha-yatra was coming up, and I was needed in town to help build the rath car.

Prabhupada set July 5, 1970 as the date for the second annual London Ratha-yatra. Groan. I really didn’t want to build another rath car by myself again. But we had saved much of the wood from last year’s car in the basement at Bury Place, so it wouldn’t be so difficult to bolt it back together again. And Murari was there to help me now, along with a number of other London devotees. They had somehow acquired a little fenced yard near King’s Cross Station where we could build Lord Jagannath’s monster machine.

At first, Murari and I commuted back and forth to London every day on the small, narrow gauge train between Henley and Paddington Station. From there we’d ride the tube to the building site. Later, we took our sleeping bags with us and on most nights crashed at Bury Place. Using mostly wood from last year’s car, we went for quick and showy. We assembled the car around a truck chassis, with the wheels closer together this year for strength—to avoid last year’s collapsing wheel disaster—and enclosed them in the lower deck. The upper deck was reduced to the size of the lower deck, 15' x 20', to make the cart less bulky and easier to maneuver through narrow streets. We eliminated the diagonal timbering, allowing twenty simple, ten-foot 4" x 4"s to support the upper deck and extend three feet above to serve as posts for the railing. We had time to paint the wood this year too—a basic color scheme of pink and yellow with little blue flower petals and ducks all around.

And we went for tall, very tall: a four-cornered framework was extended upward using 1" x 6" boards to a height of thirty-six feet above the upper deck. At the very top we mounted a four-foot amalaka sun lotus crown carved from a single block of Styrofoam and spray-painted gold. At fifty-four feet, this was the tallest rath car yet made. The vertical frame was eight feet square at the bottom and tapered to four feet square at the top, cross-braced by 1" x 6" boards at four-foot intervals. The entire frame was covered by crimson satin that had been cut with vents, so that when the cart moved the air billowed inside and gave the canopy a round shape, like a huge linga.

London swelled with devotees as the Ratha-yatra neared. More than a hundred devotees from around Britain, France, Germany, and America now occupied every square inch at Bury Place. On the day of the parade, thousands of people—businessmen in suits, long-haired hippies, housewives, tourists, and devotees—pushed, pulled, and followed the rath car from Marble Arch to Trafalgar Square, escorted by fifty London bobbies. Dozens of flags and banners flew above the crowd, including Malati’s huge “Prabhupada’s World Sankirtan London” banner with its orange with black letters. We couldn’t find an elephant, but we tethered two peacocks to the lower deck. The parade went off pretty much without a hitch this time, except we misjudged some overhead utility wires near Piccadilly Circus and the entire parade had to halt, coppers blowing whistles, the crowd bunching, the chanters roaring ever louder—Jagannath is stuck!—while I climbed to the top of the framework, raised the wires with a long pole, and we inched beneath.

At the base of Nelson’s Column the devotees reenacted “Lord Chaitanya’s Civil Disobedience” drama, with Dhananjaya as the evil Kazi who orders his soldiers to stop the devotees’ kirtan and stomps on their papier mache drum. Gurudas narrated, while Yogeshwara, as the Golden Avatar, explained to the Kazi the meaning and purpose of sankirtan, and all was well in the end.

The next day, the pages of London’s major newspapers were filled with descriptions of our Ratha-yatra. They had never seen anything like it in London. Normally, when this many people gathered for a parade it was some kind of protest march. This was a holy celebration. We sent news clippings to Prabhupada, and he sent copies to other ISKCON centers, saying, “You see, they have conquered London.”

Yamuna: Prabhupada said, “Make the Deities so beautiful that everyone will be attracted to them. Make it very opulent ” So that was the direction that we were heading—to try to make the Deities visible for those who were witnessing them for the first time, the passersby, and to increase the love of the devotees already familiar with the Lord. How nice to see people who would never have had access to Lord Jagannatha.

George was in Liverpool with his dying mother, Louise, so he missed the Ratha-yatra, but he remarked when he got back that he’d seen us on the telly up there, and he was proud of us. Prabhupada wrote: “I am very glad to learn that this time you made Lord Jagannatha s car as strong as a warship. I can see from the picture it is very sturdy and tall also.”

A couple of days after the Ratha-yatra I was totally burned-out and needed a break, so I grabbed Jai Hari and went to Amsterdam for a week to see if we could spark some interest in a temple there. Due to its lax drug laws and long tradition of social freedom, Amsterdam had become an international center for the hippie culture. Hippies gathered by the hundreds at a number of large clubs—sort of like the Arts Lab in London—clubs with names like Cosmos, Fantasia, and Paradiso. Amsterdam was the beginning of the “hippie trail,” which stretched through Europe and the Middle East, Turkey and Afghanistan, and on to India.

Jai Hari and I got permission to hold nightly kirtans in a room at the Cosmos, and at night we crashed in buildings occupied by squatters and heroin addicts. In Amsterdam we met a fellow named Burton Greene, who was playing piano at a local jazz club. Turned out he was an old friend of Mukunda’s. He had even met Swami Bhaktivedanta in early 1965 on the Lower East Side of New York. Burton was happy to hear of the Swami’s success and seemed like a very spiritual person. We gave him a copy of Who Is Crazy? and wished him well.

For our kirtans at Cosmos, we prepared big bowls of yogurt and cut fruit to pass out in paper cups. As we were leaving one night—paper cups finished—we were met on the stairs by an enthusiastic young American who looked hungry. Richard Slavin, who later became Radhanath Swami, describes our encounter in The Journey Home:

It was there \[Cosmos\] one night that I met a tall American with a partly shaved head, ponytail, and white robes. “Do you want some spiritual food?” he inquired. I nodded meekly. “Cup your hands.” When I did, he dumped a huge ladle of fruit salad mixed with runny yogurt into them. As the concoction dripped down my arms, I stood there perplexed.

“What do I do now?” I asked.

“Eat it!” He laughed, taking his leave. Little did I know that destiny would have us meet again, thousands of miles away in a setting beyond my wildest imagination.

I wrote “Wish you were here” on a Dutch postcard depicting tulips, cows, and windmills, and mailed it to Prabhupada in L.A. He replied on July 19, “I received one postcard from you in Amsterdam. I was expecting your letter since a long time, but I could understand that you were very busy with Rathayatra festival... So let us meet by Sankirtana. I also do not feel separation from my Guru Maharaja. When I am engaged in His service His pictures give me sufficient strength. To serve the Spiritual Masters word is more important than to serve him physically. Please try to open as many branches as possible in European countries.”

**\* \* \***

In mid-July, George arranged for us to start recording a Radha Krishna Temple LP. We had to come up with about five more songs besides the “Hare Krishna Mantra” and “Govinda” tunes, which we’d use as lead songs on the album. It was time to practice our music together again, get ready for this album, so the devotees at Friar Park had to move back to London—no way around it. Gradually, we pulled up stakes and left Friar Park with a little sadness, but good feelings all around. I gave George the small wooden temple I had built, and he installed it in his own temple room somewhere upstairs in that vast house. Prabhupada wrote, “Regarding George Harrison, I think you have sufficiently helped him. He has also rendered service to Krsna, undoubtedly, so gradually, surely he will improve in Krsna consciousness—there is no doubt about it.”

Under Yamuna’s instruction, we learned the words to the “Sri Guruvastakam” prayers. These were written in the mid-seventeenth century by the great devotee Visvanath Chakravarti Thakur. Mukunda came up with a catchy melody, and we recorded this song one afternoon at the Dean Street Studios in Soho, a short walk from Bury Place. Murari sang lead and George played guitar. “Govinda Jaya Jaya” was a no-brainer, so we laid that track down the same afternoon.

A few days later, we returned to Dean Street studio with Eric Clapton, and he and George laid down lilting guitar harmonics to the Ishopanishad prayer (om purnam adah purnam idam) on a separate track behind Yamuna’s otherworldly voice.

It took us a long time to learn new songs. Sometimes we’d show up at the studio barely knowing what we were going to do—and George was busy with his own album—so recording sessions for the RKT album were spread out over a couple of months. Our album was finally released in May 1971, long after most of its performers had moved from London.

In retrospect, the four-month period between April and July 1970 was probably the peak of George’s active involvement with Krishna consciousness and one of the most exhilarating and inspired times of his life. He had a new house, a new career, new friends, and a new direction for his creative energy. For me this period was the consummation of a deep bond of friendship with one of the rarest, coolest personalities on the planet. In some ways, too, though, it marked the end of our days of innocent fun together as we each went on to grapple with our demons. In a way, this was the end of an era. All things must pass.

**\* \* \***

Relieved from the constant train travel back and forth between Henley and London, Malati and I found a flat for rent near Marylebone Station and moved into the sunny, third-story, southfacing room in late July. The room was painted a bright shade of blue and had a view of trees, and it was just a short tube ride from Bury Place. Our spirits lifted after such a long exile in the countryside.

Malati: I’m not by nature a morose person, but going on in that situation at Friar Park—I wasn’t particularly happy there. I got lethargic. What was I supposed to be doing there? I was so glad to go to the temple again and to meet some of the new devotees. There were so many when we returned. The girls were crammed into one room in triple bunk beds. We could participate in devotee activities again.

On August 1, some of us flew down to Avignon in southern France for the three-day Festival d’Aix-en-Provence. An article announcing the event appeared in Billboard magazine on July 25,1970:

EUROPE’S “BIGGEST” FEST SET NEAR PARIS AUG. 1-3

PARIS—What its organizers claim will be continental Europe’s biggest pop festival yet takes place near the historic city of Aix-en-Provence Aug. 1-3.

Booked for a reputed $200,000 bill are Arthur Conley, Johnny Winter, Flock, Renaissance, Wallace Collection, Mungo Jerry, Deep Purple, Colosseum, Radha Krishna Temple, Les Irresistibles, Pete Brown, Pacific Drift, Titanic, Leonard Cohen, Majority One, Triangle, Labyrinthe, Dynasty Crisis, Aleph, Rare Bird, and Family.

The festival is being promoted by Paris impresario Jean-Pierre Rawson in association with General Clement, a leading figure in Aix-en-Provence’s prestigious classical music festival. It is expected to draw 150,000, with an all-in entry ticket for the three days of about $10.

Concerts will be broadcast on the independent Europe No. 1 radio network’s pop music program, Campus, and by Radio Monte Carlo.

There will be 50 hostesses, 200 tents, mobile surgical and maternity units, and a 20-line telephone center.

Like most such huge events in those days, chaos reigned. Many of the acts were delayed, so we took the stage on the afternoon of August 1 and held kirtan for hours to calm the unsettled crowd. Then we jumped off the stage to lead a conga-line kirtan out through thousands of seminude campers sprawled over several acres in the boiling sun.

Malati: This was the first time I had ever seen bottled water, maybe Evian or Vichy. It had been raining, and everyone was covered with mud—no clothes, just mud. Then the sun came out boiling hot. We had to go onstage with all these drunk and naked people. The people were wild because no one else had showed up yet. We went first. But when the chanting started it was very profound. Everyone joined in, and we snaked around the festival grounds in a long Hare Krishna line—hundreds of people. And when we came back to our tent there was a big stack of water bottles people had left for us out of gratitude.

By the end of July 1970, for seven months Srila Prabhupada had scarcely stirred from his Los Angeles “world headquarters”— by far the longest time he’d stayed in one place since arriving in America in 1965. When our group had left for London two years ago, in August 1968, there were only three ISKCON branches; now there were thirty-four temples around the world. Each center, big or small, operated on a single principle: the devotees’ love for their spiritual master. Devotees who operated these centers were mostly in their twenties, some even younger, and unschooled in corporate management, business affairs, public relations, and legal issues. They were eager to please Prabhupada. That’s all. What a unique enterprise was this Krishna Consciousness Society—that in just two years’ time its members had opened so many branches in major cities without much preparation or planning or even a clear idea how to proceed or what would happen once they had an address and a phone number. These pioneer devotees relied completely on letters of guidance from Srila Prabhupada. No wonder the Swami had to sit down in one place to sort out this wild-child Society he had created.

In L.A., on July 28 Prabhupada met with a few senior disciples and drafted his “Directions for Management,” a document that divided the world into twelve zones and appointed twelve zonal secretaries which he called Governing Body Commissioners to manage ISKCON’s affairs. The next day he established the Bhaktivedanta Book Trust (BBT) to manage the publication and distribution of his books. In his founding documents he stated that BBT profits should be used to build temples in India—in Mayapur, Vrindaban, and Jagannath Puri.

Prabhupada also decided to initiate several senior and reliable men into the renounced order of sannyasa to help him expand the preaching mission. To set an example for them, he announced that he would himself be going to Japan. He wrote to Brahmananda and Gargamuni on July 31, “In order to set example to my other Sannyasi students I am personally going to Japan with a party of three other Sannyasi students. Although it is beyond my physical condition, still I am going out so that you may learn the responsibility of Sannyasa.”

He added, “It is the injunction of the Vedas that the Spiritual Master should not be treated as ordinary man even if sometimes the Spiritual Master behaves like ordinary man. It is the duty of the disciple to accept Him as Superhuman Man... We are on the battlefield of Kuruksetra—one side Maya, the other side Krsna. So the regulative principles of a battlefield, namely to abide by the order of the commander, must be followed. Otherwise it is impossible to direct the fighting capacity of the soldiers and thus defeat the opposing elements. Kindly therefore take courage. You are capable in so many ways. Let things be rightly done so that our mission may be correctly pushed forward to come out victorious.”

On our side of the Atlantic, we had no idea that these major changes were taking place.

In early August, after returning to London from the festival in the south of France, I got a letter from Karandhar informing me that I was now the Governing Body Commissioner for the UK, and that I was being called to a meeting of GBC members to be held at New Vrindaban, West Virginia, from August 22-25 “for the purpose of organizing for the maintaining and expansion of ISKCON.” Furthermore, Karandhar explained, “Srila Prabhupada has said that our Society is like a family, it must be run on love and trust,’ and so our prime mission of life is simply to execute the will of our spiritual master.... Never before in the history of the Western world has such a meeting been called for the benefit of all living entities.”

What?! I don’t know what all this means, but I’m excited now: Prabhupada called me! I get to travel to the US, do something important for a change. I’m thrilled to be singled out by Prabhupada, but also dread the responsibility. I’m just not a management guy. I’m more of a dreamer and methodical builder, not a people person the way managers have to be. But hey, it’s a break from the humdrum of London, and I start to raise money for my round-trip ticket.

On August 9, Prabhupada left L.A. for Honolulu on his way to Japan. When I heard that, I was greatly disappointed that he wouldn’t be in New Vrindaban for the meeting. But I supposed I should attend the meeting anyway.

On August 14, from Tokyo, Prabhupada wrote to each of his new GBC men: “Please accept my blessings. Now that I have formed this Governing Board Commission, the twelve members should each act as my zonal secretaries. So kindly keep me informed of your zone’s activities at least once a week or once fortnightly. In this way I will be encouraged and can give you direction and inspiration.”

**\* \* \***

New Vrindaban covers several hundred acres of grasslands and wooded hills in the Appalachian Mountains of West Virginia. In that third week of August 1970, more than a hundred devotees were camped out in this remote, wide-open space. I was thrilled to be reunited with old friends like Hayagriva, Brahmananda, Gargamuni, and Hamsaduta. And I was happy to meet Rupanuga, Bhagavan, Karandhar, and Satsvarupa—devotees I’d known only through correspondence.

Over the next few days, besides our GBC meetings, we will also celebrate Janmashtami (Krishna’s birthday) and vyasa-puja (Prabhupada’s birthday). I was sort of the new kid on the block—in those days, London was a faraway place.

I felt a little awkward, like a stranger to these American guys, who had been working closely together for years. Their most frequent question to me was, “Hey, what’s George Harrison really like?”

There was an air of conspiracy, almost paranoia, at New Vrindaban. It seemed Brahmananda and Gargamuni had misread Prabhupada’s statement about “Superhuman Man” and were preaching that Prabhupada was Krishna! “I don’t know, maybe he is. D’you suppose?” everyone whispered. The scuttlebutt was that the Swami had heard these offensive rumors and was now so angry with Brahmananda and Gargamuni—and by default, with all of us— that he had left us, fled to Tokyo, and may never come back! Wow. It was a lot to absorb.

Each day we met in a small cottage on top of a hill to hash out these weighty matters. There was a flurry of telegrams and phone calls back and forth to Tokyo. Finally, Hayagriva announced that Prabhupada had severely chastised Brahmananda and Gargamuni, and that he was not Krishna. Furthermore, the Swami explained that he should be honored as much as Krishna, because he is Krishna’s confidential servant, and that his words to us should be regarded as Krishna’s instructions. Prabhupada is not leaving us—we are his sons and daughters, and in fact, he would soon be calling devotees to help him in India. There was a great sense of relief, the matter was cleared, and we were inspired more than ever to work together as a team to please Srila Prabhupada. Within a few moments, our agony had turned to ecstasy.

On August 29, Prabhupada flew from Tokyo to Calcutta.

On the same day, I hopped on a plane from Pittsburg to Portland, Oregon, to visit my folks in Salem for a few days before returning to England. Karandhar drove up to Portland from L.A. with Danavir and a few other devotees to start a new ISKCON temple there, so I spent two days showing them around the Rose City.

**\* \* \***

There had been some buzz for more than a year that Prabhupada wanted to form a World Sankirtan Party and travel with it around the globe, and especially in India. Swamiji had mentioned this plan to me only once—in a letter back in June 1969: “I still maintain the idea of a World Sankirtana Party, so we have got men now... who can form a nice group of Sankirtana, at least with 25 heads. If we travel, beginning from London, around the world and again come back to London, surely this World Sankirtan will be a factual event.”

I kind of forgot about the idea, put it in the back of my head, thinking, well, something like this is for sankirtan guys, those who like to play mridangas and dance on the street. I’m a householder. I like steady, goal-oriented service. I just wouldn’t fit into such a traveling party.

But what I didn’t know is that Prabhupada had been writing many letters to others—like this one to Hamsaduta, dated April 26, 1970: “Now our next program will be to lead a Sankirtana Party round over the world. It is already advertised in India from mouth to mouth that I am coming there next year with forty disciples.”

And by the time I return to London in early September, rumors are flying: Prabhupada wants to bring some devotees to India to help him there. I’m thinking, he’ll only want big sannyasis and brahmacharis with him, for sure. Meanwhile, looks like I’m stuck here in the UK for another lousy winter, babysitting—as GBC, I’ll be stuck in an office managing cash flow and book sales and devotees’ unending personal problems.

George is busy making All Things Must Pass, so I almost never see him. Our popularity as a rock group has faded, and the RKT does only one more small engagement, on September 17, at the Campaign for Nuclear Disarmament “Peace Pie” Concert at Fairfield Hall, Croyden, along with Roy Harper, Trees, and the Spontaneous Music Ensemble.

Prabhupada is now in India, and we hear he’ll probably remain there for a long time. I dwell constantly on what’s next for me. Should I take some devotees to Birmingham or Manchester or maybe Amsterdam and open a center? Should I get a job? I even think of going to L.A., maybe try to convince show-biz personalities about Krishna.

So you can imagine the great surprise and relief I feel when a letter arrives from Prabhupada, dated September 20, requesting twenty devotees to come to India immediately, and Malati and I are on the list.

Malati: I was upstairs at Bury Place, working on the sewing machine, and I got electrocuted—thrown across the floor—and within moments someone came up with this aerogram and Prabhupada was instructing us to come to India, so he gave a list of twenty names. We were on the list—and by hand he wrote, “And baby Saraswati.”

The list: Gurudas and Yamuna, Shyamasundar and Malati, Giriraj, Durlabh and Kausalya, Dinananth, Rishikumar, Rebatinandan,

Srimati, Madri dasi, Chidananda, Gopal, Nanda Kumar, Aravinda, Madhudvisa, Madhavananda, Hamsaduta and Himavati.

I’m of two minds: should I stay in the UK and deepen my relationship with George, remain behind as his trusted guide and keep him pumped-up in КС? Or should I take off on a wild adventure, a rhino-chase with my spiritual master and the merry pranksters? It was no contest, really. First, it was Prabhupada’s desire that I join him, and second, we would finally see the land of Krishna, the source of the philosophy and culture that now shaped our lives. But I was in great anxiety as we prepared for the trip—and Lord knows how long we’d be gone—that I was leaving George out there alone. How would he fare?

Over the past few months, George and I had had many discussions about the crowded conditions at Bury Place and “finding an ashram for the devotees.” He had experienced the pleasures of country living at Friar Park, and told me he would find a similar place for the Krishnas. When I see him in late September to say good-bye, George again promises to look for suitable properties while I’m gone—he’ll liaise with Mukunda, he says, who will be remaining in London.

The Bury Place temple is in good hands with Mukunda holding down the fort. I’m not sure how things will work out, though, because Janaki is unhappy and wants to go back to America—her sister and best friends are all running off to India and leaving her behind.

The plan was that the American devotees would fly to London, join the European contingent, and we would all fly together to India. Mukunda and Tamai Krishna had befriended an Indian gentleman in London named Dipak Jashapara, who managed an airline (Brothers Air Services Company, or BASCO) that flew between Brussels and Bombay. It was arranged that we would all meet in Brussels on October 1, 1970, and travel together to India on Jashapara’s BASCO airlines. The fare was cheap—about £50 per person one-way.

We re elated, and start making preparations, like getting visas and shots. Gurudas buys a slide projector. Were allowed one suitcase each, so we pack a few clothes (no pants-dhotis only), kartals, a toothbrush, a couple of books. Malati insists we bring her giant “Prabhupada’s World Sankirtan London” banner, so we roll that up and stuff it into a suitcase. On the way to Brussels it finally sinks in: We’re on our way to India! To meet Prabhupada!

This will be the first time we are in the field with our spiritual master, together, on a big mission. We’re on safari, on the campaign trail, a maximum camp-out. In San Francisco: we lived there and the Swami came to visit. In London: we did reconnaissance, built a fort, and Prabhupada came to stay for a while and attend our programs. But in India: Srila Prabhupada will command every aspect of the expedition from day one. This is a bold new mood in our spiritual master’s ever-changing eternal play: Srila Prabhupada leading the charge, showing us how to do it. We will be an army now, with our commander-in-chief, the ultimate general, calling the shots and, like a father, caring for our everyday needs.

Prabhupada is going global! The great international rhinochase begins!
